Chapter 4 Schizophrenia Spectrum and Other Psychotic Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 4.1 From Dementia Praecox to Schizophrenia: trace how the concept was built, from Morel, Hecker and Kahlbaum through Kraepelin and Bleuler to Schneider's first-rank symptoms and the modern narrow definition.
  2. 4.2 Positive Symptoms: Delusions, Hallucinations and Disorganisation: describe what delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech and disorganised or catatonic behaviour look and sound like in schizophrenia.
  3. 4.3 Negative and Cognitive Symptoms, and the Symptom Dimensions: explain the negative and cognitive symptoms and the models that group the symptoms into types and dimensions.
  4. 4.4 Diagnosing Schizophrenia: DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11: apply the three systems' criteria, durations and subtypes or specifiers, and tell schizophrenia apart from the conditions that mimic it.
  5. 4.5 The Rest of the Spectrum: distinguish brief psychotic, schizophreniform, schizoaffective and delusional disorders, acute and transient psychoses, shared psychotic disorder, catatonia and the secondary psychoses.
  6. 4.6 Who Develops Schizophrenia and What Happens Next: describe the epidemiology, phases, course and outcome of schizophrenia, the predictors of a good or poor outcome, and what the WHO studies found in India and other countries.
  7. 4.7 Genes, Development and the Brain: evaluate the family, twin, adoption and molecular evidence, the neurodevelopmental hypothesis and the structural and functional brain findings.
  8. 4.8 Neurochemistry: Dopamine, Glutamate and Serotonin: explain the dopamine hypothesis and its revisions, the four dopamine pathways, and the glutamate and serotonin hypotheses.
  9. 4.9 Psychological, Family and Social Explanations: evaluate the psychodynamic, cognitive, family, stress and social accounts of schizophrenia, including expressed emotion and the stress-vulnerability model.
  10. 4.10 Treating Schizophrenia: Antipsychotic Drugs and Beyond: compare the typical and atypical antipsychotics, recognise their side effects, and outline the physical and psychosocial treatments that complete care.

Weightage. 79 of the 128 questions mapped in this book (61.7%) test this chapter, 34 of them from real past papers. 4 of its 10 sections are High yield.

On 19 May 1930, in a hospital in Lansing, Michigan, a woman gave birth to four baby girls. They were identical quadruplets: all four had grown from a single fertilised egg that had divided and divided again, so they carried exactly the same genes. Births like that are extraordinarily rare, and this one made the newspapers. As small children the sisters took singing and dancing lessons and appeared together on stage in touring shows, four matching girls who delighted their audiences. Behind the public image the home was a very different place. Later accounts describe a family that was highly controlling and abusive, and parents who did not treat the four alike. One daughter, Hester, was treated as the problem child: her parents described her as a habitual masturbator, and she had more social problems than her sisters as she grew up.

The illness came first to Hester, who had severe symptoms by the age of 18. The others broke down one after another in their early twenties, and all four had developed the illness by the age of 24; Myra, the last, was not hospitalised until about six years after Hester's illness had begun. All four were diagnosed with schizophrenia. From 1955 to 1958 a team at the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH) in the United States, led by David Rosenthal, studied them intensively: their histories, their family, their bodies and their minds. The results filled a book, The Genain Quadruplets (1963), edited by Rosenthal. To protect the family the researchers gave the sisters pseudonyms whose initials spell the name of the institute: Nora, Iris, Myra and Hester. The surname they invented, Genain, comes from Greek words meaning, roughly, "dire birth" or "dreadful gene". Decades later the psychologist Allan F. Mirsky and his colleagues estimated that the chance of identical quadruplets all developing schizophrenia was about one in 1.5 billion.

What made the Genains famous was not that all four fell ill but how differently the same illness ran in four people who shared every gene. Hester was the most severely ill: she deteriorated and spent most of her adult life in hospital. Iris spent long, chronic periods in hospital, and her state ranged from severe catatonic withdrawal to spells of marginal adjustment when she was able to leave hospital. Nora was admitted again and again and managed only a marginal life outside. Myra, in contrast, was never readmitted after her first episode; she married and raised a family. When Mirsky's team retested the sisters at the age of 66, in a follow-up published in 2000, their performance on neuropsychological tests had stayed stable or even improved since they were first tested in their twenties.

Hold on to that picture: identical genes, one disorder, four different lives. It tells you at once that genes load the dice for schizophrenia but do not decide everything, because even sisters raised in the same house had different experiences, beginning in the womb and continuing in the way their parents treated them. It also tells you something about the concept of schizophrenia itself. The first psychiatrist to define this illness believed that it usually ended in mental ruin, and called it a "dementia". The Genain sisters, like the patients who made later psychiatrists doubt him, show a far more varied disorder. This chapter follows that disorder from the name to the symptoms, the diagnosis, its relatives on the spectrum, its course, its causes and its treatment. Remember the Genain sisters; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

4.1 From Dementia Praecox to SchizophreniaHigh yield · 17 Qs

There is no blood test for schizophrenia, no brain scan that settles the diagnosis and no single symptom that every patient has. So where did the category come from, and why do the diagnostic manuals draw its boundaries where they do? The honest answer is that schizophrenia is a concept built by clinicians over about a hundred and fifty years. Each builder asked a different question. Emil Kraepelin asked what the illness does over time. Eugen Bleuler asked what its psychological core is. Kurt Schneider asked which symptoms a doctor can recognise reliably at a single interview. The authors of the modern manuals asked how to make two psychiatrists in different cities give the same diagnosis to the same patient. Their answers are layered inside today's criteria like the strata of a rock, and once you can see the layers, the criteria in Section 4.4 stop looking arbitrary. Figure 4.1 sets out the whole story; this section tells it.

Before the name: Haslam, Morel and démence précoce

People who heard voices, believed strange things and withdrew from the world were described long before anyone gave the condition a name: Ayurvedic texts such as the Charaka Samhita describe unmada, madness, and Greek and Arab physicians wrote about mania and melancholia. The long history of how madness was explained, feared and confined, from the temple to the asylum, is told in Book 2, Section 1.2. For our story, two pieces of that history matter. In 1809 the English apothecary John Haslam (1764 to 1844) of Bethlem Hospital in London described, in Observations on Madness and Melancholy, young patients who changed from promising and vigorous to dull and withdrawn, and in 1810 he published a whole book, Illustrations of Madness, about a single patient, the tea merchant James Tilly Matthews. Matthews believed that a gang of villains hidden nearby worked a machine he called the "Air Loom", whose rays forced thoughts into his head, drew thoughts out of it and moved his body against his will. Keep that description in mind: more than a century later, Kurt Schneider would make exactly these experiences the centre of the diagnosis. At about the same time in Paris, Philippe Pinel (1745 to 1826) was describing similar patients.

The first label came from France. Bénédict Augustin Morel (1809 to 1873), a physician who spent his career in French asylums, used the phrase démence précoce ("early" or "premature loss of mind") in passing in his clinical studies of 1852, and more often in his textbook of mental diseases of 1860. He used it for young people, previously bright and lively, who in their teens sank into apathy, lost their intellectual powers and never came back. Notice what Morel meant by it. Démence précoce was not a disease with its own causes and symptoms; it was a description of an end state, the mental ruin that normally came at the end of a long illness, arriving tragically early in life.

Morel is remembered for a second, darker idea. In his Treatise on Degenerations (1857) he set out the degeneration theory: the belief that mental illness is the sign of a hereditary taint that grows worse with each generation, so that nervous parents have insane children, whose own children are idiots, until the family line ends in sterility. Degeneration theory was enormously influential in late nineteenth-century psychiatry. It encouraged doctors to look at the whole family and at the course of illness over time, which was useful, but it also bred deep therapeutic pessimism (what could anyone do against a curse of the blood?) and, later, it fed the eugenics movement. Keep both legacies in mind, the attention to course and the pessimism, because they flow directly into Kraepelin.

Hecker, Kahlbaum and the clinical method

The next step was taken in an asylum at Görlitz, in eastern Germany. Its director, Karl Ludwig Kahlbaum (1828 to 1899), was impatient with the way psychiatrists classified their patients. Grouping illnesses by what they looked like on the day of the examination was, he argued, futile: a patient might be excited one month, sad the next and stuporous the next, and a cross-sectional label would change each time. The proper unit was the whole clinical course: how the illness began, how it unfolded and how it ended. This "clinical method", classifying by course and outcome as well as by symptoms, is the most important idea in this subsection, because it is the method Kraepelin later used to build dementia praecox.

Kahlbaum's younger colleague at Görlitz, Ewald Hecker (1843 to 1909), applied the method in 1871 to a group of patients whose illness began around puberty. He named the condition hebephrenia, after Hebe, the Greek goddess of youth. Hecker's patients were silly and giggling, with an emotional tone that did not fit the situation; they rambled, adopted odd mannerisms, behaved in a foolish, childish way and then, often quite quickly, sank into a lasting weak-mindedness. Three years later, in 1874, Kahlbaum published his own monograph, Die Katatonie oder das Spannungsirresein ("Catatonia, or tension insanity"). In catatonia the most striking disturbance is of movement and will: patients who hold strange postures for hours, who let a doctor mould their limbs like wax, who fall mute, resist every instruction or repeat words and phrases over and over, and who may suddenly burst into purposeless excitement. Kahlbaum saw catatonia as a brain disease that passed through stages (phases resembling melancholia and mania, then stupor and confusion) and that typically ended in dementia.

Today hebephrenia survives as the hebephrenic subtype of ICD-10 (called the disorganised subtype in DSM-IV), and the individual catatonic signs are described in Section 4.2. Kahlbaum's belief that catatonia is a single disease, however, did not survive: catatonia is now understood as a syndrome that can accompany schizophrenia, mood disorders and many medical illnesses, and DSM-5-TR notes that most cases of catatonia occur in people with depressive or bipolar disorders, not schizophrenia (Section 4.5). Hecker and Kahlbaum also gave psychiatry several other words still in use, among them cyclothymia. Alongside these two forms, many psychiatrists described patients with chronic delusions of persecution or grandeur, the "paranoid" forms. Table 4.1 lists the pieces that were lying on the table, waiting for someone to fit them together.

Table 4.1 The nineteenth-century pieces that Kraepelin assembled into dementia praecox.
FormDescribed byThe pictureWhat became of it
Démence précoceMorel, 1852 and 1860Bright youngsters sinking early into lasting mental ruinGave the concept its name and its pessimism
HebephreniaHecker, 1871Onset near puberty; silly, incongruous affect; rambling; rapid declineHebephrenic (ICD-10) or disorganised (DSM-IV) subtype
CatatoniaKahlbaum, 1874Stupor, posturing, waxy flexibility, mutism, negativism, excitementCatatonic subtype; now a syndrome across disorders
Paranoid formsMany authorsChronic delusions of persecution or grandeurParanoid subtype, the commonest in ICD-10
Exam trap

Match the person to the word: Morel, démence précoce (French, 1850s); Hecker, hebephrenia (1871); Kahlbaum, catatonia (1874); Kraepelin, dementia praecox (Latin, 1893 to 1899); Bleuler, schizophrenia (1908 to 1911). A favourite swap is to credit catatonia to Hecker or hebephrenia to Kahlbaum: Kahlbaum was the senior partner and described catatonia; Hecker, his younger colleague, described hebephrenia, and the name comes from Hebe, goddess of youth.

Kraepelin: dementia praecox and the great divide

The man who fitted the pieces together was Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926), the most influential psychiatrist of his age. Kraepelin had trained in experimental psychology with Wilhelm Wundt in Leipzig (you met Wundt's laboratory in Book 1), and he brought to psychiatry a scientist's taste for counting. As professor at Heidelberg from 1891 and at Munich from 1903, he kept a card for every patient admitted to his clinic. On it he recorded the symptoms at admission and then, often years later, what had become of the patient: recovered, relapsed, or sunk into a permanent defect. Periodically he went through the whole stack and sorted the cards, looking for groups of patients whose illnesses had ended in the same way. His conviction, taken from Kahlbaum, was that a true disease, a "natural disease entity", would reveal itself not in any single symptom but in a shared course and outcome. Like a botanist who classifies a plant by its whole life cycle rather than by the colour of one leaf, Kraepelin classified a psychosis by its whole history.

He set out his results in the successive editions of his textbook, Psychiatrie, and the concept grew in steps. In the fourth edition (1893) he used the Latin term dementia praecox, "premature dementia", echoing Morel's French phrase, for one of a small group of "psychic degenerative processes", listed alongside catatonia and a paranoid dementia. In a lecture at Heidelberg in 1898 and in the sixth edition of the textbook (1899) the construction was complete. Hebephrenia, catatonia and the paranoid forms, which earlier authors had treated as separate diseases, now became three forms of a single illness, dementia praecox, because the cards showed that all three tended to begin in youth and to end in the same kind of mental weakening. And the whole territory of the major psychoses was divided into two great groups by their course:

  • Dementia praecox began early, usually in adolescence or young adulthood, ran a chronic course, and ended in a lasting deterioration of personality and intellect, which Kraepelin called "mental weakness".
  • Manic-depressive insanity came in episodes of mania, depression or mixtures of the two, with recovery between episodes and no lasting defect, however many episodes the patient had. It included what we now call bipolar disorder and recurrent major depression.

This division is known as the Kraepelinian dichotomy, and Kraepelin's "two clinical types" are among the most examined facts in the whole history of psychiatry. Figure 4.2 shows the two courses he had in mind.

To feel the force of the idea, imagine two patients whom Kraepelin might have met in a modern Indian hospital. Arun was a quiet, studious boy from a village near Madurai. In the first year of his engineering course in Chennai, when he was 19, his marks fell; he stopped going to classes, stayed in his hostel room for days, and told his roommate that the lecturers were sending him messages through the ceiling fan. He was treated, improved a little and went home, but over the next four years he never returned to his old self. Each relapse left him a little flatter, a little more withdrawn and a little less able to look after himself. Vasanthi, now a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Coimbatore, had her first illness at 26: a few weeks of sleeplessness, ceaseless talk, lavish spending and the conviction that she had been chosen to reform every school in the state. Two years later she had a month-long depression in which she barely ate or spoke. Between and after these episodes she has been entirely herself, teaching, joking with colleagues and running her household. Kraepelin's cards would have placed Arun with dementia praecox and Vasanthi with manic-depressive insanity, and they would have done so on the course, not on any single symptom; both, after all, had held beliefs that were plainly delusional.

Kraepelin believed that dementia praecox was a physical disease of the brain, and he speculated that it might be a kind of self-poisoning, a disturbance of the body's metabolism, perhaps connected with the sex glands because it so often began after puberty. He also kept two other conditions apart. Paranoia, in his sense, was a chronic, systematised delusion in a person whose thinking and personality otherwise stayed intact; it is the ancestor of today's delusional disorder (Section 4.5). In his later editions he added paraphrenia, for paranoid psychoses with delusions and hallucinations but little deterioration of the personality.

Why both words turned out to be partly wrong. Kraepelin's name makes two claims, and both have weakened. "Praecox" claims that the illness begins early. It usually does, in the late teens and twenties, but by no means always: many people fall ill in their thirties or later, and women show a second peak of onset in midlife (Section 4.6). "Dementia" claims that the illness ends in mental ruin. Kraepelin's own data never fully supported this. In his own later editions he reported that a minority of patients recovered, and in 1920, near the end of his life, he conceded that the outcome was less uniformly grim than he had claimed in the 1890s and that the two great groups could not always be told apart. Modern follow-up studies show the whole range of outcomes, from full recovery to lasting disability (Section 4.6). Nor is the cognitive impairment of schizophrenia a dementia in the modern sense. It is real and it matters (Section 4.3), but much of it is present before the first episode and then stays fairly stable, rather than marching steadily downhill as it does in Alzheimer's disease (Chapter 10). The Genain sisters, whose test scores at 66 were no worse than in their twenties, are a vivid example.

What survived. Yet Kraepelin's framework has outlived every objection. His insistence on course survives in DSM-5-TR's rule that six months of illness must pass before the word schizophrenia may be used (Section 4.4). His dichotomy survives in the structure of every modern manual, which places schizophrenia and its relatives in one chapter and the bipolar and depressive disorders in others (Chapter 5). And the awkward patients who straddled his line, with both full mood episodes and schizophrenic symptoms, eventually needed a category of their own, schizoaffective disorder (Section 4.5). One more piece of his legacy carries an irony worth knowing. It was Kraepelin, in the 1910 edition of his textbook, who gave the name "Alzheimer's disease" to the brain disease described by his Munich colleague Alois Alzheimer; yet dementia praecox has nothing to do with Alzheimer's disease.

Exam trap

Kraepelin's two clinical types were dementia praecox and manic-depressive insanity (manic-depressive illness), separated by course: deteriorating versus episodic with recovery. Not depression and anxiety, not schizophrenia and borderline personality disorder (the borderline concept came decades later), not obsessive-compulsive disorder and bipolar disorder.

"Dementia praecox" ("premature dementia") is the ancestor of schizophrenia. It is not Alzheimer's disease, not frontotemporal dementia (Arnold Pick's disease) and not bipolar I disorder, whose ancestor is manic-depressive insanity. On dates, sources differ because the concept grew in steps: the term in the fourth edition (1893), the Heidelberg lecture (1898), the full split in the sixth edition (1899); some Indian notes give 1896, the fifth edition. If asked when Kraepelin separated the two psychoses, answer the sixth edition, 1899.

Bleuler and the group of schizophrenias

Kraepelin looked at his patients from the outside and across the years. The Swiss psychiatrist Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939) looked at them from the inside. Before he became, in 1898, professor of psychiatry at the University of Zurich and director of its hospital, the Burghölzli (a post he held until 1927), Bleuler had spent years running the rural asylum at Rheinau, where he came to know his patients unusually well. He talked with them at length, and he was one of the first university psychiatrists to take Sigmund Freud's ideas seriously, hoping that psychoanalysis could make sense of what his patients said and did. Among his assistants at the Burghölzli was Carl Gustav Jung, whose book on the psychology of dementia praecox appeared in 1907. What Bleuler saw did not fit Kraepelin's name. Many of his patients had not fallen ill young, and many did not deteriorate: some improved and lived outside hospital for years, although Bleuler believed that nobody recovered so completely that no trace of the illness remained. If neither "early" nor "dementia" described the illness, what did?

Bleuler gave his answer in a lecture in Berlin on 24 April 1908 and in his great monograph of 1911, Dementia Praecox oder Gruppe der Schizophrenien (Dementia Praecox, or the Group of Schizophrenias; English translation 1950). He proposed a new name, schizophrenia, from the Greek schizein, to split, and phren, mind; colleagues in Zurich had been using it since 1907. He also pointed out, with practical good sense, that a single word would give an adjective, "schizophrenic", which "dementia praecox" did not. The splitting he meant was a splitting of psychic functions: a loosening of the associative threads that normally bind thinking, feeling and will into one coordinated whole, so that thought goes one way, emotion another and action a third. A patient may describe his mother's death with a smile, or say that he longs to leave the hospital while making no move to go. Bleuler did not mean a split into two or more personalities. That condition, once called multiple personality, is dissociative identity disorder (Chapter 7), and confusing the two is the commonest error about schizophrenia in newspapers, films and everyday speech. Notice too the plural in Bleuler's title. He believed that schizophrenia was not one disease but a group of diseases sharing a common psychological picture, a view that the four different illnesses of the Genain sisters, and the research of Section 4.7, would support.

Fundamental and accessory symptoms. Bleuler's most influential move was to rank the symptoms. Fundamental symptoms, he said, were present in every case and at every stage of the illness, though sometimes only faintly; they were what made a case schizophrenia. Accessory symptoms might or might not appear, came and went, and also occurred in other illnesses: hallucinations, delusions, catatonic signs, and acute states of confusion or excitement. This is the reverse of how most people (and many students) picture schizophrenia. The voices and the delusions, the most dramatic features, were to Bleuler secondary, because a fever, a poison or a brain disease could produce them too. The fundamental symptoms were subtler, and textbooks later compressed them into the famous four As (Table 4.2):

  • Associations (loosening of associations). The links between one idea and the next are broken, so that the patient's talk drifts or jumps between ideas with no logical connection. Bleuler regarded this as the central disturbance from which the others followed; today it belongs to formal thought disorder (Section 4.2).
  • Affect (blunted or incongruous affect). Emotional expression is flattened, or it does not fit what the patient is saying or experiencing: laughter while describing a tragedy, indifference to people and things that once mattered.
  • Ambivalence. Opposite feelings, wishes or ideas are held at the same time towards the same object, without being resolved: loving and hating the same person at once, wanting to eat and refusing to eat, believing a statement and its opposite. Bleuler distinguished emotional ambivalence, ambivalence of the will (sometimes called ambitendency) and intellectual ambivalence. He coined the word itself, around 1910, and Sigmund Freud, who borrowed it, called it "Bleuler's happily chosen term".
  • Autism. Withdrawal from shared reality into a private inner world, with thinking that follows wishes and fantasies rather than the logic of the outside world. Bleuler coined this word too. It does not mean the neurodevelopmental condition: Leo Kanner borrowed Bleuler's word in 1943 for the children he described, and autism spectrum disorder is an entirely different disorder (Chapter 3).
Table 4.2 Bleuler's four As, the fundamental symptoms of schizophrenia.
The AWhat it meansWhat it looks likeDo not confuse with
AssociationsLoosening of the links between ideasTalk drifts or jumps between unconnected topicsSchneider's thought insertion, an experience the patient reports
AffectBlunted, flattened or incongruous emotionSmiling while describing a deathDepressed mood, which is sad rather than blunted
AmbivalenceOpposite feelings or wishes held together, unresolved"I want to go home. I must never go home."Ordinary mixed feelings; it is not a negative symptom
AutismWithdrawal into a private inner worldAbsorbed in fantasy, indifferent to othersAutism spectrum disorder (Kanner, 1943)

Here is how the four As might sound at an interview. By 23, Arun spent most of his days at home. Asked by the psychologist how he passed the time, he said: "I sit. The fan turns. Turning is the engine of time, and time is money, and money is in the bank, the river bank, where my grandfather used to fish." Each step follows from a sound or a passing meaning of the last word, not from any plan of what he wanted to say: that is loosening of associations. He smiled faintly as he mentioned that his grandfather had died the year before: incongruous affect. Asked whether he would like to visit his cousins at Pongal, he said, "Yes. No. I want to go, and I must not go, and I want to go," and then sat without moving: ambivalence. His mother said that he spent hours writing numbers in a notebook according to a system he would not explain and no longer seemed to notice whether anyone was in the room: autism in Bleuler's sense. Arun also heard voices, but to Bleuler that would have been an accessory symptom; the diagnosis rested on the four As.

Bleuler drew a second, more theoretical distinction, between primary symptoms, the direct product of a disease process in the brain (above all the loosening of associations), and secondary symptoms, the person's psychological reactions to that underlying disturbance, which included much of the content of the delusions and hallucinations. It explains why Bleuler, unlike Kraepelin, believed that what a patient said could be understood and that human contact could help.

A broader concept, and where it travelled. Bleuler's schizophrenia was broader than Kraepelin's dementia praecox in two ways. It did not require early onset or deterioration, and because its fundamental symptoms could be faint, it reached far into the mild end of the spectrum. To Kraepelin's hebephrenic, catatonic and paranoid forms Bleuler added a simple form, a slow drift into apathy and withdrawal without florid symptoms (described by Otto Diem in 1903); these four became the classical subtypes, and ICD-10's "simple schizophrenia" descends from it. He also wrote of latent schizophrenia in odd, eccentric people who never became psychotic, and he believed that the illness was far commoner than diagnosed because of these simple and latent forms. Latent schizophrenia is an ancestor of today's schizotypal disorder (Section 4.5 and Chapter 11).

Bleuler's broad concept travelled especially well to the United States, where psychoanalysis and the "psychobiology" of the Swiss-born Adolf Meyer (1866 to 1950) dominated psychiatry. Meyer taught that mental disorders were reaction types, the reactions of the whole person to the stresses of his or her life history, rather than disease entities, and the first edition of the DSM (1952) accordingly spoke of "schizophrenic reactions". The consequence, as the last part of this section shows, was that by the 1960s American psychiatrists were calling schizophrenia a great deal that their British colleagues called something else. Bleuler's other legacies are just as visible today: his blunted affect and autistic withdrawal are the ancestors of the negative symptoms (Section 4.3), and his loosening of associations is the core of formal thought disorder (Section 4.2).

Exam trap

Bleuler's four As are loosening of Associations, blunted or incongruous Affect, Ambivalence and Autism. Auditory hallucinations are not one of them: hallucinations and delusions were Bleuler's accessory symptoms. The look-alike As are distractors: alogia, avolition, anhedonia and asociality are the later negative-symptom list associated with Nancy Andreasen (Section 4.3; her negative-symptom scale also rates attention); apathy, anxiety and anger are not Bleuler's; aphasia is a language disorder caused by brain damage, amnesia is memory loss, anosognosia is unawareness of one's own illness (Section 4.2), and audible thoughts are one of Schneider's first-rank symptoms.

Ambivalence was coined by Eugen Bleuler, not Kraepelin and not Freud (who borrowed it). "Richmond Fromm" in one paper is a garbled name: Erich Fromm (1900 to 1980) was a social psychoanalyst, and Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (1889 to 1957) proposed the schizophrenogenic mother (Section 4.9). Ambivalence is Bleulerian, not a negative symptom. And schizophrenia means a split between mental functions, never a split personality.

Clinical link

Families in India often first meet the word schizophrenia through films and television, where it is used for a "split personality" or for someone who is violent and unpredictable. Both images frighten families and feed stigma. When explaining the diagnosis, it helps to say what Bleuler meant: the person's thinking, feelings and actions have fallen out of step with one another, rather like an orchestra whose players have lost the conductor, and the person has not turned into someone else.

Jaspers: understanding, explaining and the primary delusion

While Kraepelin counted and Bleuler interpreted, a young psychiatrist at Heidelberg asked a more basic question: how can a doctor know what a patient is actually experiencing? Karl Jaspers (1883 to 1969), who later left psychiatry to become one of the major philosophers of the twentieth century, published Allgemeine Psychopathologie (General Psychopathology) in 1913, at about the age of thirty. The book founded phenomenology in psychiatry: the careful description of a patient's experiences as the patient lives them, set down without theory, so that every clinician can recognise the same experience in the same way. Chapter 2 of this book and Book 2, Section 2.5 are built on his method.

Jaspers distinguished two ways of knowing another mind. Understanding (Verstehen) is grasping, by empathy, how one mental state grows meaningfully out of another: a man who has been cheated becomes suspicious; a mother who has lost her child becomes sad and blames herself. Explaining (Erklären) is the natural scientist's way of tracing a mental state to causes outside meaning, such as a tumour or a toxin. Most of what mentally ill people experience can be understood, at least in part. Some of it cannot. A man glances at a row of parked cars and suddenly knows, with complete certainty and no reason he can give, that he has been chosen to save the world. Jaspers called a belief of this kind a primary delusion: a delusion that arises directly, as if out of nowhere, and cannot be traced back to any mood, experience or earlier belief. A secondary delusion, by contrast, is understandable in the light of something else, such as the guilt-laden beliefs of a severely depressed woman, or the persecutory explanation a man builds to account for the voices he hears. For Jaspers this un-understandability marked a process: something new breaking into a person's life and changing it, as opposed to a development of the personality that can be followed step by step. The primary delusion was, for him, close to the heart of schizophrenia.

Jaspers also set out the classic marks of a delusion (the extraordinary conviction with which it is held, its imperviousness to argument and experience, and the falsity or impossibility of its content) and the forms that primary delusions take (delusional mood, delusional perception, delusional intuition and delusional memory); these are taught in Section 4.2. What matters for this history is his principle that a symptom is diagnosed by its form, the way it is experienced, rather than by its content. The belief "my neighbours are poisoning me" can be an understandable suspicion in a lonely, quarrelsome old man or a primary delusion in a young man who "knew" it the moment he saw a red flower in a pot; what decides is how the belief arose and how it is held. That principle passed directly to Jaspers's younger Heidelberg colleague, Kurt Schneider.

Schneider's first-rank symptoms

Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967) was a German psychiatrist trained in the phenomenological tradition, and with Jaspers he is counted among the founders of the Heidelberg school. From 1931 he directed the clinical work of the research institute in Munich that Kraepelin had founded, and after the Second World War he became professor and dean at Heidelberg. Schneider's problem was a practical one. Kraepelin's criterion, the course, could be known only in hindsight, years after the doctor had to decide what to do. Bleuler's fundamental symptoms were subtle: one psychiatrist's "loosening of associations" or "flattened affect" was another's shyness, eccentricity or poor schooling, so diagnoses built on them varied from doctor to doctor. Schneider wanted a short list of experiences that a clinician could identify with reasonable confidence at a single interview and that, when present in a person with no physical brain disease, would point strongly to schizophrenia. He made no claim that these symptoms explained the illness; he valued them only for diagnosis. He first published the list in 1939. It became known worldwide through his book Klinische Psychopathologie (published under that title from 1950) and its English translation, Clinical Psychopathology (1959).

Schneider called them first-rank symptoms (FRS). The phrase means first in rank of diagnostic weight; it does not mean that they appear first in the illness or that they are the commonest symptoms. The list is usually counted as eleven experiences, which fall into four groups (Table 4.3):

  • Three kinds of hallucinatory voices. Audible thoughts (in German Gedankenlautwerden, "thoughts becoming loud"; in French écho de la pensée, thought echo): the patient hears his own thoughts spoken aloud, as he thinks them or just afterwards. Voices arguing or discussing the patient among themselves, referring to him in the third person ("he", "she"). Voices giving a running commentary on the patient's actions or thoughts as they happen.
  • Thought alienation, three experiences in which the patient's own thinking seems to be under outside control: thought insertion (thoughts that are not his own are put into his mind), thought withdrawal (thoughts are taken out of his mind, often felt as a sudden emptying in mid-sentence) and thought broadcasting (his thoughts leave his mind and spread out, so that other people know them).
  • Passivity experiences, also called delusions of control: four experiences in which feelings, drives, actions or the body itself are felt to be controlled by an outside agency. They are made feelings (emotions imposed from outside), made impulses (urges or drives imposed from outside), made volitional acts (actions carried out by the patient's own body but willed by someone else) and somatic passivity (bodily sensations imposed from outside, such as being burned by rays or "electrified").
  • Delusional perception: a normal perception that suddenly acquires a private, delusional meaning, with no understandable link between the two. It is "two-membered": first the ordinary perception (a red traffic light, a dog lifting its paw, a pen lying on a table), then the unrelated meaning (I have been chosen; the world is about to end).
Table 4.3 Schneider's first-rank symptoms and how they sound in a patient's own words.
GroupFirst-rank symptomWhat the patient might say
VoicesAudible thoughts (thought echo)"I hear my own thoughts spoken out loud, a second after I think them."
VoicesVoices arguing or discussing (third person)"Two men talk about me: 'He is useless.' 'No, give him one more chance.'"
VoicesRunning commentary"A voice describes everything I do: 'Now he opens the door. Now he sits.'"
Thought alienationThought insertion"These thoughts are not mine. Someone is putting them into my head."
Thought alienationThought withdrawal"My mind goes blank in the middle of a sentence. They pull the thoughts out."
Thought alienationThought broadcasting"My thoughts leak out of my head; everyone on the bus knows them."
PassivityMade feelings"This anger is not mine. They are pouring it into me."
PassivityMade impulses"The urge to smash the window came from them, not from me."
PassivityMade volitional acts"My hand lifts the cup, but they are moving it. I only watch."
PassivitySomatic passivity"They send currents through my stomach from a satellite."
Delusional perceptionDelusional perception"The pen on my desk pointed east, and I knew I was the new prophet."

Look at the list as a whole and a single theme appears (Figure 4.3). In almost every first-rank symptom the normal boundary between the self and the outside world has failed. My thoughts, feelings, impulses and actions are felt as not mine, or as no longer private, or as made by someone outside; my own thinking is heard as voices; an ordinary piece of the world speaks to me with a meaning meant for me alone. Psychopathologists call these disturbances of the self, or of the ego boundaries (in German, Ich-Störungen). Now think back to James Tilly Matthews and his Air Loom: thoughts forced in, thoughts drawn out, a body moved from outside. Haslam had recorded a near-complete set of first-rank symptoms in 1810, a century and more before Schneider gave them their name.

Schneider also gave a second list, of second-rank symptoms, which occur in schizophrenia but carry less diagnostic weight because they are common in other conditions too: other hallucinations (including voices that speak to the patient in the second person, and hallucinations in the other senses), sudden delusional ideas that arise without a perception (sometimes called delusional intuitions), perplexity, depressive or euphoric changes of mood, and feelings of emotional impoverishment, that is, emotional blunting. He was explicit that schizophrenia could be diagnosed without a single first-rank symptom, from the second-rank symptoms and the whole clinical picture, and that first-rank symptoms counted only when no physical illness could explain them.

What is not first-rank. For examinations, what is missing from the list matters as much as what is on it. Students tend to assume that anything typical of schizophrenia must be first-rank, and examiners exploit the assumption. None of the following is a first-rank symptom:

  • Loosening of associations and other formal thought disorder. This is Bleuler's fundamental symptom, a feature of the patient's speech judged by the listener, not an experience the patient reports.
  • Ideas and delusions of reference (self-reference): the belief that the news, strangers' remarks or chance events refer to oneself. They are common in schizophrenia, but they are defined by their content. Delusional perception is defined by its form: an ordinary perception followed, without understandable connection, by a sudden new meaning.
  • Second-person voices ("You are dirty") and visual, olfactory, gustatory or tactile hallucinations; smells, tastes and touches in particular should make you look for a physical cause (Section 4.2).
  • Delusions in general, persecutory, grandiose or any other, unless they take one of the forms in the list (passivity or delusional perception).
  • Catatonic signs such as echolalia, echopraxia, stereotyped movements, posturing and catatonic excitement.
  • Negative symptoms such as flat affect, alogia, avolition and anhedonia.
  • Depersonalisation and derealisation: the feeling that one's self or the world is unreal or changed. These are experienced "as if" ("I feel as if I were a robot"), with insight, and they are common in anxiety and dissociative states (Chapter 7). In a passivity experience the patient says, as plain fact, "They are moving my arm."
  • Memory loss and disorientation, which point to delirium, dementia and other organic states (Chapter 10).
  • Obsessional thoughts, which are unwanted and distressing but recognised as products of the patient's own mind (Chapter 6). In thought insertion the thought is experienced as someone else's.
Worked example: sorting Arun's experiences

At 23, Arun told the psychiatrist seven things. (1) "My thoughts come out of my head and spread through the house; everyone knows what I am thinking." (2) "The man reading the evening news was talking about me." (3) "A voice tells me, 'You are dirty, you are useless.'" (4) "Two voices argue about me: 'He should be punished.' 'No, he is only a boy.'" (5) "I saw a crow land on the gate, and at that moment I knew the government had chosen me." (6) "Sometimes I feel as if the world were made of cardboard." (7) His own speech ran "train, rain, brain, the drain of the brain", rhyme leading meaning. Only (1) thought broadcasting, (4) third-person voices arguing and (5) delusional perception are first-rank. (2) is a delusion of reference, (3) a second-person voice (second-rank), (6) derealisation with an "as if" quality, and (7) a clang association, a form of thought disorder that the interviewer observes. Section 4.4 shows how these symptoms are weighed by ICD-10, which can accept a single clear first-rank symptom lasting a month, and by DSM-5-TR, which counts them like any other delusion or hallucination.

Characteristic, but not specific. Schneider's symptoms are strongly associated with schizophrenia, but they are not confined to it. Remember Vasanthi. During her manic episode at 26 she told the doctor that her thoughts were being broadcast on the radio, a textbook first-rank symptom, yet she recovered completely within weeks, and since her later depression she has stayed well on lithium. Her diagnosis is bipolar disorder with psychotic features (Chapter 5), exactly as Kraepelin's course criterion would predict. First-rank symptoms are found in a substantial minority of people with mania, in some with psychotic depression, in drug-induced and other organic psychoses such as temporal lobe epilepsy (which is why Schneider insisted on the absence of physical illness), and, as DSM-5-TR notes, in dissociative identity disorder, where voices of other identity states can resemble voices arguing and intruding thoughts can resemble thought insertion.

How good a test are they? A Cochrane review by Karla Soares-Weiser and colleagues (2015) pooled 21 studies with 5,515 participants analysed. Compared with all other diagnoses, the presence of first-rank symptoms had a sensitivity of about 57 percent and a specificity of about 81 percent; compared only with other psychoses, the specificity fell to about 75 percent. Put into words: roughly four in ten people diagnosed with schizophrenia did not show first-rank symptoms when they were assessed, and about one in four people with other psychoses did. A sign found only in one disease is called pathognomonic; first-rank symptoms are characteristic of schizophrenia but not pathognomonic. That is why DSM-5 stopped giving them special weight, while ICD-10 built them into its first groups of criteria and ICD-11 still lists experiences of influence, passivity or control among its core symptoms (Section 4.4).

Exam trap

First-rank symptoms were described by Kurt Schneider: not Kraepelin (dementia praecox), not Bleuler (four As), not Karl Jaspers (phenomenology and primary delusions), not Gregory Bateson (the double bind, 1956, Section 4.9) and not "Wayne", a misprint for Lyman Wynne (pseudomutuality, Section 4.9). Not first-rank: depersonalisation, loose associations, delusions of self-reference, catatonic signs (echolalia, stereotyped movements, catatonic excitement), negative symptoms (avolition, anhedonia, flat affect), olfactory hallucinations and tactile illusions, memory deficits and disorientation. First-rank: audible thoughts, voices arguing or discussing, running commentary, thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting, made feelings, impulses and acts, somatic passivity and delusional perception; so delusions of control, insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting are all correct. Delusional perception is first-rank, but a sudden delusional idea without a perception is second-rank.

If an options list offers "auditory hallucination" beside an obvious non-first-rank item such as delusion of self-reference, read the hallucination as standing for Schneider's voices and pick the other item. Strictly, only three forms of voices are first-rank; a simple voice addressing the patient as "you" is second-rank. Finally, FRS are most associated with schizophrenia, not bipolar disorder, borderline personality disorder or obsessive-compulsive disorder, but they are not specific to it: they occur in mania too.

Clinical link

Eliciting first-rank symptoms is a skill taught in Book 2, Section 2.5.6. The key is form, not content. Many patients answer "yes" to a loose question such as "Do thoughts come into your head that are not your own?" when they mean an unwanted worry, a mother's advice that keeps replaying or an obsessional doubt, all of which they recognise as their own thoughts. In India, a patient may also say that someone has done black magic on him or cast the evil eye. That is a culturally shared explanation, not in itself a passivity experience; the clinician has to ask what the patient actually experiences, for example whether his arm moves without his willing it or whether a thought arrives that he knows is someone else's. Possession states are discussed in Chapter 7.

From broad to narrow: the modern definition

By the 1960s the word schizophrenia meant quite different things in different countries. In the United States, where Bleuler's broad concept, Meyer's reaction types and psychoanalysis held sway, the diagnosis was given to patients with mild, odd or mixed pictures; Paul Hoch and Phillip Polatin even described a "pseudoneurotic schizophrenia" (1949) hidden behind severe neurotic symptoms. In Britain and much of Europe, where Schneider's symptoms were used, the concept was narrower. In Norway, Gabriel Langfeldt had separated "true" or process schizophrenia, with its poor prognosis, from good-prognosis schizophreniform states in studies published in 1937 and 1939, a word you will meet again in Section 4.5. In the Soviet Union, the school of Andrei Snezhnevsky used an extremely broad concept that included "sluggish schizophrenia", a diagnosis notoriously given to political dissidents. Did these differences reflect different patients, or different doctors?

Key study: the US-UK Diagnostic Project (1972)

Official hospital statistics showed schizophrenia to be far commoner among admissions in New York than in London, and manic-depressive illness far commoner in London. Was the illness really distributed so differently? A joint American and British team led by John E. Cooper, Robert Kendell and Barry Gurland interviewed patients admitted to hospitals in both cities using the same standardised interview, the Present State Examination devised by John Wing (Assessment book), and the same diagnostic rules. When the project team made the diagnoses, the rates of schizophrenia in the two cities were broadly similar. The American excess had been produced by hospital psychiatrists who called schizophrenia many patients whom the project team diagnosed as having mania, depression or a personality disorder. A companion study (Kendell and colleagues, 1971) showed videotaped interviews to audiences of psychiatrists on both sides of the Atlantic; for one patient, about 69 percent of the Americans diagnosed schizophrenia against 2 percent of the British. The conclusion, published in Psychiatric Diagnosis in New York and London (Cooper and colleagues, 1972), was that diagnostic habits, not patients, differed. The WHO International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia, published the following year (Section 4.6), found the same pattern: its Washington and Moscow centres used much broader concepts than the other centres.

A diagnosis that depends on which city you are admitted in is of little use for research or treatment, so the response was to write the rules down. In 1972 a group at Washington University in St Louis, with John P. Feighner as first author, published explicit research criteria that, among other things, required at least six months of illness. The Research Diagnostic Criteria of Robert Spitzer and his colleagues followed in 1978, and in 1980 the third edition of the DSM (DSM-III) built explicit criteria into the official manual. DSM-III's schizophrenia was deliberately narrow: it required psychotic symptoms, deterioration from an earlier level of functioning, continuous signs of illness for at least six months and, in that edition only, onset before the age of 45; and it sent patients with full mood episodes to the mood disorders. Look at what the new definition contains, and you can see every builder at once. The six-month rule is Kraepelin's course in miniature. The weight that DSM-III and its successors gave to bizarre delusions and Schneiderian voices is Schneider's. The exclusion of mood disorders is Kraepelin's dichotomy. And Bleuler lives on in the negative symptoms that later editions placed among the core criteria.

The story since then is one of refinement rather than revolution, and Section 4.4 tells it in detail. ICD-10 (1992) wrote Schneider's symptoms into its first groups of criteria but required only one month of illness. DSM-5 (2013) abolished the classical subtypes, which were unreliable and told clinicians little about treatment or outcome, and it stopped allowing a single bizarre delusion or Schneiderian voice to satisfy the symptom criterion on its own. ICD-11, in force since 2022, replaced the subtypes with specifiers for the kinds of symptoms present and for the course. The general history of classification, and the statistics of reliability such as kappa, are in Book 2, Section 2.10.

The modern concept, then, is narrow where Bleuler's was broad. Psychotic symptoms (delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech) are required; a period of illness long enough to show something of its course is required (one month in ICD, six months in DSM); mood psychoses are excluded; and no single symptom, not even a first-rank one, is enough. Table 4.4 sets the three great builders side by side.

Table 4.4 Kraepelin, Bleuler and Schneider compared.
FeatureKraepelin (1899)Bleuler (1911)Schneider (1939 to 1959)
Name usedDementia praecoxThe group of schizophreniasSchizophrenia
What defines itCourse and outcomeFundamental symptoms (the four As)First-rank symptoms at one interview
Onset and outcomeEarly onset, deteriorationAny age; variable, no complete cureNot central to the diagnosis
Hallucinations and delusionsCommon featuresAccessory symptomsSpecific forms are central
BreadthFairly narrowBroad (simple and latent forms)Narrow
Survives today inDuration rules; the split from mood disordersNegative symptoms; thought disorder; schizotypal disorderICD-10 groups (a) to (c); ICD-11 passivity symptoms
Exam trap

The US-UK Diagnostic Project (Cooper and colleagues, 1972) showed that American psychiatrists diagnosed schizophrenia far more broadly than British ones; standardised interviews removed most of the difference. Do not confuse it with Rosenhan's pseudopatient study "On being sane in insane places" (1973), which asked whether hospitals could detect sane people (Book 2, Section 1.6), or with the WHO International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia (1973), which compared the course of the illness in nine countries, including India (Section 4.6). DSM-III (1980) narrowed the concept with explicit criteria and the six-month rule, an idea taken from the Feighner criteria (1972).

With the concept in place, the rest of the chapter can look at the illness itself. Today's definition rests on symptoms, and those symptoms fall into groups: the positive symptoms, in which something is added to normal experience, come first in Section 4.2, and the negative and cognitive symptoms, in which something is taken away, follow in Section 4.3.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. Ewald Hecker named (1871) after the Greek goddess of youth.
  3. Karl Kahlbaum described catatonia in 1874 and called it " insanity".
  4. In the sixth edition of his textbook (1899), Kraepelin separated dementia praecox from insanity by its deteriorating course.
  5. Bleuler's schizophrenia refers to a splitting of psychic , not a split personality.
  6. Bleuler's four As are associations, affect, and autism.
  7. To Bleuler, hallucinations and delusions were symptoms.
  8. Jaspers called a delusion that cannot be understood from any earlier mood or experience a delusion.

Answers: (1) démence précoce, (2) hebephrenia, (3) tension, (4) manic-depressive, (5) functions, (6) ambivalence, (7) accessory, (8) primary, (9) delusional perception, (10) broadly (or widely).

4.2 Positive Symptoms: Delusions, Hallucinations and DisorganisationLower yield · 2 Qs

Kraepelin, Bleuler and Schneider argued about which symptoms mattered most, but they were all looking at the same people. This section and the next turn from the history of the concept to the illness itself: what a person with schizophrenia actually experiences, says and does. We begin with the positive symptoms, so called because they are the presence of something unusual, something that a healthy mind does not produce: beliefs that no evidence can shake, voices that nobody else can hear, speech that cannot be followed, and behaviour that has lost its purpose or its ordinary movement. The negative symptoms of Section 4.3 are the opposite: the absence or reduction of something normal, such as the outward expression of emotion, the drive to act or the wish for company. Think of addition and subtraction. Positive symptoms add something to experience; negative symptoms take something away. The words describe what has happened to normal function, not whether the symptom is good or bad. No symptom of schizophrenia is "positive" in the everyday sense.

DSM-5-TR defines the whole family of psychotic disorders by abnormalities in five domains: delusions, hallucinations, disorganised thinking (inferred from speech), grossly disorganised or abnormal motor behaviour (including catatonia), and negative symptoms. The first four are the positive domains and the subject of this section. Every sign is defined in Chapter 2 of this book, and Book 2, Section 2.5 shows how a clinician elicits and records each one in the mental status examination. The question here is different: how does each look in schizophrenia, what does the patient say about it, and what does it tell the clinician? One young man will carry us through most of the section.

A first episode, seen from the inside

Suresh was 22 and in the final year of an engineering degree in Coimbatore when the change began. He was the elder son of a bank clerk, a steady student who opened the batting for his department's cricket team. For the first few months nothing dramatic happened. He stopped going to practice, stayed in his hostel room, slept through morning lectures and sat up at night. His roommate noticed that he no longer laughed at the usual jokes. Suresh noticed something else. "The whole hostel had changed," he said later. "Nothing was different, the same corridor, the same mess, but everything felt arranged, like a film set before the shooting starts. I knew something was going to happen and that it was about me, but I did not know what."

Then, one morning in the canteen, he watched the hostel warden take off his spectacles and wipe them. "At that moment I understood everything. The warden, the professors and my project guide were together in a plan to steal my final-year design and sell it to a company. The spectacles were the signal that the plan had started." Over the next weeks the evidence multiplied. The number plates of cars parked outside the hostel carried coded messages. A forwarded joke on the class WhatsApp group was "obviously" about him. He began to hear two men talking about him, quite clearly, from somewhere above the ceiling of his room: "He is going to the canteen now. Look at him. Useless fellow. He thinks we do not know." When he sat down to work they commented on what he was doing ("Now he is opening the file"), and he became convinced that people could hear his thoughts too, because his thoughts were "leaking out" of his head so that everyone near him knew them.

His mother brought him to the psychiatry outpatient department of a teaching hospital after he stopped eating food from the mess, certain that it was being poisoned to "slow down his brain". He was polite, guarded and very tense. He answered questions carefully, now and then glancing up at the ceiling and pausing as if listening. He explained the plot in detail and with complete certainty, and he did not think that he was ill at all. "I don't need a psychiatrist," he said. "I need a lawyer."

Look at how much of the positive picture of schizophrenia is packed into this one story. There is a period of strange, uneasy expectation; a sudden private meaning attached to an ordinary perception; delusions of persecution, of reference and of poisoning; voices discussing him in the third person and commenting on his actions; the experience that his thoughts are broadcast to others; behaviour (refusing food) driven by a delusion; and, binding it all together, a complete lack of insight. Schneider would have found four of his first-rank symptoms in it (Section 4.1). Notice too what is not there. Suresh's speech was organised, his movements were normal and his mood, though tense, was not elevated or deeply depressed. Not every patient has every symptom, which is exactly why the diagnostic criteria of Section 4.4 ask for "two or more" from a list rather than for any single sign. The rest of this section takes the positive experiences one at a time.

Exam trap

Positive symptoms are the presence of something unusual (an excess or distortion of normal function); negative symptoms are the absence of something normal. Any option that swaps the two ("positive symptoms indicate the presence of something normal", "negative symptoms indicate the presence of something unusual") is wrong. Delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech and grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour are positive. Flattened affect is negative, so it is the answer when a paper asks which of these is not a positive symptom.

Delusions in schizophrenia

A delusion, in DSM-5-TR's single sentence, is a fixed belief that is not amenable to change in the light of conflicting evidence. The fuller British definition adds that it is held with unusual conviction and is out of keeping with the person's educational, cultural and social background (Book 2, Section 2.5.4). The German psychiatrist and philosopher Karl Jaspers, in his General Psychopathology of 1913, gave three external marks of a delusion: an extraordinary subjective certainty, imperviousness to experience and to argument (incorrigibility), and an impossibility or falsity of content. He also warned that falsity is the weakest of the three. A man may be right, by chance, that his wife is unfaithful and still be deluded, because of the way he reached the belief and the way he holds it. Suresh's belief was false, but what made it a delusion was that he reached it from a pair of spectacles and that nothing anyone said could move it.

Jaspers drew a further distinction that matters greatly in schizophrenia. A secondary delusion can be understood as growing out of something else in the person's mental state: the severely depressed widow who believes she has ruined her family, the manic businessman who believes he has been chosen to lead the nation, the man who hears threatening voices and concludes that a gang is after him. A primary delusion cannot be traced back to any other experience. It arrives directly and is, in Jaspers' word, "ununderstandable": the listener cannot follow how the person got there. Mood disorders mostly produce secondary delusions whose content fits the mood. Primary delusions are the special territory of schizophrenia, and Suresh's moment in the canteen is a textbook example. (Many delusions in schizophrenia are also secondary, of course: Suresh's belief that the mess food was poisoned grew partly out of the voices and partly out of the plot he already believed in.)

How a delusion forms. Suresh described his illness as a sequence, and that sequence is common enough to have been studied. Before the full delusion there was a period of delusional mood (in German, Wahnstimmung): a feeling that the world has subtly and sinisterly changed, that something is going on and that it concerns oneself, without any idea of what it is. Patients find this state almost unbearable, and the arrival of a definite belief can come as a relief, because at last the strangeness has an explanation. Then came a delusional perception: a normal perception (the warden wiping his spectacles) suddenly given a private, momentous meaning, with no understandable link between the two. Delusional perception is one of Schneider's first-rank symptoms; delusional mood is not. After that the belief spread, gathering "evidence" from number plates and messages and organising everything around itself. A related form, the delusional memory, gives a real past event a new delusional significance ("Now I see why the teacher gave me that pen in the seventh standard; it was the first sign that I had been chosen").

The German psychiatrist Klaus Conrad (1905 to 1961) built this sequence into a phase model in his 1958 monograph Die beginnende Schizophrenie ("Beginning schizophrenia"), based on patients he had examined in the 1940s. He called the opening phase trema, the actor's word for stage fright: tension, suspicion, loss of the ability to relax, and a growing sense that something important is about to happen. It gives way to apophany, a "revelation" in which meanings suddenly click into place and the patient feels that he now sees what is really going on. In what Conrad called anastrophe, the patient experiences himself as the centre around which the world turns, so that everything refers to him. Some patients go on to an apocalyptic phase in which experience fragments into catatonic or severely disorganised states, and the illness then consolidates into a settled delusion or a residual state. Figure 4.4 sets out the phases and the clinical terms that match them.

Conrad's model was a careful description, not an experiment, and it has since been tested. Martin Hambrecht and Heinz Häfner (1993), working at the Central Institute of Mental Health in Mannheim, used the ABC Schizophrenia Study, a representative sample of 267 people admitted for the first time whose early symptoms had been carefully reconstructed. Trema was confirmed: a tense, uneasy opening phase was a frequent first stage of the illness. But the order Conrad proposed for the later phases, apophany first and then apocalypse, could not be shown. The fair conclusion is that the opening of the model is common and clinically useful, while the later phases describe some patients rather than all. Two modern ideas, taught later in this chapter, explain why the opening phase feels as it does and why the belief then sticks. Section 4.8 describes Shitij Kapur's proposal that excess dopamine makes ordinary things feel strangely significant, so that the delusion is the patient's attempt to explain the feeling; Section 4.9 describes the reasoning biases, such as jumping to conclusions, that help fix the explanation once it has formed.

What the delusions say. DSM-5-TR is clear about frequency. Persecutory delusions, beliefs that one is going to be harmed, harassed or plotted against by a person, an organisation or a group, are the most common, and referential delusions, beliefs that gestures, comments, broadcasts and other cues in the environment are directed at oneself, are also common. Suresh had both. Table 4.5 lists the main themes as they are heard in schizophrenia, with a note on where else each one points. Three deserve a word here. A delusion of control is the belief that one's will, actions, feelings or body are directed by an outside force; together with the delusional explanations patients give for thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting, these are the passivity experiences that Schneider ranked first (Section 4.1; how to ask about them is in Book 2, Section 2.5.6). Grandiose and religious delusions occur in schizophrenia as well as in mania; the difference is usually that the grandiosity of schizophrenia sits beside flat or incongruous mood and other symptoms instead of flowing out of an elevated mood. And delusions about the body can be strikingly bizarre in schizophrenia: organs rotting, a snake living in the abdomen, the brain being slowly drained through a tube. DSM-5-TR also notes that gender-themed delusions (beliefs about belonging to another gender, which come and go with the psychotic episodes) occur in up to a fifth of people with schizophrenia.

Table 4.5 The main delusional themes as they are heard in schizophrenia, and where else each points.
ThemeThe patient saysNote
Persecution"My guide and the warden are stealing my design. They put something in the mess food."The commonest delusion in schizophrenia; also the commonest type of delusional disorder
Reference"That WhatsApp forward was about me. The newsreader coughed as a message to me."Common. Ideas of reference without full conviction belong to schizotypal traits and social anxiety
Control (passivity)"A machine at the mobile tower moves my hands. I only watch."First-rank (made acts, feelings, impulses); counted as bizarre in DSM-5-TR
Thought alienation, explained"A satellite pulls my thoughts out." "Everyone on the bus hears what I think."The delusional account of withdrawal, insertion or broadcasting; first-rank
Grandiose"I have been chosen to end all corruption. Delhi is waiting for my plan."Also in mania, where it flows from elevated mood
Religious"I am the eleventh avatar. God speaks through my left ear."Content shaped by culture; elevated religiosity can itself be a feature of psychosis
Somatic"My brain is drying up. There is a snake in my stomach."Often bizarre in schizophrenia; usually plausible in delusional disorder (infestation, odour)
Nihilistic"My intestines have stopped. I am already dead."Classic of severe psychotic depression (Cotard syndrome); possible in schizophrenia
Erotomanic"The TV anchor loves me; she signals it with her earrings."The core of erotomanic delusional disorder (Section 4.5)
Jealousy"My wife meets men while I am at work. I checked the scooter's mileage."Commoner in delusional disorder and alcohol dependence (Othello syndrome)
Guilt"The floods came because of my sins."A mood-congruent theme of psychotic depression; less typical of schizophrenia

Several features of the way delusions are held are characteristic of schizophrenia. They are often multiple and poorly systematised: persecution, reference, control and grandiosity coexist without being knitted into one logical story. The elaborate, internally consistent, single-theme delusion that a patient can defend point by point is more typical of delusional disorder (Section 4.5). Their content is frequently bizarre. And they are held in a curious way that Eugen Bleuler noticed on the wards of the Burghölzli and described in 1911 as double bookkeeping: his patients who called themselves kings, emperors or popes went on doing their ordinary work and made no real attempt to live according to their imagined rank. The delusional reality and the shared reality run side by side, like two sets of accounts kept by the same clerk. Double bookkeeping is a reminder that conviction and action are separate questions. Many patients never act on their delusions, but some do, and a persecutory delusion that comes with anger, a named persecutor and a wish to strike first is one of the situations in which violence becomes more likely (risk assessment is in Book 2, Section 2.7). Suresh's refusal of mess food was action on a delusion, and it was what brought him to hospital.

Bizarre and non-bizarre delusions. For decades one more distinction carried great weight. A bizarre delusion is, in DSM-5-TR's words, clearly implausible, not understandable to people of the same culture and not derived from ordinary life experience. The manual's example is the belief that an outside force has removed one's internal organs and replaced them with someone else's without leaving any wound or scar. A non-bizarre delusion could happen, at least in principle: being watched by the police, being cheated by a business partner, being poisoned by a jealous relative. Delusions that express a loss of control over mind or body (thought withdrawal, thought insertion and delusions of control) are generally counted as bizarre. Under DSM-IV (and DSM-III-R before it), a single bizarre delusion, or a single voice of Schneider's commenting or conversing kind, was enough on its own to satisfy the symptom criterion for schizophrenia. DSM-5 (2013) removed that special status for two reasons. Schneider's symptoms had turned out not to be specific to schizophrenia (Section 4.1), and clinicians agreed poorly about which delusions were bizarre, a judgement that depends heavily on culture and on the clinician's own idea of what is possible. In DSM-5-TR a bizarre delusion therefore counts as one Criterion A symptom like any other, and delusional disorder may now carry the specifier "with bizarre content" (Sections 4.4 and 4.5). ICD-10 kept an echo of the older idea in its group (d), persistent delusions that are culturally inappropriate and completely impossible, such as being able to control the weather.

Delusions and culture. Whether a belief is delusional cannot be decided without knowing where the person comes from. DSM-5-TR warns that some religious and supernatural beliefs (the evil eye, illness caused by curses, the influence of spirits) may look bizarre in one cultural setting and be generally accepted in another, and that people who have survived torture, political violence or discrimination may report fears that sound persecutory but are realistic fears of recurrence or post-traumatic symptoms. It also warns that when the interview is held in a language other than the person's own, an apparent symptom may be no more than a language barrier: brief, halting replies, for example, should not be taken as alogia. The theme of a delusion is universal, but its machinery is borrowed from the patient's world. Clinicians have long noticed that the influencing machines of one generation's delusions were radio waves and wireless sets, of the next television and satellites, and of today's patients mobile towers, CCTV cameras, phone hacking and social media.

Indian context

In Indian clinics persecution is very often expressed in the language of black magic: a relative or neighbour has "done something" (jadu-tona, banamathi, seyvinai) to the patient, the family or the house. The belief in sorcery is shared by millions of healthy people, so it is not a symptom in itself. The clinician asks three questions. Does the family hold the same belief, in the same way? Is the patient's conviction far greater, more elaborate or more personal than theirs (for example, certain that the neighbour sends spirits through the ceiling fan every night to stop his heart)? And has it come with other changes, such as withdrawal, voices or a fall in functioning? A mother who ties a black thread on her son's wrist against the evil eye is within her culture. A son who believes that the thread itself is a transmitter through which his thoughts are read has gone beyond it.

Exam trap

Delusions are positive symptoms, never negative: "delusion" (or "persecutory delusions") is the odd one out when a question lists apathy, paucity of speech, incongruity of emotional responses or alogia. The commonest delusion in schizophrenia is persecutory, with referential close behind. Delusional perception is first-rank; delusional mood is not, and neither is a delusion of self-reference. Primary delusions and the three marks (certainty, incorrigibility, impossibility) are Jaspers'; double bookkeeping is Bleuler's; trema and apophany are Conrad's. DSM-5 stopped letting a single bizarre delusion satisfy Criterion A.

Hallucinations in schizophrenia

A hallucination is a perception-like experience that occurs without an external stimulus. DSM-5-TR adds the features that make it more than vivid imagination: it is clear, with the full force and impact of a normal perception, and it is not under voluntary control. It differs from an illusion, which is a misperception of something that really is there (the shirt on the hook that, for a moment in the dark, looks like a man). And in a primary psychosis it occurs in a clear sensorium. Hallucinations in a drowsy, fluctuating, disoriented patient point to delirium, not schizophrenia (Chapter 10).

Hallucinations may occur in any sense, but auditory hallucinations are by far the most common in schizophrenia and related disorders; studies put them at around 60 to 80 percent of people with a schizophrenia spectrum diagnosis. They are usually voices, familiar or unfamiliar, that the person experiences as distinct from his own thoughts. So if you ask in which disorder hallucinations most often occur, the answer is schizophrenia, although mania, psychotic depression and several other conditions can produce them too (the end of this subsection explains how).

What the voices are like. Six weeks into treatment, Suresh trusted his doctor enough to describe his voices properly. "There are two men, maybe forty years old. One is louder. They talk to each other about me, 'He is eating now, he is useless, he will fail.' Sometimes they say my thoughts before I can finish thinking them. Sometimes one talks straight to me: 'You should jump from the terrace.' The voice comes from the ceiling, very clear, like you are talking now. When the fan is on, it is louder."

Each phrase is clinically meaningful. A third-person hallucination talks about the patient ("he"): two or more voices discussing or arguing about him, or a voice giving a running commentary on his actions as they happen. Hearing one's own thoughts spoken aloud, just before, during or after thinking them, is thought echo (Gedankenlautwerden, audible thoughts). These three are Schneider's hallucinatory first-rank symptoms (Section 4.1). A second-person hallucination addresses the patient directly ("you"): abuse, threats, advice or commands. Second-person voices are common in schizophrenia but also occur in psychotic depression and other psychoses, so they carry less diagnostic weight. Voices may be single words, phrases or long conversations; they may come from outside (the ceiling, the fan, the next room, a distant city) or be heard inside the head; and in schizophrenia their content is usually unpleasant: insults, mockery, threats, sexual taunts, orders. A minority of patients find one or two voices companionable and are reluctant to lose them. Suresh's last remark, that the voices grew louder when the fan was on, describes a functional hallucination, explained below.

The voices that worry clinicians most are command hallucinations, voices that tell the person to do something. Many commands are trivial ("Turn off the tap") and many are resisted, but DSM-5-TR notes that suicidal behaviour in schizophrenia is sometimes a response to command hallucinations to harm oneself or others. The clinician therefore asks not only what the voices say but whether the person feels he must obey, whether he has obeyed before, and whether he believes that the voice is powerful or that something terrible will happen if he refuses.

Clinical link

Patients often do not mention voices; they show them. Watch for hallucinatory behaviour: pausing in mid-sentence as if listening, looking towards a corner or the ceiling, muttering, smiling or laughing to oneself, answering an empty room, cotton in the ears or earphones playing loud music to drown the voices. Ask in a normalising way ("Sometimes, when people are under a lot of strain, they hear voices when nobody is around. Has anything like that happened to you?") and record the voice in the patient's own words: whose voice, how many, where from, second or third person, how often, what it says, any commands, whether he obeys, and how distressing it is (Book 2, Section 2.5.7). A command to harm self or others moves the interview straight to risk assessment (Book 2, Section 2.7).

Pseudohallucinations. The term pseudohallucination is used in two different senses. In the European tradition of the Russian psychiatrist Victor Kandinsky (1849 to 1889), who described these experiences in the 1880s, and of Jaspers, who built on his work, it is a vivid, involuntary image that lacks the objective, out-there quality of a perception and is located in inner, subjective space ("inside my head", "in my mind's ear") rather than in the outside world. In much British and American usage it means instead a hallucination-like experience that the person recognises as not real. The two definitions do not pick out the same experiences, and the term causes a good deal of confusion (Book 2, Section 2.5.7 sets out both senses). Two practical points matter.
