Many people with schizophrenia hear their voices inside the head, so the location of a voice does not by itself make it harmless; and a "pseudohallucination" can be as distressing and as commanding as any other voice.

The other senses. The older teaching that people with schizophrenia hear but do not see is wrong. A 2014 review led by Flavie Waters put the weighted average for visual hallucinations at 27 percent in schizophrenia, against 15 percent in affective psychosis and about 7 percent in the general community; in schizophrenia they usually come together with voices, are complex, are negative in content and are taken to be personally meaningful. In a Dutch study of 750 patients with schizophrenia spectrum disorders, Lim and colleagues (2016) found a lifetime prevalence of 80 percent for hallucinations in any of four senses, and more than half the sample (53 percent) had experienced them in more than one sense. What should raise the suspicion of an organic cause is not visual hallucinations as such but visual hallucinations that are prominent, occur without voices, appear in a clouded or fluctuating state, come with neurological signs or begin late in life. Table 4.6 summarises the senses.

Table 4.6 Hallucinations by sense: how they appear in schizophrenia and what else to suspect.
SenseIn schizophreniaSuspect also
AuditoryThe commonest (about 60 to 80 percent); voices, often hostile; commenting, discussing and echoing voices are first-rankPsychotic mania and depression; alcoholic hallucinosis; hearing loss (music, noises)
VisualAbout a quarter; usually alongside voices; complex and personally meaningfulDelirium (including delirium tremens), drugs, Lewy body dementia, epilepsy, failing eyesight (Charles Bonnet syndrome)
TactileElectric shocks, touching, sexual interference, usually experienced as done by othersFormication ("insects under the skin"): cocaine, amphetamine, alcohol withdrawal
Somatic (inside the body)Organs twisting, burning or moving; often feeds somatic and passivity delusionsPsychotic depression (with nihilistic ideas); physical illness
OlfactoryGas, rotting or poison; often linked to delusions of poisoningTemporal lobe epilepsy (a brief, stereotyped aura); brain tumour
GustatoryA bitter or metallic taste "added" to foodTemporal lobe epilepsy; medication effects

Special forms. Three named varieties are worth knowing, and all occur in schizophrenia. In a functional hallucination a real stimulus triggers a hallucination in the same sense, and both are experienced together: Suresh's voices came with the noise of the fan, and some patients hear voices only when a tap is running or a train passes, stopping when the noise stops. In a reflex hallucination a real stimulus in one sense produces a hallucination in another, as when a patient feels a pain in his back every time a door slams. An extracampine hallucination is experienced outside the possible field of the sense concerned: a man in Coimbatore hears his brother talking in Mumbai, or a woman "sees" someone standing behind her head. Each of these is defined, with the rest of the perceptual disorders, in Chapter 2 of this book.

When hearing voices is not a symptom. Hallucinations are not confined to illness. DSM-5-TR states that hypnagogic hallucinations (while falling asleep) and hypnopompic hallucinations (while waking) are within the range of normal experience, and that hallucinations may be a normal part of religious experience in some cultural settings; many bereaved people briefly hear or see the person who has died. The largest survey of the question, by John McGrath and colleagues (2015), questioned 31,261 adults in 18 countries for the WHO World Mental Health Surveys: 5.8 percent reported at least one psychotic experience in their lifetime, hallucinatory experiences (5.2 percent) being far commoner than delusional ones (1.3 percent), and for most people the experience had happened only once or a few times. In the late 1980s the Dutch psychiatrist Marius Romme and the researcher Sandra Escher began the Hearing Voices Movement, which grew out of their discovery that many people hear voices and cope well without psychiatric care, and which argues that voices can be understood and lived with. What turns voice-hearing into a symptom of schizophrenia is therefore not the voice alone but its company and its consequences: frequency, hostile content, conviction that it comes from outside, distress, loss of functioning and the presence of the other symptoms in this section.

Why does a brain produce a voice? One of the first imaging studies to catch hallucinations in the act was carried out in London by Philip McGuire, G. M. Shah and Robin Murray (1993). They measured regional blood flow with single photon emission tomography in twelve men with schizophrenia while they were hallucinating, and scanned them again under identical conditions some months later, when the voices had stopped. The one region in which blood flow was significantly greater during hallucinations was Broca's area, which produces speech; flow was also somewhat higher in the left anterior cingulate cortex and in parts of the left temporal lobe, where speech is understood, but those differences fell short of significance. The patients seemed to be listening to their own inner speech without recognising it as their own. Section 4.9 develops this idea of a failure of source monitoring, and Section 4.8 the dopamine changes that make such experiences feel real and urgent.

Hallucinations outside schizophrenia. Since hallucinations are not confined to schizophrenia, it helps to know what they look like in the other conditions that produce them. In mania with psychotic features the voices are usually mood-congruent, consistent with grandiosity and invulnerability (God telling the patient that he has been chosen), although they can be paranoid. In a depressive episode with psychotic features the mood-congruent voices accuse and condemn ("You are a sinner; your family will starve because of you"), matching the DSM-5-TR themes of inadequacy, guilt, disease, death, nihilism and deserved punishment. In borderline personality disorder, DSM-5-TR notes that some people develop psychotic-like symptoms such as hallucinations, ideas of reference or body-image distortions during times of stress; these are brief and tied to the stress. Hallucinations also occur in delirium, dementia, substance intoxication and withdrawal, and epilepsy. Hallucinations most often occur in schizophrenia, both in how often they are found and in how central they are to the picture, but no hallucination is specific to it.

Exam trap

"Hallucinations most often occur in": schizophrenia (not mania, depression or personality disorders, where they are possible but less frequent). Auditory hallucinations are the commonest kind and are positive symptoms, so they are the exception in any list of negative symptoms; they are also not among Bleuler's four As (he called them accessory). Third-person voices (discussing, commenting) and thought echo are first-rank; second-person voices are not. Hypnagogic and hypnopompic hallucinations are normal. Visual hallucinations are not rare in schizophrenia (about a quarter), but prominent, isolated visual hallucinations suggest an organic cause.

Disorganised thinking and speech

Nobody can see a thought; we can only hear speech. The third positive domain is therefore defined by what the patient says. Formal thought disorder is a disturbance in the form of thinking, the way ideas are linked to one another, as distinct from its content (delusions are disorders of content: what is believed, not how thoughts are joined). DSM-5-TR lists the main forms. The person may switch from one topic to another (derailment or loose associations); answers to questions may be obliquely related or completely unrelated (tangentiality); and, rarely, speech may be so disorganised that it is nearly incomprehensible and resembles the speech of receptive aphasia (incoherence or "word salad").

Two qualifications follow. First, mildly disorganised speech is common and nonspecific; tired, anxious, excited and garrulous people ramble. So the symptom counts towards a diagnosis only when it is severe enough to substantially impair communication. Second, speech that is disorganised by the standards of one culture may be normal in another. DSM-5-TR gives the examples of glossolalia ("speaking in tongues") in some religious groups and the speech of possession trance, in which the person's identity is replaced by a possessing one. Both are disorganised, but neither is a sign of psychosis unless clearly psychotic symptoms accompany it (possession states are discussed in Chapter 7). DSM-5-TR adds that the severity of disorganisation may be hard to judge when the clinician and the patient do not share a first language, a daily problem in a country where the interview may move between Hindi, Kannada, Tamil and English in ten minutes.

The idea that the associative links between thoughts loosen in schizophrenia comes from Bleuler, for whom it was the fundamental disturbance (Section 4.1). The American psychologist Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) later called the same thing cognitive slippage (1962), a phrase that captures how the patient's thinking slides off its track. And in 1979 Nancy Coover Andreasen gave each form a precise definition with examples, the basis of her Scale for the Assessment of Thought, Language and Communication; she also found, to many people's surprise, that "positive" forms such as derailment and tangentiality were as common in mania as in schizophrenia (Book 2, Section 2.5.3 tells that study). What follows is what thought disorder sounds like in a patient with schizophrenia.

Thomas was 34 and lived with his elderly parents near Kottayam in Kerala. He had been ill for eight years, had held no job for six, and was admitted after he stopped his tablets. Part of the admission interview went like this.

Clinician: "Thomas, how have you been sleeping?"
Thomas: "Sleeping is for the sheep. The sheep are in the Bible, and the Bible is printed in Kottayam, so the bishop knows. He sends the electrograms at night."
Clinician: "What are electrograms?"
Thomas: "Electrograms. The things. The gramophone of the electric. Through the plug point. Plug, slug, drug, they give me the drug in the mug."
Clinician: "Why did you stop your tablets?"
Thomas: "Tablets are... [he stops, stares at the window for several seconds] ... What was I saying? It went. The rubber tree has its own time, you see, and time is money, and money is the root, so I stopped."
Clinician: "What are your plans for when you go home?"
Thomas: "Plans are plans. A man must have his plans, and the plans are what a man has, so I will do the things that are planned."

Take it answer by answer. The first reply begins near the question and then slides from "sleeping" to "sheep" (a link of sound), to the Bible, to Kottayam, to the bishop and into a delusion: this is derailment, Andreasen's preferred name for loosening of associations, in which each idea slips onto a track that is only obliquely related or unrelated to the one before. Notice that you can sometimes see the hinge (sleep and sheep, time and money); the trouble is that the hinges are trivial, private or based on a single shared feature, and they lead nowhere near the question. "Electrograms" is a neologism, a new word with a private meaning, and "the gramophone of the electric" is what Andreasen called a word approximation, an odd new phrase built out of ordinary words. "Plug, slug, drug, mug" is a clang association, words chosen for their sound rather than their meaning. The sudden stop in the third answer, with a blank stare and "It went", is thought blocking: the train of thought stops dead and a new, unrelated one starts. If Thomas had said "They took it out of my head", the blocking would have been explained by thought withdrawal, a first-rank delusion; the block is the sign, the withdrawal is the patient's interpretation of it. The rest of that answer is tangentiality, an answer that heads off in another direction and never returns, with a final "so I stopped" that has the shape of a reason but none of its content. And the last answer shows poverty of content of speech: there are enough words, but they convey almost no information, circling vaguely and repetitively around the question.

Several other forms complete the picture. In incoherence (word salad) even the grammar within a sentence collapses: "Tree when blue the bishop running Tuesday sad." In perseveration the patient keeps returning to the same word or idea in answer to new questions. Thinking may be concrete, tied to the literal meaning (asked what "Don't cry over spilt milk" means, the patient says "Milk is costly, you should wipe it"), or over-inclusive, with the boundaries of concepts so widened that unrelated things are grouped together, a pattern the American psychiatrist and psychologist Norman Cameron described. Circumstantiality, the long roundabout answer that does finally reach its goal, is less typical of schizophrenia than of temporal lobe epilepsy, obsessional personalities and intellectual disability. All of these are defined in Chapter 2 of this book. In schizophrenia, milder disorganisation (vague, oddly worded but understandable speech) is common in the prodromal and residual phases, and Section 4.3 explains why modern research treats disorganisation as a dimension of its own, separate from delusions and hallucinations.

Mania is the other great source of disordered speech, so Table 4.7 sets the speech of the two side by side. The differences are tendencies, not rules (Andreasen's finding of overlap is the warning), but together they are often decisive.

Table 4.7 Disordered speech in schizophrenia and in mania: the usual pattern.
FeatureSchizophreniaMania
Rate and amountNormal or reduced; in chronic illness often sparsePressured, rapid, hard to interrupt
Links between ideasLost, private or trivial (derailment)Present but rapid; each jump can be followed (flight of ideas)
NeologismsCharacteristic, though uncommonRare
Clangs and punsOccurCommon, often playful
Poverty of speech or of contentCommon, especially later in the illnessRare
Emotional toneFlat, odd or incongruousElated, expansive or irritable
How the listener feelsPuzzled, unable to make contactSwept along, entertained, exhausted
Exam trap

Loose associations (derailment) are Bleuler's fundamental symptom, not one of Schneider's first-rank symptoms: they are the answer when a question asks which of thought insertion, audible thoughts, delusional perception and loose associations is not first-rank. Derailment has no followable links; flight of ideas (mania) has. Poverty of speech (too few words) is alogia, a negative symptom; poverty of content (enough words, no information) belongs to disorganisation. Thought blocking is a sign; thought withdrawal is the delusional explanation. A neologism is one invented word; word salad is a jumble of real words. Disorganised speech is a positive symptom.

Grossly disorganised behaviour and inappropriate affect

When the organisation of thought fails, the organisation of action often fails with it. DSM-5-TR describes grossly disorganised behaviour as ranging from childlike "silliness" to unpredictable agitation, with problems in any form of goal-directed behaviour, so that ordinary activities of daily living become difficult. Thomas's parents described it in their own way. In the Kerala summer he wore a woollen sweater over two shirts. He collected bottle caps and kept them in his pillowcase. He walked out at midnight to stand in the paddy fields, laughed loudly in the middle of family prayers and once undressed in the courtyard in front of the neighbours. The example his mother gave first was the most telling: "He goes to bathe, and then he just stands there holding the mug." It is not that he refuses to bathe (that would be avolition, Section 4.3) or that he is afraid of the water (that might be a delusion). The sequence of steps that turns an intention into an action has come apart.

Disorganisation also shows in emotion. Inappropriate affect, also called incongruity of affect, is emotional expression that does not fit what the person is saying or what is happening: laughing while describing his mother's death, giggling during a serious discussion, looking angry while talking of a happy event. DSM-5-TR lists it among the associated features of schizophrenia ("laughing in the absence of an appropriate stimulus"), and it was part of Bleuler's disturbance of affect. Two cautions apply before recording it. The patient who suddenly laughs or smiles may be responding to a voice that has just said something to him, which is hallucinatory behaviour rather than incongruity, so the clinician asks. And inappropriate affect must be kept apart from blunted affect, which is a reduction in the amount of emotional expression and is a negative symptom; incongruity is a mismatch, not a reduction. (ICD-10 confusingly lists "blunting or incongruity of emotional responses" together among its negative symptoms, a point Section 4.3 returns to.) The old hebephrenic subtype of ICD-10, the disorganised subtype of DSM-IV, was built around exactly this picture of early onset, shallow and silly affect, fragmented speech and disorganised behaviour, with a poor outlook (Section 4.4).

Exam trap

Inappropriate (incongruous) affect is a mismatch between emotion and situation; blunted affect is a reduction in emotional expression. Modern dimensional models put inappropriate affect in the disorganisation dimension and blunting among the negative symptoms, but ICD-10 lists both together ("blunting or incongruity of emotional responses") under negative symptoms, so in an ICD-10 question incongruity counts as negative. A patient who laughs to himself may be hallucinating: ask before you call it incongruity.

Catatonia in schizophrenia

Kiran was 19 and in the second year of a commerce degree in Hubballi. For three months she had been hearing voices and spending most of the day in her room. Then she stopped speaking. On the fourth day her parents brought her to hospital. She sat bolt upright on the edge of the bed, staring at the wall, hardly blinking, and had taken neither food nor water for two days. When the nurse measured her blood pressure and let go of her arm, the arm stayed up in the air, at the angle the nurse had left it, for several minutes. When the doctor tried to bend her elbow she resisted, and she would not open her mouth for a spoonful of water. Yet when he raised his own hand, she slowly raised hers; and when he asked, "What is your name?", she whispered, "What is your name?" That night the nurses found her lying with her head held several centimetres above the pillow, a posture she kept for hours. Her father added that in the week before she fell silent she had paced the house for two days without stopping, grimacing and rubbing her thumb along the same seam of her dupatta.

Kiran's picture is catatonia, the syndrome of motor and behavioural signs that Karl Kahlbaum described in 1874 and Kraepelin later folded into dementia praecox (Section 4.1). DSM-5-TR describes catatonic behaviour as a marked decrease in reactivity to the environment, ranging from resistance to instructions, through rigid, inappropriate or bizarre postures, to a complete lack of verbal and motor response; it can also take the opposite form of purposeless, excessive motor activity without obvious cause, called catatonic excitement. Map Kiran's signs onto the twelve that DSM-5-TR lists. Not relating to her surroundings and not moving was stupor; not speaking was mutism. The arm left in the air was catalepsy, a posture placed by someone else and then held against gravity. Resisting the doctor's attempt to bend her elbow, and keeping her mouth shut, was negativism, opposition or no response to instructions or to being handled. Raising her hand when he raised his was echopraxia, and repeating his question was echolalia. The head held above the pillow (an old clinical name for it is the "psychological pillow") was posturing, a posture the patient adopts and maintains herself. The week before, the pacing was agitation not influenced by external stimuli, the rubbing of the seam was a stereotypy (a repetitive movement with no goal) and the facial contortions were grimacing. Over the fortnight she showed ten of the twelve signs; three are enough for the DSM-5-TR syndrome.

Several of these signs sound alike, and they are best separated by two questions, as Figure 4.5 shows. Who put the limb in its position, the patient or the examiner? And what happens when the examiner moves it? In waxy flexibility the examiner feels a slight, even resistance, like bending a stick of soft wax, and the limb then stays where it is put. In Mitgehen ("going with") the limb moves in whatever direction the examiner's lightest touch suggests, even when the patient has been told to resist. In Gegenhalten ("counter-holding") the patient resists passive movement with a force that matches the force applied. Other classical signs, described in older British and Indian texts and in some rating scales but not in the DSM-5-TR list of twelve, include automatic obedience (carrying out every instruction literally, whatever the consequence), ambitendency (starting a movement and stopping it, over and over, as if two opposed impulses were at war) and verbigeration (repeating the same words or phrases). A mannerism, finally, is an odd, stilted caricature of a normal, purposeful action (saluting every time one picks up a glass); a stereotypy is a repetitive movement with no purpose at all. Table 4.8 groups the signs, and Book 2, Section 2.4.4 shows how each is tested at the bedside.

Table 4.8 The catatonic signs grouped by what has happened to movement.
GroupSigns in the DSM-5-TR listOlder signs also described
Too little movement and responseStupor, mutism, catalepsy, waxy flexibilityStaring, rigidity
Opposing or over-complyingNegativismGegenhalten; automatic obedience, Mitgehen; ambitendency
Odd voluntary movementPosturing, mannerism, stereotypy, grimacing"Psychological pillow" (a posture); verbigeration
ImitatingEcholalia (words), echopraxia (movements)
Too much movementAgitation not influenced by external stimuli (catatonic excitement)Sudden purposeless violence

How common is catatonia in schizophrenia, and how specific is it? DSM-5-TR answers both. Catatonia is usually diagnosed in inpatient settings and occurs in up to 35 percent of people with schizophrenia, but the majority of catatonia cases involve people with depressive or bipolar disorders; across clinical samples about 9 percent of patients have it. When George Bush, Max Fink and colleagues published their Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale (1996; 23 items, with a 14-item screening version), they found catatonia in 15 of 215 consecutive admissions (7 percent) to an academic psychiatric unit. Catatonia also arises from medical illness (encephalitis, metabolic and autoimmune disorders), from drugs, and, as a dangerous side effect of antipsychotics, from neuroleptic malignant syndrome (Section 4.10). That is why DSM-5 no longer treats it as a subtype of schizophrenia but as a specifier and syndrome that cuts across disorders, the classification taught in Section 4.5.

The stuporous patient is in physical danger. A person who neither eats nor drinks becomes dehydrated within days; immobility brings pressure sores and clots in the leg veins that can travel to the lungs; and in malignant catatonia fever and unstable blood pressure and pulse appear and the condition can be fatal. The good news is how well catatonia responds to treatment. Within an hour of an injection of lorazepam, a benzodiazepine, Kiran was sitting up, drinking water and answering questions in a small voice, a response that is itself diagnostic. Section 4.5 explains this lorazepam challenge and the place of ECT, and Kiran's underlying psychosis was then treated as Section 4.10 describes.

Exam trap

Posturing is a posture the patient adopts; catalepsy is a posture the examiner imposes that the patient keeps; waxy flexibility is the slight, even resistance felt while moving the limb. Mannerism is an odd version of a purposeful act; stereotypy is a purposeless repetition. Echolalia copies words, echopraxia copies movements. Catatonic excitement is a positive (motor) symptom, not a negative one, and no catatonic sign is first-rank. Catatonia was described by Kahlbaum (1874); in DSM-5 it is a specifier across several disorders, and most cases occur in mood disorders, not schizophrenia.

Lack of insight

Suresh's "I need a lawyer" was not stubbornness or denial in the everyday sense. In psychiatry, insight means a person's awareness that he has a mental illness, his recognition that particular experiences (the voices, the conviction about the plot) are symptoms of it, and his acceptance that treatment may help. Poor insight is so characteristic of schizophrenia that in the WHO International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia, the first large cross-national study of the disorder (Section 4.6), lack of insight was the most frequent of all the symptoms recorded, rated in about 97 percent of patients; no other symptom recorded in the study was as frequent. Insight was rated there as a single item, and later multidimensional measures (below) show that it varies by degree, but the lesson stands.

Why do people with schizophrenia so often not know that they are ill? DSM-5-TR takes a clear position. Unawareness of illness is typically a symptom of schizophrenia itself rather than a coping strategy, and it is comparable to the lack of awareness of neurological deficits that follows some kinds of brain damage, called anosognosia (the stroke patient who insists that his paralysed arm works perfectly). It may last through the whole course of the illness. And it matters enormously: DSM-5-TR calls it the most common predictor of non-adherence to treatment, and it predicts higher relapse rates, more involuntary treatment, poorer psychosocial functioning, aggression and a poorer course.

Insight is not all or none. The British psychiatrist Anthony S. David (1990) proposed that it has three distinct, overlapping dimensions: the recognition that one has a mental illness, compliance with treatment, and the ability to relabel unusual mental events, such as delusions and hallucinations, as pathological. Xavier Amador and colleagues (1993) built the Scale to Assess Unawareness of Mental Disorder on the same multidimensional view and showed that insight is best rated on a continuum. The dimensions can come apart. By his sixth week of treatment Suresh said, "Maybe I was very stressed, and the tablets help me sleep," which was partial recognition and good compliance; but he still believed that "the warden did do something", so he had not relabelled the delusion. A patient may accept that he hears voices and still attribute them to a transmitter, and another may take his tablets faithfully "to keep my mother happy" while denying any illness at all. Book 2, Section 2.6 describes how insight is graded at the end of the mental status examination.

Gaining insight is not simply good news. A meta-analysis of 40 studies by Mintz, Dobson and Romney (2003) found that better insight went with slightly fewer positive, negative and overall symptoms, but also with slightly more depression. For a young man like Suresh, understanding that he has an illness that may recur, and that may have cost him his degree and his place among his friends, can bring grief and hopelessness. This is one reason why the early years after diagnosis carry a raised risk of suicide (Section 4.6), and why insight work is always paired with hope.

Clinical link

Arguing a patient out of his lack of insight does not work and costs the relationship. Work instead with what he does accept and what he wants: Suresh wanted to finish his degree and to sleep, and he agreed to medicine "for the sleep" long before he accepted the word schizophrenia. In India it is usually the family who notices the illness and brings the patient, as Suresh's mother did, so the family is part of the treatment alliance from the first day. When a person's capacity to make treatment decisions is impaired, the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 provides supported admission with safeguards, nominated representatives and advance directives (Book 2, Section 1.10).

4.3 Negative and Cognitive Symptoms, and the Symptom DimensionsMedium yield · 7 Qs

The positive symptoms are what bring most patients to hospital, because voices and persecutory beliefs frighten families and neighbours and sometimes lead to dangerous acts. They are also, as Section 4.10 shows, the symptoms that antipsychotic drugs treat best. Yet when people with schizophrenia are followed for years, it is often not the voices that decide how their lives turn out. It is what has been taken away: the capacity to feel and show emotion, to start and finish things, to want the company of others, to think quickly and remember clearly. Those losses are the negative and cognitive symptoms, and after them come the attempts made since 1980 to sort all the symptoms of schizophrenia into a small number of types or dimensions.

What is missing: the idea of negative symptoms

Meenakshi was 41 and lived with her brother's family in Madurai. She had first fallen ill at 24, in her first year as a primary school teacher, with voices and the belief that her colleagues were poisoning her tea. Over the years the episodes had come and gone; for the last five she had received a monthly depot injection, and the voices were now faint and infrequent. But her sister-in-law's complaint, when she brought Meenakshi to the clinic, was about something else. "She sits on the verandah from morning to night. She will not comb her hair unless I tell her. She does not help in the kitchen, she does not watch the serials, she does not talk to anybody. When my grandson comes running to her, she just looks at him. Earlier she was the one who told stories to all the children. Now she has become lazy. Or is it the injection?"

In the consulting room Meenakshi sat still, her hands in her lap. Her face barely moved and her voice was low and even. Asked how she was, she said, "Fine." Asked what she did during the day, she said, "Sit." Asked whether she would like to go back to work, she was silent for several seconds and then said, "Maybe." Asked whether she enjoyed her grand-nephew's visits, she said, after a pause, "Yes. It is nice." Asked whether she looked forward to them, she shrugged.

Nothing in this interview is a symptom in the sense that a voice is a symptom. There is nothing to hear, nothing to argue with. What the clinician notices is an absence. Negative symptoms are the absence or reduction of normal functions: emotional expression, speech, motivation, pleasure and social interest. DSM-5-TR says that they account for a substantial part of the illness and disability caused by schizophrenia, and that they are less prominent in the other psychotic disorders; they are one of the features that most clearly separate schizophrenia from its neighbours in Section 4.5.

The words "positive" and "negative" are older than schizophrenia. They come from nineteenth-century British neurology. The London physician John Russell Reynolds, writing about epilepsy around 1861, separated symptoms that are an excess of normal function (convulsions, abnormal movements, hallucinations) from those that are a loss of it (paralysis, loss of sensation). The neurologist John Hughlings Jackson (1835 to 1911) gave the distinction its theory: damage to the highest levels of the nervous system causes negative symptoms directly, through loss of function, and positive symptoms indirectly, by releasing lower levels from their control. The clinical observations were older still. Kraepelin had described a weakening of the will and an emotional dullness as central to dementia praecox, and Bleuler's disturbance of affect and his autism were, in part, negative symptoms (Section 4.1). In schizophrenia research the distinction was revived in 1974 by John Strauss, William Carpenter and John Bartko in the United States, and it became the organising idea of the 1980s through the work of Timothy Crow and Nancy Andreasen (Section 4.3.6).

The five negative symptoms

For years every research group had its own list. In 2006 a consensus conference convened under the American National Institute of Mental Health's MATRICS programme (Brian Kirkpatrick, Wayne Fenton, William Carpenter and Stephen Marder) agreed on five domains, which are now the standard: blunted affect, alogia, avolition, anhedonia and asociality. DSM-5-TR names all five and singles out two as particularly prominent in schizophrenia: diminished emotional expression and avolition. Table 4.9 summarises them; Meenakshi shows every one.

Blunted affect. Blunted affect, which DSM-5-TR calls diminished emotional expression, is a reduction in the outward expression of emotion: in the face, in eye contact, in the intonation of speech (prosody) and in the movements of the hands, head and face that normally give emotional emphasis to what we say. When it is severe and nearly total it is called flat affect. Meenakshi's still face as her grand-nephew ran towards her is the classic picture. The crucial point is that blunting is a reduction in expression, and expression and experience are not the same thing.

Key study: Feeling without showing (Berenbaum and Oltmanns, 1992)

Do patients with blunted affect feel less, or do they only show less? Howard Berenbaum and Thomas Oltmanns compared 23 patients with schizophrenia who had blunted affect, 20 patients with schizophrenia who did not, 17 people with unipolar depression and 20 healthy people. Each tasted pleasant and unpleasant drinks (sugar water and diluted vinegar, coloured to look alike) and watched brief film clips chosen to arouse positive or negative emotion, reported what they felt, and was videotaped so that facial expressions could be coded.

The patients with blunted affect were the least facially expressive of all the groups, yet the emotions they reported did not differ from those of the other groups. Blunting, the study suggested, is mainly a difficulty in expressing emotion, not an absence of feeling. The finding has been replicated with computer analysis of facial movement, and it matters for families: a mother whose son's face does not change when she visits should not conclude that he no longer cares.

Blunting has two common mimics. The parkinsonian side effects of antipsychotic drugs produce a mask-like, unmoving face that looks exactly like emotional blunting (Section 4.10), and depression reduces expression too, although the depressed patient usually also looks and says that he is sad. Blunting must also be told apart from inappropriate affect (Section 4.2.5), which is a mismatch between emotion and situation, not a reduction.

Alogia. Alogia (Greek, "without words"), also called poverty of speech, is diminished speech output. Replies are brief, concrete and unelaborated, and often come after a long delay; the interviewer feels that he is pulling each word out with forceps. Meenakshi's "Fine", "Sit" and "Maybe" are typical. Compare a healthy aunt asked whether she has children ("Yes, two, a boy in Pune doing his MBA and a girl in the tenth standard, very naughty") with Meenakshi's equivalent ("Yes." "How many?" "Two." "What do they do?" "Study."). Alogia must be distinguished from three things: from poverty of content of speech, in which there are plenty of words but little information (a sign of disorganisation, Section 4.2.4); from thought blocking, in which speech stops suddenly in mid-flow; and from the ordinary quietness of a shy person, or of a person interviewed in a language that is not his own.

Avolition. Avolition (a, "without", and volition, "an act of willing") is, in DSM-5-TR's words, a decrease in motivated, self-initiated, purposeful activity. The person may sit for long periods and show little interest in work, study or social activities; personal hygiene and self-care decline; jobs and courses are abandoned. ICD-10 calls the same thing "marked apathy". Avolition is the negative symptom most closely tied to disability, and DSM-5-TR links it directly to the decline in functioning required by Criterion B. It is also the one most often misread. Meenakshi's sister-in-law called it laziness, but laziness implies a choice, and the person with avolition does not choose not to act: the spark that turns a wish into a start is missing. Explaining this to families is one of the most useful things a clinician does.

Anhedonia. Anhedonia is a decreased ability to experience pleasure. In schizophrenia it has a surprising shape. DSM-5-TR notes that people with schizophrenia can still enjoy a pleasurable activity in the moment and can recall it, but they engage in pleasurable activities less often. David Gard, Ann Kring and colleagues (2007) explained the puzzle by separating two kinds of pleasure. Consummatory pleasure is the enjoyment felt while doing something; anticipatory pleasure is the pleasure of looking forward to it, the pleasure that motivates us to seek it out. Using experience sampling (prompting patients at random moments in their daily lives to report what they were doing and feeling) and a trait questionnaire, they found a deficit in anticipatory pleasure but not in consummatory pleasure, and anticipatory pleasure was related to clinical ratings of anhedonia and to functioning in the community. Meenakshi showed exactly this: the visit was "nice" while it happened, but she did not look forward to it, and so nothing pulled her up from the verandah. The pattern is different in depression, in which in-the-moment enjoyment is usually lost as well.

Asociality. Asociality is an apparent lack of interest in social interaction. It often goes with avolition, but DSM-5-TR adds an important caution: it can also reflect limited opportunities for social contact, as when a person who has been ill for years has lost his friends, his job and his place in community life. It should also be separated from social anxiety, in which the person wants company but fears it, and from the withdrawal of a paranoid patient who avoids people because he believes they mean him harm.

Table 4.9 The five negative symptoms (NIMH-MATRICS consensus, 2006).
DomainWhat is reducedAt interview you noticeDo not confuse with
Blunted affectOutward expression of emotionStill face, little eye contact, monotonous voice, few gesturesDrug-induced parkinsonian mask; depressed mood; inappropriate affect
AlogiaAmount of speechBrief, empty replies; long delay before answeringPoverty of content; thought blocking; shyness or a language barrier
AvolitionSelf-started, purposeful activitySits for hours; neglects hygiene; abandons work or studyLaziness (a choice); depression; sedation
AnhedoniaPleasure, especially anticipated pleasureFew pleasant activities; looks forward to nothingDepression, in which present enjoyment is also lost
AsocialityInterest in relationshipsFew friends; no wish for companySocial anxiety; paranoid avoidance; lack of opportunity

Factor analyses show that the five fall into two groups. Blunted affect and alogia form an expressive factor (diminished expression), while avolition, anhedonia and asociality form a motivation and pleasure factor (sometimes called the experiential factor). The two groups have different correlates, and the motivation and pleasure factor is the more closely linked to how a person manages in daily life.

Two older lists are still widely quoted, and they differ in instructive ways. Nancy Andreasen's Scale for the Assessment of Negative Symptoms (SANS, 1982) had five "symptom complexes": affective flattening, alogia, avolition-apathy, anhedonia-asociality and attentional impairment; the modern consensus has moved attention out of the negative symptoms and into the cognitive ones (Section 4.3.4) and given asociality its own place. ICD-10 describes the negative symptoms of its group (h) as "marked apathy, paucity of speech, and blunting or incongruity of emotional responses", usually resulting in social withdrawal and lowering of social performance, and adds that it must be clear that they are not due to depression or to neuroleptic medication. Notice the word "incongruity": ICD-10 counts incongruous affect among the negative symptoms, although most dimensional research places it with disorganisation (Section 4.2.5). In ICD-10's terms, then, marked apathy, paucity of speech and incongruity of emotional responses are all negative symptoms.

Exam trap

The negative symptoms are blunted (flat) affect, alogia (poverty or paucity of speech), avolition (apathy), anhedonia and asociality. The exception in a "negative symptoms are all except" question is a positive symptom (delusion, hallucination, disorganised speech, catatonic excitement) or a Bleulerian term: ambivalence is one of Bleuler's four As, not a negative symptom, and neither are autism or loose associations. Do not mix the lists: Bleuler's four As (associations, affect, ambivalence, autism), Andreasen's SANS complexes (which include attention) and the modern five (which include asociality). Anosognosia, aphasia and anxiety are on none of them. ICD-10 counts "incongruity of emotional responses" as negative.

Primary or secondary? The deficit syndrome

Meenakshi's sister-in-law asked a good question: "Or is it the injection?" Not every negative symptom in a person with schizophrenia comes from the illness itself. Secondary negative symptoms are caused by something else and can often be relieved by treating that cause; primary negative symptoms are part of the disease process. There are five usual sources of secondary negative symptoms, summarised in Table 4.10. Positive symptoms can produce them: a man who believes his neighbours are plotting against him stays indoors and stops speaking to anyone, and a woman whose voices talk all day may sit motionless, listening. Depression produces them: reduced interest, energy and expression are part of a depressive episode, and depression is common in schizophrenia, especially after an acute episode (post-psychotic depression, Section 4.4). Antipsychotic drugs produce them: drug-induced parkinsonism causes an unmoving face, slowed movement and reduced spontaneity (akinesia), and sedation flattens everything (Section 4.10). Substance use and physical illness, such as hypothyroidism, produce them. And, as a famous British study showed, an empty environment produces them.

Key study: Institutionalism and schizophrenia (Wing and Brown, 1960 to 1968)

John Kenneth Wing and George W. Brown chose three English mental hospitals that differed in their social conditions and policies: Netherne in Surrey, just south of London, Severalls in Essex and Mapperley in Nottingham. In 1960 they surveyed the long-stay patients with schizophrenia in each, rating the "social poverty" of their surroundings (measures such as how few personal possessions the patients had, how little contact they had with the outside world and how much of the day they spent doing nothing) and the patients' "clinical poverty" (flatness of affect, poverty of speech and social withdrawal). The poorer the social environment, the more severe the clinical poverty.

Over the next eight years the environments of all three hospitals improved, and in a proportion of patients the negative symptoms improved with them. Wing and Brown's book, Institutionalism and Schizophrenia (1970), concluded that a substantial proportion, though by no means all, of the disability of long-stay patients was a product of their institutional setting. They also proposed that people with schizophrenia need an optimal level of stimulation: too little worsens withdrawal and negative symptoms, too much (pressure, criticism, over-stimulation) provokes a relapse of positive symptoms, an idea that led directly to the research on expressed emotion in Section 4.9.

Table 4.10 Secondary negative symptoms: where they come from and what to do.
SourceCluesWhat helps
Positive symptomsWithdraws because frightened, suspicious or absorbed in voicesTreat the psychosis
DepressionSad mood, guilt, hopelessness, suicidal thoughtsTreat the depression; assess suicide risk
Antipsychotic side effectsRigidity, tremor, slowed movement; drowsinessLower dose, change drug, treat parkinsonism (Section 4.10)
Understimulating environmentLong hospital stay or years at home with nothing to doActivity, occupation, rehabilitation
Substances and physical illnessHeavy cannabis or alcohol use; hypothyroidismTreat the cause

What remains when all of these have been dealt with? In 1988 William T. Carpenter and his colleagues Heinrichs and Wagman, at the Maryland Psychiatric Research Center, proposed that the primary, enduring negative symptoms of schizophrenia, which they called deficit symptoms, mark off a distinct group of patients. The deficit syndrome is diagnosed when a person with schizophrenia shows at least two of six negative features (restricted affect, diminished emotional range, poverty of speech, curbing of interests, diminished sense of purpose and diminished social drive), when these have been present for at least the preceding 12 months and were always present during periods of clinical stability, and when they are judged primary, not secondary to depression, anxiety, drug effects, suspiciousness or other factors. The point of the 12-month rule and of the "always present" rule is to rule out the transient negative symptoms that come and go with episodes and treatments. The group's Schedule for the Deficit Syndrome (Brian Kirkpatrick and colleagues, 1989) made the judgement reliable.

About a fifth to a quarter of people with schizophrenia have traditionally been said to have the deficit syndrome, and a 2018 meta-analysis of 13 studies with 2,092 patients from eight countries put the figure higher, at about a third. The classification is stable over time. Patients with deficit schizophrenia have poorer adjustment before their first psychotic episode and a worse long-term outcome, and in 2001 Kirkpatrick, Carpenter and colleagues argued that deficit schizophrenia may be a separate disease within the syndrome, because the deficit and non-deficit groups differ in their signs and symptoms, course, biological correlates, treatment response and risk factors. Whether it is a separate disease or the severe end of one continuum is still debated. Its practical message is clear: primary negative symptoms respond poorly to every treatment now available, while secondary ones often respond well.

Clinical link

Before recording "negative symptoms" in a patient like Meenakshi, work through the secondary causes, because each is treatable. Ask about sadness, guilt and hopelessness (depression). Look for rigidity, tremor and a slow, shuffling walk (parkinsonism from the depot injection; a lower dose or a change of drug may help). Ask whether voices or fears keep her on the verandah. And ask what there is for her to do all day. Meenakshi's depot dose was reduced, she was referred to a day-care rehabilitation programme, and within three months she was helping in the kitchen, though her face stayed still and she still did not look forward to things: some of her symptoms were secondary and some were primary.

Exam trap

The deficit syndrome is Carpenter and colleagues' (1988): primary, enduring negative symptoms (two of six) present for at least 12 months and not explained by depression, drugs or other factors. Secondary negative symptoms come from positive symptoms, depression, antipsychotic side effects, institutional understimulation (Wing and Brown) or substances. ICD-10 requires that its negative symptoms are "not due to depression or to neuroleptic medication".

Cognitive symptoms

Return to Suresh. Eight weeks after he started treatment the voices had stopped and the plot no longer occupied his mind. He went back to college determined to finish his degree. Within a month he was struggling. He could not follow a lecture past the first fifteen minutes. He read a page of his textbook and found that none of it had stayed. He forgot the second half of instructions ("Submit the drawing to the office and then collect your hall ticket"). He could not plan the stages of his project, and when one stage failed he did not know how to change course. He took twice as long as his classmates over everything, and he misread his friends: twice he took a joke as an insult. None of this was a delusion or a voice. These are the cognitive symptoms of schizophrenia, and for many patients they are the most disabling of all.

DSM-5-TR describes cognitive deficits in schizophrenia as common and strongly linked to vocational and functional impairment. They include problems with declarative memory, working memory, language function and other executive functions, slower processing speed, abnormalities in sensory processing and in the capacity to inhibit irrelevant responses, and reduced attention. They frequently persist when the positive symptoms are in remission, they can be detected before the first psychotic episode (Section 4.7 tells the developmental evidence), and DSM-5-TR adds that social-cognitive deficits may appear during development and remain as stable impairments in adulthood, resistant to antipsychotic medication. Cognitive impairment is not one of the five Criterion A symptoms of DSM-5-TR, but it is rated in the manual's dimensional assessment (Section 4.3.6), and ICD-11 has a cognitive symptom specifier (Section 4.4).

How large and how general are the deficits? R. Walter Heinrichs and Konstantine Zakzanis (1998) pooled 204 studies comparing people with schizophrenia and healthy controls on standard neuropsychological tests and calculated 22 average effect sizes, covering verbal and nonverbal memory, motor skill, attention, general intelligence, spatial ability, executive function and language. Every one showed a moderate to large deficit (an effect size above 0.6 standard deviations), and none of the confidence intervals included zero. Their conclusion was that schizophrenia is characterised by a broadly based cognitive impairment, with varying degrees of deficit in every ability domain measured. This is not dementia in the sense of the progressive neurocognitive disorders of Chapter 10, which is one reason Kraepelin's name for the illness was abandoned (Section 4.1), but it is a real and measurable impairment.

To test new treatments, researchers needed agreement on what to measure. The National Institute of Mental Health's MATRICS initiative (Measurement and Treatment Research to Improve Cognition in Schizophrenia) asked a panel led by Keith Nuechterlein to review the factor-analytic evidence. In 2004 they concluded that seven separable cognitive domains are impaired in schizophrenia, and these became the structure of the MATRICS Consensus Cognitive Battery (the battery itself belongs to the Assessment book). Table 4.11 lists them with what each looks like in daily life.

Table 4.11 The seven MATRICS cognitive domains impaired in schizophrenia (Nuechterlein and colleagues, 2004).
DomainWhat it coversIn daily life
Speed of processingHow quickly simple information is handledSlow at forms, billing, keeping up with conversation
Attention or vigilanceSustaining focus over timeLoses the thread of a lecture or a film
Working memoryHolding and using information for secondsForgets the second half of an instruction
Verbal learning and memoryLearning and recalling spoken or written materialCannot recall what the doctor said about the tablets
Visual learning and memoryLearning and recalling what is seenGets lost on a new bus route
Reasoning and problem solvingPlanning and flexible thinking (executive function)Cannot plan a project or change course when it fails
Social cognitionPerceiving emotions and intentionsTakes a joke as an insult; misses a friend's distress

Social cognition, the last domain, covers the mental operations that underlie social life: recognising emotions in faces and voices, inferring what other people believe and intend (theory of mind, which Christopher Frith's account of schizophrenia places at the centre, Section 4.9), explaining why things happen in social situations (attributional style) and reading social cues. DSM-5-TR notes that some people with schizophrenia attend to irrelevant events or stimuli and interpret them as meaningful, which may help to generate explanatory delusions, a reminder that the cognitive and positive dimensions are not sealed off from each other.

Key study: Cognition and the outside world (Green, 1996)

Everyone knew by the 1990s that people with schizophrenia did badly on cognitive tests. Michael F. Green, at the University of California, Los Angeles, asked whether this mattered for their daily lives. He reviewed every study he could find that related cognitive measures to functional outcome: prospective studies of community, social and work functioning, studies of social problem solving, and studies of how well patients acquired skills in psychosocial rehabilitation.

Verbal memory was associated with every kind of functional outcome; vigilance was related to social problem solving and skill learning; and card sorting (a test of executive function) predicted community functioning. Strikingly, psychotic symptoms were not significantly associated with the outcome measures in any of the studies reviewed. Green called the cognitive deficits "rate-limiting factors": a patient whose voices have gone may still be unable to hold a job if he cannot remember instructions or sustain attention. Later reviews with meta-analysis confirmed the pattern. It is why cognition, not positive symptoms, is now a main target of rehabilitation (cognitive remediation, Section 4.10 and Book 2, Section 9.8).

Exam trap

Cognitive deficits in schizophrenia are broad (all domains, moderate to large), begin before the first episode, persist in remission and respond little to antipsychotics. They predict work and social functioning better than positive symptoms do. The MATRICS domains are seven: speed of processing, attention or vigilance, working memory, verbal learning and memory, visual learning and memory, reasoning and problem solving, and social cognition. Cognitive impairment is not one of the five DSM-5-TR Criterion A symptoms.

Mood, anxiety and the other associated features

Schizophrenia is not an illness of thought alone. DSM-5-TR lists a dysphoric mood that can take the form of depression, anxiety or anger; a disturbed sleep pattern, often with daytime sleeping and night-time activity; a lack of interest in eating or outright food refusal; depersonalisation, derealisation and somatic concerns, which may reach delusional proportions; and anxiety and phobias, which are common. About half of those whose illness begins gradually show depressive symptoms during the onset. Mood symptoms and even full mood episodes can occur during the active phase, but in schizophrenia they are present for only a minority of the total duration of the illness; when they occupy the majority, the diagnosis moves towards schizoaffective disorder (Sections 4.4 and 4.5). Depression after an acute episode, post-psychotic depression, is taught in Section 4.4.

Two associated features matter most for safety. DSM-5-TR estimates that about 5 to 6 percent of people with schizophrenia die by suicide and about 20 percent attempt suicide at least once, with the risk raised especially in younger men who use substances, in the period after an episode or a hospital discharge, and close to the onset of the illness (Section 4.6 and Book 2, Section 2.7). Hostility and aggression can occur, more often in younger men and in people with a history of violence, non-adherence, substance use and impulsivity, but DSM-5-TR stresses that the vast majority of people with schizophrenia are not aggressive and are more often the victims of violence than the general population. Many patients also show neurological soft signs, minor problems with motor coordination, sensory integration and the sequencing of complex movements, which point back to the developmental story of Section 4.7.

Remember

A full description of a person with schizophrenia covers more than the five Criterion A domains. It adds cognition, mood (depression and mania), anxiety, insight, suicide and violence risk, sleep and eating. DSM-5-TR calls the assessment of cognition, depression and mania "vital" for telling the schizophrenia spectrum disorders apart.

Types and dimensions: Crow, Andreasen, Liddle and the five factors

DSM-5-TR states plainly that no single symptom is pathognomonic of schizophrenia and that people with the disorder vary substantially on most features: it is a heterogeneous clinical syndrome. Two patients can share the diagnosis and share no symptoms at all, as Suresh and Meenakshi nearly do. Kraepelin's answer to this variety was a set of subtypes (paranoid, hebephrenic, catatonic), which survived into DSM-IV and ICD-10 and were then abandoned because they were unreliable, unstable over time and of little use in predicting treatment (Section 4.4). From 1980 onwards a different approach took over: instead of sorting patients into boxes, sort the symptoms into groups that tend to occur together and may reflect different underlying processes.

Crow's two syndromes. The first influential scheme came from the British psychiatrist Timothy John Crow, who led the division of psychiatry at Northwick Park Hospital near London. In a short paper in the British Medical Journal in 1980, titled "Molecular pathology of schizophrenia: more than one disease process?", Crow proposed two syndromes. Type I schizophrenia was characterised by positive symptoms (delusions, hallucinations and thought disorder), tended to be acute, responded well to neuroleptic drugs, showed no intellectual impairment, was potentially reversible, and was attributed to a neurochemical disturbance, an increase in dopamine D2 receptors. Type II schizophrenia was characterised by negative symptoms (affective flattening, poverty of speech, loss of drive), tended to be chronic, responded poorly to drugs, was sometimes accompanied by intellectual impairment, was relatively irreversible, and was attributed to structural brain change with cell loss, the kind of change seen in the enlarged lateral ventricles that Crow's own group at Northwick Park, with Eve Johnstone, had shown on CT scans in 1976 (Section 4.7). In 1985 Crow added abnormal involuntary movements, cognitive impairment and behavioural deterioration to the Type II picture. Table 4.12 sets out the two.

Table 4.12 Crow's (1980) Type I and Type II syndromes.
FeatureType IType II
Characteristic symptomsPositive: delusions, hallucinations, thought disorderNegative: affective flattening, poverty of speech, loss of drive
Typical courseAcute, episodicChronic
Response to neurolepticsGoodPoor
Intellectual impairmentAbsentSometimes present
OutcomePotentially reversibleRelatively irreversible, sometimes progressive
Proposed basisNeurochemical: more dopamine D2 receptorsStructural: cell loss, enlarged ventricles
Added by Crow in 1985Abnormal involuntary movements, cognitive impairment, deterioration

Crow's scheme was elegant because it tied each group of symptoms to a different biology and a different response to treatment, and it fitted what clinicians saw: the acute, florid, drug-responsive patient and the chronic, withdrawn, drug-resistant one. But Crow himself recognised its main weakness. Both syndromes often occur in the same patient, sometimes at the same moment, so they are better thought of as two dimensions of pathology within one illness than as two diseases. And the clean links did not all hold up: later studies found the association between enlarged ventricles and negative symptoms inconsistent.

Andreasen's positive, negative and mixed schizophrenia. At the University of Iowa, Nancy Andreasen gave the positive-negative distinction its measuring instruments. Her Scale for the Assessment of Negative Symptoms (SANS, 1982) and Scale for the Assessment of Positive Symptoms (SAPS, 1984) are described in the Assessment book. With her colleague Olsen she proposed in 1982 three subtypes: positive schizophrenia, with prominent delusions, hallucinations, positive formal thought disorder and persistently bizarre behaviour; negative schizophrenia, with affective flattening, alogia, avolition, anhedonia and attentional impairment; and mixed schizophrenia, in which both kinds are prominent or neither is. The subtypes differed on premorbid adjustment, cognitive measures, ventricular size and course in hospital. The very need for a "mixed" group, however, showed that many patients do not fit either pole.

Key study: Three syndromes, not two (Liddle, 1987)

Is two the right number? Peter F. Liddle, in London, examined how symptoms clustered in 40 patients with chronic schizophrenia, chosen because their symptoms were persistent and stable. If the positive-negative model were right, the symptoms should have fallen into two groups. They fell into three. Psychomotor poverty comprised poverty of speech, lack of spontaneous movement and various aspects of blunted affect. Disorganisation comprised inappropriate affect, poverty of content of speech and disturbances of the form of thought. Reality distortion comprised particular types of delusions and hallucinations.

The crucial discovery was that thought disorder and inappropriate affect did not go with delusions and hallucinations, even though all are "positive": disorganisation was its own dimension. The syndromes also had different consequences, psychomotor poverty going with poor personal relationships and disorganisation with poor self-care and impersistence at work. In 1992 Liddle, Karl Friston, Christopher Frith and colleagues measured cerebral blood flow with positron emission tomography in 30 patients and found that each syndrome had its own pattern: psychomotor poverty and disorganisation were linked with altered blood flow at different sites in the prefrontal cortex, and reality distortion with the medial temporal lobe.

The PANSS and the five factors. In 1987 Stanley Kay, Abraham Fiszbein and Lewis Opler published the Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale (PANSS), a 30-item interview-based scale with 7 positive items, 7 negative items and 16 items of general psychopathology. It became the standard outcome measure in drug trials (details are in the Assessment book). When researchers factor-analysed PANSS ratings from large samples, the data were described better by five factors than by the scale's three subscales, and a consensus model (Wallwork and colleagues, 2012) names them: positive, negative, disorganised or concrete, excited, and depressed. Other analyses call the third factor cognitive and the fifth anxiety and depression. The five-factor picture keeps Liddle's three dimensions and adds two that clinicians had always seen: excitement and hostility, and depression and anxiety. Figure 4.6 shows how the schemes map onto one another.

Dimensions in the classifications. The diagnostic manuals have followed the research. DSM-5 (2013) dropped the old subtypes and, in Section III, introduced the Clinician-Rated Dimensions of Psychosis Symptom Severity, retained in DSM-5-TR: an 8-item measure on which the clinician rates hallucinations, delusions, disorganised speech, abnormal psychomotor behaviour, negative symptoms, impaired cognition, depression and mania, each on a five-point scale from 0 (not present) to 4 (present and severe) for its worst point in the past seven days. The same scale supplies the optional "current severity" specifier for schizophrenia. ICD-11 replaced its subtypes with symptom specifiers (positive, negative, depressive, manic, psychomotor and cognitive), explained in Section 4.4. Table 4.13 brings the models together.

Table 4.13 How the symptoms of schizophrenia have been grouped.
ModelWho and whenGroups
Two syndromesTimothy Crow, 1980Type I (positive), Type II (negative)
Three subtypesAndreasen and Olsen, 1982Positive, negative, mixed
Three syndromesPeter Liddle, 1987Reality distortion, disorganisation, psychomotor poverty
PANSS subscalesKay, Fiszbein and Opler, 1987Positive (7 items), negative (7), general psychopathology (16)
Five factorsPANSS factor analyses; consensus 2012Positive, negative, disorganised or cognitive, excited, depressed or anxious
Negative symptom domainsNIMH-MATRICS consensus, 2006Blunted affect, alogia, avolition, anhedonia, asociality
Dimensional ratingDSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR (2022), Section IIIEight domains, each rated 0 to 4, including cognition, depression and mania

What does all this sorting buy? First, a better description of each patient: "schizophrenia with prominent negative and cognitive symptoms and little reality distortion" says far more about Meenakshi than "schizophrenia" alone, and it says something quite different from what it says about Suresh at his first admission. Second, a guide to treatment: reality distortion responds best to antipsychotic drugs, while negative and cognitive symptoms need rehabilitation, activity and cognitive remediation as well (Section 4.10). Third, a bridge to biology: the different dimensions seem to involve different brain systems, as Liddle's blood-flow study suggested and as the dopamine pathways of Section 4.8 will show, with too much dopamine in one pathway linked to positive symptoms and too little in another to negative and cognitive ones.

Exam trap

Crow (1980): Type I is positive, acute, drug-responsive, without intellectual impairment, with normal brain structure and a dopamine (D2) excess; Type II is negative, chronic, drug-resistant, with intellectual impairment and enlarged ventricles. Do not swap them. Liddle (1987): reality distortion, disorganisation, psychomotor poverty; "psychomotor poverty" is the negative syndrome, and disorganisation (thought disorder, inappropriate affect) was separated from delusions and hallucinations. Andreasen: SANS and SAPS, and the positive, negative and mixed subtypes. PANSS (Kay, Fiszbein and Opler, 1987) has 30 items.

  2. According to DSM-5-TR, the most common delusions in schizophrenia are delusions.
  3. Conrad called the tense opening phase in which "something is about to happen" .
  4. Bleuler's term for a patient who believes he is an emperor yet goes on sweeping the ward is bookkeeping.
  5. Hallucinations while falling asleep are called and are within the range of normal experience.
  6. When the examiner places a patient's arm in the air and it stays there, the sign is .

Answers: (1) absence, (2) persecutory, (3) trema, (4) double, (5) hypnagogic, (6) catalepsy, (7) anosognosia, (8) avolition, (9) deficit, (10) psychomotor poverty.

4.4 Diagnosing Schizophrenia: DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11High yield · 11 Qs

Ganesh is 22, the younger son of a bus conductor in Hubballi, and until last year he was a steady, quiet B.Com student who played cricket with his cousins on Sundays. About five months ago his parents noticed that he had stopped going to college. He stayed in his room, slept through the day and paced at night, gave up cricket, stopped bathing regularly and answered questions with a shrug. He spent hours with a notebook in which he wrote lists of numbers that he said were "important". Nobody thought of illness; his mother thought he was upset about an examination, and his father thought he was lazy. Then, about six weeks ago, things changed sharply. Ganesh began to say that two men were talking about him from behind the wall ("He is useless. He will be finished by Deepavali"), and that the neighbours had fixed a device inside the ceiling fan that pulled his thoughts out and played them over the loudspeaker of the temple down the road, so that the whole colony knew what he was thinking. He covered the fan with a bedsheet. His speech, when he did talk, drifted from one subject to another so that his brother could not follow it. His family brought him to the outpatient department of the district hospital.

Does Ganesh have schizophrenia? You might think that the answer depends only on Ganesh. It does not. It also depends on which diagnostic manual the clinician opens. A psychiatrist using the International Classification of Diseases (ICD) would, on this history, diagnose schizophrenia today. A psychologist using the American Psychiatric Association's Diagnostic and Statistical Manual (DSM) would not, at least not yet. By the end of this section you will be able to say exactly why, and to work through the rules of all three systems that Indian examiners ask about: DSM-5-TR (2022), ICD-10 (1992) and ICD-11 (in force since 2022). You will also learn the other conditions that can look exactly like Ganesh's illness, because the most dangerous mistake in diagnosing schizophrenia is not choosing the wrong subtype; it is missing a treatable medical cause.

No single symptom: why schizophrenia is diagnosed by rules

Start with an uncomfortable fact. There is no blood test, brain scan or psychological test that diagnoses schizophrenia. DSM-5-TR says so directly: there are currently no radiological, laboratory or psychometric tests for the disorder, and although groups of patients differ from healthy people on brain imaging, eye tracking and electrophysiology, none of these differences can diagnose one person. Nor is there a single symptom that settles it. A symptom that occurs in one disorder and in no other is called pathognomonic (Section 4.1; from the Greek for "judging the disease"), and schizophrenia has none. Thought broadcasting, the symptom that frightens Ganesh most, can occur in mania. Voices can occur in severe depression, in delirium, after a bereavement and in temporal lobe epilepsy. Blunted affect can come from depression or from the antipsychotic drugs themselves.

If no one symptom is decisive, how is the diagnosis made? By recognising a pattern: a particular combination of symptoms, lasting a particular length of time, with a particular effect on the person's life, after the other possible causes have been excluded. For most of the twentieth century that pattern lived in the heads of individual psychiatrists, and different heads held different patterns. Section 4.1 told the story of the US-UK Diagnostic Project of the early 1970s, which showed that New York psychiatrists diagnosed schizophrenia far more readily than London psychiatrists did when they watched the same patients. The cure for that unreliability was to write the pattern down as explicit rules that any trained clinician could apply in the same way. Such rules are called operational criteria: each symptom is defined, the number required is stated, and so are the duration and the exclusions. The general story of classification, reliability and the kappa statistic is told in Book 2, Section 2.10; here we apply it to one disorder.

The first influential operational criteria for schizophrenia were published in 1972 by a group at Washington University in St Louis (Eli Robins, Samuel Guze and their colleagues), with the psychiatry resident John P. Feighner as first author. The Feighner criteria demanded a chronic illness with at least six months of symptoms without a return to the earlier level of adjustment. That six-month rule entered the official American manual with DSM-III in 1980 and has stayed there ever since. Its purpose was deliberate: by refusing the label to short illnesses, the Americans were narrowing schizophrenia back towards the chronic, deteriorating disorder that Kraepelin had described, and away from the very broad concept that the US-UK Project had exposed. The World Health Organization took a different view: when ICD-10 finally set a duration for schizophrenia in 1992 (ICD-9 had none), it chose one month. Almost every difference between DSM and ICD that you will meet in this section flows from that one disagreement about how long to wait.

Notice also that modern criteria are polythetic: they require some number out of a list of features ("two or more of the following five") rather than every feature on the list. A polythetic rule accepts that schizophrenia is a heterogeneous syndrome. Two people can both meet the criteria while sharing none of their symptoms: one may be hallucinating and deluded, the other incoherent in speech and without any drive. That heterogeneity is part of the reason Section 4.3 needed symptom dimensions, and part of the reason DSM-5 eventually gave up on subtypes.

Exam trap

"Schizophrenia can be confirmed by a CT or MRI scan" and "first-rank symptoms are pathognomonic of schizophrenia" are both false. Brain scans in a first episode are done to exclude other causes (a tumour, a stroke, encephalitis), not to confirm schizophrenia. Kurt Schneider himself called his symptoms "first rank" in importance, not unique to schizophrenia; they occur in mania and in organic psychoses too (Section 4.1).

DSM-5-TR schizophrenia, criterion by criterion

DSM-5-TR sets out six criteria, A to F. All six must be satisfied. It helps to read them not as a list to memorise but as six questions the clinician asks, each designed to rule something out.

Criterion A: are the characteristic symptoms there? Two or more of five symptoms must each be present for a significant portion of time during a one-month period: (1) delusions, (2) hallucinations, (3) disorganised speech (frequent derailment or incoherence), (4) grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour, and (5) negative symptoms (diminished emotional expression or avolition). At least one of the two must be delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech. These five are the symptoms described in Sections 4.2 and 4.3. The period during which Criterion A is met is called the active phase.

Why insist that one of the first three be present? Imagine the rule without it. A young man who has become withdrawn, flat and unmotivated (negative symptoms) and who behaves oddly (disorganised behaviour) would qualify. So would many people with severe depression, autism, frontal lobe damage or a chronic institutionalised life. The requirement that at least one symptom be a clear psychotic symptom of thought or perception keeps the diagnosis anchored to psychosis. It was added in DSM-5 (2013); DSM-IV had allowed any two.

Two small phrases in Criterion A matter in practice. "A significant portion of time" means the symptoms must be more than fleeting; a single night of hearing a voice does not count. And the month can be shorter "if successfully treated". If Ganesh is started on an antipsychotic and his voices vanish in three weeks, the clinician may still count Criterion A as met if, in her judgement, the symptoms would have persisted for a month without treatment. Without that clause, effective treatment would make the diagnosis impossible.

Criterion B: has the person's life fallen apart? For a significant portion of the time since onset, functioning in one or more major areas, such as work, relationships or self-care, must be markedly below the level achieved before onset. When the illness begins in childhood or adolescence, the criterion is met by a failure to reach the expected level; comparing the young person with unaffected brothers and sisters is often the best guide. Criterion B is what makes schizophrenia a disorder of the whole life rather than a set of experiences. It is also a requirement that schizophrenia's closest neighbours lack: schizophreniform disorder, schizoaffective disorder and brief psychotic disorder do not require a decline (Section 4.5).

Criterion C: has it lasted six months? Continuous signs of the disturbance must persist for at least six months. That six months must include at least one month of active-phase symptoms (Criterion A), less if successfully treated, and it may include periods of prodromal or residual symptoms. The prodromal phase is the period of gradual change before the psychosis becomes obvious; the residual phase is the period after an active episode settles. During both, the signs of the disturbance may consist only of negative symptoms, or of two or more Criterion A symptoms in a weakened, attenuated form: odd beliefs that are not yet delusions (ideas of reference, magical thinking), unusual perceptual experiences (sensing the presence of an unseen person), speech that is vague but understandable, behaviour that is odd but not grossly disorganised (mumbling in public). Figure 4.7 shows how the six months are counted. The phases themselves, and what happens over the long course of the illness, are described in Section 4.6.

Criterion D: is this really a mood disorder? Psychotic symptoms are common in severe depression and mania, and mood episodes are common in schizophrenia. Criterion D rules out schizoaffective disorder and depressive or bipolar disorder with psychotic features. It is satisfied in one of two ways: either no major depressive or manic episode has occurred at the same time as the active-phase symptoms, or, if mood episodes have occurred during the active phase, they have been present for only a minority of the total duration of the active and residual periods. DSM-5-TR adds that a diagnosis of schizophrenia requires delusions or hallucinations at some time in the absence of a mood episode. How these timing rules separate the three disorders is shown in Figure 4.9 in Section 4.5.

Criterion E: is there a physical cause? The disturbance must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance (a drug of abuse or a medication) or to another medical condition. You will see in Section 4.4.6 how much clinical work hides behind that one sentence.

Criterion F: is this autism or a childhood communication disorder? Children and adults with autism spectrum disorder or a childhood-onset communication disorder may show odd speech, social withdrawal, flat expression and unusual behaviour, which could be misread as disorganised speech and negative symptoms. So if there is such a history, schizophrenia is diagnosed as an additional condition only if prominent delusions or hallucinations, along with the other required symptoms, are also present for at least one month (or less if successfully treated). Autism itself is covered in Chapter 3. Table 4.14 gathers the six criteria and the job each one does.

Table 4.14 The DSM-5-TR criteria for schizophrenia, and what each one is there to rule out.
CriterionWhat it requiresWhat it rules out
A: symptomsTwo or more of five (delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech, grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour, negative symptoms) for a significant part of one month; at least one of the first threeDiagnosis from negative symptoms and odd behaviour alone, without psychosis
B: functionMarked decline in work, relationships or self-care (or failure to reach the expected level in the young)Psychotic experiences that leave life intact
C: durationContinuous signs for at least 6 months, including at least 1 month of Criterion A; prodromal and residual periods countBrief psychotic disorder and schizophreniform disorder
D: moodNo concurrent mood episodes, or mood episodes for only a minority of the active and residual periodsSchizoaffective disorder; depression or bipolar disorder with psychotic features
E: causeNot due to a substance, medication or another medical conditionSubstance-induced psychosis; psychosis due to a medical condition
