F: developmentWith a history of autism or childhood communication disorder, prominent delusions or hallucinations for 1 monthMistaking autistic features for schizophrenia
Exam trap

The DSM duration question is set again and again, and its options are built to catch half-learned answers. The full answer is: at least 6 months of continuous signs of disturbance, of which at least 1 month must be active-phase (Criterion A) symptoms. So "1 month of active phase (6 months total)" is right, and so is "6 months" when the question asks for the total or the "minimum duration of continuous signs". "1 month" alone is the ICD answer, or the DSM active-phase answer, but not the DSM answer to "minimum duration of the disorder". Distractors of 1 week, 2 weeks, 3 months, 12 months and 2 years belong to other disorders: 2 weeks is a major depressive episode, 1 week a manic episode, 2 years persistent depressive disorder. And Criterion C does not require six months of active psychosis.

Specifiers, and the subtypes DSM-5 abandoned

Once the diagnosis is made, DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to describe it more precisely with specifiers, extra labels that add information without changing the diagnosis. Schizophrenia has three kinds.

The course specifiers may be used only after the disorder has lasted a year, because before that the pattern of the illness cannot be known. They are: first episode (currently in acute episode, in partial remission, or in full remission); multiple episodes (currently in acute episode, in partial remission, or in full remission), which requires at least two episodes, that is a first episode, a remission and at least one relapse; continuous, when symptoms meeting the criteria persist for most of the illness with only brief subthreshold periods; and unspecified. An acute episode is a period when the symptom criteria are fulfilled; partial remission is a period of maintained improvement in which the criteria are only partly fulfilled; full remission is a period with no disorder-specific symptoms.

The second specifier is with catatonia, used when the criteria for catatonia (three or more of twelve psychomotor signs, Section 4.5.7) are met, and coded with an extra code for catatonia associated with schizophrenia. The third is current severity. Each of the primary symptoms of psychosis (delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech, abnormal psychomotor behaviour and negative symptoms) is rated for its worst level in the last seven days on a five-point scale from 0 (not present) to 4 (present and severe), using the Clinician-Rated Dimensions of Psychosis Symptom Severity in Section III of the manual, which also rates impaired cognition, depression and mania. The diagnosis can be made without the severity rating; its purpose is to give a dimensional picture alongside the categorical label.

What DSM-5-TR does not contain is just as important. From DSM-III to DSM-IV-TR, the American manuals divided schizophrenia into subtypes according to the symptoms that dominated the current picture. DSM-IV (1994) had five:

  • Paranoid type: preoccupation with one or more delusions or frequent auditory hallucinations, with no prominent disorganised speech, disorganised or catatonic behaviour, or flat or inappropriate affect. Often later onset and better preserved functioning.
  • Disorganised type (Hecker's hebephrenia, Section 4.1): disorganised speech, disorganised behaviour and flat or inappropriate affect all prominent. Early, insidious onset and a poor outlook.
  • Catatonic type: a picture dominated by catatonic signs such as immobility, purposeless excitement, extreme negativism or mutism, peculiar voluntary movements, and echolalia or echopraxia.
  • Undifferentiated type: Criterion A met, but not the pattern of the paranoid, disorganised or catatonic types.
  • Residual type: a past active episode, with only negative symptoms or attenuated positive symptoms continuing.

DSM-5 (2013) deleted all five. The reasons, set out by the work group, were that the subtypes had limited diagnostic stability, low reliability and poor validity. Patients did not stay in one subtype: a man diagnosed paranoid at 25 might be undifferentiated at 30 and residual at 40, so the label described the moment, not the illness. The subtypes did not predict different responses to treatment or clearly different long-term courses. And in practice the disorganised, catatonic and residual types were rarely used, so most patients ended up paranoid or undifferentiated and the labels carried little information. In their place DSM-5 put the dimensional severity ratings, which describe the same information (how much psychosis, how much disorganisation, how many negative symptoms) without forcing a patient into a box.

DSM-5 also removed a rule that gave special weight to certain symptoms. Under DSM-IV, Criterion A needed only one symptom instead of two if the delusions were bizarre or if the hallucinations consisted of a voice keeping up a running commentary on the person's behaviour or thoughts, or two or more voices conversing with each other. Those two kinds of voice are, of course, Schneider's first-rank symptoms (Section 4.1). DSM-5 dropped the exception, for two reasons. Clinicians could not agree reliably on what made a delusion bizarre, and the special voices were shown not to be specific to schizophrenia. Today a bizarre delusion counts like any other delusion, so a person with one bizarre delusion and nothing else does not meet Criterion A; such a person may instead have delusional disorder, which DSM-5 now allows to have bizarre content (Section 4.5.4).

Exam trap

If an option offers "paranoid schizophrenia" or "disorganised schizophrenia" as a DSM-5 or DSM-5-TR diagnosis, it is wrong: those are DSM-IV subtypes (and paranoid, hebephrenic and others are ICD-10 subtypes). DSM-5 dropped the subtypes because of low reliability, poor validity and instability, not because they were "too rare". DSM-5 also dropped the special weight of bizarre delusions and Schneiderian voices; in DSM-5-TR, two Criterion A symptoms are always needed. Course specifiers are used only after one year of illness.

ICD-10 F20: the Schneiderian legacy and the one-month rule

The tenth revision of the ICD, whose chapter on mental disorders (chapter V, the F codes) came with a separate volume, Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Guidelines, published in 1992, is still the system on which many Indian hospital records, case-file codes and university syllabuses are built. Its schizophrenia, coded F20, is organised quite differently from DSM's. Instead of five symptoms of equal weight, ICD-10 lists nine groups of symptoms, labelled (a) to (i), and gives the first four groups more weight than the rest. Table 4.15 sets them out.

Table 4.15 The ICD-10 symptom groups for schizophrenia (F20), in plain words.
GroupSymptomsWeight
(a)Thought echo, thought insertion or withdrawal, thought broadcastingOne very clear symptom is enough
(b)Delusions of control, influence or passivity, clearly referred to body or limb movements or specific thoughts, actions or sensations; delusional perceptionOne very clear symptom is enough
(c)Hallucinatory voices giving a running commentary on the person's behaviour, or discussing the person among themselves, or voices coming from some part of the bodyOne very clear symptom is enough
(d)Persistent delusions of other kinds that are culturally inappropriate and completely impossible (controlling the weather, being in contact with aliens, a superhuman religious or political identity)One very clear symptom is enough
(e)Persistent hallucinations in any modality, with fleeting or half-formed delusions or persistent overvalued ideas, or occurring every day for weeks or monthsNeed two groups from (e) to (h)
(f)Breaks or interpolations in the train of thought, giving incoherent or irrelevant speech, or neologismsNeed two groups from (e) to (h)
(g)Catatonic behaviour: excitement, posturing, waxy flexibility, negativism, mutism, stuporNeed two groups from (e) to (h)
(h)Negative symptoms: marked apathy, paucity of speech, blunting or incongruity of emotional responses, not due to depression or medicationNeed two groups from (e) to (h)
(i)A significant, consistent change in personal behaviour: loss of interest, aimlessness, idleness, self-absorption, social withdrawalUsed only for simple schizophrenia

Look at groups (a), (b) and (c) and you will recognise them at once: they are Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms almost word for word. Thought echo and the three kinds of thought alienation, passivity and delusional perception, voices commenting and voices discussing the patient in the third person. Group (d) captures the "impossible" delusion that DSM-IV called bizarre. ICD-10 was, in this sense, the most Schneiderian of the modern manuals, and that is why so many Indian examination questions on first-rank symptoms sit right beside questions on ICD criteria.

The rule built on these groups is this. The normal requirement is a minimum of one very clear symptom (and usually two or more if less clear-cut) from any one of groups (a) to (d), or symptoms from at least two of groups (e) to (h), clearly present for most of the time during a period of one month or more. Read the second half carefully. A person who has only negative symptoms and catatonic behaviour, two groups from (e) to (h), qualifies under ICD-10, which is a looser rule than DSM-5's insistence on one of delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech. Notice too that group (h) lists "blunting or incongruity of emotional responses" among the negative symptoms; incongruous affect, laughing while describing a death, is placed there by ICD-10, which is why an option such as "incongruity of emotional responses" counts as a negative symptom in questions built on ICD wording (Section 4.3).

Three further rules complete the picture. First, the one-month period applies only to the specific symptoms in the table, not to any prodromal phase; a long prodrome does not count towards the month, and its absence does not prevent the diagnosis. Second, a condition that meets the symptom requirements but has lasted less than a month, whether treated or not, is diagnosed in the first instance as acute schizophrenia-like psychotic disorder (F23.2, Section 4.5.2), and reclassified as schizophrenia if the symptoms persist. Third, there are exclusions much like DSM's: schizophrenia should not be diagnosed in the presence of extensive depressive or manic symptoms unless it is clear that the schizophrenic symptoms came before the mood disturbance (if both develop together and are evenly balanced, the diagnosis is schizoaffective disorder, F25), nor in the presence of overt brain disease, nor during drug intoxication or withdrawal. ICD-10 has no criterion of functional decline equivalent to DSM's Criterion B.

ICD-10 kept subtypes, and examiners still ask about them. Each is coded by a fourth character, F20.0 to F20.9, and a fifth character can record the course: continuous, episodic with progressive deficit, episodic with stable deficit, episodic remittent, incomplete remission, complete remission, other, or period of observation less than a year. Table 4.16 lists the subtypes.

Table 4.16 The ICD-10 subtypes of schizophrenia.
CodeSubtypeWhat defines it
F20.0Paranoid schizophreniaRelatively stable, often paranoid delusions, usually with hallucinations (especially voices); affect, speech and volition relatively spared. The commonest type in most parts of the world
F20.1Hebephrenic schizophreniaProminent affective change (shallow, silly, inappropriate mood, giggling, mannerisms, pranks), disorganised thought and rambling speech, fleeting delusions and hallucinations. Onset usually between 15 and 25; poor outlook because negative symptoms develop rapidly
F20.2Catatonic schizophreniaPsychomotor disturbance dominates: stupor, excitement, posturing, negativism, rigidity, waxy flexibility, command automatism. Now rare in industrial countries, still seen elsewhere
F20.3Undifferentiated schizophreniaGeneral criteria met, but no single subtype fits, or features of several without one predominating
F20.4Post-schizophrenic depressionA depressive episode (at least 2 weeks) arising within 12 months after a schizophrenic illness, while some schizophrenic symptoms are still present
F20.5Residual schizophreniaA chronic stage after at least one clear psychotic episode: prominent negative symptoms, with florid symptoms minimal for at least a year
F20.6Simple schizophreniaInsidious, progressive development over at least a year of negative symptoms and decline (group (i)) without any history of hallucinations, delusions or other overt psychotic symptoms
F20.8 and F20.9Other; unspecifiedResidual categories

Two of these subtypes deserve a second look, because they are unusual. Simple schizophrenia is the only form of schizophrenia anywhere in modern classification that can be diagnosed without a psychotic symptom ever having occurred: a slow slide into apathy, idleness, aimlessness and social withdrawal over at least a year. ICD-10 itself warns that it is a difficult diagnosis to make with confidence, because the same picture can come from depression, a personality change or a drifting life; it was dropped in ICD-11. Post-schizophrenic depression recognises that a depressive episode commonly follows an acute psychotic episode, often as insight returns, and that this is a period of high suicide risk (Section 4.6). DSM handles the same situation differently: a depressive episode after psychosis is coded as an additional other specified or unspecified depressive disorder rather than as a form of schizophrenia.

Exam trap

The ICD-10 duration for schizophrenia is 1 month (symptoms present most of the time during a period of one month or more), not 6 months, 3 months or 2 weeks. When an option lists something that is not in any group, such as catastrophic thinking, it is the odd one out: catastrophic thinking is a cognitive distortion from Aaron Beck's cognitive model of anxiety and depression (Book 2, Section 5.3), not a psychotic symptom. Delusions, hallucinations and disorganised speech all appear in the ICD groups. Simple schizophrenia (F20.6) needs one year, and is the subtype with no positive symptoms.

ICD-11 6A20: fewer rules, more description

The eleventh revision was adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and came into effect on 1 January 2022, and its clinical guidance for mental disorders, the Clinical Descriptions and Diagnostic Requirements, followed. In India this matters legally as well as clinically: Section 3(1) of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 says that mental illness shall be determined in accordance with nationally or internationally accepted medical standards, including the latest edition of the International Classification of Disease of the World Health Organisation, as notified by the Central Government (Book 2, Section 1.10).

ICD-11 places schizophrenia in a block called schizophrenia or other primary psychotic disorders. "Primary" means that the psychosis is not the result of a substance or a medical condition; those secondary psychoses are coded elsewhere. The block contains schizophrenia (6A20), schizoaffective disorder (6A21), schizotypal disorder (6A22), acute and transient psychotic disorder (6A23), delusional disorder (6A24), the symptom specifiers (6A25), and other specified and unspecified categories. You will meet each member in Section 4.5.

The ICD-11 description of schizophrenia (6A20) lists seven kinds of symptom: (a) persistent delusions; (b) persistent hallucinations, most commonly auditory; (c) disorganised thinking or formal thought disorder, such as tangentiality, loose associations, irrelevant speech or neologisms, which in its severe form becomes incoherent "word salad"; (d) experiences of influence, passivity or control, that is the experience that one's feelings, impulses, actions or thoughts are not one's own, are inserted or withdrawn by others, or are broadcast; (e) negative symptoms such as affective flattening, alogia or paucity of speech, avolition, asociality and anhedonia; (f) grossly disorganised behaviour that impedes goal-directed activity; and (g) psychomotor disturbances such as catatonic restlessness or agitation, posturing, waxy flexibility, negativism, mutism or stupor. The requirement is that at least two of these be present most of the time for a period of one month or more, with at least one from (a) to (d), and that the symptoms not be a manifestation of another medical condition or of a substance or medication, including withdrawal.

Compare this with the two older systems and you can see what the WHO borrowed from each. From ICD-10 it kept the one-month rule and gave passivity experiences their own place among the core symptoms. From DSM-5 it took the idea that two symptoms are needed, with one of them a core psychotic symptom, so that a single first-rank symptom is no longer enough on its own. Like ICD-10, and unlike DSM-5-TR, it does not demand a decline in functioning. Table 4.17 sets the three systems side by side.

ICD-11 abolished all the subtypes, simple schizophrenia and post-schizophrenic depression included, and replaced them with two sets of qualifiers. The course is coded in the code itself: first episode (6A20.0), multiple episodes (6A20.1) or continuous (6A20.2), each further described as currently symptomatic, in partial remission or in full remission. The current clinical picture is then described with the symptom specifiers of 6A25, which can be applied to any primary psychotic disorder: positive symptoms (6A25.0), negative symptoms (6A25.1), depressive mood symptoms (6A25.2), manic mood symptoms (6A25.3), psychomotor symptoms (6A25.4) and cognitive symptoms (6A25.5), each rated for severity. Any number may be used together. If the full syndrome of catatonia is present, it is coded separately as catatonia associated with another mental disorder (6A40). The design is plainly dimensional: rather than asking "which kind of schizophrenia is this?", ICD-11 asks "how much of each kind of symptom does this person have now?"

Table 4.17 Schizophrenia in the three classification systems.
FeatureDSM-5-TR (2022)ICD-10 F20 (1992)ICD-11 6A20 (2022)
Minimum duration6 months of continuous signs, including at least 1 month of active symptoms1 month of characteristic symptoms (prodrome not counted)1 month of characteristic symptoms
Symptom rule2 of 5, at least one delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech1 very clear from (a) to (d), or 2 groups from (e) to (h)2 of 7, at least one from (a) to (d)
First-rank symptomsNo special weightOne clear first-rank symptom can sufficeCore symptom, but a second symptom is needed
Functional declineRequired (Criterion B)Not requiredNot required
SubtypesDropped in DSM-5 (2013)Paranoid, hebephrenic, catatonic, undifferentiated, post-schizophrenic depression, residual, simpleAbolished; course and symptom specifiers instead
Under 1 month of psychosisBrief psychotic disorderAcute schizophrenia-like psychotic disorder (F23.2)Acute and transient psychotic disorder, or other primary psychotic disorder
1 to 6 monthsSchizophreniform disorderSchizophreniaSchizophrenia (a fluctuating, polymorphic episode under 3 months stays acute and transient)
Exam trap

ICD-11, like ICD-10, needs symptoms for at least 1 month; the 6-month rule is purely American (DSM-III, DSM-IV and DSM-5). The trap options are 2 weeks (the ICD acute and transient psychotic disorder onset window, Section 4.5.2), 3 months (the ICD-11 limit for acute and transient psychotic disorder and the ICD minimum for delusional disorder) and 6 months (DSM). Also remember that ICD-11 has no subtypes: "paranoid schizophrenia, ICD-11" does not exist. Schizophreniform disorder as a one-to-six-month category is a DSM diagnosis; ICD calls an illness of that length schizophrenia.

What else could it be? The differential diagnosis

Criteria D and E, and the exclusions of ICD, are short sentences, but behind them lies the most important clinical work of the first assessment. The differential diagnosis is the list of other conditions that could produce the same picture, each of which has to be considered and, where possible, ruled out. For a first episode of psychosis the list falls into four families: mood disorders, the other psychotic disorders, physical causes (substances and medical illnesses), and non-psychotic conditions that can look psychotic.

Mood disorders with psychotic features. A severely depressed woman may hear a voice telling her she is a sinner, and a manic man may believe he has been chosen by God; both are psychotic, and neither has schizophrenia. The rule that separates them is timing: if delusions or hallucinations occur only during a major depressive or manic episode, the diagnosis is depressive or bipolar disorder with psychotic features, however florid the psychosis. A useful, though not decisive, clue is the content of the psychotic symptoms. In mood-congruent psychotic features the content fits the mood: guilt, deserved punishment, poverty, disease, nihilism ("my intestines have rotted") or death in depression, and inflated worth, power, knowledge or a special relationship with a deity or a famous person in mania. Mood-incongruent features, such as thought insertion or voices commenting in a neutral tone in a depressed patient, raise the possibility of schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder but do not settle it. First-rank symptoms are reported in a substantial minority of people with mania, which is why Schneider's symptoms cannot by themselves distinguish the two great psychoses that Kraepelin separated. Depressive and bipolar disorders are taught in Chapter 5.

The other psychotic disorders. If the psychosis has lasted less than six months, DSM-5-TR offers brief psychotic disorder or schizophreniform disorder; if a mood episode runs through most of the illness, schizoaffective disorder; if there is a single persistent delusion with little else, delusional disorder. These neighbours, and the rules that divide them, are the subject of Section 4.5. Schizotypal personality disorder lies on the near side of the psychotic threshold: odd beliefs, unusual perceptions and eccentric behaviour that are long-standing traits rather than a change of state (Chapter 11).

Substances and medications. Ask every person with a first episode about cannabis, alcohol, stimulants and prescribed drugs, and test the urine. Amphetamines and cocaine can produce a paranoid psychosis within hours or days, cannabis an anxious persecutory state, hallucinogens and phencyclidine vivid perceptual changes, alcohol withdrawal a delirium with visual and tactile hallucinations, and long heavy drinking an auditory hallucinosis in clear consciousness. Corticosteroids, levodopa and other antiparkinsonian drugs, anticholinergic drugs and many others can do the same. The key is the time relationship: substance-induced psychosis begins during or soon after intoxication, withdrawal or a change of medication, and psychotic symptoms that persist for a substantial period, about a month, after the substance is stopped point towards an independent psychotic disorder (Section 4.5.6 and Chapter 10). The two can, of course, coexist; many people with schizophrenia also use cannabis.

Medical and neurological illness. DSM-5-TR offers three questions for deciding whether a medical condition is causing psychosis. Biological plausibility: is there an illness that can cause psychosis through a known mechanism? Temporality: did the psychosis begin, worsen or remit along with the illness? Typicality: are there features unusual for a primary psychotic disorder, such as onset in later life, visual or olfactory hallucinations, or a dreamlike quality to the delusions? The causes are many: delirium (marked by clouded and fluctuating consciousness, disorientation and poor attention); epilepsy, especially of the temporal lobe (olfactory hallucinations are a classic clue, and postictal psychosis is the commonest epileptic psychosis); brain tumours, strokes, head injury and multiple sclerosis; infections of the brain, HIV and neurosyphilis; thyroid, parathyroid and adrenal disease; vitamin B12 deficiency; systemic lupus erythematosus; Huntington's disease, Wilson's disease, Parkinson's disease and the dementias, especially Lewy body dementia. An important newer cause is anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis, an autoimmune illness described by the neurologist Josep Dalmau and his colleagues in 2007, in which antibodies attack the brain's NMDA glutamate receptors. It typically strikes young women, sometimes in association with an ovarian teratoma; it often begins, sometimes after a flu-like illness, with an acute psychosis and anxiety, and progresses over days to weeks to seizures, abnormal movements, catatonia, autonomic instability and falling consciousness. Recognised early, it responds to immunotherapy and removal of the tumour. It is also a striking piece of evidence for the glutamate hypothesis of Section 4.8. Age matters too: DSM-5-TR estimates that about 60 percent of older individuals with new-onset psychosis have a medical cause.

Conditions that can look psychotic. In obsessive-compulsive disorder and body dysmorphic disorder, insight can be poor or absent, so that the person is completely convinced that the door really is contaminated or that his nose really is deformed; DSM-5-TR then diagnoses obsessive-compulsive or body dysmorphic disorder "with absent insight/delusional beliefs", not a psychotic disorder, because the belief belongs to the obsessive-compulsive picture. In post-traumatic stress disorder, flashbacks can have a hallucinatory quality and hypervigilance can reach paranoid proportions, but the trauma and the reliving symptoms identify the disorder. Autism spectrum disorder was discussed under Criterion F. Dissociative disorders and possession states can produce voices and altered identity (Chapter 7). Finally, culture: beliefs in the evil eye, sorcery, spirit possession or illness caused by curses are widely held in many communities, and hearing the voice of a recently dead parent is common in bereavement. DSM-5-TR stresses that such culturally shared beliefs and experiences are not delusions or hallucinations; the test is whether the person's own cultural group regards the belief or experience as normal. Table 4.18 gathers the clues.

Table 4.18 The differential diagnosis of a first episode of psychosis: what points away from schizophrenia.
PossibilityClues that point to itDeciding test or rule
Mood disorder with psychotic featuresPsychosis appears only inside a depressive or manic episode; mood-congruent contentCareful longitudinal history: is there ever psychosis without a mood episode?
Substance-induced psychosisOnset during intoxication or withdrawal; stimulants, cannabis, alcohol, steroidsUrine drug screen; does the psychosis clear within about a month of stopping?
DeliriumClouded, fluctuating consciousness; disorientation; visual hallucinations; acute onset in illnessBedside cognitive testing; search for infection, metabolic or drug cause
Epilepsy, tumour, stroke, encephalitisOlfactory or visual hallucinations, seizures, headache, focal signs, late onsetNeurological examination, EEG, brain imaging, cerebrospinal fluid
Anti-NMDA receptor encephalitisYoung woman, acute psychosis, then seizures, dyskinesias, catatonia, autonomic instabilityAntibodies in serum and cerebrospinal fluid; pelvic imaging for teratoma
OCD or body dysmorphic disorderConviction is about obsessions, contamination or appearance; rituals"With absent insight/delusional beliefs" specifier, not psychosis
Cultural belief or normal experienceBelief shared by the person's community; bereavement voices; religious ritualAsk whether the person's own group sees it as abnormal
Clinical link

A first episode of psychosis is never diagnosed on the mental status examination alone. The routine work-up includes a physical and neurological examination, a full blood count, blood sugar, kidney, liver and thyroid function, vitamin B12, tests for HIV and syphilis, a urine drug screen, and usually brain imaging; an EEG, cerebrospinal fluid and autoimmune antibodies are added when the picture is atypical (rapid onset, confusion, seizures, abnormal movements, catatonia, fever). As a psychologist you will often be the person who notices that something does not fit: a patient whose "voices" are accompanied by smells of burning, or whose concentration fluctuates from hour to hour. Saying so is part of the job.

Exam trap

No single feature proves a medical cause or excludes schizophrenia. Visual hallucinations are not rare in schizophrenia, and olfactory hallucinations are consistent with it; they only raise the index of suspicion. Equally, first-rank symptoms do not exclude mania. The decisive rules are about time: whether psychosis occurs outside mood episodes, and whether it began and ended with a substance or an illness.

Ganesh, diagnosed three ways

Now return to Ganesh and push his case through all three systems. Begin with the facts the clinician gathered. The work-up found nothing physical: no fever or confusion, a normal neurological examination, normal blood tests, a negative urine drug screen, and a family who were sure he had never used cannabis or alcohol. He had never had a depressive or manic episode; his mood was flat rather than low, and he denied sadness. His symptoms were (1) voices of two men discussing him in the third person, for six weeks, most days; (2) the belief that his thoughts were being pulled out and broadcast over the temple loudspeaker, for the same six weeks; (3) speech that derailed from topic to topic; and (4) for about four months before that, and continuing, withdrawal, loss of drive and a decline in self-care. So his illness has lasted about five months in all, of which about six weeks has been the active phase.

ICD-10. Thought withdrawal and thought broadcasting belong to group (a); voices discussing him belong to group (c). One very clear symptom from (a) to (d) would have been enough, and he has two, present most of the time for more than a month. Mood, brain disease and drugs have been excluded. The diagnosis is schizophrenia, F20; because delusions and hallucinations dominate while affect and speech are less affected, the subtype is paranoid schizophrenia, F20.0, with a fifth character of 9 (period of observation less than one year).

ICD-11. He has persistent delusions (a), persistent hallucinations (b), disorganised thinking (c), experiences of influence (d) and negative symptoms (e): far more than the two required, for more than a month. The diagnosis is schizophrenia, first episode, currently symptomatic (6A20.00), with symptom specifiers for positive symptoms (severe) and negative symptoms (moderate), and perhaps cognitive symptoms once he has been tested.

DSM-5-TR. Criterion A: delusions, hallucinations and disorganised speech, three of the first three, for a significant part of a month. Criterion B: his studies and self-care have collapsed. Criteria D, E and F: no mood episodes, no substance, no medical condition, no history of autism. Criterion C: here is the snag. The question is not how long he has been psychotic, but how long continuous signs of the disturbance have lasted, prodrome included. His withdrawal began about five months ago, so even counting the prodrome the six months are not yet complete. Today Ganesh has had symptoms for less than six months and has not recovered, and his DSM-5-TR diagnosis is schizophreniform disorder (provisional). If he is still ill a month from now, when the six months are complete, the diagnosis changes to schizophrenia; if he recovers before then, it stays schizophreniform disorder (Section 4.5.1). The clinician's dating of the prodrome therefore matters a great deal: had his parents placed the first change seven months back, the same interview would have produced a DSM diagnosis of schizophrenia today. The course specifiers cannot be applied for another year.

Notice what this exercise has taught you. The same young man, on the same day, can have schizophrenia in one manual and a provisional, potentially recoverable disorder in another. The disagreement is not about his symptoms but about time, and it goes all the way back to the different choices the St Louis group (1972) and the WHO (1992) made decades ago. Notice too what all three systems agree on: before any of them can be applied, the clinician must look for a mood disorder, a drug and a disease. In Ganesh's case the agreement on the treatment is complete. Whatever the label, he needs an antipsychotic (Section 4.10), a family who understand what is happening to him (Section 4.9), and a clinician who will keep watching how his illness unfolds.

4.5 The Rest of the SpectrumMedium yield · 10 Qs

Schizophrenia sits at the centre of its chapter in DSM-5-TR, but it is surrounded by neighbours. Some of them have exactly the same symptoms and differ only in how long they last. Some have a narrower set of symptoms: a single fixed delusion, or a disturbance of movement. Some mix psychosis with a mood disorder. And some have the same symptoms but a cause that can be found: a drug, a medication, an inflamed brain. DSM-5-TR says that the chapter is organised along a gradient of psychopathology, and tells the clinician to work along it in order: first consider conditions that fall short of a full psychotic disorder or affect only one domain, then the time-limited conditions, and only then schizophrenia, after excluding the conditions that can give rise to psychosis. This section follows roughly that path, beginning with the disorders defined by the clock.

A spectrum organised by time: brief psychotic disorder and schizophreniform disorder

Fatima, 26, had been married for eight months and had moved from her parents' home in Lucknow to her husband's joint family in Pune when her father died suddenly of a heart attack. She travelled home for the funeral and came back to Pune a week later. Within three days she was sleeping barely two hours a night. She sat on the floor of the kitchen, frightened and bewildered, asking her mother-in-law where she was. She said she could hear her father calling her name from the terrace, then that the house was full of people who were not really people, then that her in-laws were mixing poison into her tea. One hour she laughed loudly; the next she wept and begged to be taken home. Her husband brought her to a general hospital psychiatry unit on the fifth day of the illness. A physical examination and blood tests found nothing, she had never taken alcohol or drugs, and she had no history of depression or mania. She was given a small dose of an antipsychotic and admitted for ten days. By the end of the third week she was sleeping normally, embarrassed by what she had said, grieving for her father in the ordinary way, and back to her usual self.

What does Fatima have? Not schizophrenia: her illness lasted three weeks and left no trace. DSM-5-TR calls it brief psychotic disorder. The criteria are simple. Criterion A requires one or more of four symptoms, delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech (frequent derailment or incoherence), and grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour, at least one of which must be one of the first three; a symptom does not count if it is a culturally sanctioned response. Notice that negative symptoms are not on the list: a few weeks of apathy cannot make a brief psychotic disorder. Criterion B is the defining one: an episode lasts at least one day but less than one month, with eventual full return to the premorbid level of functioning. Criterion C excludes depressive or bipolar disorder with psychotic features, other psychotic disorders such as schizophrenia, catatonia, substances and medical conditions.

Three specifiers describe the setting. With marked stressor(s), also called brief reactive psychosis (the name of the disorder in DSM-III), applies when the symptoms occur in response to events that, singly or together, would be markedly stressful to almost anyone in similar circumstances in the person's culture; Fatima's sudden bereavement, so soon after leaving her own family, qualifies. Without marked stressor(s) applies when there is no such event. With peripartum onset applies when the illness begins during pregnancy or within four weeks after delivery; psychosis after childbirth is often an early form of bipolar disorder and is taught in Chapter 5. As with schizophrenia, catatonia and current severity can also be specified.

The clinical picture is often dramatic. DSM-5-TR describes emotional turmoil or overwhelming confusion, with rapid shifts from one intense feeling to another, exactly what Fatima's family saw. Although the illness is short, impairment may be severe, and supervision may be needed to keep the person fed, clean and safe from acting on delusions; the risk of suicidal behaviour is raised during the acute episode. Brief psychotic disorder accounts for about 2 to 7 percent of first-onset psychoses. It can begin at any age, with an average onset in the mid-30s, later than schizophrenia. The outlook is mixed. By definition the episode remits within a month, and for most people social functioning and symptoms recover well, but more than half later have a relapse, and in a minority the diagnosis later changes, more often to a schizophrenia spectrum disorder than to a mood disorder.

Two other differential points are worth knowing. In some people with personality disorders, borderline personality disorder above all, severe stress can bring brief psychotic experiences lasting hours; these do not usually warrant a separate diagnosis, but if they last at least a day, brief psychotic disorder may be added. And because the symptoms are short-lived and dramatic, factitious disorder and malingering have to be considered when there is a clear gain from appearing ill. Fatima's grief also raises a cultural question. In many cultures it is common for bereaved relatives to hear, see or speak with a recently dead person without any pathology following, and a single experience of hearing her father's voice would have meant nothing. What made Fatima psychotic was the whole picture: perplexity, persecutory delusions, disorganised behaviour and the collapse of her daily functioning.

Now stretch the clock. Suppose Fatima's psychosis had not cleared at three weeks but had continued for three months, with voices and delusions and, by the second month, some flatness and withdrawal, and had then lifted completely. That illness meets the symptom criteria of schizophrenia (Criterion A) but not its duration, and DSM-5-TR calls it schizophreniform disorder. The criteria are those of schizophrenia with three differences. The episode, counting prodromal, active and residual phases, lasts at least one month but less than six months. There is no requirement of functional decline. And when the diagnosis must be made before the person has recovered, it is labelled provisional, because no one yet knows whether the illness will stop before six months. Ganesh in Section 4.4.7 is exactly such a case: if he is still ill at six months, his diagnosis becomes schizophrenia.

The term goes back to the Norwegian psychiatrist Gabriel Langfeldt (Section 4.1), who in follow-up studies published in 1937 and 1939 divided the patients then called schizophrenic into two groups: a "true" or typical schizophrenia with a poor outcome, and the schizophreniform psychoses, which looked less typical and recovered. The idea was influential, but it had a curious fate. When Norwegian researchers re-examined Langfeldt's own schizophreniform cases fifty years later (Bergem and colleagues, 1990), they found that most of them did not look schizophrenia-like at all and were mainly mood disorders. The modern DSM category keeps Langfeldt's name and his interest in prognosis, but defines the disorder purely by duration.

That interest in prognosis survives in a specifier. Schizophreniform disorder is coded with good prognostic features when at least two of the following are present: onset of prominent psychotic symptoms within four weeks of the first noticeable change in behaviour or functioning; confusion or perplexity at the height of the episode; good premorbid social and occupational functioning; and absence of blunted or flat affect. Otherwise it is coded without good prognostic features. Each of these four mirrors one of the classic predictors of a good outcome in schizophrenia itself (Section 4.6): acute onset, a confusional, affect-laden picture, a good life before the illness, and no negative symptoms. The course bears this out only partly. About a third of people first diagnosed with schizophreniform disorder (provisional) recover within six months and keep that diagnosis; most of the remaining two-thirds are eventually diagnosed with schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder. In the United States and other high-income countries schizophreniform disorder is uncommon, perhaps five times less common than schizophrenia, but in lower-income countries, DSM-5-TR notes, it may be more common, especially the form with good prognostic features, and in some settings may be as common as schizophrenia. That observation will matter when you meet the acute psychoses of the next subsection.

Put the three DSM-5-TR disorders side by side and you have the duration ladder of Figure 4.8: the same psychotic symptoms are called brief psychotic disorder if they last a day to under a month, schizophreniform disorder if they last a month to under six months, and schizophrenia if they last six months or more. The ICD rungs are placed differently, which is why the figure shows both systems.

Exam trap

The official key to one IHBAS 2025 question calls psychosis "lasting for at least one month but less than six months" brief psychotic disorder. That is wrong. Brief psychotic disorder ends within a month (at least 1 day, less than 1 month, with full recovery). An illness with schizophrenia's symptoms lasting 1 to under 6 months is schizophreniform disorder. Schizoaffective disorder needs a major mood episode, delusional disorder has only delusions, and schizophrenia needs 6 months in DSM (the stem's wording, "grossly disorganized or abnormal motor behavior", is DSM-5's; under ICD-10 or ICD-11, with their one-month rule, the same illness would be schizophrenia). If you meet that exact question again with schizophreniform disorder absent from the options, know that the key given was brief psychotic disorder; whenever schizophreniform disorder is offered, choose it. Also remember: schizophreniform disorder does not require a decline in functioning, and brief psychotic disorder does not count negative symptoms.

Acute and transient psychotic disorders: the ICD view

If Fatima had been seen in a hospital that codes with ICD-10, as many Indian hospitals still do, she would not have received a diagnosis of brief psychotic disorder, because ICD-10 has no such category. She would have been given one of the acute and transient psychotic disorders (F23), a group the WHO introduced in 1992 to bring together acute psychoses of this kind, which clinicians in many countries, India among them, see often. The group is built on three key features, which ICD-10 lists in order of priority, and an expectation about recovery. First and most important, acute onset: a change from a state without psychotic features to a clearly abnormal psychotic state within two weeks or less (with "abrupt" onset, within 48 hours or less, recorded separately). Second, the presence of typical syndromes, either a rapidly changing, variable "polymorphic" state or one with typical schizophrenic symptoms. Third, the presence or absence of an associated acute stress, recorded with a fifth character: events that would be stressful to most people in the same culture, such as bereavement, the unexpected loss of a partner or a job, or the psychological trauma of combat, terrorism or torture, occurring within about two weeks before the first psychotic symptoms. Beyond these three, ICD-10 expects early recovery: complete recovery usually occurs within two to three months, often within a few weeks or even days. Organic causes, intoxication and mood episodes are excluded.

The polymorphic picture is the heart of the concept. ICD-10 describes hallucinations, delusions and perceptual disturbances that are obvious but markedly variable, changing in type and intensity from day to day or even within a day, with emotional turmoil, intense transient feelings of happiness or ecstasy, or of anxiety and irritability, and often perplexity. Read that description beside Fatima's story and you will see that she fits it closely. The subtypes are shown in Table 4.19. They are separated by two things: whether the picture is polymorphic, schizophrenia-like or predominantly delusional, and how long it may last before the diagnosis must change.

Table 4.19 Acute psychoses in ICD-10, ICD-11 and DSM-5-TR.
CategoryPictureIf it lasts longer
ICD-10 F23.0 acute polymorphic psychotic disorder without symptoms of schizophreniaVariable, rapidly changing delusions, hallucinations and emotional turmoil; includes bouffée délirante and cycloid psychosis without schizophrenic symptomsBeyond 3 months: change the diagnosis (persistent delusional disorder or other psychosis)
ICD-10 F23.1 acute polymorphic psychotic disorder with symptoms of schizophreniaPolymorphic picture plus typical schizophrenic symptoms; includes bouffée délirante and cycloid psychosis with schizophrenic symptomsSchizophrenic symptoms beyond 1 month: schizophrenia
ICD-10 F23.2 acute schizophrenia-like psychotic disorderStable schizophrenic symptoms without the polymorphic turmoilBeyond 1 month: schizophrenia
ICD-10 F23.3 other acute predominantly delusional psychotic disorderRelatively stable delusions (often persecution or reference) or hallucinationsDelusions beyond 3 months: persistent delusional disorder
ICD-11 6A23 acute and transient psychotic disorderOnset without prodrome, maximal severity within 2 weeks; rapidly changing symptoms; perplexity; no negative symptomsEpisode does not exceed 3 months (usually a few days to 1 month)
DSM-5-TR brief psychotic disorderAny of delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech or behaviour; full recovery1 month or more: schizophreniform or another disorder

The two polymorphic subtypes gathered up two older European ideas. In France, Valentin Magnan (1835 to 1916) and his followers in the 1880s described bouffée délirante, literally a "puff" or sudden gust of delusion: a delusional state that appears out of a clear sky in a previously well person, with fleeting, many-sided delusions and hallucinations, and clears within weeks or months, which they contrasted with the chronic, systematised delusional illnesses that led to deterioration. In Germany, Karl Kleist introduced the term cycloid psychosis in the 1920s (sources give 1926 or 1928) for phasic illnesses that came and went like manic-depressive illness, often swinging between contrasting poles, and left no defect; Karl Leonhard (1957) described three forms, the anxiety-happiness psychosis, the confusion psychosis and the motility psychosis, disturbing affect, thinking and movement respectively, and held that they belonged neither with schizophrenia nor with manic-depressive illness. Both concepts share what ICD-10 wanted to capture: sudden onset, a polymorphic picture and recovery.

ICD-11 kept the category but trimmed it. Acute and transient psychotic disorder (6A23) is now restricted to the polymorphic form: an acute onset of psychotic symptoms that emerge without a prodrome and reach their maximal severity within two weeks; symptoms that may include delusions, hallucinations, disorganised thinking, perplexity or confusion, and disturbances of affect and mood, sometimes with catatonia-like disturbance; and above all symptoms that change rapidly in nature and intensity from day to day, or even within a single day. Negative symptoms must be absent during the episode. The episode does not exceed three months and most commonly lasts from a few days to a month. Acute presentations with stable schizophrenic symptoms are no longer placed here: the ICD-11 diagnostic guidance says that an illness meeting the requirements for schizophrenia except that it has lasted less than a month, with no previous history of schizophrenia, is coded as other specified primary psychotic disorder (6A2Y), not as acute and transient psychotic disorder, and if it lasts a month it is schizophrenia. The rule also works the other way. A fluctuating, polymorphic episode that has met the symptom requirements of schizophrenia for more than a month, but is still under three months and has no history of schizophrenia behind it, is coded as acute and transient psychotic disorder, not schizophrenia. ICD-11 also codes the course: first episode or multiple episodes, currently symptomatic or in partial or full remission.

Key study: Susser and Wanderling's acute remitting psychoses (1994)

Were acute, quickly remitting psychoses really commoner in poorer countries, or did psychiatrists there simply label them differently? Ezra Susser and Joseph Wanderling went back to the WHO Determinants of Outcome study (Section 4.6), which had counted every new case of psychosis in defined areas of several countries, and picked out what they called nonaffective acute remitting psychosis: psychosis of acute onset, without mood disorder, that remitted fully. Its incidence in the developing-country setting was about ten times that in the industrialised-country setting, and in men about half that in women, a pattern sharply different from that of schizophrenia, which varied little between settings or sexes. The conclusion was that these psychoses may be a distinct group with causes of their own; other studies have linked them to stress and to fever and infection. The criticism came partly from the same group: when Ramin Mojtabai, Vijoy Varma and Susser (2000) measured how long such psychoses lasted, the commonest duration was two to four months, so that few fitted the ICD-10 time limits, and the criteria have kept being adjusted.

Indian context

Acute and transient psychoses are a common diagnosis in Indian hospitals, especially among young women, and Indian psychiatrists have contributed much of the research on them. At the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh, Das, Malhotra and Basu (1999) found that acute and transient psychotic disorder ran in families differently from schizophrenia, and the same group (2001) tested the stress-vulnerability idea in these disorders. The outcome is usually good, which is one reason why the better outcome of psychosis in India reported by the WHO studies (Section 4.6) has been questioned: part of the difference may reflect more acute, remitting illness rather than better recovery from the same illness. In the clinic, a sudden psychosis after a marriage, a death, an examination or a fever, in someone who was well a fortnight earlier, should make you think first of this category, and also of infection, delirium and drugs.

Exam trap

Two weeks is the ICD onset window for acute and transient psychotic disorder, not the minimum duration of schizophrenia. ICD-11 allows an acute and transient psychotic disorder to last up to 3 months, longer than DSM's brief psychotic disorder (under 1 month), so the two categories overlap but are not identical. Bouffée délirante is French (Magnan); cycloid psychosis is German (Kleist, Leonhard); both are folded into ICD-10 F23.0 (or F23.1 when schizophrenic symptoms are present).

Schizoaffective disorder: when psychosis and mood run together

Kraepelin's great division of 1899 separated a deteriorating psychosis (dementia praecox) from an episodic one (manic-depressive insanity). Almost at once clinicians began to meet patients who would not stay on either side of the line. In 1933 the American psychiatrist Jacob Kasanin published a paper called "The acute schizoaffective psychoses", describing a small group of young people whose illnesses began suddenly, often after a stress, with a striking mixture of schizophrenic and emotional symptoms, who had been well adjusted before, and who recovered. The word he coined has lasted, though the concept has changed almost beyond recognition. Kasanin's patients had a good outlook; today's schizoaffective disorder is usually a long illness whose prognosis, DSM-5-TR says, is somewhat better than that of schizophrenia but worse than that of the mood disorders.

Bindu is a 31-year-old schoolteacher in Kochi. Her illness began four years ago, when over a few weeks she started to hear a woman's voice calling her a prostitute and became convinced that her colleagues had hidden cameras in the staffroom. Her mood at that time was not unusual. After about a month of this she became manic: she stopped sleeping, spent her salary on gifts for strangers, and announced that she had been chosen to reform the state's education system, while the voice continued. The mania lasted two months; when it settled, the voice and the belief about the cameras lingered for another three weeks before fading. A year later she had a severe depressive episode, during which the same voice told her that she deserved to die. Looking back over the four years, her treating team calculated that she had been in a manic or depressive episode for most of the time she had been ill, and that she had twice had periods of more than two weeks of psychosis with no mood episode at all.

Bindu has schizoaffective disorder, and the DSM-5-TR criteria show why. Criterion A requires an uninterrupted period of illness during which a major mood episode, depressive or manic, is present at the same time as Criterion A of schizophrenia. If the mood episode is depressive it must include depressed mood itself; loss of interest or pleasure alone is not enough, because anhedonia is so common in schizophrenia that it would make every patient eligible. Criterion B requires delusions or hallucinations for two weeks or more in the absence of a major mood episode at some point in the lifetime of the illness. Criterion C requires that symptoms meeting criteria for a major mood episode be present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness. Criterion D excludes substances and medical conditions. Schizophrenia's own Criteria B (functional decline), C (six months) and F (autism) need not be met.

Each criterion has a job, and the jobs become clear if you set schizoaffective disorder between its two neighbours, as Figure 4.9 does. Criterion B separates it from a depressive or bipolar disorder with psychotic features, in which the psychosis appears only inside the mood episodes: a patient who has never been psychotic outside a mood episode cannot have schizoaffective disorder. Criterion C separates it from schizophrenia, in which mood episodes may occur but occupy only a minority of the illness. DSM-5-TR gives two worked examples. In one, a person has two months of hallucinations and persecutory delusions, then four months in which a full major depressive episode and the psychosis run together, then one month of psychosis alone after the depression lifts: seven months in all, with depression present for the majority, so the diagnosis is schizoaffective disorder. In the other, a person with a four-year history of schizophrenia develops depressive and manic episodes that together occupy no more than one year of the four: mood episodes for a minority, so the diagnosis stays schizophrenia. The majority rule was introduced in DSM-5; DSM-IV had asked only that mood symptoms be present for a "substantial" portion of the illness, and the change makes schizoaffective disorder a rarer diagnosis than it used to be.

The type is specified by the mood episodes: the bipolar type applies if a manic episode is part of the presentation (major depressive episodes may also occur), as in Bindu's case, and the depressive type if only major depressive episodes occur. Catatonia, course (after one year) and current severity can be specified as in schizophrenia. Because the judgement depends on the proportion of mood to psychosis over the whole course, it needs a long view of the illness, often reconstructed from records and from relatives, and the diagnosis can legitimately change: a person diagnosed with schizoaffective disorder whose later years bring only residual psychosis may be rediagnosed with schizophrenia, and a person first diagnosed with a mood disorder may become schizoaffective when psychosis appears between episodes.

Schizoaffective disorder is about a third as common as schizophrenia; a Finnish study estimated a lifetime prevalence of 0.3 percent, higher in women, under the looser DSM-IV criteria. It usually begins in early adulthood. The bipolar type may be commoner in young adults and the depressive type in older adults. The risk of schizoaffective disorder is raised in people who have a first-degree relative with schizophrenia, bipolar disorder or schizoaffective disorder itself, and polygenic risk scores for schizophrenia, bipolar disorder and major depression may all be raised, which is exactly what you would expect of a disorder that sits on the boundary. The lifetime risk of suicide in schizophrenia and schizoaffective disorder together is about 5 percent, and depressive symptoms raise it. Treatment combines an antipsychotic with treatment of the mood episodes, a mood stabiliser for the bipolar type and an antidepressant where appropriate for the depressive type (Section 4.10 and Chapter 5).

The ICD systems draw the boundary in a completely different place, and the difference is a favourite of examiners. ICD-10 (F25) and ICD-11 (6A21) define schizoaffective disorder by the episode, not the lifetime course. In ICD-11 the requirements for schizophrenia must be met at the same time as those for a moderate or severe depressive episode, a manic episode or a mixed episode, with the psychotic and mood symptoms starting simultaneously or within a few days of each other, and lasting at least a month. ICD-10 similarly required definite schizophrenic and definite affective symptoms to be prominent simultaneously, or within a few days, in the same episode, and it had manic, depressive and mixed types; it explicitly excluded people whose schizophrenic and affective symptoms appeared only in different episodes. Consider a young man whose first and only illness begins with mania and thought insertion on the same day and ends with both a month later. ICD-10 calls this schizoaffective disorder, manic type (F25.0); ICD-11, which has no manic or depressive types, codes it as schizoaffective disorder (6A21) provided the full requirements for schizophrenia (two symptoms, at least one of them core, for a month) were met alongside the mania. DSM-5-TR cannot, because he has never had two weeks of psychosis without a mood episode; his DSM diagnosis is bipolar I disorder with mood-incongruent psychotic features.

Exam trap

DSM-5 schizoaffective disorder = a major mood episode concurrent with Criterion A of schizophrenia, plus at least 2 weeks of delusions or hallucinations without a major mood episode, with mood episodes present for the majority of the illness. The two weeks are of psychosis without mood, not mood without psychosis. Wrong options: "mood symptoms only during acute psychosis" (that describes a mood disorder with psychotic features turned round), "lifetime absence of psychosis", "bipolar features only" (there is also a depressive type). Jacob Kasanin coined the term in 1933. ICD (F25, 6A21) uses the single episode, with onset of both kinds of symptom within a few days of each other.

Delusional disorder: one false belief in an otherwise ordinary life

Raghav is 56, a retired bank officer in Madurai, and for two years he has been certain that his wife is having an affair with the son-in-law of the family next door. His evidence is a collection of small things: the neighbour's scooter was parked facing their gate; his wife changed the way she tied her hair; there was a crease on her sari after she came back from the temple. He checks her phone each night, times her trips to the market and has twice followed her. When she weeps and denies it, he takes her tears as proof of guilt. Yet in every other way Raghav is unchanged. He keeps the household accounts, attends the meetings of his pensioners' association, talks sensibly about cricket and politics, and has no voices, no disorganisation of speech and no flattening of feeling. He drinks no alcohol, has never been depressed or manic, and his memory is good. He has come to the clinic only because his wife's brother insisted, and what he wants from the doctor is help in making her confess.

Raghav has delusional disorder, jealous type. Its DSM-5-TR criteria tell you what it is by telling you what it is not. Criterion A: one or more delusions lasting one month or longer. Criterion B: Criterion A for schizophrenia has never been met; hallucinations, if present, are not prominent and are related to the delusional theme, such as a sensation of insects crawling in someone convinced he is infested. Criterion C: apart from the impact of the delusion and its ramifications, functioning is not markedly impaired and behaviour is not obviously bizarre or odd. Criterion D: if manic or major depressive episodes have occurred, they have been brief relative to the delusional periods. Criterion E: the disturbance is not due to a substance or medical condition and is not better explained by another mental disorder, such as body dysmorphic or obsessive-compulsive disorder. Since DSM-5 the delusion no longer has to be non-bizarre; a specifier, with bizarre content, records a delusion that is clearly implausible, not understandable and not derived from ordinary life experience. As with schizophrenia, course specifiers may be added after a year and severity may be rated.

The disorder has a long history under another name. Kraepelin used paranoia (Section 4.1) for an insidiously developing, permanent and unshakeable delusional system, with clear and orderly thinking, willing and acting completely preserved, which he set apart both from dementia praecox and from paraphrenia, his term for patients with delusions and hallucinations whose personality remained relatively intact. Later the British psychiatrist Martin Roth, working in the 1950s, described late paraphrenia, a paranoid psychosis beginning in old age, often in women living alone and in people with impaired hearing. ICD-10 still listed paranoia, paranoid state and late paraphrenia among the inclusion terms of its delusional disorder.

The type is named by the theme of the delusion, and Table 4.20 lists the seven. Three of them have eponyms that examiners like. Erotomania is also called de Clérambault's syndrome, after the French psychiatrist Gaëtan Gatian de Clérambault (1872 to 1934), who described it in detail in the 1920s; his best-known patient was a woman convinced that King George V of Britain was in love with her, who waited for hours outside Buckingham Palace and read the movement of a curtain as a signal from him. Pathological jealousy was named the Othello syndrome by the British psychiatrists John Todd and Kenneth Dewhurst in 1955, after Shakespeare's Moor, whose jealousy is built from a handkerchief. And the somatic delusion of being infested by insects or parasites, delusional parasitosis, is also called Ekbom's syndrome after the Swedish neurologist Karl-Axel Ekbom, who described it in 1938; such patients often arrive at a skin clinic, not a psychiatric one, bringing "specimens" of the parasites in a matchbox.

Table 4.20 The types of delusional disorder in DSM-5-TR.
TypeCentral themeNotes
ErotomanicAnother person, usually of higher status (a film star, a politician, a superior at work), is in love with the patientDe Clérambault's syndrome; attempts at contact, stalking, legal trouble
GrandioseThe patient has a great but unrecognised talent or insight, or has made an important discoveryMay be religious, or a special relationship with a famous person
JealousThe spouse or lover is unfaithful, inferred from small bits of "evidence"Othello syndrome; probably commoner in men; confrontation and violence
PersecutoryBeing conspired against, cheated, spied on, followed, poisoned or drugged, maligned, harassed or obstructedThe commonest type; resentment, repeated litigation, violence
SomaticBodily functions or sensations: emitting a foul odour, infestation by insects, an internal parasite, a part of the body not workingDelusional parasitosis (Ekbom's syndrome)
MixedNo single theme predominates
UnspecifiedThe dominant belief cannot be determined or is not one of the aboveFor example referential delusions without a persecutory or grandiose element

What is it like to talk to someone with delusional disorder? The most striking thing, DSM-5-TR says, is how normal the person seems when the delusion is not being discussed or acted on. Raghav could sit beside you on a bus for an hour without you noticing anything. Some patients have what DSM-5-TR calls factual insight without true insight: Raghav knows perfectly well that his wife's family think he is imagining things, but he cannot accept that they are right. The delusions differ in quality from those of schizophrenia. In schizophrenia they tend to be more disorganised, less internally consistent and less systematised; in delusional disorder they show greater conviction, greater extension into the different areas of the person's life, and greater pressure, meaning preoccupation and concern. Irritable or dysphoric mood is common and is often an understandable reaction to the belief. Anger and violence can occur with the persecutory, jealous and erotomanic types, and persecuted patients may write hundreds of letters to the authorities or bring case after case to court (the litigious or querulous form). Legal difficulties are commonest in the jealous and erotomanic types.

Delusional disorder is uncommon. A Finnish study put its lifetime prevalence at about 0.2 percent, with the persecutory type the most frequent. There are no major sex differences overall, though the jealous type is probably commoner in men, and men tend to have more severe symptoms and worse functional outcomes. It may be more common in older people, and global functioning is generally better than in schizophrenia. The diagnosis is generally stable once established, but about a third of people with delusional disorder of one to three months' duration are later diagnosed with schizophrenia, whereas the diagnosis is much less likely to change once the disorder has lasted six to twelve months. It runs in families with schizophrenia and schizotypal personality disorder.

The ICD systems demand more time. ICD-10 grouped the persistent delusional disorders under F22: delusional disorder (F22.0) needed delusions present for at least three months, clearly personal rather than shared by the person's subculture, with no more than occasional transitory voices and no history of schizophrenic symptoms; other persistent delusional disorders (F22.8) included delusional dysmorphophobia and the litigious paranoia querulans. ICD-11 delusional disorder (6A24) similarly requires a delusion or set of related delusions typically persisting for at least three months and often much longer, in the absence of a depressive, manic or mixed episode and of the other characteristic symptoms of schizophrenia (persistent hallucinations, negative symptoms, disorganised thinking and experiences of influence, passivity or control), although perceptual disturbances related to the delusional theme are allowed. Apart from actions and attitudes directly related to the delusion, affect, speech and behaviour are typically unaffected.

The differential diagnosis follows from the criteria. If other psychotic symptoms appear, think of schizophrenia; if the delusions occur only during mood episodes, of a mood disorder with psychotic features; if they began in later life with memory loss, of a neurocognitive disorder; if with a drug such as cocaine or amphetamine, of substance-induced psychosis. Pathological jealousy in particular is common in alcohol use disorder, so a drinking history is essential. If the fixed belief is about contamination, symmetry or a perceived flaw in appearance, the diagnosis is obsessive-compulsive or body dysmorphic disorder with absent insight. And paranoid personality disorder, a lifelong suspicious and grudging style, falls short of delusion: the person mistrusts everyone but holds no single fixed false belief (Chapter 11). Treatment is difficult chiefly because the patient does not think he is ill. Antipsychotic medication helps some patients, and cognitive behavioural approaches to delusions (Book 2, Section 5.6) can loosen the hold of the belief, but often the realistic goal is to reduce distress, risk and conflict rather than to remove the delusion.

Clinical link

With a patient like Raghav, do not argue with the delusion and do not pretend to share it. Arguing ends the conversation; agreeing confirms the belief and betrays the spouse. Work instead with what he does accept: his sleeplessness, his anger, the strain at home. Always ask directly about risk. Delusional jealousy can lead to violence against the partner or the imagined rival, and erotomania to stalking; where the risk is serious, the partner's safety comes first, and temporary separation may be needed (Book 2, Section 2.7, on violence risk).

Exam trap

The belief that a celebrity or person of higher status is secretly in love with the patient is the erotomanic type (de Clérambault's syndrome), not grandiose, persecutory or somatic. Othello syndrome is jealous type; delusional parasitosis (Ekbom's syndrome) is somatic type; the commonest type is persecutory. Do not confuse these with the delusional misidentification syndromes taught in Chapter 2: Capgras (a familiar person replaced by an impostor), Fregoli (strangers are a familiar persecutor in disguise) and Cotard (nihilistic delusions of being dead or having no organs). DSM-5-TR delusional disorder needs only 1 month; ICD-10 and ICD-11 need about 3 months. Since DSM-5 the delusion may be bizarre.

Shared psychotic disorder: folie à deux

In 1877 two Paris physicians, Charles Lasègue (1816 to 1883) and Jules Falret (1824 to 1902), published a paper called "La folie à deux ou folie communiquée", the madness of two, or communicated madness. They described something that seemed to contradict everything known about psychosis: a delusion passing from one person to another, as if it were an infection. Their cases followed a pattern that has been confirmed many times since. One partner, the primary (also called the inducer, or the active partner), has a genuine psychotic illness with an established delusion. The other, the secondary (the induced or passive partner), comes to share it. The two have lived together for a long time in close intimacy and are cut off from other people, by language, culture, geography or their own withdrawal, so that the primary's voice is the only one the secondary hears. The primary is usually the more dominant and often the more intelligent; the secondary is more passive, dependent and suggestible. The shared belief is usually persecutory or grandiose, and it is usually plausible rather than fantastic, built on fears or hopes the two already shared. Most pairs are relatives: two sisters, husband and wife, a parent and a child.

The most useful observation Lasègue and Falret made was about treatment. When the pair is separated, the secondary partner's belief often fades within weeks, while the primary's does not. Consider a widow in her sixties with a long-standing persecutory delusion that a neighbour is pumping gas into her flat, and her unmarried daughter, who has looked after her for twenty years and has helped her seal the windows with newspaper. When the daughter is admitted to a medical ward with pneumonia and spends three weeks away from her mother, she begins to wonder aloud whether the smell might have come from the drain after all. The mother, meanwhile, is as certain as ever. Separation is therefore both a test and a treatment: it shows who is the primary, and it often frees the secondary. The primary partner needs treatment of her own psychotic disorder; the secondary may need brief support, sometimes a short course of an antipsychotic, and both need help when they are reunited.

Later authors divided the phenomenon into forms, which still appear in examinations:

  • Folie imposée, the classic form of Lasègue and Falret: the delusion of a psychotic person is imposed on a previously healthy partner, and the secondary's belief usually disappears on separation.
  • Folie simultanée: two predisposed people, living closely together, develop the same psychosis at about the same time, each triggering the other; there is no clear dominant partner, and separation may help neither.
  • Folie communiquée: the secondary resists for a long time, then adopts the delusion, develops it further, and keeps it even after separation.
  • Folie induite: a person who is already psychotic adds the delusions of another psychotic person to his own, as can happen between two patients in hospital.

When three people share the delusion the term is folie à trois, with four folie à quatre, when a whole family is involved folie à famille (also written folie en famille), and with several people folie à plusieurs.

The classifications have handled the condition very differently. ICD-10 coded it as induced delusional disorder (F24), with three requirements: two or more people share the same delusion or delusional system and support one another in the belief; they have an unusually close relationship; and there is temporal or other contextual evidence that the delusion was induced in the passive member or members by contact with the active partner. DSM-IV called it shared psychotic disorder. DSM-5 abolished the separate category. If the secondary partner meets the criteria for delusional disorder, that is the diagnosis; if not, the presentation is recorded as other specified schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorder, using the example DSM-5-TR names "delusional symptoms in the context of relationship with an individual with prominent delusions", in which the delusional material from the person with a psychotic disorder provides the content of the same delusions held by the other person, who may not otherwise meet criteria for a psychotic disorder. ICD-11 also has no separate code: "induced delusional disorder" is an index term of delusional disorder (6A24), within the block of schizophrenia or other primary psychotic disorders, and the ICD-11 diagnostic guidance for 6A24 notes that delusional disorder may rarely occur at the same time in two people with a strong emotional or situational link, "often referred to as shared or induced Delusional Disorder or folie à deux", with the delusion often remitting in the less dominant person when the two are separated.

Exam trap

Folie à deux is the transmission of a delusion from a primary person with an established psychotic disorder to a previously healthier, more passive person in a close and usually isolated relationship. It is not two people having independent psychotic episodes at the same time, not a brief stress psychosis in two relatives, not a belief shared by a whole cultural, religious or subcultural group (that is a shared belief, not a delusion), not group hallucinations in a cult, not mass psychogenic illness, and not schizophrenia passed on genetically. ICD-10 code: F24 induced delusional disorder (F21 is schizotypal disorder, F23 the acute and transient psychoses). In DSM-5-TR it falls under delusional disorder or other specified schizophrenia spectrum disorder; in ICD-11 under delusional disorder (6A24), among the "schizophrenia or other primary psychotic disorders", not as a separate category and not under schizoaffective or schizotypal disorder; that is why a key of "other primary psychotic disorders (induced delusional disorder)" is accepted. Some textbooks, Barlow and Durand among them, say that DSM-5 added a "shared" specifier to delusional disorder; neither DSM-5 nor DSM-5-TR contains such a specifier.

Psychosis with a known cause: substances, medications and medical illness

Every disorder so far has been a primary psychosis, one for which no external cause can be found. The last two members of the DSM-5-TR spectrum are the secondary psychoses, in which the cause is known. They matter for two reasons. They are common, and they are often reversible if the cause is found and treated.

Substance/medication-induced psychotic disorder requires delusions or hallucinations or both (Criterion A), with evidence from the history, examination or laboratory findings that they developed during or soon after intoxication or withdrawal, or after exposure to or withdrawal from a medication, and that the substance can produce such symptoms (Criterion B). Criterion C asks whether an independent psychotic disorder would explain the picture better, and lists the signs that it would: symptoms that began before the substance use; symptoms that persist for a substantial period, about a month, after acute withdrawal or severe intoxication has ended; or a history of recurrent episodes unrelated to substances. The disturbance must not occur only during a delirium (Criterion D) and must cause distress or impairment (Criterion E). The onset is specified as during intoxication, during withdrawal or after medication use. One subtle point: if a person hallucinates but knows that the experience comes from the drug, reality testing is intact, and the diagnosis is not a psychotic disorder but intoxication or withdrawal "with perceptual disturbances".

The substances that can produce psychosis during intoxication are alcohol, cannabis, the hallucinogens (including phencyclidine), inhalants, sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics, and stimulants including cocaine; those that can produce it during withdrawal are alcohol and the sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics. Each has its own flavour. Amphetamines and cocaine can bring persecutory delusions within hours, sometimes with the hallucination of insects crawling under the skin (formication), which leads to scratching and excoriations. Cannabis can produce persecutory delusions with marked anxiety, emotional lability and depersonalisation, usually remitting within a day. Alcohol-induced psychosis with hallucinations, the old alcoholic hallucinosis, usually follows prolonged heavy drinking in people with moderate to severe alcohol use disorder, and the hallucinations are generally voices heard in clear consciousness. The list of medications that can cause psychosis is long: corticosteroids, anticholinergic drugs, antiparkinsonian drugs, anticonvulsants, some antimicrobial and cardiovascular drugs, and many others. Between 7 and 25 percent of people presenting with a first episode of psychosis in different settings are reported to have a substance or medication-induced disorder.
