And the label is not always the end of the story: in a Danish registry study that followed people with substance-induced psychosis for 20 years, about a third were later diagnosed with a schizophrenia spectrum disorder (26 percent) or a bipolar disorder (8 percent), and the rate was highest, 44 percent, for cannabis-induced psychosis. The drugs themselves, their intoxication and withdrawal syndromes, and the evidence linking cannabis to schizophrenia (Section 4.7) are taught in Chapter 10.

Psychotic disorder due to another medical condition requires prominent hallucinations or delusions, evidence that they are the direct physiological consequence of another medical condition, and that they are not better explained by another mental disorder, do not occur only during a delirium, and cause distress or impairment. It is specified as with delusions or with hallucinations, depending on which predominates. The three questions of biological plausibility, temporality and typicality, and the list of causes, were given in Section 4.4.6. Two further points complete the picture. The diagnosis is not made if the person keeps reality testing for the hallucinations and understands that they come from the illness, and a psychotic reaction that is a psychological response to a frightening illness, rather than a physiological effect of it, is a brief psychotic disorder with marked stressor. In epilepsy, psychosis may be ictal, postictal or interictal; postictal psychosis is the commonest, seen in about 2 to 7.8 percent of people with epilepsy. Lifetime prevalence of the disorder has been estimated at 0.21 to 0.54 percent in Swedish and Finnish studies, rising in older people, and in late life Alzheimer's disease is commonly accompanied by psychosis and psychosis is a defining feature of Lewy body disease.

The ICD names are different but the logic is the same. ICD-10 coded psychotic disorders due to psychoactive substances within the substance block (F10 to F19, with the fourth character .5), and medical causes as organic hallucinosis (F06.0) or organic delusional disorder (F06.2). ICD-11 codes substance-induced psychotic disorders in the chapter on substance use, and psychosis due to a medical condition as secondary psychotic syndrome (6E61). Because the word "primary" in ICD-11's block title means "not secondary to a substance or medical condition", this is the distinction the WHO wants clinicians to make first.

Exam trap

A psychosis that continues for about a month or more after the drug has been stopped suggests an independent psychotic disorder, not a substance-induced one. Hallucinations that the person recognises as drug-induced are not a psychotic disorder (they are intoxication or withdrawal with perceptual disturbances). Psychosis occurring only during delirium is part of the delirium. And a medical patient whose psychosis is an emotional reaction to the illness has a brief psychotic disorder with marked stressor, not a psychotic disorder due to the medical condition.

Catatonia across the disorders

When Karl Kahlbaum described catatonia in 1874 (Section 4.1) he regarded it as a disease in its own right, often with a cyclical course that included melancholy and excitement. Kraepelin absorbed it into dementia praecox, and for most of the twentieth century catatonia was taught as a subtype of schizophrenia. The clinical evidence slowly undid this. Catatonia turned out to be common in mood disorders, in medical and neurological illness and in autism, and to respond dramatically to benzodiazepines, which do little for the psychotic symptoms of schizophrenia. DSM-5-TR now states that catatonia occurs in up to 35 percent of people with schizophrenia, but that the majority of catatonia cases involve people with depressive or bipolar disorders; in clinical samples about 9 percent of patients show catatonia.

DSM-5 (2013) drew the logical conclusion. It does not treat catatonia as an independent class of disorder but recognises it in three forms: catatonia associated with another mental disorder, which works as a catatonia specifier attached to a neurodevelopmental, psychotic, bipolar, depressive or other mental disorder (schizophrenia with catatonia, major depressive disorder with catatonia); catatonic disorder due to another medical condition; and unspecified catatonia, used when the underlying condition is unclear, full criteria are not met, or there is not enough information, as in an emergency department. For the first two, the same criterion applies: the clinical picture is dominated by three or more of twelve psychomotor features, listed in Table 4.21. How each sign looks at the bedside, and the related signs such as automatic obedience and Mitgehen, are described in Section 4.2.

Table 4.21 The twelve DSM-5-TR signs of catatonia, grouped (three or more are required).
GroupSignWhat it is
Reduced movementStuporNo psychomotor activity; not actively relating to the environment
Reduced movementCatalepsyA posture passively induced by the examiner is held against gravity
Reduced movementWaxy flexibilitySlight, even resistance as the examiner positions the limb
Reduced movementPosturingA posture spontaneously and actively held against gravity
Reduced engagementMutismNo, or very little, verbal response (not if there is a known aphasia)
Reduced engagementNegativismOpposition to, or no response to, instructions or external stimuli
Excessive or peculiar movementMannerismAn odd, circumstantial caricature of a normal action
Excessive or peculiar movementStereotypyRepetitive, abnormally frequent, non-goal-directed movements
Excessive or peculiar movementAgitationExcitement not influenced by external stimuli
Excessive or peculiar movementGrimacingOdd, fixed facial expressions
Echo phenomenaEcholaliaMimicking another person's speech
Echo phenomenaEchopraxiaMimicking another person's movements

Look at the first group and you will see a distinction examiners like. In catalepsy the examiner puts the arm in a position and the patient leaves it there; in posturing the patient adopts the posture himself; in waxy flexibility the examiner feels a slight, even resistance as he moves the limb, like bending a soft candle. The table also shows why catatonia is so easily missed. Its signs point in opposite directions, from complete stillness to purposeless excitement, and the same person can swing between the two; DSM-5-TR warns that teaching has overemphasised rare, dramatic signs such as waxy flexibility, so that the commoner quiet forms, mutism, negativism and staring, go unrecognised. The consequences of missing it can be grave: a person in catatonic stupor may stop eating and drinking and is at risk of malnutrition, exhaustion, blood clots, pressure ulcers, contractures and high fever.

Many medical conditions can cause catatonia: neurological illnesses such as tumours, head injury, stroke and encephalitis (anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis among them), and metabolic disturbances such as hypercalcaemia, hepatic encephalopathy, homocystinuria and diabetic ketoacidosis. Catatonia occurring only during a delirium is not diagnosed separately. In a patient taking an antipsychotic, two medication effects must be considered before the word catatonia is used: acute dystonia, which can produce abnormal postures, and above all neuroleptic malignant syndrome, which can look catatonic but comes with fever, rigidity, autonomic instability and abnormal laboratory findings (Section 4.10). The resemblance is not accidental. In 1934 the German psychiatrist Karl Stauder described malignant catatonia (he called it lethal catatonia), a catatonic state with high fever, autonomic instability and stupor that was often fatal before modern treatment; many authorities regard neuroleptic malignant syndrome as a drug-induced form of malignant catatonia. Because antipsychotics can worsen catatonia or precipitate neuroleptic malignant syndrome, they are generally held back while catatonia is being treated.

The treatment of catatonia is one of the most satisfying in psychiatry. The lorazepam challenge is both a diagnostic test and the first step of treatment: a benzodiazepine, usually lorazepam in a dose of 1 to 2 mg given intravenously or intramuscularly, is followed within minutes to hours by a clear improvement in a majority of patients, and a positive response supports the diagnosis. Regular benzodiazepine treatment is then continued. When benzodiazepines fail, and in malignant catatonia, electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) is highly effective and can be life-saving (ECT is described in Chapter 5). Severity is rated with instruments such as the Bush-Francis Catatonia Rating Scale (1996), described in the Assessment book. The underlying disorder, whether depression, mania, schizophrenia or a medical illness, must of course be treated as well.

The ICD systems have followed the same path. ICD-10 still had catatonic schizophrenia (F20.2) and an organic catatonic disorder (F06.1). ICD-11 created a separate grouping for catatonia, with catatonia associated with another mental disorder (6A40), catatonia induced by substances or medications, and a secondary catatonia syndrome for medical causes, so that catatonia is no longer tied to schizophrenia in either system.

Exam trap

In DSM-5, catatonia is a specifier across several disorders (psychotic, bipolar, depressive, neurodevelopmental and others), plus catatonic disorder due to another medical condition and unspecified catatonia. It is not an independent disorder, not a personality disorder, and not a subtype of bipolar disorder or of schizophrenia. Three of twelve signs are needed. Catatonia is more often associated with mood disorders than with schizophrenia. First-line treatment is a benzodiazepine (lorazepam), then ECT; antipsychotics are not the first step.

At the edges: attenuated psychosis, the high-risk state and schizotypal disorder

Most people who develop schizophrenia pass through a prodrome first (Section 4.6). If that prodrome could be recognised, could treatment begin before the first full episode, and could the episode even be prevented? That question drove one of the most debated proposals in DSM-5. Attenuated psychosis syndrome is described in Section III of DSM-5-TR, among the conditions for further study, which means it is a research proposal, not an official diagnosis. Its proposed criteria are: at least one of attenuated delusions, attenuated hallucinations or attenuated disorganised speech, severe or frequent enough to warrant clinical attention (A); present at least once a week for the past month (B); begun or worsened in the past year (C); distressing and disabling enough to warrant attention (D); not better explained by another disorder, including a mood disorder with psychotic features, or by a substance or medical condition (E); and the criteria for a psychotic disorder never having been met (F).

What makes a symptom "attenuated"? It is psychosis-like but below the threshold. The experiences are less severe and more transient, and the person keeps reasonable insight: doubt about the belief can be elicited, and scepticism about the perception can be induced, by a question such as "I see that this is how you experience the world; could there be a different explanation?" A young woman who has begun to feel that strangers on the bus may be watching her, but who can say that it is probably her imagination, has an attenuated persecutory idea; if she becomes certain, the threshold has been crossed. The research findings are sobering in both directions. In help-seeking groups meeting the criteria, the three-year cumulative risk of developing psychosis was up to 22 percent, against 1.54 percent in those who did not meet them, and over ten years the risk of transition was 34.9 percent; most who made the transition developed a schizophrenia spectrum disorder (73 percent), a minority an affective psychosis (11 percent). Overall, DSM-5-TR summarises, about a third develop psychosis, a third remit and a third remain disabled. In other words, the syndrome identifies people at real risk, but most of them will not become psychotic, which is one reason the proposal was kept in Section III: labelling and treating everyone with attenuated symptoms would expose many people to stigma and to medication they would never have needed. In clinical practice the presentation can be recorded in Section II as other specified schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorder.

The proposal grew out of the clinical high-risk state (also called the ultra-high-risk or at-risk mental state), identified largely at a clinic for young people in Melbourne, Australia, by Patrick McGorry, Alison Yung and their colleagues in the mid-1990s. Their criteria take three routes: attenuated psychotic symptoms; brief limited intermittent psychotic symptoms, that is frank psychotic symptoms that resolve on their own within about a week; and a genetic risk, such as a first-degree relative with psychosis, combined with a recent decline in functioning. Specialised interviews for the high-risk state are described in the Assessment book, and the early intervention services built on this work are outlined in Section 4.10.

On the other side of attenuated psychosis lies a stable trait rather than a changing state. Schizotypal personality disorder, a lifelong pattern of social and interpersonal deficits with odd beliefs, unusual perceptions and eccentric behaviour below the psychotic threshold, is listed in the DSM-5-TR psychotic disorders chapter because it belongs to the schizophrenia spectrum, but its criteria are given in the chapter on personality disorders (Chapter 11). The WHO goes further. ICD-10 placed schizotypal disorder (F21) beside schizophrenia, not among the personality disorders, requiring typical features continuously or episodically for at least two years, and ICD-11 keeps it in the schizophrenia block as 6A22. The difference between it and attenuated psychosis syndrome is the difference between a climate and the weather: schizotypy is how the person has always been, attenuated psychosis is a recent change.

Finally, DSM-5-TR provides two residual categories. Other specified schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorder is used when the clinician wants to record why the criteria are not met, and the manual gives four examples: persistent auditory hallucinations with no other features; persistent delusions with overlapping mood episodes that are too long for delusional disorder; attenuated psychosis syndrome; and delusional symptoms in the partner of a person with prominent delusions, the old folie à deux. Unspecified schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorder is used when the reason is not specified or the information is insufficient, as in the emergency room. Three related presentations are taught elsewhere in this book: postpartum psychosis in Chapter 5, possession and trance states and the culture-bound syndromes in Chapter 7, and delirium and the psychoses of dementia in Chapter 10. Table 4.22 brings the whole spectrum together.

Table 4.22 The schizophrenia spectrum and other psychotic disorders at a glance.
DisorderCore pictureDSM-5-TR durationICD counterpart

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. In ICD-10 and ICD-11, the characteristic symptoms of schizophrenia must be present for most of the time during a period of at least .
  3. DSM-5 dropped the paranoid, disorganised, catatonic, undifferentiated and residual because of their poor reliability, poor validity and instability.
  4. The ICD-10 subtype in which negative symptoms develop over at least a year without any overt psychotic symptoms is schizophrenia.
  5. Psychotic symptoms lasting at least one day but less than one month, with full return to the premorbid level, are diagnosed in DSM-5-TR as disorder.

Answers: (1) 6, (2) 1 month, (3) subtypes, (4) simple, (5) brief psychotic, (6) schizophreniform, (7) 2 weeks, (8) erotomanic, (9) induced, (10) three; lorazepam.

4.6 Who Develops Schizophrenia and What Happens NextLower yield · 1 Q

When a young person is first admitted with psychosis, the family usually asks two questions within the first hour. The first is "Why him?" The second, asked more quietly, is "Will he get better?" The first question is about causes, and Sections 4.7 to 4.9 deal with it. This section answers the questions that come before and after it. Who develops schizophrenia: how many people, at what age, of which sex, in what places and circumstances? And what happens after the illness begins: how does it unfold over months and decades, how many people recover, what predicts a good or a poor course, and why did the World Health Organization find, to everyone's surprise, that patients in Agra and Chandigarh did better than patients in Europe and the United States? These are the facts of epidemiology, the study of how a disorder is distributed in populations, and of prognosis, the expected course and outcome of an illness. They matter in the examination hall, and they matter even more at the bedside, because a family's hope depends on them.

How common is schizophrenia?

Before counting cases we need three different ways of counting, because examiners like to swap them. Prevalence is the proportion of a population that has the disorder: at one moment (point prevalence), during a stated period such as the past year (12-month prevalence), or at any time in their lives so far (lifetime prevalence). Incidence is the rate at which new cases arise, usually expressed as new cases per 100,000 people per year. The lifetime morbid risk is the probability that a person will develop the disorder if they live through the whole age range in which it usually begins; it is "age-corrected", which means that people who are still young, and so may yet fall ill, are counted in a way that allows for the years of risk they have not lived through. Prevalence tells a health planner how many beds and clinics are needed today; incidence tells a researcher whether a cause is operating more strongly in one place or one period than another; morbid risk is the figure a worried relative is really asking about.

The number almost every textbook repeats is that schizophrenia affects about 1 percent of people at some point in their lives. It is a useful round figure and the one most multiple-choice papers expect. The more careful figure in DSM-5-TR (2022) is a lifetime prevalence of approximately 0.3 to 0.7 percent, with a fivefold variation between the nationally representative surveys that were pooled. Barlow and Durand give a range of 0.2 to 1.5 percent and conclude that the disorder will affect about 1 percent of the population. The most careful pooling of the world literature came from the Australian epidemiologist John McGrath and colleagues, who summarised their systematic reviews in 2008: the median incidence was 15.2 new cases per 100,000 people per year (the middle 80 percent of studies lay between 7.7 and 43.0), and the median lifetime morbid risk was 7.2 per 1,000, a little under 1 percent. Notice the size of these numbers. In a district of one million people, about 150 people develop schizophrenia every year, and at any given time several thousand are living with it.

Why do the estimates differ so much? Partly because studies define the disorder differently (a narrow definition that requires six months of illness yields fewer cases than one that accepts a single month), partly because some surveys count only people in treatment, and partly because the risk genuinely varies with place and population, as you will see in Section 4.6.3. DSM-5-TR adds a fourth reason: some groups are more likely to be misdiagnosed or overdiagnosed, so a high rate in a minority community may partly reflect the diagnostician rather than the illness.

For India, the best figures come from the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016, carried out by the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS) in 12 states. It found a lifetime prevalence of 1.41 percent and a current prevalence of 0.42 percent for "schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders", a broader group than schizophrenia alone. The same survey found that roughly three in every four people with a current psychotic disorder were receiving no treatment, a figure known as the treatment gap. In a country of more than a billion people, that gap means millions of untreated people, most of them living with their families.

Exam trap

The round answer for lifetime risk is about 1 percent; DSM-5-TR's own figure is 0.3 to 0.7 percent. If both appear as options, read the stem: "according to DSM-5-TR" points to 0.3 to 0.7. Do not confuse prevalence (existing cases) with incidence (new cases per year). And do not repeat the old claim that the incidence of schizophrenia is "identical in every culture": the WHO ten-country study found that, with its broad definition of the illness, incidence varied almost threefold between centres (from about 1.5 to 4.2 new cases per 10,000 a year), a statistically significant difference, and later reviews (McGrath and colleagues) found more variation still.

Who falls ill: age and sex

Schizophrenia is above all a disorder of young adults. DSM-5-TR states that the psychotic features typically emerge between the late teens and the mid-30s and that onset before adolescence is rare. The peak age of onset is the early to mid-20s for men and the late 20s for women. Think about what that timing means. The illness usually strikes just as a person is finishing college, starting a first job, moving away from home or getting married, so it interrupts the very years in which an adult life is built. That is one reason why schizophrenia, though less common than depression, causes such a large share of the world's disability.

The age rule has edges worth knowing. Childhood-onset schizophrenia, beginning before about 13 years, is very rare; DSM-5-TR notes that in children delusions and hallucinations are less elaborate, visual hallucinations are commoner, and the disorganised speech and behaviour must not be confused with the far commoner autism spectrum disorder or attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (Chapter 3). Childhood-onset cases tend to resemble poor-outcome adult cases, with gradual onset and prominent negative symptoms. At the other end, late-onset schizophrenia, beginning after 40, is overrepresented by women, and is often marked by persecutory delusions and hallucinations with relatively preserved affect and social functioning. An international consensus in 2000 suggested calling onset after 60 "very-late-onset schizophrenia-like psychosis", because at that age a new psychosis is so often linked to brain disease, sensory loss or dementia (Chapter 10) that it may not be the same condition.

The sex difference is one of the most consistent findings in psychiatry, and it is more subtle than "men get it more". McGrath's reviews found that the incidence in men is about 1.4 times that in women. Yet over a whole lifetime the prevalence is roughly equal, and a large worldwide study cited in DSM-5-TR found no sex difference in prevalence at all. How can both be true? Because women fall ill later. Barlow and Durand, summarising Assen Jablensky's work, note that new cases are commoner in men until about age 36, after which the balance switches, and women contribute more of the cases that begin in midlife. DSM-5-TR adds that the sex ratio depends on the definition: definitions that emphasise negative symptoms and long duration give higher rates in men, while definitions that admit more mood symptoms and brief illnesses give equal rates.

Why should women fall ill later? The leading explanation is the oestrogen hypothesis, associated especially with the German psychiatrist Heinz Häfner and his ABC (Age, Beginning and Course) study in Mannheim. Oestrogen appears to dampen dopamine sensitivity and may protect a vulnerable brain. DSM-5-TR lists the clinical observations that fit: psychotic symptoms worsen in the premenstrual days when oestrogen falls, admissions rise just before and during menstruation, symptoms tend to improve in pregnancy when oestrogen is high and to worsen after delivery when it drops sharply, and the falling oestrogen of the menopause may explain the second peak of onset in women in midlife, around 45 years. The sexes also differ in the shape of the illness. Men, especially those with a long period of untreated psychosis and poor adjustment before the illness, have more negative symptoms, more cognitive impairment and generally worse functional outcomes. Women show more mood-laden symptoms, less disorganisation, fewer negative symptoms and better preserved social functioning, though DSM-5-TR warns that there are frequent exceptions.

Exam trap

"Schizophrenia is most commonly found in" has the answer young adults, not adolescents (onset before adolescence is rare and the peak is in the 20s), not the middle-aged (only the smaller second peak in women) and not the elderly. Men: early to mid-20s. Women: late 20s, with a second peak around the menopause. Incidence is somewhat higher in men (about 1.4 to 1); lifetime prevalence is roughly equal. Men have the worse outcome.

Where and when: place, migration, season and social class

If schizophrenia were purely a matter of genes, its rate would be the same in a Danish city and a Danish village. It is not. The most convincing demonstration came from Denmark, which keeps national registers of births, addresses and psychiatric admissions. In 1999 Preben Bo Mortensen and colleagues followed 1.75 million Danes and identified 2,669 cases of schizophrenia. A parent or sibling with schizophrenia raised a person's risk about seven to nine times, which was expected. More surprising, being born in the capital, Copenhagen, rather than in a rural area raised the risk 2.4 times, and the season of birth mattered too: risk was highest for people born in February and March and lowest for those born in August and September. The authors then calculated the population attributable risk, the share of all cases in the population that would disappear if a risk factor were removed. For family history it was 5.5 percent; for urban birth it was 34.6 percent; for season of birth 10.5 percent. A strong but rare risk factor (an affected parent) accounts for few cases in a population; a weak but common one (a city birth) accounts for many. This link between city life and schizophrenia is called urbanicity, and it is stronger the longer a child lives in a city, which suggests that something about urban upbringing, not merely urban birth, is involved.

The same logic applies to migration. In their 2005 meta-analysis Elizabeth Cantor-Graae and Jean-Paul Selten found that first-generation migrants had about 2.7 times the risk of the native population, and the children of migrants, the second generation, about 4.5 times. The risk was highest for migrants from countries where most people are Black, living in mainly white European countries; in the United Kingdom, people of African-Caribbean and Black African origin have much higher rates of first-episode schizophrenia than the white population. Because the second generation has the higher risk, the effect cannot simply be that ill people are more likely to emigrate (selective migration). DSM-5-TR adds the ethnic density effect: the rates are highest when members of a minority live in areas with few others of their own background. The proposed reasons are discrimination, social isolation, less support and fewer ordinary explanations available for odd experiences. Selten's idea that the chronic experience of being an outsider, which he calls social defeat, sensitises the brain's dopamine system is taken up in Section 4.9.

The season-of-birth effect is small but remarkably consistent. In 1997 E. Fuller Torrey and colleagues reviewed more than 250 studies from 29 Northern and five Southern Hemisphere countries and found a 5 to 8 percent excess of winter and spring births among people who later develop schizophrenia (the same excess appears in bipolar disorder). Statistical artefacts and parents' timing of conception were judged unlikely explanations; seasonal infections, nutrition, light and temperature during pregnancy are the main suspects, and Section 4.7 returns to them. DSM-5-TR adds that the rare deficit form of the illness (Section 4.3) shows a summer excess instead.

Finally there is social class, the oldest finding of all. In 1939 the Chicago sociologists Robert Faris and Warren Dunham showed that hospital admissions for schizophrenia were concentrated in the poorest inner-city districts; their study and the general debate it opened are taught in Book 2, Section 1.7. Two explanations compete. The social causation hypothesis says that the stresses of poverty (crowding, violence, poor nutrition, insecurity, discrimination) help to cause the illness. The social drift hypothesis, also called social selection, says that the illness, often during its slow prodrome, pushes people down the social ladder: they fail examinations, lose jobs, cannot pay rent and drift into cheap housing in poor areas. A neat test was devised in England by E. M. Goldberg and S. L. Morrison in 1963. If poverty caused schizophrenia, the patients should have been born into poor families. They looked up the occupations of the patients' fathers at the time of the patients' birth and found that the fathers' social class was distributed much like that of the general population, whereas the patients themselves had slid into the lowest occupational classes. For these patients, at least, the low social class was more a consequence of the illness than its cause. Later work has been more mixed: there is evidence for drift, but also for real effects of social adversity, childhood trauma and neighbourhood deprivation, and DSM-5-TR lists social deprivation, social adversity and adverse childhood experiences among the environmental risk factors. Table 4.23 gathers these markers.

Table 4.23 Environmental risk markers for schizophrenia and the size of effect the classic studies report.
Risk markerWhat is foundSize of effect
Urban birth and upbringingHigher risk in cities (Mortensen and colleagues, Denmark, 1999)About 2.4 times, capital versus rural birth
Migration and minority statusHigher risk in migrants and their children (Cantor-Graae and Selten, 2005)About 2.7 times (first generation) and 4.5 times (second)
Season of birthExcess of late winter and spring births (Torrey and colleagues, 1997)Small: a 5 to 8 percent excess
Low social classPatients concentrated in the poorest groups; fathers not (Goldberg and Morrison, 1963)Largely explained by social drift
Childhood adversityTrauma and neglect linked to more severe symptoms (DSM-5-TR)Modest; causal role debated
Cannabis, obstetric and prenatal factors, older fatherTaught in Section 4.7See Section 4.7
Exam trap

Social causation: poverty and stress cause schizophrenia. Social drift (social selection): schizophrenia causes the fall in social class. Goldberg and Morrison's fathers' occupations (1963) supported drift. Faris and Dunham (1939) found the original inner-city concentration and themselves favoured social causation, but their map of addresses could not settle the question. A higher rate in second-generation migrants argues against selective migration. Every risk marker in Table 4.23 is far weaker than having an affected identical twin, and most people exposed to any of them never develop schizophrenia.

The phases of the illness

Schizophrenia rarely arrives out of a clear sky. It usually unfolds through recognisable phases, and the easiest way to learn them is to follow one person through them.

Mohan grew up in a small town near Coimbatore, the elder son of a bus conductor. His mother remembers him as a quiet, slightly clumsy boy who walked late, preferred to play alone and was teased at school for being "lost in his own world", though his marks were average and nobody thought he was ill. At 18 he began a commerce degree. In his second year things changed slowly. He stopped playing cricket with his friends, then stopped meeting them at all. He spent whole days in his room, bathed only when his mother insisted, and his marks fell from the sixties to the thirties. He became irritable when questioned and spent hours with a notebook of numbers, explaining that the dates of his examinations "had a pattern meant for him". His parents thought he was lazy, then that he had been affected by the evil eye; they took him to a temple and later to a healer, who tied an amulet on his arm. About fourteen months after the first change, Mohan began to hear two men discussing him from the next house, calling him a coward and commenting on everything he did, and he became convinced that the neighbours were reading his thoughts through their mobile tower. He stopped eating food his mother had not tasted first. When he threw a stone at the neighbour's window, his family brought him to the psychiatric unit of a hospital in Coimbatore.

Mohan's story contains all four phases (Figure 4.10). The premorbid phase is the period before any recognisable illness. In many people who later develop schizophrenia it is not entirely normal: the late walking, clumsiness, solitary play and social awkwardness in Mohan's childhood are exactly the subtle motor, cognitive and social signs that cohort studies have found (Section 4.7.6), though they are far too common and too non-specific to predict the illness in any single child.

The prodromal phase (from the Greek prodromos, running before) is the period of deterioration before the first frank psychotic symptoms. Barlow and Durand, citing Jablensky, state that up to 85 percent of people who develop schizophrenia go through a prodrome, typically lasting one to two years. In Häfner's ABC study of 232 first-episode patients, 73 percent of illnesses began with a prodromal phase, which lasted on average about five years when dated from the first non-specific sign. The two figures differ because the start of a prodrome can only be reconstructed afterwards, from what the patient and family remember, and different researchers draw the line at different points. The signs are mostly negative and non-specific: social withdrawal, a fall in work or study, neglect of hygiene, loss of interest, initiative and energy, blunted or odd emotional reactions, vague or circumstantial speech, unusual beliefs that stop short of delusion (ideas of reference, magical thinking), unusual perceptual experiences (sensing a presence in an empty room), irritability, depression and anxiety. DSM-III-R (1987) listed nine such prodromal and residual signs, and DSM-5-TR still describes the same picture. Notice how many of them resemble the features of schizotypal personality (Chapter 11), and how easily they are mistaken for adolescent moodiness, laziness or depression.

The active phase is the period of florid psychosis: the delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech and behaviour, and catatonic features taught in Section 4.2, which bring the person to attention, often in crisis. DSM-5-TR requires at least one month of these Criterion A symptoms (less if successfully treated), and the six months that schizophrenia requires in DSM-5-TR may include the prodromal and residual periods (Section 4.4). With treatment the active symptoms usually lessen over weeks. The residual phase follows: the florid symptoms have faded, but negative symptoms, cognitive difficulties and attenuated positive symptoms (odd beliefs, mild suspiciousness, occasional voices) may linger. In some people the residual phase is brief and functioning returns almost to its previous level; in others it lasts for decades. Most people then experience relapse, a return of active symptoms after a period of improvement. The commonest triggers are stopping medication, stressful life events, heavy cannabis or alcohol use and a critical, hostile family atmosphere (Section 4.9).

Between the first psychotic symptom and the start of proper treatment there is usually a gap. The duration of untreated psychosis (DUP) is defined as the time from the manifestation of the first psychotic symptom to the initiation of adequate treatment. For Mohan it was several months, spent at the temple and the healer's; Barlow and Durand note that in Western samples it typically takes one to two years from the first symptoms to diagnosis and treatment, partly because people hide symptoms as their suspiciousness grows. Does the delay matter? In 2005 Max Marshall and colleagues reviewed 26 cohorts of first-episode patients, 4,490 people in all, and found a modest but convincing association: a longer DUP went with worse total, positive and negative symptoms, worse overall and social functioning, and a lower chance of remission. Crucially, the association was not explained by poor premorbid adjustment, the obvious alternative explanation (people whose illness creeps in slowly might both be recognised late and do badly anyway). Whether untreated psychosis actually damages the brain is still argued, but the practical lesson is accepted everywhere, and it is the basis of the early intervention services described in Section 4.10.

Clinical link

In India the pathway to care very often passes through temples, dargahs, churches and traditional healers before it reaches a psychiatrist or psychologist, and that pathway lengthens the DUP. A clinical psychologist who meets a family like Mohan's should not ridicule the healer they consulted first; the aim is to add effective treatment to what the family believes, not to win an argument about causes. The young person with only prodromal signs, who is help-seeking but not psychotic, belongs to the clinical high-risk state and attenuated psychosis syndrome described in Section 4.5.

Exam trap

The phases in order are premorbid, prodromal, active, residual. The prodrome consists mainly of negative and non-specific signs (withdrawal, declining function, odd ideas), not full delusions. DSM-5-TR's six months of illness include the prodromal and residual phases; only one month need be active phase. DUP runs from the first psychotic symptom, not from the first prodromal sign, to adequate treatment.

What happens next: course and long-term outcome

Emil Kraepelin built his concept of dementia praecox on the belief that the illness ended in mental deterioration (Section 4.1). For half a century, textbooks repeated his pessimism, and families were told to expect a lifetime in hospital. Was he right? The answer came from researchers who did the slow, unglamorous work of following patients for decades.

The first great long-term study was carried out by Manfred Bleuler (1903 to 1994), the son of Eugen Bleuler, who directed the same Burghölzli hospital in Zurich where his father had coined the word schizophrenia. He followed 208 patients (100 men and 108 women) admitted in 1942 and 1943 until 1963 to 1965 or until their death, more than 20 years for most of them, and knew many of them and their families personally. His book, published in German in 1972 and in English in 1978 as The Schizophrenic Disorders: Long-term Patient and Family Studies, overturned Kraepelin's picture. Courses were enormously varied: some patients recovered fully and stayed well, many had an undulating course of episodes and remissions, and a minority went on to a severe chronic state. Bleuler also observed that the illness did not, on average, keep getting worse: after the first years it tended to stabilise or even improve. When Swiss researchers re-examined his records in 2003 using modern criteria, about 30 percent of his patients no longer qualified for schizophrenia (most were rediagnosed with schizoaffective disorder), but even among the strictly diagnosed patients, half had an undulating course with remissions and 12 to 15 percent recovered.

In Lausanne, Luc Ciompi and Christian Müller traced the lives of patients first admitted decades earlier (published in German in 1976 and summarised in English in 1980). Of 1,642 original patients, 289 survived to be examined after an average of about 37 years. The long-term course was favourable in at least half of them, and outcome depended mainly on the person's premorbid personality, on certain features of the illness, and on the effects of advanced age. Old age, for many, brought a quieting of the illness.

A third approach pooled everything. In 1994 James Hegarty and colleagues meta-analysed a century of outcome studies (1895 to 1992): 320 studies with 51,800 subjects. After an average follow-up of 5.6 years, 40.2 percent of patients were considered improved. Outcome was better under broad diagnostic criteria (46.5 percent improved) than narrow ones (27.3 percent), which is exactly what you would expect if broad criteria sweep in good-prognosis brief psychoses. The improvement rate rose after the mid-1950s, the era of antipsychotic drugs (48.5 percent for 1956 to 1985 versus 35.4 percent for 1895 to 1955), and fell again to 36.4 percent in the last decade studied, perhaps because narrower diagnostic concepts returned. Barlow and Durand cite a classic analysis by Joseph Zubin and colleagues (1992) of four course types: about 22 percent of patients had a single episode and recovered without lasting impairment, while the remaining 78 percent had several episodes, with varying degrees of impairment between them. DSM-5-TR reports two more recent meta-analyses. Across 79 studies of first-episode schizophrenia, the pooled remission rate was 56 percent and the pooled recovery rate 30 percent; across 50 studies of broadly defined schizophrenia, the median proportion meeting strict recovery criteria was only 13.5 percent.

These numbers look contradictory until you notice that they measure different things. Remission means that symptoms are absent or mild for a sustained period; an international working group led by Nancy Andreasen proposed in 2005 that eight core symptoms rated mild or less for at least six months should define it. Recovery is a higher bar: symptomatic remission plus a return of social and occupational functioning, sustained for at least two years. Many people reach remission; fewer reach recovery in this full sense. Clinicians and exam guides compress all this into a rough rule of thirds: about a third of people recover or nearly recover, about a third improve but have recurrent episodes or residual symptoms, and about a third remain persistently ill and disabled. It is a teaching simplification, not a finding, but it captures the essential truth that Kraepelin missed: schizophrenia is heterogeneous, and deterioration is not inevitable. DSM-5-TR puts the modern view plainly: course and outcome are heterogeneous, prognosis is uncertain at the onset of psychosis, and many people experience periods of remission and even recovery. It adds two useful details: cognitive impairment tends to appear before the first episode and then remain fairly stable, and psychotic experiences tend to lessen in late life. Table 4.24 lists the long-term studies.

Table 4.24 Classic long-term outcome studies of schizophrenia.
StudySample and follow-upMain finding
Manfred Bleuler, Burghölzli, Zurich (1972)208 patients admitted 1942 to 1943, followed for over 20 yearsVery varied courses; illness tends to stabilise rather than keep worsening
Ciompi and Müller, Lausanne (1976; English summary 1980)289 survivors of 1,642 patients, about 37 yearsCourse favourable in at least half
Hegarty and colleagues, meta-analysis (1994)320 studies, 51,800 subjects, 1895 to 199240.2 percent improved; worse with narrow criteria
International Study of Schizophrenia, WHO (Harrison and colleagues, 2001)18 cohorts, 15 and 25 yearsAbout half of survivors had favourable outcomes
Madras Longitudinal Study (Thara, 1994 and 2004)90 first-episode patients, 10 and 20 yearsBetter functioning than Western cohorts; few full recoveries
Exam trap

Kraepelin's prediction of inevitable deterioration was wrong; the long-term studies of Manfred Bleuler (the son, not Eugen) and Luc Ciompi showed that at least half of patients have a favourable long-term course. "A third recover, a third improve, a third stay ill" is a rough teaching rule. Remission (symptoms mild or absent) is not the same as recovery (symptoms and functioning, sustained). Outcome looks better when the diagnosis is broad, because broad criteria include brief, good-prognosis psychoses.

The WHO studies and the Indian evidence

In the 1960s nobody knew whether schizophrenia was the same illness everywhere, or even whether psychiatrists in different countries meant the same thing by the word (the US-UK Diagnostic Project described in Section 4.1 was showing at about the same time that they often did not). The World Health Organization (WHO) set out to find out. The International Pilot Study of Schizophrenia (IPSS) began in 1966 and published its first report in 1973. It examined 1,202 patients in nine centres: Aarhus (Denmark), Agra (India), Cali (Colombia), Ibadan (Nigeria), London (United Kingdom), Moscow (Soviet Union), Prague (Czechoslovakia), Taipei (Taiwan) and Washington (United States). Every patient was interviewed with the same standardised instrument, the Present State Examination, and classified by the same computer program. The first finding was methodological and reassuring: a similar core syndrome of schizophrenia could be identified in every centre, across very different cultures and languages. The second finding was the surprise. At the two-year follow-up, published in 1979, patients in the centres in developing countries had a considerably better course and outcome than patients in the centres in developed countries. At the five-year follow-up (Julian Leff and colleagues, 1992), covering 807 patients in eight centres, clinical and social outcomes were still significantly better in Agra and Ibadan; in Cali only social outcome was better.

Critics pointed out that the IPSS patients were not a representative sample: each centre picked cases from its own hospital, and a hospital in Agra might admit a very different kind of patient from one in Washington. So the WHO ran a second, better-designed study, the Determinants of Outcome of Severe Mental Disorders (DOSMeD), often called the WHO ten-country study, published by Assen Jablensky, Norman Sartorius and colleagues in 1992. It enrolled 1,379 patients at the point of their first contact with any helping agency, including traditional healers, in twelve centres in ten countries, among them Agra and Chandigarh in India, Ibadan and Cali, alongside centres in Europe, the United States and Japan. Starting from first contact avoided the selection bias of the IPSS. Of the 1,078 patients (78 percent) followed for about two years, complete clinical remission was more than twice as common in the developing-country centres (37 percent) as in the developed ones (15.5 percent), although the proportions with a continuous, unremitting illness did not differ significantly. The strongest predictors of outcome were the type of onset (acute onset did better than gradual) and the setting (developing country better than developed). The WHO then traced the surviving cohorts for 15 and 25 years in the International Study of Schizophrenia (Glynn Harrison and colleagues, 2001), whose authors included the Indian investigators K. C. Dube, Savita Malhotra, V. K. Varma and R. Thara: about half of the surviving patients had favourable outcomes, there was marked variation between centres, the pattern of the first two years was the best predictor of the fifteen-year outcome, and even 16 percent of patients whose early illness had never remitted achieved recovery later. Table 4.25 summarises the three studies.

Table 4.25 The World Health Organization studies of schizophrenia across cultures.
StudyDesignIndian centresKey finding
IPSS (began 1966; report 1973)1,202 hospital patients, nine countriesAgraSame core syndrome everywhere; better 2- and 5-year outcome in developing centres
DOSMeD, ten-country study (1992)1,379 first-contact patients, twelve centres in ten countriesAgra, ChandigarhFull remission 37 percent (developing) versus 15.5 percent (developed); acute onset and setting predicted outcome
International Study of Schizophrenia (2001)18 cohorts traced for 15 and 25 yearsAgra, Chandigarh, MadrasAbout half of survivors had favourable outcomes; early course predicted late course

Why should a poorer country produce better outcomes? Several explanations were proposed, and you should be able to discuss them in an interview. Extended families shared the burden of care and kept the patient within a web of relationships. Relatives in Chandigarh were found to be far less often critical, hostile or over-involved than relatives in London, a finding on expressed emotion taught in Section 4.9. Agricultural and family work allowed a person to return to a useful role at their own pace, without the competitive job interview. Illness was more often explained by external causes (spirits, fate, the evil eye) that did not blame the person. And a larger share of the patients in developing countries had an acute onset, which predicts a good outcome anywhere.

The finding became an axiom of international psychiatry, and axioms invite challenge. In 2008 Alex Cohen, Vikram Patel, R. Thara and Oye Gureje published "Questioning an axiom: better prognosis for schizophrenia in the developing world?" They tabulated 23 longitudinal studies from 11 low- and middle-income countries and found that outcomes varied widely, with many poor outcomes, so that the WHO conclusions needed re-examination. They also argued that outcome studies must take account of the high mortality and suicide in poorer settings, since patients who die are not counted among the poor outcomes. Other critics noted that "developing country" lumps together places as different as Agra, Ibadan and Cali, that brief acute psychoses (Section 4.5), which recover by their nature, may have inflated the developing-country samples, and that follow-up was harder in some centres. The fairest summary is that the WHO studies showed that the course of schizophrenia is shaped by its social setting, but that being poor is not in itself protective.

India has its own long-term evidence. The Schizophrenia Research Foundation (SCARF) in Chennai, founded in 1984 by the psychiatrist M. Sarada Menon with R. Thara and colleagues, ran the Madras Longitudinal Study, which followed 90 first-episode patients prospectively. At ten years (Thara and colleagues, 1994), 76 could be fully assessed; the pattern of illness was rated good in 67 percent, the commonest pattern was one of recurrent episodes, and positive and negative symptoms had declined steeply. At twenty years (Thara, 2004), complete data were available for 61 patients, while 16 had died and 13 could not be traced. Only 5 had recovered completely and 5 had been continuously ill; most had had several relapses, with or without full remission between them. Yet symptoms and social functioning were much better than in many Western cohorts, and rates of marriage and employment were higher than usually reported. Read the two halves of that result together: the Indian course was better than the Western one, but "better" did not mean benign.

Indian context

Two Indian cities are written into the history of schizophrenia research. Agra, through its mental hospital, was the Indian centre of the IPSS (1973) and also took part in DOSMeD; Chandigarh, through the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, was a DOSMeD centre and the site of the WHO expressed-emotion study of Indian relatives. Chennai's SCARF produced the Madras Longitudinal Study, one of the few long-term follow-ups of first-episode schizophrenia from a low- or middle-income country.

Exam trap

IPSS: WHO, report 1973, nine countries, 1,202 patients, Indian centre Agra. DOSMeD: WHO ten-country study, 1992, first-contact cases, twelve centres including Agra and Chandigarh. Both found a better outcome in developing countries, not a better outcome in developed ones and not a lower incidence. The critique "Questioning an axiom" (2008) is by Cohen, Patel, Thara and Gureje.

Predicting the outcome

If outcomes vary so widely, can a clinician tell at the start which way a particular patient is likely to go? Not with certainty, but decades of follow-up studies have produced a reliable list of prognostic factors, features present early in the illness that predict a better or worse course (Table 4.26). The list is among the most frequently examined in this chapter, and it is easiest to remember if you understand why each factor works.

Table 4.26 Prognostic factors in schizophrenia.
FactorBetter prognosisWorse prognosis
OnsetAcute (days to weeks)Insidious (months to years)
Age at onsetLater (late 20s or older)Early (adolescence)
Precipitating stressorClear precipitant presentNo precipitant
Premorbid adjustmentGood social, sexual and work historyPoor; withdrawn, schizoid or schizotypal traits
Symptom pictureMood symptoms (especially depression), positive symptoms, confusion or perplexityProminent negative symptoms, disorganisation, cognitive impairment
Sex and marital statusFemale; marriedMale; single, separated, divorced or widowed
Family historyOf mood disorderOf schizophrenia
Duration of untreated psychosisShortLong
Early courseRemissions in the first two yearsNo remission; many relapses
Brain and neurological signsNormalNeurological soft signs, enlarged ventricles
Insight and adherenceGoodPoor; continued substance use
SupportGood support; low expressed emotion at homePoor support; high expressed emotion

Most of the factors fall into three families. The first concerns how much of a person was built before the illness struck: a late onset, good premorbid functioning, marriage and a work history all mean that the person has skills, relationships and roles to return to. The second concerns how much of the illness is a neurodevelopmental deficit and how much an episode: insidious onset, early onset, negative symptoms, cognitive impairment, neurological soft signs and enlarged ventricles all suggest a long-standing brain abnormality of the kind described in Section 4.7, whereas an acute onset after a clear stressor, with mood symptoms and perplexity, suggests an episode laid on top of a fairly intact brain. Prominent mood symptoms and a family history of mood disorder also hint that the illness lies closer to the mood end of the psychotic spectrum, where outcome is better. The third family concerns what happens after onset: a short DUP, good insight, taking medication, avoiding cannabis and living in a calm, supportive home all reduce relapse. Notice that the sex difference in outcome is partly explained by the first two families: men fall ill earlier, before marriage and work are established, and more often with negative symptoms.

Now push two cases through the table. You already know Mohan: early onset at 19, insidious prodrome over more than a year, no precipitant, poor premorbid social adjustment, prominent withdrawal and self-neglect, single, male, several months of untreated psychosis. Almost every factor points to a guarded prognosis. Leela, by contrast, is a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Thrissur, married with two children, popular with her colleagues and an active member of her church choir. Ten days after her father died suddenly, in the middle of a bitter property dispute with his brothers, she became frightened and sleepless, then convinced that her uncles had hired men to poison her and her children, and she heard her dead father's voice warning her not to eat. She cried for hours and said she deserved to die for failing him. She was treated within a fortnight. The active symptoms settled over six weeks, and after a few further months of mild suspiciousness and low mood she returned to teaching. Leela has acute onset, late onset, a clear precipitant, good premorbid functioning, marked mood symptoms, a supportive husband and a short DUP: nearly every factor points to a good prognosis. Notice also that her picture, with prominent mood symptoms, sits close to the boundaries with psychotic depression and schizoaffective disorder, which is exactly why Section 4.4 teaches how mood congruence and timing decide the diagnosis. The table cannot tell you that Mohan will do badly or that Leela will stay well. It tells you where the odds lie, and it tells the clinician which modifiable factors (DUP, adherence, cannabis, the family atmosphere) are worth fighting for.

Exam trap

Good prognosis: acute onset, late onset, clear precipitant, good premorbid functioning, mood symptoms, positive symptoms, female, married, family history of mood disorder, short DUP. Poor prognosis: the reverse, especially insidious onset, early onset and prominent negative symptoms. A family history of schizophrenia is a poor sign; a family history of mood disorder is a good one. Positive symptoms respond better to treatment than negative symptoms, so a mainly positive picture is the better one.

Death, disability and daily life

Schizophrenia shortens life. The bluntest measure is the standardised mortality ratio, the number of deaths observed in a group divided by the number expected for people of the same age and sex in the general population. McGrath's reviews found a median standardised mortality ratio of 2.6 for all causes of death, meaning that people with schizophrenia die at about two to three times the expected rate, and the gap has widened over recent decades as the general population's health has improved faster than theirs. In 2017 Carsten Hjorthøj and colleagues pooled 11 studies covering up to 247,603 patients and estimated that schizophrenia costs a person, on average, 14.5 years of life (15.9 years for men, 13.6 for women), with an average life expectancy of 64.7 years.

Suicide is part of the explanation but not the largest part. DSM-5-TR states that approximately 5 to 6 percent of people with schizophrenia die by suicide, about 20 percent attempt suicide at least once, and many more have serious suicidal thoughts. Older textbooks quoted a lifetime suicide rate of 10 percent; when Brian Palmer and colleagues re-examined the evidence in 2005 they showed that the figure came mainly from studies of chronic patients, and estimated the true lifetime risk at about 4.9 percent, with the deaths concentrated near the onset of the illness. DSM-5-TR lists the risk factors: being a younger man, especially with substance use; depressive symptoms and hopelessness; unemployment; the period just after a psychotic episode or discharge from hospital; many admissions; closeness to the onset of illness; a history of attempts; poor adherence to treatment; and, perhaps surprisingly, higher intelligence and older age at onset, which may give a person a sharper sense of what the illness has taken from them. Some suicidal acts are responses to command hallucinations. The depression that can follow an acute episode (post-psychotic depression, ICD-10's post-schizophrenic depression) is described in Section 4.4, and the assessment of suicide risk is taught in Book 2, Section 2.7.

Most of the remaining excess deaths come from ordinary physical illness: cardiovascular disease, diabetes and the metabolic syndrome, respiratory disease and some cancers. DSM-5-TR notes that more than half of people with schizophrenia smoke regularly, that they engage less in health maintenance such as screening and exercise, and that weight gain and metabolic effects of some antipsychotic drugs (Section 4.10) add to the risk. Health services add their own share: a breathless patient with a psychiatric label may be dismissed as anxious, a failure of care called diagnostic overshadowing.

The public fear of the "violent schizophrenic" deserves a correction. DSM-5-TR states that the vast majority of people with schizophrenia are not aggressive, and that they are more frequently victimised than people in the general population. Aggression, when it occurs, is commoner in younger men and in those with a history of violence, substance use, impulsivity and poor adherence to treatment, which are the same predictors of violence in anyone. Spontaneous or random assault is uncommon.

Disability is the daily face of the illness. DSM-5-TR notes that most people with schizophrenia are employed at a lower level than their parents and that most, particularly men, do not marry or have limited social contacts outside the family. In India the family is the main, and often the only, system of care: parents, spouses and siblings supervise medication, manage crises, find work and pay for treatment, frequently for decades. The resulting caregiver burden is financial (lost earnings, the cost of medicines and travel), practical and emotional, and it is sharpened by stigma, the shame and social exclusion attached to the illness. Families may hide the diagnosis to protect the marriage prospects of the patient's siblings; marriage is sometimes arranged in the hope that it will "cure" the illness, and a woman whose illness is discovered after marriage risks being sent back to her parents. Ageing parents carry a particular fear, voiced in almost every family meeting: "What will happen to him after we are gone?" Psychologists who work in Indian settings spend much of their time with these questions; the rehabilitation services that address them are taught in Book 2, Section 9.8, and the rights that the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 gives to people with mental illness, including the right to community living, in Book 2, Section 1.10.

Clinical link

When you take a history from a family like Mohan's or Leela's, ask about the carers as well as the patient: who supervises the tablets, who lost a job to stay at home, who in the house is exhausted, and whether anyone is hiding the illness from relatives or prospective in-laws. A caregiver who is supported is less likely to become critical and over-involved, and a calmer home lowers the risk of relapse (Section 4.9). Asking about suicidal thoughts, especially in the first years after diagnosis and after discharge, is never optional.

Exam trap

Lifetime suicide in schizophrenia: about 5 percent (DSM-5-TR: 5 to 6 percent die by suicide; about 20 percent attempt). The old figure of 10 percent overstates it. Risk is highest early in the illness, in young men, after discharge, and with depression and hopelessness. Life expectancy is reduced by roughly 15 years, mostly through physical illness, not suicide. People with schizophrenia are more often victims than perpetrators of violence.

4.7 Genes, Development and the BrainLower yield · 1 Q

Section 4.6 showed that schizophrenia strikes about one person in a hundred, mostly in early adulthood, more often in cities and among migrants, and with a course that ranges from full recovery to lifelong disability. Now we turn to the first and largest of the causal questions: what in the person's biology makes them vulnerable? The story has three strands, and they turn out to be one story. The first is genetic: schizophrenia runs in families, and twin and adoption studies show that most of that family resemblance comes from shared genes, not shared upbringing. The second is developmental: many of the risk factors act before birth, and the illness seems to be set up years before it appears. The third concerns the brain: people with schizophrenia have, on average, larger ventricles, less grey matter and a prefrontal cortex that does not switch on properly when it is needed. The neurotransmitters that carry this vulnerability into symptoms, dopamine above all, are the subject of Section 4.8; the psychological, family and social stresses that interact with it are the subject of Section 4.9.

Does schizophrenia run in families?

The first systematic answer came from the German-born psychiatrist Franz Josef Kallmann (1897 to 1965), who in 1938 published a study of the families of more than 1,000 people diagnosed with schizophrenia in a Berlin psychiatric hospital. Two of his observations still guide research. The more severe the parent's illness, the more likely the children were to develop it. And the subtypes of the day did not breed true: a parent with paranoid schizophrenia might have a child with the catatonic form. What runs in families, in other words, is a general vulnerability to schizophrenia, and later research showed that it extends to a broader group of related psychotic and schizotypal conditions, the schizophrenia spectrum.

A family study compares the rate of a disorder in the relatives of affected people (the probands) with the rate in the general population or in the relatives of unaffected controls. The definitive summary was compiled by the American psychologist Irving Gottesman (1930 to 2016) in his book Schizophrenia Genesis: The Origins of Madness (1991). Gottesman pooled about 40 family and twin studies conducted in European populations between 1920 and 1987 and calculated the average lifetime morbid risk for each kind of relative. His figures (Figure 4.11) are the most frequently examined numbers in the whole of this chapter, and they are worth learning as a set, because the set tells a story.

Read the chart from the bottom. In the general population the lifetime risk is 1 percent; for a spouse of a patient, who shares no genes with the patient but often shares a home, it is only 2 percent. Third-degree relatives (first cousins, who share about one-eighth of their genes with the patient) and second-degree relatives (uncles and aunts, nephews and nieces, grandchildren and half siblings, who share about a quarter) carry risks of 2 to 6 percent. First-degree relatives, who share about half their genes, carry 6 to 17 percent: siblings about 9, children of one affected parent about 13, fraternal twins about 17. At the top are the two situations in which a person inherits the largest dose of risk genes: the child of two affected parents (about 46 percent) and the identical twin (about 48 percent). The orderly rise of risk with genetic closeness is the single most striking fact about the figure.

Two details repay a second look. Why are parents (6 percent) at lower risk than siblings and children? Because people with schizophrenia have fewer children than other people, partly because the illness begins in early adulthood and impairs relationships, so the parents of patients are a selected group: they were well enough to marry and have children, and some of them would have been excluded from parenthood had they fallen ill early. And why do fraternal twins (17 percent) exceed ordinary siblings (9 percent), when both share half their genes? Because twins also share the womb, the same birth date and an unusually similar upbringing. That gap is a first clue that environment matters, and it is why family studies alone can never settle the question of genes. A family shares its kitchen, its quarrels and its neighbourhood as well as its DNA.

There is one more number to keep beside Gottesman's figure. DSM-5-TR points out that, despite the strong genetic contribution, most people who are diagnosed with schizophrenia have no family history of psychosis. The risks to relatives are high relative to 1 percent, but the absolute numbers are small: 91 percent of the siblings of a patient will never develop the illness.

A worked example shows how these figures are used in a real conversation. Paul is a 26-year-old software engineer in Bengaluru. His younger brother developed schizophrenia two years ago. Paul's family is now discussing his marriage, and the young woman's parents have asked, politely but directly, whether the illness is hereditary. Paul asks the psychologist three questions. What is his own risk? As a sibling, about 9 percent over a lifetime, against 1 percent in the general population; but since he is already 26 and well, he has lived through much of the peak period of risk for men, so his remaining risk is lower than that lifetime figure. What is the risk for his future children? They would be the nephews and nieces of an affected person, so about 4 percent, which means that about 96 children in 100 in that position never develop the illness. And is there a test? No: there is no genetic test that can predict schizophrenia in an individual, for reasons Section 4.7.4 explains. The psychologist adds one more piece of advice, prompted by something Paul mentions in passing: he smokes cannabis with his friends most weekends. For a man with an affected brother, that is a risk worth removing (Section 4.7.8).

Exam trap

Gottesman's (1991) figures to memorise: general population 1, sibling 9, child of one affected parent 13, fraternal twin 17, child of two affected parents 46, identical twin 48 (so "about 50 percent" is the exam answer). Parents are lower (6 percent) because people with schizophrenia have fewer children. Family studies show that schizophrenia is familial; they cannot by themselves show that it is genetic.

Twin studies: identical genes, different fates

Twins offer a natural experiment. Monozygotic (identical) twins develop from a single fertilised egg that splits, so they share essentially all their genes. Dizygotic (fraternal) twins develop from two eggs fertilised by two sperm, so, like ordinary siblings, they share on average half the genes that vary between people. Both kinds of twins share a womb and are usually raised together. If schizophrenia were caused entirely by the family environment, identical and fraternal twins should be equally alike for it. If it were caused entirely by genes, an identical twin of a patient should always be affected. The truth lies between, and the twin method measures where.

The key measure is concordance, the extent to which both members of a twin pair have the disorder. There are two ways of calculating it, and they give different numbers. Pairwise concordance is the proportion of twin pairs in which both twins are affected. Probandwise concordance is the proportion of affected twins (probands) whose co-twin is also affected; pairs in which both twins were independently found by the researchers are counted twice. Probandwise figures are higher, and they are the ones that can be compared with the risk for other relatives. Early twin studies, including Kallmann's, reported very high concordance for identical twins, but they relied on unblinded diagnosis, uncertain zygosity and samples drawn from chronic hospital patients.

The modern era began in London. James Shields and Irving Gottesman studied every twin admitted to the Maudsley and Bethlem Royal Hospitals over 16 years (from 1948, when the hospital's twin register began, to 1964), reporting first in 1966 and then in their book Schizophrenia and Genetics: A Twin Study Vantage Point (1972). The identical co-twins were affected several times as often as the fraternal co-twins. In 1967 the two men proposed a polygenic theory of schizophrenia: the illness is not caused by one gene but by many, each of small effect, which add up to a person's liability; only when the total of genetic and environmental liability passes a threshold does the illness appear. This liability-threshold model, borrowed from the British geneticist Douglas Falconer's work of 1965, explains at a stroke why most patients have no affected relative, why risk rises smoothly with genetic closeness, and why identical twins can be discordant.

Later studies have refined the numbers. In 2003 Patrick Sullivan, Kenneth Kendler and Michael Neale pooled 12 twin studies and estimated the heritability of liability to schizophrenia at 81 percent, with a further 11 percent of the variation attributable to environment shared by the twins. The most complete modern study used the whole Danish twin register: Rikke Hilker and colleagues in 2018 followed 31,524 twin pairs born between 1951 and 2000 and found a probandwise concordance of 33 percent in identical twins and 7 percent in fraternal twins, with a heritability of 79 percent.

Heritability is the most misunderstood number in psychiatry, so be precise about it. It is the proportion of the variation in liability within a particular population that is attributable to genetic differences between people in that population. A heritability of 80 percent does not mean that 80 percent of any one person's illness is caused by genes, and it does not mean that a person with risk genes has an 80 percent chance of falling ill. It is perfectly compatible with an identical co-twin falling ill only about half the time. Indeed, the most important fact in Figure 4.11 is not the 48 percent but the 52 percent: more than half of the identical twins of patients, with the same genome and usually the same upbringing, never develop schizophrenia. Something other than the inherited DNA sequence must decide.

You have already met the most famous illustration of this. The Genain quadruplets with whom this chapter opened shared one genome and one disturbed home, and all four developed schizophrenia; yet their age at onset, their symptoms, their hospital stays and their eventual functioning differed widely. Identical genes, it turns out, do not guarantee identical lives. Even identical twins differ in their position in the womb, their blood supply and birth weight, the complications of their delivery, the illnesses of their childhood and the way their parents treat them; behaviour geneticists call these experiences the non-shared environment. Later sections show how such differences may leave marks on the brain itself (Section 4.7.7).

Key study: The children of discordant twins (Fischer 1971; Gottesman and Bertelsen 1989)

If an identical twin escapes schizophrenia, has she escaped the genes, or merely failed to express them? The Danish psychiatrist Margit Fischer found a way to ask. In 1971 she identified Danish twin pairs in which one twin had schizophrenia and looked at their children. Gottesman and Aksel Bertelsen later followed all those children through the Danish psychiatric register to 1985. The age-corrected risk of schizophrenia and related disorders was 16.8 percent in the children of the affected identical twins and 17.4 percent in the children of their healthy identical co-twins. For fraternal twins the figures were 17.4 percent for children of the affected twin but only 2.1 percent for children of the healthy co-twin. Conclusion: a healthy identical co-twin carries, and passes on, the same risk as her ill sister. A schizophrenia genotype can remain unexpressed unless something, perhaps an environmental stressor, releases it. Criticism: the samples were small, and "related disorders" broadened the outcome.

Exam trap

"The chance of developing schizophrenia if you have an identical twin with the disorder" is about 50 percent (Gottesman's 48 percent; modern probandwise figures 33 to 50 percent). The tempting 80 percent is the heritability, a different quantity; 10 percent is roughly the risk for a sibling; 1 percent is the general population. Twin concordance is not 100 percent, which is the strongest single proof that environment matters too.

Adoption studies: separating genes from upbringing

Twin studies have a weakness: identical twins may be treated more alike than fraternal twins, and they share a womb, so a critic can always argue that their greater similarity is partly environmental. Adoption breaks the link completely. A child adopted at birth receives genes from one set of parents and upbringing from another, so the two influences can finally be pulled apart. In the 1960s the idea that schizophrenia was caused by disturbed parenting (the "schizophrenogenic mother" of Section 4.9) was still widely taught, which made the adoption studies a direct test of a popular theory.

Key study: Heston's foster children (1966)

The American psychiatrist Leonard Heston traced children born between 1915 and 1945 to mothers with schizophrenia who were confined in an Oregon state psychiatric hospital. By hospital policy, every baby was removed from the mother within three days of birth and raised by relatives or in foster or adoptive homes, with no further contact with the mother. Heston compared 47 of these children, now adults, with 50 control adults who had also been separated from their mothers in infancy but whose mothers were not ill. Psychiatrists who did not know which group a person belonged to made the diagnoses. Result: 5 of the 47 children of mothers with schizophrenia had developed schizophrenia, and none of the 50 controls; the age-corrected morbid risk was 16.6 percent, about the same as for children raised by their own ill mothers. About half of the experimental group (26 of 47) showed marked psychosocial disability, and besides schizophrenia they had more sociopathic personality, mental deficiency and neurotic personality disorder, and more criminal convictions, than the controls. Conclusion: being raised by a mother with schizophrenia was not necessary for the risk to be passed on. Criticism: the samples were small, and the children had shared their mothers' wombs and pregnancies, so prenatal influences could not be excluded.

The definitive adoption studies were carried out in Denmark, whose national registers of adoptions and psychiatric admissions made it possible to trace every adoptee and every relative. The American team of Seymour Kety (1915 to 2000), David Rosenthal and Paul Wender, working with the Danish psychiatrist Fini Schulsinger, began reporting in 1968. They started from the 5,483 people adopted in Copenhagen and found the adoptees who had later developed schizophrenia; then they examined both sets of relatives of each one, the biological family who had given them genes and the adoptive family who had raised them, and compared them with the relatives of matched control adoptees. Schizophrenia and the milder conditions they called the schizophrenia spectrum (a term this team introduced) were concentrated among the biological relatives of the adoptees with schizophrenia; the adoptive relatives, who had raised them, did not differ from the controls. In the Copenhagen sample, 5.6 percent of the biological relatives of chronically ill adoptees had chronic schizophrenia and a further 14.8 percent had what Eugen Bleuler had called latent schizophrenia. A replication across the rest of Denmark, published in 1994, found that in the combined national sample chronic schizophrenia occurred exclusively in the biological relatives and was ten times commoner than in the biological relatives of controls. The team also used the opposite design, cross-fostering (Wender and colleagues, 1974): children of healthy biological parents who happened to be raised by an adoptive parent who developed schizophrenia did not show the raised risk seen in adoptees whose biological parent was ill.

The adoption studies did not end the story of the family environment; they changed it. The largest, the Finnish Adoptive Family Study of Schizophrenia led by Pekka Tienari, searched the records of nearly 20,000 Finnish women hospitalised with schizophrenia or a paranoid psychosis and found about 190 of their children who had been adopted away, then compared them with control adoptees. Unlike the Danish studies, it observed the adoptive families directly and rated how well they functioned. In 2004 Tienari and colleagues reported the long-term results (adoptees were rediagnosed after a median of 12 years, with register follow-up after 21 years).
