Among adoptees at high genetic risk, the quality of the adoptive family significantly predicted who developed schizophrenia-spectrum disorders: those raised in disturbed adoptive families fared worst, those raised in healthy ones much better. Among adoptees at low genetic risk, the adoptive family made no significant difference. This is a gene-environment interaction: the environment matters, but chiefly for people whose genes make them sensitive to it. It is exactly the pattern that the stress-vulnerability model of Section 4.9 predicts. Table 4.27 compares the three classic designs.

Table 4.27 Three designs for separating genes from environment in schizophrenia.
DesignClassic studyMain findingMain weakness
Family study: risk by closeness of relationshipKallmann (1938); Gottesman's pooled figures (1991)Risk rises with genes shared, from 1 to 48 percentFamilies share environment as well as genes
Twin study: identical versus fraternal concordanceGottesman and Shields (1966, 1972); Danish twin register (2018)Identical concordance about 33 to 48 percent, fraternal 7 to 17; heritability about 80 percentIdentical twins may share more similar environments
Adoptee study: children of ill parents raised by othersHeston (1966)5 of 47 versus 0 of 50 controlsSmall samples; prenatal environment shared with the mother
Adoptee family study: relatives of ill adopteesKety, Rosenthal and Wender (Denmark, from 1968)Illness in biological, not adoptive, relativesBroad "spectrum" diagnoses
Adoption with family ratingsTienari (Finland, 2004)Disturbed adoptive homes raise risk only in high-risk adopteesAdoptive families may react to a difficult child
Exam trap

Heston (1966): Oregon, children of mothers with schizophrenia separated at birth; 5 of 47 developed schizophrenia, none of 50 controls. Kety, Rosenthal and Wender (1968): the Danish adoption studies; illness concentrated in biological, not adoptive, relatives; they coined "schizophrenia spectrum". Tienari: the Finnish study; gene-environment interaction. David Rosenthal also led the NIMH study of the Genain quadruplets (1963), so his name can appear in either context.

From heritability to genes

If schizophrenia is about 80 percent heritable, where are the genes? For three decades the search was disappointing. Linkage studies, which track chromosome regions through families with many affected members, pointed to regions on many different chromosomes that rarely replicated. Candidate gene studies tested genes chosen because their function seemed plausible, such as the gene for catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT, an enzyme that breaks down dopamine), neuregulin 1 and dysbindin; each had its moment, and most failed to hold up when much larger samples were examined. One striking exception was a large Scottish family in which a chromosomal translocation disrupting a gene later named DISC1 (Disrupted in Schizophrenia 1) travelled with schizophrenia, depression and bipolar disorder; Pinel describes transgenic mice carrying the defective human gene, which developed a reduced cerebral cortex, enlarged ventricles and behaviour reminiscent of schizophrenia. The failure of the hunt for single "schizophrenia genes" was itself informative. It is just what Gottesman and Shields's polygenic theory predicted.

The breakthrough came with the genome-wide association study (GWAS), which scans hundreds of thousands of common genetic variants across the whole genome in very large numbers of cases and controls, looking for variants that are slightly commoner in the cases. In 2014 the Schizophrenia Working Group of the Psychiatric Genomics Consortium, an international collaboration, analysed up to 36,989 cases and 113,075 controls and identified 108 genetic loci associated with schizophrenia. The associations were enriched among genes expressed in the brain; they included the gene for the dopamine D2 receptor (the target of every antipsychotic drug, Section 4.8) and several genes involved in glutamate transmission, and, unexpectedly, genes expressed in tissues important for immunity. A larger study in 2022 (Trubetskoy and colleagues, 76,755 cases and 243,649 controls) raised the count to 287 loci, concentrated in genes expressed in excitatory and inhibitory neurons and involved in the organisation and transmission of synapses. Each common variant raises risk only very slightly. Adding up a person's risk variants gives a polygenic risk score, which is useful in research but explains only a modest part of who becomes ill, so it cannot diagnose schizophrenia or predict it in an individual such as Paul.

Alongside the many common variants of small effect there are a few rare variants of large effect. The most important are copy number variants (CNVs), stretches of DNA that are deleted or duplicated. The best known is the 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (also called velocardiofacial or DiGeorge syndrome), the loss of a small piece of chromosome 22, which causes heart defects, a cleft palate, a characteristic face, learning difficulties and immune problems. About one in four people with this deletion develop schizophrenia, more than twenty times the general population risk, which makes it the strongest single known genetic risk factor; it is still rare, and accounts for only a small fraction of all cases. Other rare deletions and duplications, many of them shared with autism and intellectual disability, add further risk. Some of these variants arise as de novo mutations, new mutations not present in either parent. Because sperm-producing cells keep dividing throughout a man's life, mutations accumulate in older fathers. In 2001 Dolores Malaspina and colleagues followed 87,907 people born in Jerusalem between 1964 and 1976 and found that the risk of schizophrenia rose steadily with the father's age at conception, reaching about 2 times in children of fathers aged 45 to 49 and about 3 times for fathers aged 50 or more, whereas the mother's age had no effect once the father's was allowed for.

The most famous single discovery from the genome-wide era explained the strongest association of all, in the major histocompatibility complex, a region of chromosome 6 full of immune genes. In 2016 Aswin Sekar, Steven McCarroll and colleagues showed that the association arises partly from structurally different versions of the genes for complement component 4 (C4), a protein of the immune system. Versions that produce more of the C4A form in the brain carried more risk. C4 was found at neuronal synapses, and in mice it mediated synapse elimination during development. The finding linked a risk gene to a process, excessive pruning of synapses, that may explain why people with schizophrenia have fewer synapses in the cortex (Section 4.7.7).

Two further lessons complete the genetic picture. First, the genes are not specific to schizophrenia. DSM-5-TR notes that the risk alleles identified so far are also associated with bipolar disorder, depression and autism spectrum disorder; the boundaries between our diagnostic categories are blurrier in the genome than in the textbook. Second, genes act through, and together with, the environment. Epigenetics, the study of chemical marks such as DNA methylation and histone modification that switch genes on and off without changing the DNA sequence, offers one route: Pinel describes evidence that such marks alter the expression of synaptic genes in prefrontal neurons in schizophrenia, and early adversity may act partly through them. The clearest example of a gene-environment interaction at the level of a single gene came from the Dunedin birth cohort in New Zealand, where in 2005 Avshalom Caspi, Terrie Moffitt and colleagues found that adolescent cannabis use raised the risk of adult psychosis mainly in people who carried the valine version of a common COMT variant, and hardly at all in those with two copies of the methionine version. It was a striking result, though later studies have not consistently replicated it.

Exam trap

Schizophrenia is polygenic: many common variants of small effect plus a few rare variants of larger effect. There is no single schizophrenia gene and no predictive genetic test. Remember 2014 (108 loci, Psychiatric Genomics Consortium) and the 22q11.2 deletion (about one in four develop schizophrenia). Older fathers, not older mothers, raise the risk (de novo mutations). The C4 finding links genes to synaptic pruning.

Endophenotypes: markers between genes and symptoms

Diagnoses such as schizophrenia are a long way from genes. Between the DNA and the delusion lie many steps of brain development and function, and the symptoms themselves fluctuate with treatment and stress. Gottesman and Shields brought into psychiatry the idea of an endophenotype: a measurable, internal characteristic (neurophysiological, cognitive, neuroanatomical or biochemical) that lies on the path from genes to illness. A good endophenotype is associated with the illness, is heritable, is present whether or not the person is currently ill (it is "state-independent"), and is found more often in the unaffected relatives of patients than in the general population. Because it is closer to the genes, it should be easier to study genetically than the diagnosis itself. Barlow and Durand call the strategy endophenotyping. Three candidates are examined most often.

Smooth pursuit eye movement dysfunction is the oldest. Ask a person to keep their head still and follow a swinging pendulum with their eyes: a healthy person's eyes glide smoothly after it, but many people with schizophrenia cannot keep up, so their eyes fall behind and then jump forward in small catch-up movements called saccades, producing a jagged trace. Pinel illustrates the finding with recordings of a healthy participant and three participants with schizophrenia. It was first recorded in 1908 by Allen Diefendorf and Raymond Dodge and was rediscovered by Philip Holzman and his colleagues, who reported in Science in 1973 that a significant number of patients with schizophrenia showed tracking patterns strikingly different from those of healthy people and of other psychiatric patients. The deficit is not explained by medication or institutional life, and it appears in many unaffected relatives of patients, becoming less frequent the more distant the relative.

P50 sensory gating measures the brain's ability to filter out repeated, irrelevant input. Two identical clicks are played about half a second apart while the electrical activity of the brain is recorded. In most people the response to the second click, a wave appearing about 50 milliseconds after the sound (the P50), is much smaller than the response to the first: the brain has "gated out" a stimulus it already knows about. In many people with schizophrenia, and in many of their relatives, the second response is barely reduced. Robert Freedman's group in Colorado linked this deficit to the region of chromosome 15 that holds the gene for the alpha-7 nicotinic acetylcholine receptor, and the finding offers one explanation for why so many patients smoke heavily: nicotine briefly improves the gating. Prepulse inhibition is a related measure: a weak sound (the prepulse) presented a fraction of a second before a loud, startling one normally reduces the startle blink, and this inhibition is weaker in schizophrenia. Working memory and other cognitive deficits (Section 4.3) are also studied as endophenotypes. None of these markers is specific to schizophrenia or sensitive enough to diagnose it; their value is as tools for research.

Exam trap

An endophenotype is a heritable, state-independent marker that is also found in unaffected relatives; it is not a symptom and not a diagnostic test. The classic example is smooth pursuit eye movement dysfunction (Holzman, 1973). P50 sensory gating: the response to the second of two clicks fails to shrink. Prepulse inhibition: a weak prepulse fails to reduce the startle response.

The neurodevelopmental hypothesis

Kraepelin's very name for the illness, dementia praecox, implied a degenerative process, a brain wearing out early as it does in old age. By the 1980s that view was in trouble. Neurodegenerative diseases such as Alzheimer's disease leave behind gliosis, scars of glial cells where neurons have died, but the brains of people with schizophrenia examined after death generally showed no gliosis, which suggested that whatever had gone wrong had happened early, before the brain could react in that way. Brain scans showed abnormalities already present at the first episode. And the risk factors that epidemiologists kept finding (season of birth, obstetric complications, prenatal infection) all acted around the time of birth, twenty years before the illness appeared.

In 1987 two papers drew these threads together. The American psychiatrist Daniel Weinberger, working at the National Institute of Mental Health, argued in "Implications of normal brain development for the pathogenesis of schizophrenia" that the illness fits neither a degenerative nor a post-traumatic model but a neurodevelopmental hypothesis, in which a fixed "lesion" from early in life interacts with normal brain maturation that occurs much later. The early abnormality, he proposed, stays silent until the brain regions it affects, especially the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, and the dopamine systems that innervate them, mature in adolescence and early adulthood. In the same year the British psychiatrists Robin Murray and Shôn Lewis asked in the British Medical Journal, "Is schizophrenia a neurodevelopmental disorder?", and in a companion review concluded that patients more often had a history of obstetric complications, which were associated with enlarged ventricles, especially in patients without a family history. The hypothesis explains the most puzzling fact about schizophrenia, its timing: why should a disorder of genes and pregnancy wait twenty years to show itself? Because the parts of the brain on which the lesion falls are not fully used until then. The evidence for it comes from several directions (Table 4.28 and Figure 4.12).

Obstetric complications. In 2002 Mary Cannon, Peter Jones and Robin Murray meta-analysed the prospective population studies and found three groups of complications significantly associated with later schizophrenia: complications of pregnancy (bleeding, diabetes, rhesus incompatibility, pre-eclampsia), abnormal foetal growth and development (low birth weight, congenital malformations, a small head circumference) and complications of delivery (asphyxia, uterine atony, emergency Caesarean section). The common thread is a shortage of oxygen to the foetal brain. The effects are real but modest, with pooled estimates generally below a doubling of risk, and some complications may themselves be consequences of a foetus already developing abnormally.

Key study: The Helsinki influenza epidemic (Mednick and colleagues, 1988)

In the autumn of 1957 a type A2 influenza epidemic swept through Helsinki. Sarnoff Mednick and his colleagues saw a natural experiment: every child then in the womb had been exposed to the epidemic at a known stage of pregnancy, which could be worked out from the date of birth. They searched the psychiatric hospital records of everyone in greater Helsinki who had been a foetus during the epidemic. Those exposed during the second trimester were at raised risk of being admitted with schizophrenia as adults, in both sexes and independently in several hospitals. The second trimester is the period when neurons are migrating to their places in the developing cortex. Criticism, much of it noted by the authors themselves: there was no direct evidence that the mothers had actually been infected; diagnoses came from hospital records; the follow-up stopped at about 26 years of age; and some later studies replicated the finding while others did not. The authors' own conclusion was that the type of disturbance matters less than its timing in foetal brain development.

Famine. In the winter of 1944 to 1945 the German occupation cut off food supplies to the cities of the western Netherlands, and the official rations fell to a few hundred calories a day: the Dutch Hunger Winter. Because the famine began and ended abruptly and the Dutch kept meticulous records, it became another natural experiment. In 1996 Ezra Susser and colleagues found that people conceived at the height of the famine, and so exposed to severe maternal malnutrition in early gestation, had a twofold risk of schizophrenia in adulthood, in both men and women. Pinel notes that the Chinese famine of 1959 to 1961 produced the same result.

Season of birth, the father's age and the city. The 5 to 8 percent excess of late winter and spring births (Section 4.6.3) may reflect seasonal infections or nutritional shortages during pregnancy; the father's age effect points to de novo mutations (Section 4.7.4); and urban birth raises the risk by a mechanism not yet known.

Marks on the body. People with schizophrenia have more minor physical anomalies, small structural deviations such as a high-arched palate, malformed or low-set ears, widely spaced eyes and unusual skin creases. Because the face and the brain develop from the same embryonic tissue during the first and early second trimesters, these anomalies act as fossils of a disturbance at that time. DSM-5-TR also lists neurological soft signs, subtle impairments of motor coordination, sensory integration and the sequencing of complex movements, left-right confusion and overflow movements, which point to the same early origin.

Signs in childhood. If the illness is set up early, children destined to develop it should differ from other children long before any symptom. In a study that is now a classic, Elaine Walker and Richard Lewine (1990) collected home movies made by families of adults with schizophrenia, films that showed the patients and their healthy brothers and sisters from infancy through at least their first five years. Viewers who did not know which child had later fallen ill could nevertheless pick out the preschizophrenic children reliably, although none of them had had any psychiatric disorder in childhood. Later analyses of such films pointed to subtle motor abnormalities and less positive emotional expression. Prospective birth cohorts confirmed the picture without relying on anyone's memory. Peter Jones and colleagues (1994) examined the 5,362 people of the British 1946 birth cohort, all born in one week of March 1946, of whom 30 developed schizophrenia between the ages of 16 and 43. As children, the future patients had learned to walk later (by about 1.2 months on average), had more speech problems, scored lower on educational tests at 8, 11 and 15 years, preferred solitary play at 4 and 6, rated themselves less socially confident at 13 and were rated by teachers as more anxious in social situations at 15. Mohan's childhood in Section 4.6.4 shows the same pattern. But notice the size of the differences: a month's delay in walking, a slight preference for playing alone. These signs are found in thousands of children who never develop schizophrenia, so they are clues to the origin of the illness, not a means of predicting it in a child.

Table 4.28 Evidence for the neurodevelopmental hypothesis.
Line of evidenceKey studyFinding
Obstetric complicationsCannon, Jones and Murray (2002), meta-analysisPregnancy, growth and delivery complications each raise risk, usually less than twofold
Prenatal influenzaMednick and colleagues (1988), Helsinki 1957Second-trimester exposure raises risk
Prenatal famineSusser and colleagues (1996), Dutch Hunger WinterConception at the height of the famine doubles risk
Older fatherMalaspina and colleagues (2001), JerusalemAbout 2 to 3 times the risk with fathers aged 45 or more

Exam trap

The neurodevelopmental hypothesis is credited to Daniel Weinberger (1987) and to Robin Murray and Shôn Lewis (1987). It says the decisive abnormality arises early (before or around birth) and is unmasked by brain maturation in adolescence; it is the opposite of Kraepelin's degenerative idea. The influenza study (Mednick, Helsinki 1957 epidemic) implicates the second trimester; the Dutch Hunger Winter study implicates famine in early gestation.

The brain in schizophrenia

Every neurodevelopmental argument leads back to the brain. What, exactly, is different about the brain of a person with schizophrenia? The basic anatomy (the ventricles, the lobes, the hippocampus, the prefrontal cortex) is taught in the Physiological Psychology book; here we need only the findings and what they mean.

The most replicated structural finding concerns the lateral ventricles, the fluid-filled cavities deep in each cerebral hemisphere. As early as 1927 German researchers using pneumoencephalography (X-rays taken after replacing cerebrospinal fluid with air) saw enlarged ventricles in some patients. The modern era began in 1976, when Eve Johnstone, Timothy Crow, Christopher Frith and their colleagues at the Clinical Research Centre at Northwick Park in Harrow used the new technique of computerised axial tomography (CT) on 17 long-stay hospital patients with schizophrenia and on age-matched controls in employment. The patients' ventricles were larger, and within the patient group larger ventricles went with greater cognitive impairment. Since then dozens of studies, now using magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), have confirmed ventricular enlargement of the lateral and third ventricles. The ventricles themselves are not the problem. Ventricles enlarge when the brain tissue around them is smaller, because it either failed to develop fully or has been lost. Three cautions apply. The enlargement is modest and found on average, not in every patient; it overlaps heavily with healthy people; and it is commoner in men, in patients with negative symptoms and cognitive impairment (the profile of Crow's Type II schizophrenia, Section 4.3), and in those with a poor prognosis.

The tissue that is missing is mostly grey matter, the layer of neuronal cell bodies, dendrites and synapses. DSM-5-TR summarises the imaging evidence: reduced overall brain volume, reduced grey matter in a variety of regions such as the prefrontal and temporal cortices, altered white matter connectivity, and an exaggerated reduction of brain volume with age. Within the temporal lobe, the hippocampus and the superior temporal gyrus, which contains auditory cortex and has been linked to the auditory hallucinations of Section 4.2, are especially affected. Pinel lists four conclusions from the meta-analyses: people at genetic risk but not yet ill already show reduced volume in regions such as the hippocampus; extensive changes are already present at the first scan; the changes continue to develop after diagnosis; and different brain areas change at different rates. Part of the later progression may be due to antipsychotic treatment, alcohol, cannabis and poor physical health, which makes it hard to separate the illness from its consequences. The picture that emerges combines early development with later change: a neurodevelopmental illness, not simply a static lesion.

Key study: Identical twins discordant for schizophrenia (Suddath and colleagues, 1990)

If the brain differences in schizophrenia were wholly genetic, an ill identical twin and the healthy co-twin should have the same brain. Richard Suddath, E. Fuller Torrey, Daniel Weinberger and colleagues at the NIMH scanned 15 pairs of identical twins discordant for schizophrenia with MRI and compared the images blind. In 12 of the 15 pairs, the ill twin could be picked out simply by looking at the size of the fluid spaces in the brain. Measurement showed that the anterior hippocampus was smaller on the left in 14 of the 15 ill twins and on the right in 13, and that the lateral ventricles were larger on the left in 14 and on the right in 13; the third ventricle was larger in 13. Seven pairs of healthy identical twins scanned as controls showed none of these differences. Conclusion: small anterior hippocampi and enlarged ventricles are consistent features of schizophrenia, and since the twins share their genes, their cause is at least partly not genetic. In a follow-up study of discordant twins, the more an ill twin's left hippocampus differed from the healthy co-twin's, the more the ill twin's prefrontal cortex failed to activate during a card-sorting task, linking the temporal and frontal findings into a single network.

Structure is only half of the story; the other half is function. In 1974 the Swedish researchers David Ingvar and Göran Franzén, measuring regional cerebral blood flow with a radioactive tracer (xenon-133), reported in The Lancet that patients with chronic schizophrenia lacked the normal pattern in which the frontal regions receive relatively more blood than the rest of the cortex. They called the result hypofrontality, reduced activity of the frontal lobes. A decade later Weinberger, Karen Berman and Ronald Zec (1986) showed that hypofrontality depends on what the brain is asked to do. They measured blood flow in 20 medication-free patients and 25 healthy controls at rest, during a simple number-matching task and during the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, a task in which the person must work out a sorting rule (by colour, shape or number) from feedback and then switch when the rule changes without warning; the test itself belongs to the Assessment book. During the card-sorting test, the controls showed a clear rise in blood flow to the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, the region at the front and side of the frontal lobe that supports working memory and flexible thinking; the patients did not. The difference was specific to that region and that task, and the patients whose prefrontal cortex activated best performed best. Later studies found that some patients show too much prefrontal activity on easier tasks, which suggests that the real problem is inefficiency, a prefrontal cortex that has to work harder to achieve less. Hypofrontality is the brain counterpart of the negative and cognitive symptoms, and Section 4.8 explains how low dopamine activity in the pathway to the prefrontal cortex may produce it.

Why should the illness emerge in late adolescence? In 1982 the American psychiatrist Irwin Feinberg pointed out that the brain undergoes a profound reorganisation in adolescence: deep sleep and brain metabolism fall sharply, and the density of cortical synapses declines as unused connections are removed, a process called synaptic pruning. Pruning is normal and useful; it makes the adult brain faster and more efficient. Feinberg asked whether schizophrenia might be caused by a fault in this programmed elimination of synapses, in which too many connections, or the wrong ones, are removed. Post-mortem studies have since found fewer dendritic spines (the tiny protrusions on which synapses form) on prefrontal pyramidal neurons in schizophrenia, and the C4 discovery of 2016 supplied a plausible genetic mechanism for excessive pruning. Feinberg's hypothesis fits the timing of onset, the reduction in grey matter without loss of neurons, and Weinberger's idea of a lesion unmasked by maturation. Table 4.29 summarises the main brain findings.

Table 4.29 The main brain findings in schizophrenia.
FindingKey studyWhat it means
Enlarged lateral and third ventriclesJohnstone and colleagues (1976), first CT studySurrounding tissue is reduced; linked to cognitive impairment and poor outcome
Reduced grey matter, prefrontal and temporalMany MRI studies; DSM-5-TR summaryPresent at first episode; partly progressive
Smaller hippocampusSuddath and colleagues (1990), discordant identical twinsPresent in the ill twin only, so partly non-genetic
HypofrontalityIngvar and Franzén (1974); Weinberger, Berman and Zec (1986)Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex fails to activate on demand
Excessive synaptic pruningFeinberg (1982); Sekar and colleagues (2016)Explains adolescent onset and loss of synapses
Exam trap

The first CT demonstration of enlarged lateral ventricles was by Eve Johnstone and colleagues (1976). Hypofrontality (Ingvar and Franzén, 1974) means reduced frontal activity, shown most clearly in the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex during the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test (Weinberger, 1986). The discordant twin MRI study is Suddath (1990): the ill twin has the smaller hippocampus and larger ventricles. None of these findings is present in every patient, so none can be used to diagnose schizophrenia; DSM-5-TR states that there are no radiological, laboratory or psychometric tests for the disorder.

Cannabis, the adolescent brain and the whole picture

Of all the environmental risk factors, cannabis matters most to a clinician, because it is the one a young person can change. The question was put to the test in Sweden, where in 1969 and 1970 almost every young man was conscripted into the army and filled in a questionnaire about drug use. In 1987 Sven Andréasson and colleagues linked the questionnaires of 45,570 conscripts to psychiatric admissions over the next 15 years. Men who had used cannabis on more than 50 occasions by the time of conscription had six times the risk of schizophrenia of non-users, and the association persisted after allowing for other psychiatric illness and social background. Critics raised three objections. Perhaps the young men were already in the prodrome and using cannabis to cope (reverse causation or self-medication); perhaps other drugs, not cannabis, were responsible; perhaps a personality trait led both to drug use and to illness. In 2002 Stanley Zammit and colleagues re-analysed the extended cohort of 50,087 conscripts with a longer follow-up. The risk rose in a dose-dependent fashion; it was present in men who had used only cannabis and no other drug; and it remained when the analysis was restricted to men who fell ill more than five years after conscription, too long after for the drug use to have been prodromal.

Cannabis is neither necessary nor sufficient for schizophrenia. Most heavy users never become psychotic, and most patients developed the illness without heavy use. But the dose-response relation, the timing and the consistency of the evidence across countries are consistent with a causal contribution, especially for heavy use of high-potency cannabis in adolescence, when the prefrontal cortex is being pruned and remodelled. Its active ingredient, delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), increases dopamine release (Section 4.8), and in people who already have schizophrenia continued use predicts more relapses. Caspi's COMT study (Section 4.7.4) suggests that some people are genetically more vulnerable than others, though nobody can yet tell who. The practical advice to Paul in Section 4.7.1 follows directly: his brother's illness puts him at raised genetic risk, and cannabis is the one risk factor he controls. Substance-induced psychotic disorder, in which a drug produces a psychosis that clears when the drug is stopped, is described in Section 4.5 and Chapter 10.

Step back now and put the strands together. Schizophrenia is highly heritable, but the heritability is spread across hundreds of common genetic variants of small effect and a few rare ones of large effect, and even an identical genome produces the illness only about half the time. Many of the environmental risks act before birth, during the critical months when the foetal brain is being assembled, and they leave traces in the body, in childhood behaviour and in the brain itself. The vulnerable brain stays largely silent until adolescence, when the pruning and maturation of the prefrontal cortex, together with stresses such as cannabis, city life, migration and adversity, unmask it. Gottesman and Shields's liability threshold, Weinberger's lesion unmasked by maturation and Tienari's sensitive adoptees are three versions of one idea: a genetic and developmental diathesis that becomes an illness only when life adds enough stress. Paul Meehl's term for the inherited neural defect, schizotaxia, and the full stress-vulnerability model of Joseph Zubin and Bonnie Spring are taught in Section 4.9, and the general diathesis-stress model in Book 2, Section 1.7. How the vulnerable brain actually generates delusions and voices, through dopamine, glutamate and serotonin, is the subject of Section 4.8.

Clinical link

Families often ask, "Did we cause this?" The genetic and developmental evidence allows a clinician to answer honestly: no parent's behaviour causes schizophrenia, the vulnerability is largely inherited and laid down before birth, and the family's real power lies in what happens next, in getting early treatment, supporting adherence and keeping the home calm. When relatives ask about marriage and children, give the actual figures (Figure 4.11) rather than vague reassurance or alarm, and say plainly that no genetic test can predict the illness in an individual; refer for formal genetic counselling only where a syndrome such as the 22q11.2 deletion is suspected.

Exam trap

Andréasson and colleagues (1987): 45,570 Swedish conscripts; heavy cannabis use (more than 50 occasions) carried about six times the risk of schizophrenia. The extended follow-up (Zammit, 2002) showed a dose-response relation that persisted after excluding probable prodromal cases. Cannabis is a contributory cause: it is neither necessary nor sufficient.

4.8 Neurochemistry: Dopamine, Glutamate and SerotoninMedium yield · 10 Qs

Section 4.7 showed that schizophrenia runs in families and leaves its mark on the structure of the brain. But a gene does not hear voices, and an enlarged ventricle does not believe that the neighbours are reading its thoughts. Somewhere between the genes and the experience, something must be going wrong in the moment-to-moment chemistry by which neurons talk to one another. What is it? For more than half a century the leading answer has been a single word: dopamine. This section tells how that answer was reached, why it has survived, why it had to be rewritten twice, and why two other transmitters, glutamate and serotonin, now share the stage with it. Notice one unusual feature of the story as you read. The theory was not found by studying the brains of patients. It was worked out backwards, from the drugs that happened to help them.

Clues from drugs: how the dopamine hypothesis was born

When chlorpromazine was introduced into psychiatry in 1952 (Section 4.10 tells that story), nobody knew how it worked. A second drug soon joined it. Reserpine, the active ingredient of the snakeroot plant Rauwolfia serpentina, also calmed agitated patients and reduced their delusions and hallucinations. Chemically the two drugs had nothing in common, yet clinicians noticed that they shared two odd features. First, neither worked at once: the antipsychotic effect built up over two or three weeks of treatment. Second, at the doses that helped psychosis, both drugs often made patients stiff, slow and tremulous, with a mask-like face and a shuffling walk. They looked, in other words, as if they had Parkinson's disease. That second clue turned out to be the key.

In the late 1950s the Swedish pharmacologist Arvid Carlsson (1923 to 2018) showed that dopamine, until then regarded merely as a chemical step on the way to noradrenaline, is a neurotransmitter in its own right, concentrated in the basal ganglia, the deep motor structures of the brain. Reserpine emptied the brain of it, and animals given reserpine lay almost motionless until Carlsson restored their dopamine with its precursor, L-dopa. In 1960 Oleh Hornykiewicz (1926 to 2020) and his colleague Ehringer reported that the striatum (the caudate nucleus and putamen) of people who had died with Parkinson's disease was severely depleted of dopamine. Put these facts together and a chain of reasoning follows. Parkinson's disease is a shortage of dopamine. Antipsychotic drugs produce a Parkinson-like state. So antipsychotic drugs must reduce dopamine function. And if reducing dopamine function relieves psychosis, perhaps psychosis is a state of too much dopamine. Carlsson shared the 2000 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine with Paul Greengard and Eric Kandel for this line of work.

The next step came in 1963, when Carlsson and Margit Lindqvist measured the breakdown products of dopamine in the brains of mice given chlorpromazine or haloperidol. If the drugs simply emptied the brain of dopamine, as reserpine does, the breakdown products should have fallen. Instead they rose, while the level of dopamine itself stayed about the same. Carlsson and Lindqvist proposed an elegant explanation. The drugs sit on the receptors for dopamine without activating them, a receptor blockade, and the neurons that make dopamine, sensing that their message is not getting through, fire harder and release more, which is then broken down. Chlorpromazine thus became the first antipsychotic known to work by blocking receptors. (Pinel calls it the first receptor blocker ever identified, which overstates the case: curare and atropine had long been known to block acetylcholine receptors.) The theory now had a mechanism: antipsychotics work by blocking dopamine receptors.

A second line of evidence ran in the opposite direction. If blocking dopamine relieves psychosis, raising dopamine ought to cause it, and it does. In 1958 the British psychiatrist Philip Connell published a monograph, Amphetamine Psychosis, describing 42 people who had become psychotic after taking amphetamine, a drug that floods synapses with dopamine and noradrenaline. Their illness looked like acute paranoid schizophrenia: persecutory delusions, ideas of reference and hallucinated voices, usually in clear consciousness (only three of his patients were disoriented), without the confusion of a delirium, and it usually cleared within a week of stopping the drug. Connell noted that the picture was commonly mistaken for paranoid schizophrenia. This is amphetamine psychosis, and in 1970 Burton Angrist and Samuel Gershon reproduced it under supervision, giving repeated doses of amphetamine to volunteers who had used the drug before until paranoid ideas emerged. Two further observations completed the picture. Small doses of stimulants, too small to affect healthy people, can make the symptoms of a person with schizophrenia worse; and L-dopa, the dopamine precursor used to treat Parkinson's disease, can produce hallucinations and paranoid delusions in some of the patients who take it. What none of these drugs reliably does is relieve negative symptoms. They add positive symptoms; they do not restore lost drive or emotion.

Vinod's story shows what this looks like in practice. A 24-year-old who worked night shifts at a call centre in Gurugram, he began buying amphetamine tablets from a colleague to stay awake, and over two months took more and slept less. Then he became certain that his team leader had fitted cameras inside the office lights, and that a white car parked outside his paying-guest accommodation belonged to men sent to follow him. He heard two male voices in the corridor discussing what he was doing ("Now he is checking the window again"). He was fully alert, knew the date and could subtract sevens from a hundred without a slip, and a doctor who saw him without the history might easily have written "paranoid schizophrenia". Within a week of stopping the drug, with a small dose of an antipsychotic and plenty of sleep, the voices had gone and the white car was just a car. The chemistry of his psychosis had been produced, and then reversed, from the outside.

From these clues came the dopamine hypothesis of schizophrenia. In its classic form it is the proposal that the positive symptoms of schizophrenia arise from excessive dopamine activity in the brain, specifically at D2 receptors in the mesolimbic pathway, and that antipsychotic drugs work by blocking those receptors. The receptor and the pathway named in that sentence are the subjects of the next two subsections.

Indian context

The snakeroot plant, sarpagandha in Sanskrit and Hindi, had long been used in India to treat mental illness. In 1931 two Calcutta physicians, the Ayurvedic practitioner Gananath Sen and Kartick Chandra Bose, reported in the Indian Medical World on Rauwolfia serpentina as "a new Indian drug for insanity and high blood pressure", recommending it for insanity with violent symptoms. The work was largely ignored until the Bombay cardiologist Rustom Jal Vakil published his experience of it in high blood pressure in 1949 and reserpine was isolated from the plant in the early 1950s; the American psychiatrist Nathan Kline then reported its calming effect in psychiatric patients in 1954. Reserpine is no longer used for psychosis, because the doses needed lower blood pressure dangerously and can cause severe depression, an observation that later fed the monoamine hypothesis of depression (Chapter 5). But it gave the dopamine story its first chemical clue.

Exam trap

The order matters: the drug came first and the theory afterwards. Chlorpromazine (Delay and Deniker, 1952) was found by clinical observation; the dopamine hypothesis was built in the 1960s and 1970s to explain why it worked. Arvid Carlsson won the Nobel Prize (2000) for showing that dopamine is a neurotransmitter, not for discovering an antipsychotic. Amphetamine psychosis (Connell, 1958) is a paranoid psychosis in clear consciousness, which is exactly why it supports the theory; a confused, disoriented state would point to delirium instead. And the dopamine-raising drugs create or worsen positive symptoms: an option claiming that L-dopa "reliably improves negative symptoms" is wrong.

The key evidence: potency and the D2 receptor

By the early 1970s the dopamine hypothesis was plausible but unproven. The decisive test needed a new technique. In radioligand binding, a drug is labelled with a radioactive atom and mixed with a preparation of brain membranes; the radioactivity left stuck to the membranes after washing tells you how many receptors the drug has occupied, and adding a second, unlabelled drug shows how strongly that drug competes for the same receptors. With this method two laboratories, working independently, asked the same question in 1976. Ian Creese, David Burt and Solomon Snyder at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore, and Philip Seeman and his colleagues in Toronto, measured how strongly each of the antipsychotic drugs then in use competed for dopamine receptors in membranes taken from the striatum, the most dopamine-rich part of the brain, and compared this with how much of each drug doctors needed to give to control psychosis.

Key study: Potency and D2 binding (Seeman and colleagues; Creese, Burt and Snyder, 1976)

Question. If antipsychotics work by blocking dopamine receptors, does a drug's strength at the receptor predict its strength in the clinic? Method. For each drug the researchers measured the concentration needed to displace radioactively labelled haloperidol from dopamine receptors in striatal membranes, and set it against the drug's average effective daily dose in patients, a measure of its clinical potency. Result. Across drugs whose doses differed a hundredfold or more, from a few milligrams of haloperidol a day to several hundred milligrams of chlorpromazine, the two measures lined up closely (Figure 4.13). Binding to other receptors showed no such orderly relation. Conclusion. The clinical action of antipsychotic drugs is tied to blockade of one particular kind of dopamine receptor, later named the D2 receptor. Criticism. A correlation across drugs shows how the drugs work, not what causes the illness, a point taken up in Section 4.8.4.

There had been a puzzle on the way, and its solution is worth knowing. Earlier binding studies that used radioactive dopamine itself as the label found that chlorpromazine competed well but that haloperidol, one of the most potent antipsychotics known, seemed to bind only weakly. The answer was that dopamine acts on more than one kind of receptor. In 1979 John Kebabian and Donald Calne divided them into two families: D1 receptors, which stimulate the enzyme adenylate cyclase inside the cell, and D2 receptors, which do not. Chlorpromazine and the other drugs of its chemical class, the phenothiazines, block both; haloperidol and the other butyrophenones block mainly D2. Since both classes treat psychosis, the receptor that matters must be D2. Five dopamine receptors are now known, grouped as D1-like (D1 and D5) and D2-like (D2, D3 and D4), and until 2024 every drug approved for schizophrenia acted at least partly by blocking, or partly blocking, the D2 receptor.

Binding studies in animal brain show how the drugs work. Do the brains of patients actually contain too much dopamine, or too many D2 receptors? Post-mortem studies of the 1970s and 1980s found more D2 receptors in the striatum of patients, but nearly all of them had taken antipsychotics for years, and blocking a receptor makes the brain build more of it, so the finding could be an effect of the treatment. Positron emission tomography (PET), which images radioactive tracers in the living brain, offered a way round the problem by scanning patients who had never been treated. The results disagreed. In 1986 Dean Wong and his colleagues at Johns Hopkins reported a large increase in D2 receptors in drug-naive patients; in 1990 Lars Farde and his colleagues at the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, using a different tracer, found none. Later pooled analyses settled on a small increase at most. Whatever is wrong, it is not mainly the number of receptors.

The more telling result came from a different design. In 1996 Marc Laruelle and his colleagues gave patients a small dose of amphetamine and imaged a radioactive tracer that competes with dopamine for D2 receptors: the more dopamine the amphetamine released, the more tracer was pushed off the receptors. Patients with schizophrenia released markedly more dopamine than healthy people given the same dose, and the more they released, the more their positive symptoms worsened. The excess, in other words, seemed to lie on the presynaptic side, in how much dopamine the neurons release, rather than in the receptors that receive it. Hold on to that idea; it became the basis of the modern version of the theory (Section 4.8.5).

Exam trap

The original support for the dopamine hypothesis is that the clinical potency of typical antipsychotics correlates with their affinity for D2 receptors (Seeman and colleagues; Creese, Burt and Snyder; both 1976). The primary target of typical antipsychotics is therefore the D2 receptor, not D1, not the serotonin 5-HT2A receptor (the extra target of the atypicals) and not the muscarinic M1 receptor (blocked by some drugs as a side effect, causing dry mouth and constipation). "Potency" means how many milligrams are needed, not how well a drug works: at equivalent doses, high-potency haloperidol and low-potency chlorpromazine are about equally effective. Options such as "all antipsychotics raise serotonin levels" or "twin concordance is 100 percent" are invented distractors.

The four dopamine pathways

If dopamine were spread evenly through the brain, a drug that blocks D2 receptors would do just one thing. It is not. The cell bodies of dopamine neurons are clustered in a few small groups in the midbrain and hypothalamus, and their long axons run to particular targets, so that dopamine travels along a handful of separate pathways, each with its own job. Four of them matter in psychiatry. Because a typical antipsychotic blocks D2 receptors in all four at once, each pathway explains a different part of what the drug does, for good or ill. Learn them as a set; they are among the most frequently examined facts in this chapter.

The mesolimbic pathway runs from the ventral tegmental area (VTA) of the midbrain (the mesencephalon, hence "meso-") to the nucleus accumbens, which is the ventral part of the striatum, and to other limbic structures such as the amygdala and hippocampus. It is the brain's system for reward and motivation, and more precisely for marking which events in the world matter and deserve attention: the smell of food when you are hungry, the voice of someone you love, the sound of a threat. The same pathway is the target of addictive drugs (Chapter 10). In the classic dopamine hypothesis it is overactive in schizophrenia, and its overactivity produces the positive symptoms. Blocking D2 receptors here is the therapeutic action of every antipsychotic. Because the pathway also carries pleasure and drive, blocking it too heavily can dull them, one source of the secondary negative symptoms described in Section 4.3.

The mesocortical pathway also starts in the VTA but projects to the prefrontal cortex, the part of the brain that holds information in mind, plans, switches between tasks and regulates emotion. In the revised hypothesis (Section 4.8.5) this pathway is underactive in schizophrenia, and the shortfall contributes to the negative and cognitive symptoms: poverty of thought and drive, poor working memory, difficulty planning. Blocking D2 receptors here does not help these symptoms and may make them worse.

The nigrostriatal pathway runs from the substantia nigra ("black substance", coloured by the pigment neuromelanin) to the dorsal striatum, the caudate nucleus and putamen ("striatum" means striped body). It is part of the extrapyramidal motor system that starts, smooths and stops voluntary movement, and it is the pathway that degenerates in Parkinson's disease. Blocking D2 receptors here causes the extrapyramidal side effects of antipsychotics: acute dystonia, akathisia and drug-induced parkinsonism within days or weeks and, after months or years of blockade, tardive dyskinesia, thought to arise partly because the long-blocked receptors become supersensitive. Section 4.10 describes these side effects. They are the price of blocking D2 in the wrong place.

The tuberoinfundibular pathway runs from the arcuate nucleus of the hypothalamus (in the region called the tuber cinereum) to the median eminence at the top of the infundibulum, the stalk of the pituitary gland. Its dopamine is released into the small blood vessels that carry it to the anterior pituitary, where it continually holds back the release of the hormone prolactin; dopamine is, in effect, the body's prolactin-inhibiting factor. Block D2 receptors here and the brake comes off. Prolactin rises (hyperprolactinaemia), which can cause milk secretion from the breasts (galactorrhoea), missed periods, breast enlargement in men, sexual dysfunction and, over years, loss of bone density. A fifth, smaller projection to the thalamus is sometimes added, but the four above are the ones examiners ask about. Table 4.30 sets them side by side.

Table 4.30 The four dopamine pathways and what blocking D2 receptors does in each.
PathwayFrom and toNormal jobIn untreated schizophreniaWhen D2 is blocked
MesolimbicVTA to nucleus accumbens (ventral striatum), amygdala, hippocampusReward, motivation, marking what matters (salience)Overactive: positive symptomsPositive symptoms ease; pleasure and drive may be dulled
MesocorticalVTA to prefrontal cortexWorking memory, planning, control of emotionUnderactive: negative and cognitive symptomsNegative and cognitive symptoms may worsen
NigrostriatalSubstantia nigra to dorsal striatum (caudate and putamen)Voluntary movement (extrapyramidal system)Classically normal (but see Section 4.8.5)Extrapyramidal side effects; tardive dyskinesia after long use
TuberoinfundibularHypothalamus (arcuate nucleus) to median eminence and pituitaryHolds back prolactin releaseUsually normalRaised prolactin: galactorrhoea, missed periods, sexual problems

The four pathways explain the central dilemma of the typical antipsychotics. A drug such as haloperidol is a key that fits D2 locks everywhere. It cannot be sent only to the mesolimbic pathway, so at one and the same dose it treats positive symptoms (mesolimbic), may worsen negative and cognitive ones (mesocortical), causes extrapyramidal side effects (nigrostriatal) and raises prolactin (tuberoinfundibular). Much of the history of antipsychotic drug development, told in Section 4.10, is the search for a way out of that dilemma.

Exam trap

Match each pathway to its origin, target and consequence. Mesolimbic: VTA to nucleus accumbens (ventral striatum) and limbic structures; reward; overactive in untreated schizophrenia, producing positive symptoms. Mesocortical: VTA to prefrontal cortex (including the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex); underactive, producing negative and cognitive symptoms. Nigrostriatal: substantia nigra to caudate and putamen; voluntary movement; D2 blockade here gives the extrapyramidal side effects and parkinsonian rigidity. Tuberoinfundibular: hypothalamus to median eminence and pituitary; D2 blockade raises prolactin. The common baits give the mesolimbic pathway the substantia nigra as its origin, or the caudate and putamen as its target (both are nigrostriatal), or the median eminence (tuberoinfundibular). Notice also that underactivity of the mesocortical pathway causes negative symptoms, whereas D2 blockade there worsens them.

What the simple theory could not explain

By the late 1970s the dopamine hypothesis looked unassailable. Within a decade it was in trouble, and it is worth seeing exactly why, because the weaknesses shaped every later theory, and because "evaluate the dopamine hypothesis" is a favourite question in interviews.

The first problem was timing. An antipsychotic blocks D2 receptors within hours of the first dose, yet textbooks long taught that its antipsychotic effect takes two to six weeks to appear. If excess dopamine causes the symptoms, why does removing its effect take so long? Part of the answer turned out to be that the delay had been exaggerated. In 2003 Ofer Agid, Shitij Kapur and their colleagues pooled 42 drug trials with about 7,450 patients and found that improvement is already clear in the first week and is greater in that week than in any later week; the drugs start working quickly, but beliefs held with total conviction take longer to fade. That pattern, as you will see, fits the modern version of the theory better than the old one.

The second problem was non-response. Somewhere between a fifth and a third of people with schizophrenia gain little from typical antipsychotics, even when scans show that their D2 receptors are well blocked. If excess dopamine were the whole story, full blockade should always work. Many of these patients improve on clozapine, which is a relatively weak D2 blocker (Section 4.10). Later PET work deepened the puzzle in an instructive way: in 2012 Arsime Demjaha, Oliver Howes and their colleagues found that patients whose illness had resisted treatment had normal dopamine synthesis, unlike patients who had responded. In some people, it seems, the psychosis is not driven by dopamine, and blocking dopamine achieves little.

The third problem was the negative and cognitive symptoms. Blunted affect, avolition, alogia and the deficits of attention and working memory (Section 4.3) are barely touched by D2 blockade and may be worsened by it, and the drugs that raise dopamine do not produce them. A theory of too much dopamine explains half the illness at best.

The fourth problem was specificity. Dopamine-raising drugs produce a paranoid hallucinatory state, not the full syndrome of schizophrenia with its thought disorder, negative symptoms and decline in functioning. And D2 blockers relieve psychosis wherever it occurs: in mania, in psychotic depression, in delirium and in dementia. The drugs are anti-psychotic, not anti-schizophrenic. Dopamine seemed to be linked to psychosis as such, rather than to schizophrenia in particular.

The fifth problem was the weak direct evidence. Measures of homovanillic acid (HVA), the main breakdown product of dopamine, in cerebrospinal fluid and blood gave inconsistent results; post-mortem increases in D2 receptors were confounded by treatment; and the PET increases in receptors were small. Finally there is a problem of logic that you should be able to state in one sentence: that a drug works by blocking a chemical does not prove that the illness is an excess of that chemical. Aspirin cures a headache, but a headache is not a shortage of aspirin. Table 4.31 brings the case for and against together.

Table 4.31 The case for and against the classic dopamine hypothesis.
ObservationWho and whenVerdict
Antipsychotics cause parkinsonian stiffnessClinicians of the 1950sSupports: the drugs reduce dopamine function
Antipsychotics block dopamine receptors and raise dopamine turnoverCarlsson and Lindqvist, 1963Supports: gives the mechanism
Amphetamine causes paranoid psychosis in clear consciousness; L-dopa can cause hallucinationsConnell, 1958; Angrist and Gershon, 1970Supports
Clinical potency tracks D2 bindingSeeman and colleagues; Creese, Burt and Snyder, 1976Strongest support
Patients release more dopamine after amphetamineLaruelle and colleagues, 1996Supports, but locates the fault before the receptor
D2 is blocked in hours; full recovery takes weeksClinical trialsChallenges (partly answered by Agid and colleagues, 2003)
A fifth to a third do not respond despite D2 blockadeClinical trials; Demjaha and colleagues, 2012Challenges
Clozapine helps resistant illness yet blocks D2 weaklyKane and colleagues, 1988Challenges
Negative and cognitive symptoms barely improveClinical trialsChallenges
D2 receptor numbers barely raised in untreated patientsFarde and colleagues, 1990Challenges the receptor version
Exam trap

The neurotransmitter most implicated in the positive symptoms of schizophrenia is dopamine, acting through the mesolimbic pathway. Serotonin, GABA and acetylcholine all have parts to play (Section 4.8.7), but none is the classic answer. When a question asks for evidence against the dopamine hypothesis, look for the delay in response, the patients who do not respond, clozapine's success despite weak D2 blockade, and the poor effect on negative symptoms. When it asks for the original evidence for it, the best answer is the correlation between clinical potency and D2 binding; amphetamine and L-dopa psychosis and the parkinsonian side effects of antipsychotics are supporting clues.

Rewriting the theory: from receptors to salience

Faced with these problems, researchers did not abandon dopamine. They asked where, and in which direction, it goes wrong. The first answer came in 1991 from Kenneth Davis, René Kahn, G. Ko and Michael Davidson at Mount Sinai in New York, in a review that has come to be called the revised dopamine hypothesis. Schizophrenia, they argued, involves too little dopamine in one place and too much in another. In the prefrontal cortex, fed by the mesocortical pathway, dopamine activity is low, and this cortical deficit produces the negative and cognitive symptoms; it fits the hypofrontality that brain imaging had found in patients (Section 4.7). In subcortical regions, fed by the mesolimbic pathway, dopamine activity is high, and this produces the positive symptoms. The two faults may even be linked. When Pycock, Kerwin and Carter destroyed the dopamine terminals in the prefrontal cortex of rats in 1980, the rats' subcortical dopamine system became more active, as though the cortex normally holds it in check. Later work refined the cortical half of the story: the cortical shortfall seems to involve D1 receptors, the main dopamine receptors of the prefrontal cortex, more than D2.

The second rewriting came from Oliver Howes and Shitij Kapur in a 2009 paper subtitled "version III: the final common pathway". (Version I was the classic receptor hypothesis, version II the revision of Davis and colleagues.) By then a new PET technique had matured. If a person is given a radioactive form of DOPA, the precursor from which neurons make dopamine, the rate at which the striatum takes it up and converts it gives a measure of presynaptic dopamine synthesis capacity: roughly, how much dopamine the nerve terminals are geared to make and release. Study after study found it raised in schizophrenia, and when Howes and his colleagues pooled the PET literature in 2012, the presynaptic abnormality was large and consistent while the change in D2 receptors was small. The excess was also present before the illness began. People at clinical high risk of psychosis (Section 4.5) showed raised synthesis capacity, and it rose further in those who went on to become psychotic.

Version III makes four claims, worth knowing in order. First, many different "hits", genetic and environmental (risk genes, obstetric complications, stimulant and cannabis use, childhood adversity, migration and social defeat, stress), interact and converge on one result, dysregulated dopamine. Second, the main site of the fault is presynaptic, in the dopamine synthesis and release of the striatum, not in the D2 receptors. Third, dopamine dysregulation is the final common pathway to psychosis, not to schizophrenia as a whole, which is why the same drugs work for psychosis in other disorders and why the negative and cognitive symptoms need another explanation. Fourth, current antipsychotics act downstream of the fault: they block the receptors that receive the excess dopamine but do nothing to the overproduction itself, so if the drug is stopped the excess is still there and relapse often follows. The person's psychology and culture, finally, decide what the psychosis is about.

One detail of the new findings will surprise you if you have learned the four pathways well. The striatal excess is greatest not in the limbic (ventral) striatum, the target of the mesolimbic pathway, but in the associative part of the dorsal striatum, a region closely linked to the prefrontal cortex and supplied largely by dopamine neurons in the substantia nigra. Research has, in other words, moved the site of the fault partly away from the classic mesolimbic pathway. For an examination question about the classic dopamine hypothesis, however, the answer remains mesolimbic excess.

How does too much striatal dopamine turn into a delusion? The most influential answer is Kapur's 2003 theory of aberrant salience. Normally, dopamine released in the striatum does not simply carry pleasure; it tags events as salient, as worth noticing and acting on, because they predict something that matters. A hungry person's attention snaps to the smell of food; a parent picks out his own child's cry in a noisy room. Now imagine that dopamine is released chaotically, out of step with events. Ordinary things (a stranger's glance, a number plate, a phrase on the radio, a thought that crosses the mind) suddenly feel charged with significance. This is the strange, uneasy stage that Jaspers called delusional mood and that patients describe as a feeling that something is going on (Section 4.2). The person searches for an explanation, and the delusion is the explanation he arrives at: they are watching me, the radio is sending me messages, I have been chosen. Hallucinations, in Kapur's account, are internal representations, such as inner speech and memories, that acquire the same aberrant salience and are experienced as if they came from outside. The content of the explanation comes from the person's own fears, history and culture, which is why the persecutors of the 1950s worked through radios and those of today work through mobile phones and hidden cameras.

The theory also explains how antipsychotics work, and why the effect looks the way it does. By blocking D2 receptors, the drugs dampen salience. They do not erase a delusion; they take the heat out of it. Patients recovering on medication typically say not "I was wrong" but "It doesn't bother me so much now" or "I don't think about it as much", and only later, as new experience accumulates, does the belief itself fade, which is why full recovery takes weeks even though the drug acts in hours. Dampening salience too much has a cost: if rewards also lose their pull, the person may feel flat, slowed and uninterested, a drug-induced cousin of the negative symptoms. And if the drug is stopped, the aberrant salience returns, which is one reason relapse after stopping medication is so common (Section 4.10). Table 4.32 summarises the three versions of the hypothesis and Kapur's companion theory.

Table 4.32 Three versions of the dopamine hypothesis, and the aberrant salience theory.
VersionWho and whenCore claimKey evidence
I: classicCarlsson; Seeman; Snyder (1960s and 1970s)Too much dopamine activity at D2 receptors, in the mesolimbic pathway, causes the positive symptomsDrug potency and D2 binding; amphetamine psychosis
II: revisedDavis, Kahn, Ko and Davidson, 1991Subcortical excess gives positive symptoms; prefrontal deficit gives negative and cognitive symptomsHypofrontality; animal lesion studies
III: final common pathwayHowes and Kapur, 2009Many hits converge on raised presynaptic dopamine synthesis and release in the striatum, causing psychosis rather than schizophrenia as a wholeDOPA PET; amphetamine challenge; high-risk studies
Aberrant salienceKapur, 2003Dysregulated dopamine makes ordinary things feel significant; delusions are explanations of that feelingPatients' accounts; how antipsychotics act
Clinical link

The aberrant salience idea gives you a way to explain medication to a patient or family without arguing about the delusion. "When the brain is under strain, the chemical that marks things as important can start firing at the wrong times, so ordinary things feel loaded with meaning. The tablet turns that volume down and gives your mind a quieter space to make sense of what has happened." Many patients find this less insulting than being told that their beliefs are false, and it also explains why the tablet should not be stopped as soon as the fear has gone: the volume control is still needed.

Exam trap

Keep the versions apart. The classic hypothesis: hyperactivity of mesolimbic dopamine at D2 receptors produces the positive symptoms. The revised hypothesis (Kenneth Davis and colleagues, 1991): subcortical excess for positive symptoms plus prefrontal (mesocortical) deficit for negative and cognitive symptoms. Version III (Howes and Kapur, 2009): raised presynaptic dopamine synthesis and release in the striatum, the final common pathway to psychosis. Aberrant salience is Kapur's (2003). An option saying that positive symptoms come from "reduced dopamine in the prefrontal cortex only" describes the negative-symptom half of the revised hypothesis, not the classic account of positive symptoms.

The glutamate hypothesis

If dopamine explains the positive symptoms but not the rest, what explains the rest? The most important candidate is glutamate, the main excitatory neurotransmitter of the brain, released by most of the pyramidal neurons of the cortex and hippocampus. Glutamate acts on several kinds of receptor, and the one that matters here is the NMDA receptor, named after N-methyl-D-aspartate, a synthetic chemical that activates it selectively. The NMDA receptor is an unusual lock. It opens only when glutamate binds to it, a second chemical (glycine or D-serine) binds to a separate co-agonist site, and the cell membrane is already partly excited, so that a magnesium ion plugging the channel is pushed out. Because it opens only when several signals coincide, it works as a detector of coincidence, and it is central to learning, memory and the fine timing of cortical circuits.

The clue, once again, came from a drug. Phencyclidine (PCP) was introduced in the late 1950s as a surgical anaesthetic under the trade name Sernyl. In 1959 Elliot Luby and his colleagues gave small doses to volunteers and reported a state they called "schizophrenomimetic": distortions of body image, disorganised thinking, apathy, negativism and withdrawal, as well as hallucinations and paranoid ideas. Patients waking from PCP anaesthesia were often agitated and psychotic, so the drug was withdrawn from human use, to reappear on the street as "angel dust". Ketamine, a shorter-acting relative developed in the 1960s, produced similar but milder effects. In 1983 pharmacologists showed that PCP and ketamine both block the NMDA receptor, plugging its channel.

The decisive argument was made in 1991 by Daniel Javitt and Stephen Zukin. Amphetamine, they pointed out, mimics only the positive symptoms of schizophrenia. PCP mimics the positive symptoms, the negative symptoms, the formal thought disorder and the cognitive deficits, and in people who already have schizophrenia it rekindles their own characteristic symptoms. A drug that blocks NMDA receptors reproduces the whole illness better than any drug that releases dopamine. So perhaps schizophrenia is, at root, a state of reduced NMDA receptor function. This is the glutamate hypothesis, also called the hypothesis of NMDA receptor hypofunction, the phrase John Olney and Nuri Farber used in 1995. Experiments with ketamine soon confirmed the claim under controlled conditions. In 1994 John Krystal and his colleagues at Yale gave healthy volunteers small, sub-anaesthetic infusions of ketamine and recorded suspiciousness, unusual perceptions, conceptual disorganisation, blunted emotion and withdrawal, and poorer performance on tests of attention, memory and frontal-lobe function, all wearing off as the drug cleared. There had been an earlier hint from patients' spinal fluid too: in 1980 Kim, Kornhuber and their colleagues reported low glutamate in the cerebrospinal fluid of people with schizophrenia, although later studies did not consistently confirm it.

Nature has supplied a striking test case. In 2007 Josep Dalmau and his colleagues identified anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis, an autoimmune illness in which the body makes antibodies against its own NMDA receptors, sometimes triggered by a tumour such as an ovarian teratoma. It mostly affects young women and children, and it very often begins psychiatrically, with anxiety and insomnia followed by delusions, hallucinations, agitation or catatonia, so that many patients are first admitted to psychiatric wards. Only later come seizures, abnormal movements, unstable pulse and blood pressure, and falling consciousness. When the antibodies are removed by immunotherapy, the psychosis usually resolves. Here, then, is a psychosis caused by a known loss of NMDA receptor function, exactly as the glutamate hypothesis predicts, and it is one of the medical mimics that must be considered in a first episode (Section 4.4). Genetics points the same way. Among the 108 regions of the genome linked to schizophrenia in 2014 (Section 4.7) were several genes for glutamate receptors and for the enzyme that makes D-serine, along with the gene for the D2 receptor itself.

How could a glutamate fault produce a dopamine excess? The best-supported answer runs through inhibitory neurons. Among the neurons that carry NMDA receptors are fast-firing GABA interneurons, which release gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main inhibitory transmitter, and keep the cortex and hippocampus in rhythm. These interneurons seem especially sensitive to NMDA receptor blockade. If their NMDA receptors underperform, they fire less; the pyramidal neurons they normally restrain are released from inhibition; and cortical activity becomes noisy and poorly timed, a plausible basis for disorganised thinking and failing working memory. In the hippocampus the same disinhibition makes the region overactive, and work in Anthony Grace's laboratory with animal models showed that an overactive hippocampus, through a relay in the basal ganglia, increases the number of VTA dopamine neurons that are ready to fire. The result is the striatal dopamine excess of version III. In this account glutamate is upstream and dopamine downstream: the glutamate and GABA faults would produce the negative and cognitive symptoms directly and the positive symptoms indirectly, through dopamine. Figure 4.14 draws the chain.

If the glutamate hypothesis is right, drugs that boost NMDA receptor function should help, especially with negative and cognitive symptoms. The idea has been tested many times: with the co-agonists glycine and D-serine, with sarcosine and with drugs that slow the removal of glycine from synapses, and with drugs acting on other glutamate receptors. Small early trials looked promising, but large trials have so far been disappointing, and no glutamate drug is yet in routine use. The hypothesis is strong as an explanation and still weak as a guide to treatment.

Exam trap

The glutamate hypothesis proposes hypofunction (underactivity) of NMDA receptors, not "glutamate excess at NMDA receptors". Its evidence is that the NMDA receptor antagonists phencyclidine (PCP) and ketamine produce a schizophrenia-like state in healthy people. Learn the contrast with amphetamine: dopamine-releasing drugs mimic mainly positive symptoms, whereas PCP and ketamine mimic positive, negative and cognitive symptoms (Javitt and Zukin, 1991). Pinel's textbook puts the drug models in one line each: LSD-type drugs model the positive symptoms through serotonin 5-HT2A receptors, ketamine-type drugs model the negative symptoms through glutamate receptors.

Serotonin, GABA and the other transmitters

Serotonin entered the story through another accident. In April 1943 the Swiss chemist Albert Hofmann (1906 to 2008), working at the Sandoz laboratories in Basel, absorbed a trace of lysergic acid diethylamide (LSD), a compound he had synthesised in 1938, and experienced the first LSD trip: vivid colours, shifting shapes, a distorted sense of time and self. Because LSD was found to block some actions of serotonin in tissues outside the brain, Woolley and Shaw suggested in 1954 that disturbed serotonin might underlie mental disorders such as schizophrenia, and through the 1950s LSD was studied as a model psychosis, a drug-made copy of the illness that could be switched on and off in the laboratory. We now know that LSD, psilocybin (from "magic mushrooms") and mescaline (from the peyote cactus) produce their effects by stimulating the serotonin 5-HT2A receptor.

As a model of schizophrenia, LSD is poor. Its hallucinations are mainly visual (colours, patterns, objects that seem to breathe or melt), and it often blends the senses, so that sounds are seen as colours (synaesthesia); the hallucinations of schizophrenia, by contrast, are mainly voices. The LSD user also usually knows that the drug is responsible, whereas insight is typically lost in schizophrenia. Clinicians who knew both conditions rarely confused them, which is a large part of why the serotonin theory of schizophrenia faded in the 1960s.

Serotonin returned in a different role, through the atypical antipsychotics. Clozapine and the drugs that followed it block 5-HT2A receptors more strongly than they block D2 receptors, and in 1989 Herbert Meltzer, with Matsubara and Lee, proposed that a high ratio of 5-HT2A to D2 blockade is what makes a drug "atypical": effective against psychosis with few extrapyramidal side effects. The usual explanation is that serotonin, acting through 5-HT2A receptors, puts a brake on dopamine release. Block that brake while also blocking D2, and dopamine release rises a little in the nigrostriatal pathway (fewer extrapyramidal side effects), in the tuberoinfundibular pathway (a smaller rise in prolactin) and perhaps in the mesocortical pathway (possibly a little help for negative and cognitive symptoms), while D2 blockade in the mesolimbic pathway still controls the positive symptoms. Hence the name serotonin-dopamine antagonists for these drugs. The serotonin route can relieve psychosis on its own in at least one setting: pimavanserin, which acts on 5-HT2A receptors without blocking dopamine, was approved in the United States in 2016 for the hallucinations and delusions of Parkinson's disease psychosis.

Not everyone accepts Meltzer's explanation. In 2001 Shitij Kapur and Philip Seeman argued that atypicality comes instead from the way a drug holds the D2 receptor: clozapine and quetiapine bind loosely and let go quickly, so that natural surges of dopamine can still get through, and amisulpride behaves as an atypical drug even though it barely touches 5-HT2A receptors. Both mechanisms probably contribute. Note also that blocking 5-HT2A is not what causes the weight gain and metabolic problems of drugs such as olanzapine and clozapine; those are linked more to their blockade of histamine H1 and serotonin 5-HT2C receptors (Section 4.10).

GABA has already appeared in the glutamate story. Its own evidence comes mainly from post-mortem studies of the prefrontal cortex, in which David Lewis and his colleagues found reduced levels of GAD67, an enzyme that makes GABA, in the subgroup of fast-firing interneurons that contain the protein parvalbumin. These interneurons pace the fast gamma rhythms (roughly 30 to 80 cycles a second) that bind neurons together while information is held in mind, and gamma rhythms are weak in schizophrenia, which may help to explain the deficits in working memory. Drugs that enhance GABA, the benzodiazepines, calm agitation and are the first treatment for catatonia (the lorazepam challenge of Section 4.5), but they do not treat the core psychosis.

Acetylcholine, finally, has become a treatment target. Some older antipsychotics and clozapine block muscarinic acetylcholine receptors, which causes dry mouth, constipation, blurred vision and, in older people, confusion (Section 4.10). Stimulating certain muscarinic receptors in the brain (the M1 and M4 types), by contrast, damps the release of striatal dopamine indirectly. In September 2024 the United States approved xanomeline combined with trospium (xanomeline stimulates these receptors; trospium, which does not enter the brain, blocks the unwanted effects elsewhere in the body), the first modern drug for schizophrenia that does not act directly on D2 receptors. Notice the final common pathway again: even this new drug works, in the end, by reducing striatal dopamine. People with schizophrenia also smoke far more often and more heavily than the general population, and one suggested reason is that nicotine briefly improves attention and sensory gating through nicotinic acetylcholine receptors.

Where does this leave the chemistry of schizophrenia? No single transmitter explains it. The most defensible picture today is layered. Faults in glutamate signalling and in GABA interneurons disturb the timing of cortical and hippocampal circuits, producing negative and cognitive symptoms directly. These faults, together with stress and the other hits of version III, push striatal dopamine synthesis and release too high, the final common pathway to the positive symptoms. Serotonin and acetylcholine modulate the system and offer further handles for treatment. For an examination question, however, dopamine remains the answer for positive symptoms. Table 4.33 compares the three drug models on which all of these theories were built.

Table 4.33 The three drug models of psychosis.
DrugsMain actionPositive symptomsNegative and cognitive symptomsHow like schizophrenia?
Amphetamine, cocaine, L-dopaRaise dopamine release or supplyYes: persecutory delusions, voicesFewLike acute paranoid schizophrenia, in clear consciousness
Phencyclidine (PCP), ketamineBlock NMDA glutamate receptorsYesYes: blunting, withdrawal, thought disorder, poor working memoryClosest overall copy
