LSD, psilocybin, mescalineStimulate serotonin 5-HT2A receptorsMainly visual hallucinations and illusionsFewPoor copy: visual, insight often kept
Exam trap

The classic dopamine hypothesis says that positive symptoms come from excess dopamine activity in the mesolimbic pathways. Each distractor misplaces a real fact. "Decreased serotonin activity" belongs to the old monoamine theory of depression (Chapter 5). "Excess serotonin at 5-HT2A receptors only" overstates the LSD model; 5-HT2A is the extra target of the atypical drugs, not the cause of schizophrenia. "Decreased GABA activity in the cerebellum" puts a genuine GABA finding in the wrong place (the findings are in the prefrontal cortex; the cerebellum coordinates movement). "Excess acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction" belongs to the synapse between nerve and muscle, the territory of myasthenia gravis and organophosphate poisoning, not to psychosis. Atypical antipsychotics add 5-HT2A blockade to D2 blockade (Meltzer's serotonin-dopamine idea), and the glutamate hypothesis is NMDA hypofunction.

4.9 Psychological, Family and Social ExplanationsHigh yield · 9 Qs

Genes and neurotransmitters tell you why a person is vulnerable to psychosis and what the brain is doing during it. They do not tell you why the illness began in this person in this year, why it returned after a family quarrel, why the voices say what they say, or why the children of migrants in London and Amsterdam fall ill more often than the people of the countries their parents left. For those questions you need psychology and sociology. This section evaluates five kinds of explanation: the psychodynamic theories, which dominated American psychiatry for half a century and are now mainly of historical interest; the cognitive theories, which explain how particular symptoms arise and persist; the family theories, which first blamed parents and then, with expressed emotion, found something real; the theories of stress and vulnerability, which tie the biological and psychological strands together; and the social theories, from labelling to social defeat. Keep one distinction in mind throughout, because the examiners do. Some of these factors are claimed to cause the illness; others only to shape its course. The evidence treats the two claims very differently.

Psychodynamic accounts

For the first two-thirds of the twentieth century, especially in the United States, the most prestigious explanations of schizophrenia were psychoanalytic. They are rarely used in practice today, but the papers still ask who proposed which idea, and the theories are worth understanding as serious attempts to make sense of experiences that can seem senseless.

Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) seldom treated patients with psychosis, and he built his theory largely from a book. In 1903 Daniel Paul Schreber (1842 to 1911), a distinguished German judge who had been appointed presiding judge of a senate of the Saxon court of appeal shortly before his second breakdown, published his Memoirs of My Nervous Illness, a detailed account of his delusions: that God was turning him into a woman by means of "rays" so that he could bear a new race of humans, that his former doctor Paul Flechsig had committed "soul murder" against him, and that the world had come to an end. In 1911 Freud published an analysis of the memoirs, a case of a man he never met. He proposed two ideas. The first concerned the content of paranoia. Schreber's persecutory delusions, Freud argued, were a defence against an unacceptable homosexual wish, transformed in steps: "I (a man) love him" is denied ("I do not love him, I hate him") and then projected ("He hates me"), so that the love returns as persecution. By other transformations the same wish could produce delusions of jealousy, erotomania and grandeur. This part of the theory has found no support and is now of historical interest only.

The second idea concerned psychosis in general and has had more influence. In schizophrenia, Freud suggested, the libido (the energy of attachment) is withdrawn from people and things in the outside world and turned back onto the self, a regression to the earliest, narcissistic stage of development. The world, emptied of emotional investment, seems to end; the self, overcharged, swells into grandiosity and hypochondria. The delusions and hallucinations come later, as a crude attempt to reconnect with the world. "The delusional formation, which we take to be the pathological product," Freud wrote, "is in reality an attempt at recovery, a process of reconstruction." In 1924 he put the contrast between the two great classes of illness in a sentence: neurosis is a conflict between the ego and the id, psychosis a conflict between the ego and the external world. And because people with psychosis had withdrawn their libido from others, Freud believed they could not form a transference to the analyst and so could not be analysed.

Freud's followers disagreed about that last point and tried to adapt the method. Paul Federn (1871 to 1950), a Viennese analyst whose papers were collected as Ego Psychology and the Psychoses (1952), argued that the basic fault in psychosis is not a withdrawal of libido from objects but a weakening of the ego boundaries, the felt edges of the self that separate what belongs to me (my thoughts, my wishes) from what belongs to the world. When the boundary is weak, inner events are experienced as outer ones: a thought is heard as a voice, a wish is felt as an influence from outside. The idea is a remarkably good description of the passivity experiences and thought alienation that Schneider later ranked first (Section 4.1). Federn concluded, unusually for an analyst, that uncovering hidden conflicts was dangerous for such patients, and that therapy should instead support their defences and strengthen the boundary.

In the United States, Harry Stack Sullivan (1892 to 1949) moved the theory from drives to relationships. Working at the Sheppard and Enoch Pratt Hospital near Baltimore in the 1920s, he set up a small ward for young men with schizophrenia, staffed by aides chosen for their warmth and tact, and reported unusually good results. For Sullivan, personality is built out of relationships, and schizophrenia arises when early relationships, above all with the mothering person, are so charged with anxiety that parts of experience cannot be integrated into the self and are dissociated as "not-me". Under later stress, often a crisis of sexuality or failure in adolescence, the dissociated experience breaks through as panic and returns as psychotic symptoms. Because the illness was interpersonal, Sullivan argued, it could be reached through a patient, respectful relationship; his one-genus postulate held that "we are all much more simply human than otherwise". His colleague Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (1889 to 1957) carried the approach into intensive psychotherapy at Chestnut Lodge in Maryland. Her 1948 phrase "schizophrenogenic mother" belongs with the family theories (Section 4.9.3).

Silvano Arieti (1914 to 1981), an Italian-born American psychiatrist, is remembered for his account of the logic of schizophrenic thought in Interpretation of Schizophrenia (1955; second edition 1974). Arieti described the thinking of acute psychosis as palaeologic thinking, an older, more primitive logic that follows the von Domarus principle, named after Eilhard von Domarus, who described it in 1944. Ordinary logic treats two things as identical only when the subjects are identical; palaeologic accepts identity on the basis of a shared predicate. Arieti's best-known example is a patient who reasoned: "The Virgin Mary was a virgin; I am a virgin; therefore I am the Virgin Mary." Arieti also saw schizophrenia as the end of a long psychological story: a child who grows up with a damaged, anxious self-image, whose defences hold for years, and who, faced in early adulthood with a crisis that seems to confirm his worst view of himself, abandons ordinary logic for a private one in which the unbearable can be escaped.

Several later analysts appear in examination questions mainly as names to be matched with ideas. Margaret Mahler (1897 to 1985), the Hungarian-born child analyst of separation-individuation, proposed that the infant passes from a normal autistic phase in the first weeks, through a normal symbiotic phase in which mother and infant are experienced as one, to gradual separation. In a 1952 paper on childhood psychosis she described two failures: an autistic psychosis, in which the child never emerges from the autistic phase, and a symbiotic psychosis, in which the child cannot move beyond symbiosis to separate. Her developmental view of schizophrenia, as it is usually summarised, is that the person remains at the autistic phase and fails to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis with the mother. (Later infant research, notably Daniel Stern's, cast doubt on whether a normal autistic phase exists at all.) Ping-Nie Pao, in Schizophrenic Disorders (1979), traced the illness to an early disturbance in the mother-infant relationship that leaves the person liable, under later conflict, to organismic panic, an overwhelming anxiety that paralyses the ego; the symptoms are then the best adaptation the person can manage to restore a sense of self. Edith Jacobson (1897 to 1978), author of The Self and the Object World (1964), proposed that psychotic regression dissolves the boundary between the mental images of the self and of other people, so that self and object representations fuse. And Melanie Klein (1882 to 1960) traced psychotic anxieties to the paranoid-schizoid position of the first months of life, in which the infant splits its world into wholly good and wholly persecuting parts. Table 4.34 brings the psychodynamic theories together.

Table 4.34 Psychodynamic theories of schizophrenia.
TheoristKey workCore idea
Sigmund FreudSchreber case, 1911; "Neurosis and Psychosis", 1924Libido withdrawn from the world into the self (regression to narcissism); delusions are an attempt at recovery
Paul FedernEgo Psychology and the Psychoses, 1952Weak ego boundaries: inner and outer experience blur
Harry Stack SullivanSheppard Pratt ward, 1920sInterpersonal: overwhelming anxiety in early relationships; dissociated "not-me" returns as psychosis
Frieda Fromm-Reichmann1948 paper; Chestnut LodgeIntensive psychotherapy; the "schizophrenogenic mother"
Silvano ArietiInterpretation of Schizophrenia, 1955Palaeologic thinking: identity from shared predicates (von Domarus principle)
Margaret MahlerChildhood psychosis paper, 1952Fixation at the autistic phase; failure to form an adequate, stable symbiosis (symbiotic psychosis)
Ping-Nie PaoSchizophrenic Disorders, 1979Organismic panic; symptoms as the best possible adaptation
Edith JacobsonThe Self and the Object World, 1964Regression fuses self and object representations
Melanie KleinPaper on schizoid mechanisms, 1946Paranoid-schizoid position: splitting into good and persecuting parts

How have these theories fared? As explanations of cause, badly. None has been supported by evidence that the proposed early experiences come before the illness, and the twin and adoption studies of Section 4.7 show that much of the risk is genetic. As treatments, they were tested and found wanting. In 1968 Philip May reported a trial at Camarillo State Hospital in California in which first-admission patients with schizophrenia were randomly assigned to individual psychotherapy alone, antipsychotic medication alone, psychotherapy with medication, electroconvulsive therapy or milieu care. Psychotherapy alone did no better than milieu care; the two groups that received medication did best; and adding psychotherapy to medication added little. In the Boston Psychotherapy Study of the early 1980s, John Gunderson and his colleagues compared exploratory, insight-oriented psychotherapy with practical, reality-adaptive supportive therapy and found no advantage for the insight-oriented approach. And when Thomas McGlashan followed up the patients who had received intensive psychotherapy at Chestnut Lodge itself, most of those with schizophrenia had done poorly. What survives of the tradition is its humane insistence that psychotic experience has meaning and that the relationship with the patient matters, an insistence that cognitive therapy for psychosis has inherited in a testable form.

Exam trap

"Schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase of development, and the person fails to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis" is Margaret Mahler's developmental theory (IHBAS 2020). The other names in that question have ideas of their own: Silvano Arieti, palaeologic thinking and the von Domarus principle; Ping-Nie Pao, organismic panic; Edith Jacobson, fusion of self and object representations. Paul Federn is weak ego boundaries, Harry Stack Sullivan interpersonal anxiety, and Freud withdrawal of libido with regression to narcissism, delusions being an attempt at recovery (the Schreber case, 1911). Do not confuse Mahler's "autistic phase" with Bleuler's "autism" (withdrawal into an inner world, one of the four As of Section 4.1) or with autism spectrum disorder (Chapter 3).

Cognitive accounts

Cognitive psychology asks a narrower but more answerable question than psychoanalysis. Not "what childhood conflict causes schizophrenia?" but "what goes wrong in perception, reasoning and self-monitoring that lets a particular symptom form and persist?" Since the 1980s, British researchers in particular have built a detailed and testable account of the positive symptoms, and it is the scientific basis of cognitive behavioural therapy for psychosis (Book 2, Section 5.6.6). Here the focus is on the explanations themselves. Consider Gopal.

Gopal is a 38-year-old clerk in a cooperative bank in Madurai. Over the past year he has become convinced that a group of colleagues, led by the new branch manager, is collecting evidence to have him dismissed and arrested. The evidence, as he sees it, is everywhere. When a colleague coughed twice as Gopal walked past, it was a signal to the others. When the manager joked at a staff meeting that "some people here are counting more than money", Gopal knew at once that the remark was aimed at him. When his loan files were moved to another desk, it was proof of the conspiracy, not a reorganisation. At night he hears a man's voice saying, "We have all the papers now." Ask Gopal why these things happen and he is never in doubt: other people are doing them to him.

Listen first to how quickly Gopal reaches his conclusions. One cough becomes a signal; one joke becomes a threat. In 1988 Huq, Philippa Garety and David Hemsley at the Institute of Psychiatry in London measured this tendency with an elegant experiment, the beads task. The participant is shown two jars of beads, one holding 85 beads of one colour and 15 of another, the other with the proportions reversed. The jars are hidden, beads are drawn one at a time from one of them, and the participant may see as many beads as he likes before deciding which jar they come from. Most healthy people ask for several beads. People with delusions typically decide after one or two. This is the jumping-to-conclusions bias: a habit of gathering too little evidence before accepting a conclusion. It is a bias in data gathering, not an inability to reason with probabilities, and it is found in a large proportion of people with delusions and, more weakly, in their relatives and in healthy people prone to unusual beliefs. A person who jumps to conclusions is more likely to accept the first explanation that occurs to him for a strange experience, and less likely to collect the evidence that might dissolve it.

Listen next to how Gopal explains bad events. Every misfortune is caused by someone else, and in particular by other people's intentions towards him. Richard Bentall and his colleagues have studied this as an attributional bias. Healthy people show a mild self-serving bias: they tend to credit themselves for good events and blame circumstances for bad ones. In 1989 Kaney and Bentall found that people with persecutory delusions show an exaggerated externalising bias, blaming bad events on external causes far more than either healthy or depressed people do. Peter Kinderman and Bentall later showed that the bias is, more precisely, a personalising bias: people with persecutory delusions blame other people rather than situations or chance ("the manager moved my files to get me", not "files get moved when a branch reorganises"). Bentall proposed that the style may be defensive, protecting a fragile self-esteem by locating the cause of failure outside the self, at the cost of a world full of persecutors. A rival view, from Daniel Freeman and his colleagues, sees persecutory delusions less as a defence than as a direct expression of anxiety, a threat belief built from worry, low self-esteem and the anomalous experiences of early psychosis. Both camps agree that emotion as well as reasoning drives the delusion.

Gopal also misreads other minds. The manager's joke was in fact aimed at an accountant who had miscounted the cash the week before; everyone else in the room understood that, and Gopal did not. The ability to infer what other people believe, intend and mean, including what they mean when they hint or joke, is called theory of mind (Section 4.3 places it under social cognition). In The Cognitive Neuropsychology of Schizophrenia (1992), the British psychologist Christopher Frith proposed that many symptoms of schizophrenia reflect faults in the brain's capacity to represent mental states, its own and other people's. He sorted them into three kinds. A failure to generate willed actions produces the poverty of action and speech of the negative symptoms. A failure to monitor one's own intentions produces passivity experiences and some hallucinations (below). And a failure to infer other people's intentions produces paranoid delusions and delusions of reference: if you cannot tell what others mean, you may assume they mean you harm, or that their words and gestures are about you. Corcoran, Mercer and Frith tested the third claim in 1995 with a hinting task: short stories in which one character drops a hint (a man late for an interview tells his wife, "I want to wear that blue shirt, but it's very creased") and the participant is asked what the character really means. People with schizophrenia, especially those with paranoid or negative symptoms, were poor at taking hints.

Frith's second claim explains some of the strangest symptoms of all, thought insertion and the experience of being controlled (Section 4.1). Each time you move, your brain sends a copy of the motor command to the sensory systems, predicting the sensation the movement will produce; when the sensation arrives as predicted, it is muted and labelled as self-caused. This self-monitoring is why you cannot tickle yourself. Frith proposed that in passivity experiences the prediction fails, so that actions and thoughts the person has generated feel as if they come from elsewhere. In 2000 Sarah-Jayne Blakemore, Frith and their colleagues tested the idea with a device that stroked the palm, worked either by the participant or by the experimenter. Healthy people rated self-produced touch as much less intense and tickly than touch produced by someone else. Patients with auditory hallucinations or passivity experiences did not: their own touch felt as intense as another person's, just as the theory predicts.

A related account explains the voices. Hearing a voice in an empty room is, on this view, a failure of source monitoring (Bentall in 1990 called it a failure of reality discrimination): the person's own inner speech, the silent talking to oneself that everyone does, is produced but not recognised as self-produced, and so is experienced as an external voice. Two lines of evidence support it. People who hallucinate are more likely than others to misattribute their own words or thoughts to an outside source in laboratory memory tasks; and in 1993 Philip McGuire, Shah and Robin Murray showed with brain imaging that Broca's area, the region of the left frontal lobe that produces speech, is active while patients are hearing voices. The voice, in other words, is speech the person is generating without knowing it. Its content is not random either. It usually echoes the person's own fears, guilt and preoccupations, which is why Gopal's voice talks about papers and arrest.

Cognitive theory has turned to the negative symptoms as well. Aaron T. Beck (1921 to 2021), whose cognitive therapy is taught in Book 2, and Paul Grant showed in 2009 that people with schizophrenia who hold defeatist beliefs about performance ("If I cannot do something well, there is little point in doing it at all"; "If I fail partly, it is as bad as being a complete failure") show more avolition and social withdrawal, and that these beliefs partly carry the link between cognitive impairment and poor everyday functioning. A person who has failed repeatedly expects to fail again, stops trying and withdraws, and the withdrawal looks like a pure deficit even when part of it is a learned, and therefore changeable, expectation.

These findings were drawn together in 2001 by Garety, Elizabeth Kuipers, David Fowler, Daniel Freeman and Paul Bebbington in a cognitive model of positive symptoms, whose use in therapy is described in Book 2, Section 5.6.6. In outline: a biological and psychological vulnerability, triggered by stress, produces anomalous experiences, such as things that seem strangely significant, thoughts that seem alien and perceptions that seem altered. These are, in the language of Section 4.8.5, experiences of aberrant salience. The person then searches for an explanation, and here psychology decides the outcome. Emotion (anxiety, depression, low self-esteem), reasoning biases (jumping to conclusions, an externalising and personalising attributional style, poor theory of mind) and the social context (isolation, adversity, a threatening environment) all push towards an explanation that is external and threatening: someone is doing this to me. Once formed, the belief is kept alive by selective attention to confirming evidence, by worry and by safety behaviours that prevent it from ever being disconfirmed. The model is the meeting point of the biology of Section 4.8 and the psychology of this one. Table 4.35 summarises the cognitive findings.

Table 4.35 Cognitive biases and deficits, and the symptoms they help to explain.
Bias or deficitWho and whenHow it is shownSymptom it helps explain
Jumping to conclusionsHuq, Garety and Hemsley, 1988Beads task: decision after one or two beadsDelusions form fast and resist evidence
Externalising and personalising attributionsKaney and Bentall, 1989; Kinderman and BentallBad events blamed on other peoplePersecutory delusions
Theory of mind deficitFrith, 1992; Corcoran, Mercer and Frith, 1995Hinting taskParanoia, delusions of reference
Failure of self-monitoringFrith; Blakemore and colleagues, 2000Self-produced touch not mutedPassivity, thought insertion
Failure of source monitoringBentall, 1990; McGuire, Shah and Murray, 1993Own words misattributed; Broca's area activeAuditory hallucinations
Defeatist beliefsGrant and Beck, 2009Belief questionnairesAvolition, asociality
Clinical link

You will rarely change a belief like Gopal's by arguing with it; confrontation usually strengthens it. What the cognitive account offers instead is a set of gentler targets. You can slow the jump to a conclusion ("What else might a cough mean?"), widen the search for evidence, and test the belief in small experiments that Gopal agrees to, while also attending to the anxiety and low self-esteem that feed it. That is the logic of cognitive behavioural therapy for psychosis, an addition to antipsychotic medication, never a replacement for it (Book 2, Section 5.6.6).

Exam trap

Match each finding to its research group. The jumping-to-conclusions bias and the beads task: Garety and Hemsley's group (Huq, Garety and Hemsley, 1988). The externalising and personalising attributional biases of persecutory delusions: Richard Bentall and his colleagues. Theory of mind deficits and the failure of self-monitoring behind passivity experiences: Christopher Frith (1992), not Uta Frith, whose theory of mind research concerns autism. The cognitive model of positive symptoms: Garety and colleagues (2001). The "jumping to conclusions" of Beck's and Burns's cognitive therapy (arbitrary inference, mind reading and fortune telling, Book 2, Chapter 5) is a related everyday error, not the beads-task bias itself.

Family theories: from blame to verdict

Between the late 1940s and the early 1960s a group of gifted clinicians proposed that schizophrenia is caused by disturbed family relationships. Book 2, Section 7.2 tells their story at length, because the same researchers went on to found family therapy; here you need the theories in outline and, above all, the verdict. Table 4.36 summarises them.

The first and most notorious was the schizophrenogenic mother described by Frieda Fromm-Reichmann in 1948: a cold, dominating, rejecting yet overprotective mother whose treatment of the child was said to produce the illness. The second, and the most often examined, was the double bind proposed in 1956 by the anthropologist Gregory Bateson (1904 to 1980) with Don Jackson, Jay Haley and John Weakland in Palo Alto, California. A double bind is a repeated pattern in which a person who depends on someone receives two contradictory messages at different levels (usually a verbal message contradicted by tone, posture or gesture), cannot comment on the contradiction, and cannot leave the relationship, so that every response is wrong. Bateson's own illustration is the one examiners quote. A young man, fairly well recovered from an acute schizophrenic episode, was visited in hospital by his mother. He impulsively put his arm round her shoulders, and she stiffened. He withdrew his arm, and she asked, "Don't you love me any more?" He blushed, and she said, "Dear, you must not be so easily embarrassed and afraid of your feelings." He could stay with her only a few minutes more, and after she left he assaulted an aide and was put in the isolation room. The group offered the episode as an example of the symptomatic double bind, the double bind as a supposed cause of symptoms.

At Yale, Theodore Lidz and his colleagues (1957) described two patterns of marriage in the families of young patients: marital schism, in which the parents are in chronic open conflict and compete for the child's loyalty, and marital skew, in which one parent's serious disturbance dominates the family while the other passively accepts it and pretends that all is well. At the National Institute of Mental Health, Lyman Wynne and his colleagues (1958) described pseudomutuality, a façade of family harmony that hides emotional distance and leaves no room for a separate identity, and Wynne later worked with Margaret Singer on communication deviance, a measurable tendency of parents to speak in vague, fragmented or confusing ways that make it hard for a listener to share a focus of attention. R. D. Laing went furthest, describing the "madness" of patients as an intelligible response to impossible family situations (Section 4.9.6).

Table 4.36 Family theories of schizophrenia and their status (taught at length in Book 2, Section 7.2).
ConceptWho and whenCore ideaStatus today
Schizophrenogenic motherFrieda Fromm-Reichmann, 1948A cold, dominating, rejecting yet overprotective mother causes schizophreniaDiscredited
Double bindBateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland, 1956Repeated contradictory messages at two levels, no comment and no escape allowedNot a cause; seminal for family therapy
Marital schism and skewTheodore Lidz and colleagues, 1957Warring parents competing for the child; or one dominant disturbed parent and a passive partnerNot a cause
PseudomutualityLyman Wynne and colleagues, 1958A false front of harmony that forbids a separate identityNot a cause
Communication devianceWynne and Margaret Singer, 1960sVague, fragmented parental communicationA modest risk marker, partly genetic
Expressed emotionGeorge Brown and colleagues, 1959 to 1972; Vaughn and Leff, 1976Critical, hostile or over-involved relatives raise the risk of relapseRobust predictor of relapse, not of onset

Did any of these patterns cause schizophrenia? The verdict of half a century of research is no. The studies that proposed them were based on small numbers of families, seen after the illness had begun, usually without comparison groups and with no way of telling whether a parent's odd behaviour was a cause of the child's illness, a response to it or a sign of a shared genetic vulnerability. Double binds proved impossible to measure reliably and turned out to occur in all families. Communication deviance did predict later illness in prospective work, most notably in Michael Goldstein's UCLA High-Risk Project, which followed troubled but non-psychotic adolescents for about fifteen years, but the samples were small, and communication deviance is itself commoner in parents who carry some of the genetic liability. The most convincing evidence that family environment matters at all comes from Pekka Tienari's Finnish adoption study (Section 4.7), in which the adopted-away children of mothers with schizophrenia were at raised risk mainly when they grew up in disturbed adoptive families: a gene-environment interaction, not a family cause. The causal theories did real harm by adding guilt to the burden of parents, and they are now taught partly as a warning. But one strand of family research survived, because it changed the question from what causes schizophrenia to what makes it come back.

Exam trap

The double bind (Gregory Bateson, with Jackson, Haley and Weakland, 1956) was an early family-communication theory of the cause of schizophrenia, not of phobias, depression, autism, PTSD or bipolar disorder. The mother who stiffens at her son's embrace and then asks "Don't you love me any more?" illustrates the symptomatic double bind; "cybernetic system" (a family seen as a self-regulating system steered by feedback loops) and "homeostatic system" (Don Jackson's idea that a family resists change to keep its balance, so that one member's symptom may hold the family steady) are general systems ideas (Book 2, Section 7.1), and incongruence is Satir's and Rogers's word (Book 2, Section 7.6). The double bind is not Kraepelin's, Schneider's or Aaron Beck's. An option printed "Wayne" is almost certainly Lyman Wynne (pseudomutuality), and one printed "Richmond Fromm" is probably a garbled Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (the schizophrenogenic mother); neither proposed first-rank symptoms (Schneider) or coined ambivalence (Bleuler). All of these family causes are now discredited.

Expressed emotion and relapse

The research that survived began, as Book 2, Section 7.2.4 describes in detail, with an unexpected observation by the London sociologist George Brown. In 1959 he reported that men with schizophrenia discharged from hospital to live with their parents or wives relapsed more often than those discharged to lodgings or to live with brothers and sisters. Something about close family life was risky. To find out what, Brown and his colleagues developed a long, semi-structured, audio-recorded interview with the key relative, the Camberwell Family Interview (named after the district of south London where the research was done), and rated not only what the relative said about the patient but how it was said. From the ratings came the construct of expressed emotion (EE): the emotional climate of a home as revealed by the attitudes a key relative expresses about the patient.

A relative is rated high in EE on the basis of three components. Critical comments are remarks that express dislike or disapproval of something the patient does, judged by content or by tone ("She just sits in her room all day; she is plain lazy"). Hostility is criticism generalised from what the patient does to who the patient is, or outright rejection ("She has always been selfish; I wish she would go"). Emotional over-involvement is exaggerated, intrusive or self-sacrificing concern, dramatic distress and an inability to let the patient do anything alone ("I have not slept properly since she fell ill; I check on her every hour"). The interview also rates warmth and positive remarks, but these are not part of the high-EE index. In the version used by Christine Vaughn and Julian Leff, a relative is rated high in EE if he or she makes six or more critical comments, shows any hostility or shows marked over-involvement.

The key findings come from three London studies. In 1972 Brown, James Birley and John Wing showed that patients returning to high-EE homes were roughly three to four times as likely to relapse in the nine months after discharge as those returning to low-EE homes. In 1976 Christine Vaughn and Julian Leff replicated the finding and identified two protective factors within high-EE homes: regular maintenance medication, and less than about 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact with the high-EE relative. Patients in high-EE homes with neither protection relapsed almost without exception (about nine in ten in the pooled figures), whereas patients in low-EE homes relapsed at about one in eight; Book 2, Figure 7.2 shows the full pattern. The finding has since been replicated around the world. A meta-analysis of 27 studies by Ronald Butzlaff and Jill Hooley (1998) confirmed that high EE robustly predicts relapse in schizophrenia, and showed that it predicts relapse even more strongly in mood and eating disorders.

Three further findings shape how EE is understood today. First, EE predicts relapse, not onset. It is measured in families after the illness has begun, and nothing suggests that low-EE families protect anyone from developing schizophrenia in the first place. Second, the influence runs in both directions. Hooley's work showed that critical relatives tend to believe that the patient could control the symptoms ("she could get up if she wanted to"), while low-EE relatives see the same behaviour as part of the illness; and a more disturbed or more difficult patient provokes more criticism, which in turn raises stress and the risk of relapse. Third, the effect depends on culture. In a study of Mexican-American families in Los Angeles, Steven López and his colleagues (2004) found that family warmth predicted fewer relapses: warmth, though not a high-EE component, can be actively protective.

Shabnam's family shows what the components sound like at home. She is 29 and lives in Lucknow with her widowed mother and her elder brother, and she has had two episodes of schizophrenia. Since the second, she spends most of the day in her room, speaks little and has stopped helping in the house. Her brother, who runs the family's shop, says: "There is nothing wrong with her now; the doctor said the voices have gone. She is simply lazy, and she is making fools of us in front of the neighbours." Her mother, by contrast, has given up her own outings to stay at home with Shabnam, sleeps beside her, will not let her go to the market alone and weeps whenever she talks about the future. On the Camberwell Family Interview the brother would score high for critical comments and the mother for emotional over-involvement, and either would make the household high in EE. Shabnam's psychologist does not tell the family that they caused her illness. She explains that the withdrawal is part of the illness, not laziness; she helps the mother to take a few hours a day for herself; she arranges for Shabnam to attend a day centre five mornings a week, which cuts face-to-face contact in a way that suits everyone; and she checks that Shabnam is taking her tablets. These are the elements of family psychoeducation, a treatment shown in controlled trials to reduce relapse, taught in Book 2, Section 7.2.5 and placed among the treatments of schizophrenia in Section 4.10.

Indian context

Expressed emotion research has an important Indian chapter. As part of the World Health Organization's studies of the outcome of schizophrenia, Narendra Nath Wig, Julian Leff and their colleagues measured EE among the relatives of patients with a first episode of schizophrenia in and around Chandigarh, publishing in 1987 and 1990. The Chandigarh relatives made far fewer critical comments, fewer positive remarks and showed less over-involvement than relatives in Aarhus in Denmark (who closely resembled British relatives), and far fewer of them were rated high in EE than in a London sample. Within Chandigarh, city dwellers scored higher than villagers on every component except over-involvement, and at the one-year re-interview not a single rural relative was rated high in EE. Where EE was high, it still predicted relapse in the first year, and hostility was the component most clearly linked to relapse, over two years as well as one (over two years the overall EE index no longer predicted relapse, but hostility still did). The researchers concluded that much of the better outcome of the Chandigarh patients, compared with a London sample, was due to the lower proportion of high-EE relatives, one of the explanations offered for the better course of schizophrenia in India found by the WHO studies (Section 4.6). Why might Indian relatives be less critical? Suggested reasons include an extended family that spreads the burden and dilutes contact with any one relative, and a tendency to attribute the illness to external causes (fate, stress, spirits, the evil eye) rather than to the patient's character, which, in the light of Hooley's findings, leaves less room for blame. A caution runs the other way: an Indian mother who feeds her ill son and goes everywhere with him may look over-involved on a London scoring manual and be entirely ordinary in her own culture.

Exam trap

Expressed emotion is a family environment with emotionally loaded communication of a critical, hostile or over-involved kind; it is not a patient who has difficulty talking about feelings, not a family full of overwhelmingly positive comments and not a lack of emotion in the family. Its three components are critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement; warmth and positive regard are rated but are not high-EE dimensions. High EE predicts relapse of psychotic illness, not its onset, negative symptoms or violence, and "poor treatment outcome" is too vague to be the best answer. Reducing contact hours (below about 35 a week) is a strategy for managing high EE, not for interpersonal violence, parasuicide risk or stubbornness in children. Names: Brown, Birley and Wing (1972); Vaughn and Leff (1976); the Camberwell Family Interview.

Life events, stress and the vulnerability-stress model

Expressed emotion is one kind of stress. Do other stresses matter too? The classic study is again from London. In 1968 Brown and Birley studied patients whose first episode or relapse could be dated to within a week, and asked them and their relatives about events in the preceding months, counting only life events that could not have been caused by the illness itself (a death in the family, a burglary, an eviction ordered by a landlord). The events clustered in the three weeks before onset, at a rate well above that of a healthy comparison group over the same period. A World Health Organization study published in 1987 (Day and colleagues) found the same concentration of life events before episodes in centres in several countries. Life events seem to act as triggers, bringing forward an episode in a person who was already vulnerable, rather than as causes of the illness in someone who was not. The design has a weakness you should be able to name: the events are reported after the episode, and memory can be biased by the illness and by the family's search for an explanation.

The obvious way to combine vulnerability and stress is the vulnerability-stress model, taught in Book 2, Section 1.7. Its logic goes back to Paul Meehl's 1962 theory of schizotaxia, an inherited defect of neural integration that develops into a schizotypal personality and, under adverse conditions, into schizophrenia, and its best-known form comes from Joseph Zubin and Bonnie Spring (1977). Each person, they argued, has a threshold of vulnerability, inborn or acquired; an episode occurs when stress pushes the person over that threshold, and it ends when the stress subsides, so that what is permanent is the vulnerability, not the illness. Applied to schizophrenia, the model makes a practical prediction: anything that lowers stress or raises the threshold should reduce relapse, and anything that adds stress should increase it.

Keith Nuechterlein and Michael Dawson turned the model into a working map of relapse in 1984, and its later versions underlie much of modern psychosocial care (Figure 4.15). On one side are enduring vulnerability characteristics: in the 1984 version, a reduced capacity to process information, heightened physiological (autonomic) reactivity to stress and limited social competence and coping; later versions add genetic liability and dopamine dysregulation. On another are environmental stressors: a critical or emotionally over-involved relative, stressful life events, an overstimulating social environment. On a third are protective factors, some personal (antipsychotic medication, coping skills) and some environmental (effective family problem solving, supportive services). When stressors outweigh protection in a vulnerable person, transient intermediate states develop: information-processing overload, raised arousal, misreading of social cues. These show as the prodromal signs of relapse (poor sleep, tension, irritability, growing suspicion) and, if nothing intervenes, the psychotic symptoms return. The symptoms then feed back into the environment, because a relapse is itself a stress for the family.

Read the figure and you can see why the treatments of schizophrenia take the shape they do. Medication works on the vulnerability side, damping the dopamine dysregulation. Family psychoeducation lowers EE and improves problem solving. Day centres, supported work and reduced contact hours cut stressors and overstimulation. And teaching patients and families to recognise the early warning signs of relapse catches the prodromal stage, when a temporary increase in medication or support can prevent a full episode. Max Birchwood and his colleagues showed in the late 1980s that most relapses are preceded by days or weeks of such non-psychotic changes, and that each person tends to have a characteristic pattern of them, a "relapse signature". Each treatment plugs a different gap in the same model.

How does stress get into the brain? Elaine Walker and Donald Diforio proposed in 1997 a neural diathesis-stress model in which the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis is the link. Stress raises cortisol; cortisol increases dopamine activity, especially in the striatum; and people vulnerable to schizophrenia may have a heightened HPA response, while hippocampal abnormalities weaken the brakes on the axis. A stressful home or a run of life events could therefore raise striatal dopamine in exactly the system that Section 4.8 implicated in psychosis. The model also suggests why late adolescence and early adulthood, when cortisol output naturally rises, are the years of greatest risk. Stress at the other end of life, in childhood, fits the same picture. Adverse childhood experiences such as abuse, neglect and bullying are among the best-documented environmental risk factors for psychosis: a meta-analysis by Filippo Varese and colleagues (2012) found that people who had suffered childhood adversity had nearly three times the odds of developing psychosis, and DSM-5-TR notes that the severity of positive and negative symptoms tracks the severity of earlier trauma and neglect.

Exam trap

A question describing "a predisposition set off by a stressor" is asking for the vulnerability-stress (diathesis-stress) model (Meehl, 1962; Zubin and Spring, 1977), not for a single cause such as "genes only", "family only" or "stress alone". In schizophrenia, stress (life events, high EE) is a trigger of episodes and relapses, not a sufficient cause in a person without vulnerability. Schizotaxia, schizotypy and schizophrenia are Meehl's three terms; schizotypal personality disorder itself is in Chapter 11. Keep the two Brown studies apart: Brown and Birley (1968) is the life events study; Brown, Birley and Wing (1972) is the expressed emotion study.

Social explanations: labelling, antipsychiatry, the city and social defeat

The last group of explanations looks beyond the family to society. The most radical of them, popular in the 1960s, questioned whether schizophrenia is an illness at all. The psychiatrist Thomas Szasz called mental illness a myth (1961), and the sociologist Thomas Scheff argued in Being Mentally Ill (1966) that much of what is called mental illness is "residual rule-breaking" that becomes a stable career once society labels it, the core of labelling theory; Rosenhan's pseudopatient study (1973) seemed to dramatise the point. These ideas are evaluated in Book 2, Section 1.6. The Scottish psychiatrist Ronald David Laing (1927 to 1989) argued in The Divided Self (1960) that schizophrenia grows out of a basic "ontological insecurity", a precarious sense of being a real, whole and continuous person, and in Sanity, Madness and the Family (1964, with Aaron Esterson) he presented eleven families of women diagnosed with schizophrenia in which, he claimed, the patient's experiences became intelligible once the family's patterns of communication were understood. Laing saw psychosis as a potentially meaningful journey and, at Kingsley Hall in London from 1965, let people live through it with little or no medication. The loose movement against conventional psychiatry became known as antipsychiatry, a word coined by David Cooper in 1967.

Taken as causal theories, these ideas fail on the same evidence that sank the family theories: the genetic findings, the brain changes present at the first episode and the strong effect of antipsychotic drugs are hard to square with an illness that is only a label or only a family's way of talking. But labelling theory contains a truth that has survived. A diagnosis of schizophrenia carries a heavy stigma, and the modified labelling theory developed by Bruce Link and his colleagues from 1989 showed that people who expect to be devalued and rejected once labelled tend to withdraw, conceal their illness and lose social support, which harms its course. Laing's insistence on listening to patients' experience lives on, too, in cognitive therapy and in the service user and survivor movements.

The most active social research today is epidemiological, and it asks why schizophrenia is commoner in some social groups than in others. Three findings stand out. The first is urbanicity: being born or brought up in a city is associated with higher risk, in a dose-response fashion (the larger the city and the more years of childhood spent there, the higher the risk). Whether this reflects social causation or social drift, the debate that began with Faris and Dunham's maps of Chicago in 1939, is discussed in Section 4.6 and Book 2, Section 1.7; the fact that the risk attaches to where a person was born and raised, before any illness could cause a downward drift, favours some causal role for the urban environment. The second is migration. A meta-analysis by Elizabeth Cantor-Graae and Jean-Paul Selten (2005) found that first-generation migrants had about 2.7 times the risk of schizophrenia of the native-born population, and the children of migrants about 4.5 times, with the highest figures for migrants from developing countries and from countries where most of the population is black. The third is the ethnic density effect: members of minority groups are at higher risk when they live in neighbourhoods with few people of their own ethnicity, and at lower risk when they live among many, a pattern DSM-5-TR now describes and links to discrimination or fear of it, social isolation, weaker social support and the lack of shared, normalising explanations for unusual experiences.

What links cities, migration and minority status? Selten and Cantor-Graae proposed the social defeat hypothesis: the long-term experience of being an outsider, of being excluded, discriminated against or held in a subordinate position, sensitises the brain's mesolimbic dopamine system, as chronic social defeat has been shown to do in animals, and so raises the risk of psychosis. The hypothesis is attractive because it joins the social and the biological in one chain, from humiliation to dopamine to aberrant salience, the chain of Figure 4.14; it would also explain why raised risk has been reported in other groups who often meet exclusion, such as people with hearing impairment and people who were bullied as children. It remains a hypothesis, and the size of the migrant effect must be read with DSM-5-TR's caution that some groups are more likely than others to be misdiagnosed or overdiagnosed with schizophrenia.

Exam trap

Keep the social theorists and their ideas apart: Thomas Szasz, the myth of mental illness (1961); Thomas Scheff, labelling theory and residual rule-breaking (1966); R. D. Laing, The Divided Self (1960) and schizophrenia as an intelligible response to an unliveable situation; David Cooper, who coined "antipsychiatry" (1967); Faris and Dunham (1939), schizophrenia concentrated in the poor centre of Chicago, the start of the social causation versus social drift debate; Jean-Paul Selten, social defeat. None of these is a sufficient cause of schizophrenia, and no psychological, family or social approach displaces antipsychotic medication as the front-line treatment (Section 4.10); CBT, family psychoeducation, social skills training and stress management are adjuncts.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Carlsson and Lindqvist (1963) proposed that chlorpromazine acts as a dopamine receptor .
  2. The original support for the dopamine hypothesis was that the clinical potency of typical antipsychotics correlates with their affinity for receptors.
  3. The dopamine pathway from the ventral tegmental area to the nucleus accumbens, overactive in untreated schizophrenia, is the pathway.
  4. Blocking D2 receptors in the pathway raises prolactin.
  5. Howes and Kapur (2009) described dopamine dysregulation as the final common to psychosis.
  6. Phencyclidine and ketamine produce positive and negative symptoms by blocking the glutamate receptor.
  7. Atypical antipsychotics add blockade of the serotonin receptor to D2 blockade.
  8. The theory that the person remains at the autistic phase and fails to form an adequate, stable symbiosis was proposed by .
  9. In the beads task, people with delusions decide after very few beads, showing the bias.
  10. The three components of high expressed emotion are critical comments, hostility and .

Answers: (1) blocker, (2) D2, (3) mesolimbic, (4) tuberoinfundibular, (5) pathway, (6) NMDA, (7) 5-HT2A, (8) Margaret Mahler, (9) jumping-to-conclusions, (10) emotional over-involvement.

4.10 Treating Schizophrenia: Antipsychotic Drugs and BeyondHigh yield · 11 Qs

Picture a young man brought to a district hospital in Karnataka by his frightened parents late one night. For three months Prabhu, a 19-year-old diploma student, has heard two voices discussing him, has become certain that the neighbours are putting chemicals in the family's water tank, and has stopped going to college. Tonight he locked himself in his room with a kitchen knife, shouting that he would not let them poison him. What should be done for him? Had he arrived in 1930, the honest answer would have been: keep him safe behind a locked door and hope. Today the answer begins with a medicine that, for most people, quietens the voices and loosens the grip of the false beliefs within weeks. It does not end there. The same medicine acts on parts of the brain that have nothing to do with psychosis, and those actions produce side effects that every clinician must recognise on sight. And a medicine alone does not return a person to college, to work or to family life. This section follows the drugs from their accidental discovery to the clinic, explains how they work and how they harm, and then outlines the other physical and psychosocial treatments that complete care. The neurochemistry behind the drugs (the four dopamine pathways, the binding studies and the glutamate and serotonin hypotheses) is explained in Section 4.8; here you will apply it at the bedside.

Before 1952, and the accidental discovery of chlorpromazine

In the first half of the twentieth century the great mental hospitals of Europe, America and India were filled with people whom Kraepelin would have called cases of dementia praecox, many of whom stayed for decades (the story of the asylums is told in Book 2, Section 1.2). Psychiatry had little to offer them, and the physical treatments it invented in the 1930s were bold, crude and often dangerous. Manfred Sakel (1900 to 1957), a Vienna-trained physician, developed insulin shock treatment from 1927 and began applying it to schizophrenia in Vienna in 1933. In insulin coma therapy the blood sugar was driven down until the patient became comatose, day after day for weeks. Some patients improved, some suffered prolonged coma, brain damage or death, and when a controlled trial was finally run in the 1950s (Ackner, Harris and Oldham, 1957), patients who received the same intensive care but were put into unconsciousness with barbiturates instead of insulin did about as well. A year after Sakel, the Hungarian psychiatrist Ladislas Meduna (1896 to 1964) reasoned, wrongly, that schizophrenia and epilepsy rarely occur in the same person, so that an artificial seizure might drive out psychosis. In January 1934 he began inducing seizures with injections of camphor and then of pentylenetetrazol (Metrazol). In 1938 in Rome, Ugo Cerletti (1877 to 1963) and Lucio Bini (1908 to 1964) produced the seizure more safely with an electric current, and electroconvulsive therapy was born; it survives today, much modified, and is discussed in Section 4.10.8 and in Chapter 5. The most drastic step was surgical. The Portuguese neurologist António Egas Moniz (1874 to 1955) persuaded the neurosurgeon Almeida Lima in 1935 to cut the white matter connecting the prefrontal lobes with the rest of the brain, and Moniz received the Nobel Prize for this prefrontal leucotomy in 1949. Tens of thousands of people with schizophrenia were lobotomised before the operation fell out of use.

What replaced all of these was a drug discovered by a surgeon who was not looking for a psychiatric medicine at all. The French firm Rhône-Poulenc had been making antihistamines from a family of chemicals called phenothiazines, and one of them, promethazine, turned out to be strongly sedating. Henri Laborit (1914 to 1995), a French naval surgeon, was searching for ways to protect patients from surgical shock, and he used such drugs in a "lytic cocktail" that put patients into a state he called artificial hibernation. He asked the company for a compound with stronger effects on the brain, and in December 1950 its chemist Paul Charpentier synthesised a new phenothiazine, RP 4560: chlorpromazine. In 1951 Laborit tried it at the Val-de-Grâce military hospital in Paris and noticed something curious. His patients were not asleep, yet before their operations they lay calm and strangely detached, indifferent to what was about to happen to them. He suggested that psychiatrists try it. When a psychiatrist volunteered to take an intravenous dose, she felt the same indifference, but she fainted when she stood up to walk: you will meet that side effect, a fall in blood pressure on standing, in Section 4.10.5. In January 1952 a 24-year-old manic patient in Paris, given chlorpromazine together with other drugs and ECT, settled dramatically and went home three weeks later.

The decisive step was taken a few kilometres away at the Sainte-Anne Hospital in Paris. The psychiatrists Jean Delay (1907 to 1987) and Pierre Deniker (1917 to 1998) gave chlorpromazine on its own, in larger doses, to psychotic patients and published their first reports in 1952, describing thirty-eight patients. The effect went beyond sedation. Agitated, shouting patients became calm within days; over the following weeks hallucinations faded and delusions lost their hold; and some withdrawn, mute patients became more active and talkative. Rhône-Poulenc marketed the drug in France late in 1952 as Largactil (from "large activity"). In Montreal Heinz Lehmann (1911 to 1999) confirmed its effects, and by 1954 it was in use in American hospitals under the name Thorazine. Within a few years the populations of mental hospitals in many countries had begun to fall, the beginning of the deinstitutionalisation described in Book 2, Sections 1.2 and 9.8. Delay and Deniker later named the new class of drugs neuroleptic, from Greek words meaning "taking hold of the nerve", because they believed that the stiffness and slowness the drugs produced, so like Parkinson's disease, were part of how they worked. That belief would be overturned by clozapine, but the name stuck. You will also meet the older label major tranquilliser, coined to set these drugs apart from the "minor tranquillisers", the anxiety-relieving drugs such as the benzodiazepines, which have no antipsychotic action. Today the preferred name is antipsychotic drug: a drug that reduces psychotic symptoms such as hallucinations, delusions and disorganisation. No Nobel Prize was ever given for chlorpromazine; Laborit, Deniker and Lehmann shared the 1957 Lasker Award for clinical research, together with the Americans Nathan Kline and Robert Noce, who were honoured for their work on reserpine (below).

Indian context: sarpagandha, the "medicine for the insane"

The second antipsychotic came from India. The root of Rauwolfia serpentina, known as sarpagandha and in parts of north India as pagal-ki-dawa ("medicine for the mad"), had been used for centuries in folk medicine. In 1931 two Calcutta physicians, Gananath Sen and Kartick Chandra Bose, reported its use for "insanity" and high blood pressure, and the Bombay cardiologist Rustom Jal Vakil (1911 to 1974) established it as a treatment for hypertension in 1949, becoming the first Indian to win a Lasker Award. Its active ingredient, reserpine, was isolated in 1952, and in 1954 the American psychiatrist Nathan Kline (1916 to 1983) reported its antipsychotic effect. Reserpine works differently from chlorpromazine: it empties the vesicles in which dopamine and the other monoamines are stored, whereas chlorpromazine blocks the receptors (Section 4.8). Both, tellingly, produced parkinsonian stiffness. Reserpine was abandoned for psychosis because the doses needed caused dangerously low blood pressure and depression.

The third landmark came from Belgium. On 11 February 1958, while making variations on the painkiller pethidine, the team of Paul Janssen (1926 to 2003) produced haloperidol, the first of a new chemical class, the butyrophenones. Milligram for milligram it was far more powerful than chlorpromazine, it caused little sedation or fall in blood pressure, and it became the most widely used antipsychotic in the world; it is still on the World Health Organization's list of essential medicines and is still widely injected to calm acutely disturbed psychotic patients. It also caused movement side effects more readily than any of its predecessors, and the long search for a drug that could treat psychosis without them led, as Section 4.10.3 explains, to clozapine.

Exam trap

Jean Delay and Pierre Deniker (1952) are credited with introducing chlorpromazine into psychiatry; Henri Laborit was the surgeon who first noticed its calming effect; Paul Janssen (1958) made haloperidol; Nathan Kline is linked with reserpine (and with the antidepressant iproniazid); John Cade (1949) introduced lithium for mania (Chapter 5), not chlorpromazine. Neuroleptics are used primarily to treat schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders; they are also used in acute mania (Chapter 5), but they are not the primary treatment for major depressive disorder or anxiety disorders. "Major tranquilliser" means antipsychotic; "minor tranquilliser" means an anxiolytic such as diazepam.

How antipsychotic drugs work

Dozens of antipsychotic drugs have been marketed since 1952, from many chemical families, and they share only one property: every one of them reduces transmission at the dopamine D2 receptor. Most do it by blocking the receptor (they are D2 antagonists); a few, such as aripiprazole, occupy it and stimulate it only weakly. Section 4.8 tells how this was discovered, from the observation by Arvid Carlsson and Margit Lindqvist in 1963 that chlorpromazine blocks dopamine receptors rather than depleting dopamine, to the binding studies of Philip Seeman and of Ian Creese, David Burt and Solomon Snyder in 1976, which showed that the dose of a drug needed to treat psychosis tracks its affinity for D2 receptors. Here the important consequence is practical. A tablet swallowed by mouth goes everywhere in the brain. It cannot choose to block only the mesolimbic pathway, where excess dopamine activity is thought to drive the positive symptoms. It blocks D2 receptors in all four dopamine pathways at once, and that single fact explains most of what follows (Table 4.40 in Section 4.10.5 lists the consequences). Blockade in the mesolimbic pathway is the treatment. Blockade in the nigrostriatal pathway, which controls movement, produces the extrapyramidal side effects. Blockade in the tuberoinfundibular pathway, which controls the pituitary, raises the hormone prolactin. And blockade in the mesocortical pathway, already underactive in schizophrenia, may deepen apathy and slowness of thought, producing what clinicians call secondary negative symptoms.

How much blockade is needed? Since the 1980s this question has been answered directly by positron emission tomography (PET). A patient taking an antipsychotic is injected with a tiny dose of a radioactive tracer, usually raclopride, that sticks to D2 receptors; the fewer receptors are left free for the tracer, the more of them the drug is occupying. The pioneering work was done by the group of Lars Farde at the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, and the clearest single study came from Toronto.

Key study: Kapur and the therapeutic window (2000)

Shitij Kapur, Robert Zipursky and their colleagues randomly assigned 22 patients with a first episode of schizophrenia, under double-blind conditions, to a very low dose of haloperidol, 1 or 2.5 mg a day. After two weeks they measured each patient's striatal D2 receptor occupancy with PET, along with symptoms, movement side effects and prolactin; patients who had not responded were raised to 5 mg and scanned again at four weeks. Occupancy varied widely, from 38 to 87 percent, and it predicted everything. Clinical response became likely once occupancy passed about 65 percent, prolactin rose above about 72 percent, and extrapyramidal side effects appeared above about 78 percent (Figure 4.16). The conclusion is a therapeutic window: block roughly two thirds to three quarters of the D2 receptors and you get the benefit with the fewest movement effects; block more and you buy side effects, not extra benefit. The criticisms are that the sample was small, the study used one drug, and some atypical drugs (clozapine and quetiapine) seem to work at lower occupancies, so occupancy cannot be the whole story. The practical lesson stood: the far higher doses of haloperidol common in the 1970s and 1980s (the treatment-resistant patients in Kane's 1988 trial, Section 4.10.6, were first given an average of 61 mg a day) bought side effects, not benefit, for most patients.

How quickly do the drugs work? Textbooks long taught that antipsychotics take two or three weeks to act, and the delay was a famous puzzle for the dopamine hypothesis, because the receptors are blocked within hours of the first dose. In 2003 Ofer Agid, Kapur and their colleagues tested the idea by pooling 42 double-blind studies with 7,450 patients. Symptom scores fell by 13.8 percent in the first week, 8.1 percent in the second, 4.2 percent in the third and 4.7 percent in the fourth. The response, in other words, begins in the first week and accumulates, with more improvement in the first fortnight than in the next. What you see on the ward follows a typical order. Sleep, agitation and hostility settle within days. Hallucinations become quieter and less frequent over the following weeks. Delusions usually fade last, and often they do not vanish so much as lose their urgency. A patient may say, "I still think the neighbours did something, but it doesn't bother me now." That observation fits Kapur's aberrant salience account (Section 4.8): the drug dampens the excessive importance that dopamine lends to experiences, but the person still has to rethink the beliefs built on them, which is one reason why psychological help is added. Because the full benefit takes weeks, a fair test of a drug, an adequate trial, means a proper dose taken reliably for about four to six weeks.

What do the drugs treat, and how well? Antipsychotics are most effective against positive symptoms and disorganisation and have little effect on negative and cognitive symptoms, which is why so many patients who are free of voices remain unable to work. Barlow and Durand summarise that the first-generation drugs help roughly 60 to 70 percent of the people who take them. Roughly a fifth to a third of patients respond poorly to two or more drugs, the treatment-resistant group described in Section 4.10.6. And as Pinel reminds his readers, the drugs do not cure schizophrenia: they reduce symptoms enough for most people to leave hospital, and the illness tends to return when they are stopped (Section 4.10.7).

Finally, two terms that examiners confuse. The potency of an antipsychotic is the number of milligrams needed for an effect, not how well it works. Chlorpromazine is a low-potency antipsychotic, given in hundreds of milligrams a day; haloperidol, fluphenazine and trifluoperazine are high-potency antipsychotics, given in a few milligrams. Clinicians compare doses as chlorpromazine equivalents: about 2 mg of haloperidol does the work of 100 mg of chlorpromazine. At equivalent doses the old drugs are equally effective; what potency predicts is the side-effect profile. High-potency drugs are "clean" D2 blockers, so they cause more movement side effects. Low-potency drugs also block muscarinic acetylcholine, histamine and alpha-adrenergic receptors, so they cause more sedation, dry mouth and dizziness, but fewer movement effects, because their built-in anticholinergic action partly protects the movement system (the reason is explained in Section 4.10.4). Pinel adds one more clue that D2 is the receptor that matters: the phenothiazines such as chlorpromazine bind both D1 and D2 receptors, but haloperidol and the other butyrophenones bind D2 almost alone, and they are just as effective.

Exam trap

The primary target of typical (first-generation) antipsychotics is the dopamine D2 receptor. Not D1 (phenothiazines bind it, but it does not predict efficacy), not serotonin 5-HT2A (the extra target of the atypicals), and not muscarinic M1 (blocking it causes side effects such as dry mouth, not the antipsychotic effect). Antipsychotics do not work by lowering the amount of dopamine in the brain; they block its receptors (reserpine is the drug that depletes dopamine). And "high potency" means a small dose in milligrams, not a stronger antipsychotic effect.

Typical and atypical antipsychotics

Antipsychotics are divided into two generations. The first-generation antipsychotics, also called typical or conventional antipsychotics, are the drugs introduced from chlorpromazine in 1952 onwards. They are grouped by chemistry: the phenothiazines (chlorpromazine, thioridazine, trifluoperazine, fluphenazine and perphenazine), the butyrophenones (haloperidol), the thioxanthenes (flupentixol and zuclopenthixol), the diphenylbutylpiperidines (pimozide, and penfluridol, a once-a-week tablet still used in India), and a few others such as loxapine. The second-generation antipsychotics, also called atypical antipsychotics, begin with clozapine and include the drugs introduced from the 1990s onwards: risperidone and its derivative paliperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, ziprasidone, aripiprazole, amisulpride, lurasidone and asenapine among others.

Why "atypical"? For two decades after 1952 it was taken for granted that a drug could not relieve psychosis without also causing parkinsonian stiffness; Delay and Deniker had built the idea into the very word neuroleptic, and drug companies screened new compounds by whether they made rats rigid and motionless (catalepsy). Clozapine, synthesised by the Swiss firm Wander in 1958, broke the rule. It relieved psychosis in patients, yet it hardly caused catalepsy in animals or movement side effects in people. It was therefore "atypical", and the original meaning of the word is exactly that: an antipsychotic that causes few extrapyramidal side effects, and little rise in prolactin, at doses that treat psychosis. Later writers added further claims (better effects on negative symptoms, on cognition, on treatment-resistant illness), but strictly only clozapine meets all of them.

Three explanations have been offered for why the atypicals cause fewer movement problems, and each fits some drugs better than others.

  • Serotonin and dopamine blockade. Most atypicals block serotonin 5-HT2A receptors more strongly than D2 receptors, the ratio emphasised by Herbert Meltzer (Section 4.8). Serotonin acting at 5-HT2A receptors normally acts as a brake on dopamine release in the striatum and prefrontal cortex. Remove the brake and a little more dopamine reaches the nigrostriatal and mesocortical pathways, offsetting the D2 blockade there: fewer movement effects and, in theory, less worsening of negative symptoms. Drugs such as risperidone and olanzapine are therefore sometimes called serotonin-dopamine antagonists.
  • Loose, brief binding. In 2001 Kapur and Philip Seeman proposed the fast-off hypothesis: what makes a drug atypical is not its serotonin action but that it binds the D2 receptor loosely and lets go quickly, so that the brain's natural bursts of dopamine can still get through. Clozapine and quetiapine behave like this, and PET shows that they work at lower D2 occupancies. Kapur and Seeman also pointed out that a drug loses its atypical character when its D2 occupancy is pushed too high, whatever its serotonin blockade, which is why risperidone at high doses causes movement effects and raises prolactin as a typical drug would.
  • Partial agonism. Aripiprazole binds D2 receptors tightly but, instead of blocking them completely, stimulates them weakly. A partial agonist of this kind behaves as a blocker where dopamine is abundant and as a mild stimulant where dopamine is scarce, which is why aripiprazole is sometimes called a "dopamine system stabiliser". It raises prolactin least of all the antipsychotics and carries a low metabolic risk, but it commonly causes akathisia (Section 4.10.4). Brexpiprazole and cariprazine work in a similar way.

Amisulpride is an odd one out: it has no serotonin action at all, but blocks D2 and D3 receptors with some preference for limbic areas, and at low doses it blocks the presynaptic receptors that brake dopamine release, which is why it has been used for predominantly negative symptoms. It raises prolactin strongly.

When the atypicals arrived they were promoted as more effective, especially against negative and cognitive symptoms, as well as safer, and by the early 2000s they had largely replaced the older drugs in wealthy countries at many times the price. Then came two large trials run independently of the drug companies. In the American CATIE study (Clinical Antipsychotic Trials of Intervention Effectiveness), Jeffrey Lieberman and colleagues in 2005 randomly assigned 1,493 people with chronic schizophrenia at 57 sites to olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone, ziprasidone or the old phenothiazine perphenazine, for up to 18 months. Seventy-four percent stopped their assigned drug before the 18 months were up, because it did not work well enough, because of side effects, or for other reasons. Olanzapine kept patients on treatment longest but caused the most weight gain and the largest rises in blood sugar and lipids, and perphenazine, the typical drug, did about as well as quetiapine, risperidone and ziprasidone; it was dropped more often for movement effects. In England the CUtLASS trial (Peter Jones and colleagues, 2006) randomised 227 patients whose medication needed changing to a first- or a second-generation drug other than clozapine and found no advantage for the newer drugs in quality of life over a year; if anything, the trend favoured the old ones, and costs were similar.

The fullest comparison is the 2013 network meta-analysis by Stefan Leucht and colleagues, which combined 212 trials with 43,049 participants comparing 15 antipsychotics with placebo and with each other. Every drug beat placebo. The differences in effectiveness were small but consistent: clozapine came first by a clear margin, followed by amisulpride, olanzapine and risperidone, with haloperidol in the middle and chlorpromazine and several newer drugs lower down. The differences in side effects were large. For movement side effects the range ran from clozapine (lowest) to haloperidol (highest); for weight gain, from haloperidol (least) to olanzapine (most); for prolactin, from aripiprazole (least) to paliperidone (most); for sedation, from amisulpride to clozapine. Leucht's group concluded that the split into "first" and "second" generation hides more than it reveals: a clinician should choose a drug by its particular side-effect profile and what matters most to the particular patient. Table 4.37 sets out the classic contrast that examiners expect, and Table 4.38 gives the individual drugs most often asked about.
