Exam trap

The standard contrast is: typical drugs block D2 and cause extrapyramidal side effects; atypical drugs block 5-HT2A as well as D2 and cause fewer movement effects but more metabolic effects. The first atypical is clozapine; aripiprazole is a partial agonist, not a pure antagonist. If a question says atypicals are "better for negative symptoms", that is the expected textbook answer, but know the evidence: apart from clozapine the advantage is small, and much of it comes from causing less drug-induced slowness. Atypicals are not free of extrapyramidal effects, tardive dyskinesia or neuroleptic malignant syndrome, and high-dose risperidone behaves much like a typical drug.

Extrapyramidal side effects, in the order they appear

The pyramidal tracts carry voluntary commands from the motor cortex to the spinal cord. Everything else that shapes movement, above all the circuits of the basal ganglia that start, stop, smooth and scale our actions, was once lumped together as the extrapyramidal system, and the movement disorders caused by antipsychotics are therefore called extrapyramidal side effects (EPS). They arise because the drugs block D2 receptors in the nigrostriatal pathway, which runs from the substantia nigra to the caudate and putamen (Section 4.8). In the striatum, dopamine is normally balanced against acetylcholine released by local interneurons. Block the dopamine and acetylcholine dominates. This balance explains two practical facts at once: anticholinergic drugs such as trihexyphenidyl relieve several of the side effects, and low-potency antipsychotics, which have anticholinergic action built in, cause fewer of them. The four classic extrapyramidal syndromes appear in a regular order, over hours, days, weeks and finally months to years (Figure 4.17; Table 4.39 summarises all four), and that order is the most useful single thing to learn in this section.

Acute dystonia. In the emergency room Prabhu was given an injection of haloperidol, then haloperidol tablets. The next afternoon his mother rushed him back. His eyes had rolled upwards so that little but the whites showed, his neck was twisted hard to one side, and his jaw was clenched so tightly that he could barely speak. A neighbour had already suggested that "something" had seized him more strongly. The duty doctor recognised the picture at once, gave an intramuscular injection of an anticholinergic drug, and within twenty minutes the spasms melted away. What Prabhu had was acute dystonia, a sustained, involuntary contraction of muscles that forces the body into abnormal postures. It most often affects the head and neck: an oculogyric crisis (the eyes forced upwards, downwards or sideways for minutes to hours), torticollis (the neck twisted to one side, or bent backwards or forwards), lockjaw, a protruding tongue, grimacing, arching of the back, or a sideways lean of the trunk. Spasm of the muscles of the larynx is rare but can block the airway and is an emergency. DSM-5-TR reports that at least half of affected people develop dystonia within 24 to 48 hours of starting a drug or raising its dose, and about 90 percent within five days. The risk is greatest in children and in adults under 40, in males, with high-potency typical drugs, with injections and in people who have had a dystonic reaction before. Treatment is an anticholinergic (benztropine, procyclidine) or the antihistamine promethazine by injection; with high-potency drugs in young men, many Indian clinicians prevent it by prescribing oral trihexyphenidyl from the first day. Dystonia is painful and terrifying, and a patient who is not warned about it may never trust the medicine again.

Clinical link: when a side effect looks like the illness

Acute dystonia can be mistaken for catatonic posturing, for a conversion (functional) disorder, for "attention seeking", or, in many Indian families, for possession. Each mistake is dangerous, because it can lead to a higher dose of the very drug that caused the problem. Always ask what medicine was given in the last few days, and remember that the dramatic relief after an injection of an anticholinergic is both the treatment and the confirmation of the diagnosis. DSM-5-TR adds a useful contrast: people with dystonia are distressed and ask for help, whereas people in a retarded catatonic state are usually mute and show no distress.

Akathisia. Asha, a 34-year-old schoolteacher from Pune, was admitted with persecutory delusions and started on haloperidol tablets. Ten days later the nursing notes read "agitated, pacing the corridor". She could not sit through the ward round; standing, she shifted her weight from foot to foot. "My legs want to walk by themselves," she said. "Inside I feel I will jump out of my skin." The junior doctor read this as worsening psychosis and raised the dose. Asha became more restless and told her husband that she would rather die than go on feeling like this. The consultant recognised akathisia (Greek for "not sitting"): a subjective feeling of inner restlessness, usually felt in the legs, together with movements that relieve it, such as fidgeting, swinging the legs while seated, rocking from foot to foot, marching on the spot or pacing. DSM-5-TR describes it as typically appearing within four weeks of starting or increasing a drug, often much sooner, more often with high-potency drugs and with rapid dose increases, and persisting for as long as the drug is continued. Reported rates among people taking antipsychotics range widely, from 20 to 75 percent. Akathisia matters for three reasons. It is easily mistaken for the agitation of psychosis, mania or anxiety, and the "treatment" then makes it worse. It is one of the commonest reasons patients stop their medication. And it is associated with dysphoria, irritability, aggression and suicide attempts. Clues that restlessness is akathisia include its onset soon after a dose change, the patient's report that it feels unlike ordinary anxiety, and its worsening when the dose is raised. Management is to lower the dose or switch to a drug less likely to cause it; the beta-blocker propranolol and benzodiazepines are the usual remedies, while anticholinergics help little unless parkinsonism is also present. Asha's dose was reduced and propranolol added, and within a week she could sit and read again.

Drug-induced parkinsonism. What DSM-5-TR calls medication-induced parkinsonism reproduces the signs of Parkinson's disease: bradykinesia (slowness, with fewer spontaneous movements; at the extreme, akinesia), rigidity, tremor and an unsteady posture. Watch an affected patient walk into the room. The face is mask-like and blinks rarely, the arms do not swing, the steps are small and shuffling, the posture is stooped, the voice is soft and monotonous, and the handwriting shrinks. Moving the patient's arm feels like bending a lead pipe (constant resistance throughout the movement), or like turning a ratchet (cogwheel rigidity, a tremor superimposed on rigidity). The tremor is a slow resting tremor of about three to six beats a second; when it affects only the lips it is called rabbit syndrome. DSM-5-TR calls medication-induced parkinsonism the second most common cause of parkinsonism after Parkinson's disease itself. With antipsychotics it typically develops two to four weeks after starting, usually by three months, and at least half of outpatients on long-term typical drugs show parkinsonian signs at some point; older people, women and people with cognitive impairment are most at risk. Two features help separate it from Parkinson's disease: it starts on both sides of the body at once, within weeks of a new drug, whereas Parkinson's disease begins on one side and spreads asymmetrically over years, often with loss of smell and acting out of dreams. The greater danger is confusing it with schizophrenia itself. Akinesia and a mask face look exactly like avolition and blunted affect, so a patient may be labelled as having worsening negative symptoms (or depression) when he is in fact over-medicated; reduce the dose and the "negative symptoms" lift. Treatment is to lower the dose or switch to an atypical drug, or to add an anticholinergic (trihexyphenidyl, benztropine, procyclidine) or amantadine. Anticholinergics have costs of their own (dry mouth, constipation, blurred vision, poorer memory, and euphoria that leads some patients to misuse them), and they worsen tardive dyskinesia, so they should not be continued indefinitely by routine.

Tardive dyskinesia. Dinesh, a 52-year-old retired bus conductor from Thrissur, was first diagnosed with schizophrenia at 24 and has received an injection of fluphenazine every three weeks for almost twenty years, with trihexyphenidyl tablets alongside. His daughter brought him to the clinic because his mouth "keeps chewing even when he isn't eating". Sitting quietly, his jaw works from side to side, his lips pucker and smack, his tongue pushes against his cheek and darts out between his lips, and his fingers move as though playing an invisible harmonium. Dinesh himself barely notices the movements; his family is embarrassed by them at weddings. This is tardive dyskinesia ("late abnormal movement"), a syndrome of involuntary, repetitive, purposeless movements that appears after long exposure to dopamine-blocking drugs. The orofacial movements (chewing, lip smacking and pursing, tongue protrusion, grimacing, cheek puffing, blinking) are the commonest; about half of patients also have limb movements, which may be choreiform (quick, jerky and irregular) or athetoid (slow and writhing), and some have rocking of the trunk or irregular breathing. DSM-5-TR requires the movements to have been present for at least four weeks, after at least three months of exposure to the drug (one month in people aged 60 or over); they may appear while the drug is being taken or within four weeks of stopping a tablet (eight weeks for a long-acting injection). Among people on long-term antipsychotic treatment the prevalence is about 20 to 30 percent, and the yearly incidence in younger patients is about 3 to 5 percent. Older people are far more vulnerable, with prevalence figures up to 50 percent. The most consistent risk factors are age and cumulative exposure; others include early extrapyramidal side effects, mood disorders, alcohol use disorder and, possibly, being a woman after the menopause. Second-generation drugs carry a somewhat lower risk, but, DSM-5-TR notes, the difference is not as large as was once believed.

Several odd features of tardive dyskinesia point to its likely mechanism. The movements are worse under stress, disappear during sleep and lessen when the person deliberately moves the affected part. Above all, raising the dose of the antipsychotic temporarily suppresses them, while stopping the drug or adding an anticholinergic makes them worse. The leading explanation is dopamine receptor supersensitivity: after years of blockade, striatal neurons respond by making more D2 receptors and becoming hypersensitive to whatever dopamine reaches them, so the motor striatum becomes, in effect, overactive. Extra blockade masks the excess for a while; removing the blockade unmasks it. The hypothesis is not proven, and damage to striatal neurons from oxidative stress has also been proposed, but it explains why the illness looks like the opposite of parkinsonism. Tardive dyskinesia may be irreversible, especially in older people, although many patients improve slowly once the drug is reduced. The best treatment is prevention: use the lowest effective dose, avoid unnecessary long-term use and examine patients regularly, often with the Abnormal Involuntary Movement Scale (AIMS, covered in the Assessment book). Once it appears, anticholinergics are stopped, the patient may be switched to clozapine, which carries the lowest risk of any antipsychotic, and a VMAT2 inhibitor may be added. These drugs (valbenazine, the first medicine approved for tardive dyskinesia by the United States Food and Drug Administration in April 2017, deutetrabenazine and the older tetrabenazine) reduce the packaging of dopamine into vesicles, a gentler version of what reserpine does. DSM-5-TR warns that the two kinds of treatment pull in opposite directions: anticholinergics, used for parkinsonism, worsen tardive dyskinesia, and VMAT2 inhibitors, used for tardive dyskinesia, worsen parkinsonism. Late-appearing, persistent forms of dystonia and akathisia also occur, called tardive dystonia and tardive akathisia. Dinesh's trihexyphenidyl was stopped and, as Section 4.10.6 describes, he was later moved to clozapine.

Table 4.39 The four extrapyramidal side effects.
Side effectUsual onsetWhat you seeHighest riskManagement
Acute dystoniaHours to 5 daysPainful spasms: eyes rolled up (oculogyric crisis), twisted neck (torticollis), lockjaw, tongue thrust, arched backYoung men, high-potency drugs, injectionsAnticholinergic or promethazine by injection; prevention with oral trihexyphenidyl
AkathisiaHours to 4 weeksInner restlessness; cannot sit still; pacing, rocking from foot to footHigh-potency drugs, rapid dose increases; aripiprazoleLower the dose or switch; propranolol; a benzodiazepine
ParkinsonismUsually 2 to 4 weeks, by 3 monthsSlowness, mask face, lead-pipe or cogwheel rigidity, shuffling gait, resting tremorOlder people, women, high-potency drugsLower the dose or switch; trihexyphenidyl, benztropine or amantadine
Tardive dyskinesiaAfter at least 3 months; months to yearsChewing, lip smacking, tongue protrusion, grimacing; limb and trunk movementsOlder age, long cumulative exposure, early EPSPrevention; stop anticholinergics; switch to clozapine; VMAT2 inhibitor

Examiners also ask how drug-induced movement problems fit into neurology. Neurologists distinguish a primary movement disorder, in which the disorder arises from the nervous system itself, usually through an inherited or idiopathic degeneration, from a secondary movement disorder, which has an identifiable outside cause such as a drug, a toxin, a stroke or an infection. Parkinson's disease, first described by James Parkinson in An Essay on the Shaking Palsy (1817), is primary: the dopamine neurons of the substantia nigra degenerate. Huntington's disease, described by George Huntington in 1872, is primary too: an inherited expansion in the huntingtin gene produces chorea, dementia and psychiatric symptoms. All four extrapyramidal side effects are secondary. Drug-induced parkinsonism is a secondary parkinsonism, imitating Parkinson's disease without being it. Tardive dyskinesia must likewise be told apart from the primary choreas and from other causes of mouth and limb movements that DSM-5-TR lists, including Huntington's disease, Wilson's disease, Sydenham's chorea after rheumatic fever, thyroid overactivity, heavy metal poisoning, ill-fitting dentures and the dyskinesias caused by L-dopa. It must also be told apart from spontaneous dyskinesias, which DSM-5-TR says can occur in more than 5 percent of people, more often in the elderly, and which have been described in people with schizophrenia who have never taken an antipsychotic.

DSM-5-TR gathers all of these reactions in a chapter of Section II called "Medication-Induced Movement Disorders and Other Adverse Effects of Medication". The chapter lists medication-induced parkinsonism (with antipsychotics and other dopamine receptor blocking agents as the main cause), neuroleptic malignant syndrome, medication-induced acute dystonia, medication-induced acute akathisia, tardive dyskinesia, tardive dystonia and tardive akathisia, medication-induced postural tremor (lithium is the classic cause), other medication-induced movement disorder, antidepressant discontinuation syndrome and other adverse effect of medication. DSM-5-TR is explicit that these are not mental disorders. It includes them because they matter so much in managing medication and in differential diagnosis: telling an anxiety disorder from akathisia, malignant catatonia from neuroleptic malignant syndrome, and tardive dyskinesia from chorea. It also notes that the word neuroleptic is becoming outdated, being replaced by "antipsychotic medications and other dopamine receptor blocking agents". That longer phrase is a reminder that the anti-nausea drugs metoclopramide and prochlorperazine, and the antidepressant amoxapine, also block dopamine receptors and can cause the same movement disorders.

Exam trap

Read the time course in the stem. Years of treatment plus involuntary lip smacking, tongue movements and grimacing is tardive dyskinesia; a recent start plus subjective inner restlessness and a need to pace is akathisia; sudden painful spasms within days is acute dystonia; slowness, stiffness and tremor after some weeks is parkinsonism; fever, rigidity and confusion is neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Tardive dyskinesia is caused by neuroleptics (antipsychotics), not by antidepressants, anxiolytics or barbiturates. Akathisia and tardive dyskinesia are secondary (drug-induced) movement disorders; Huntington's chorea and Parkinson's disease are primary. Typical drugs cause extrapyramidal effects by blocking nigrostriatal D2 receptors: not by blocking serotonin reuptake (that is what SSRIs do), not by stimulating GABA-A receptors (benzodiazepines), and not by blocking only mesolimbic receptors (a drug that did that would cause no extrapyramidal effects at all).

Neuroleptic malignant syndrome and the other adverse effects

The rarest serious reaction to an antipsychotic is also the most dangerous. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) is a life-threatening reaction in which the brain's control of muscle tone, temperature and the autonomic nervous system breaks down. DSM-5-TR describes its features in detail. The person has usually been given a dopamine blocker within the previous 72 hours. The distinguishing sign is high fever (above 38.0 °C on at least two occasions) with profuse sweating. There is generalised rigidity, "lead pipe" in severe cases, which does not respond to anti-parkinsonian drugs, often with tremor, drooling and difficulty swallowing. The mental state changes, often early, from confusion and delirium to stupor and coma; the person may lie alert but dazed and unresponsive, looking catatonic. The autonomic nervous system becomes unstable, with a racing pulse, high or swinging blood pressure, rapid breathing, pallor and incontinence. In the blood, the muscle enzyme creatine kinase (CK, also called creatine phosphokinase) is raised, commonly to at least four times the normal upper limit, as muscle breaks down, and the white cell count is often high. Four features are easy to remember together: fever, rigidity, autonomic instability and altered consciousness, confirmed by a raised CK.

NMS is rare: database studies suggest 0.01 to 0.02 percent of people treated with antipsychotics, although a population study in Hong Kong found 0.11 percent. Some cases begin within 24 hours of the first dose, most within the first week and virtually all within 30 days. The risk is higher with high-potency drugs, injections, rapid dose increases and high total doses, and in patients who are agitated, exhausted, dehydrated or iron deficient; 15 to 20 percent of patients have had an earlier episode. Yet it can follow any antipsychotic at an ordinary dose, including the atypicals, and even anti-nausea drugs such as metoclopramide. Once it is recognised and the drug is stopped, NMS usually resolves, on average in 7 to 10 days and nearly always within 30 days (longer if a depot injection is the cause). Unrecognised, it kills 10 to 20 percent of patients. Treatment is an emergency in a medical ward or intensive care unit: stop the antipsychotic, cool the patient, give fluids and support breathing and circulation, and consider a benzodiazepine, the muscle relaxant dantrolene or the dopamine agonist bromocriptine; electroconvulsive therapy is used for cases that do not resolve. NMS must be told apart from infections of the brain, heat stroke, malignant hyperthermia during anaesthesia, the serotonin syndrome caused by serotonergic drugs, and malignant catatonia, from which it may be clinically indistinguishable. Some investigators regard NMS as a drug-induced form of malignant catatonia (Section 4.5).

Remember

A patient recently started on haloperidol who becomes feverish, stiff, sweaty and confused, with a fast pulse and a high CK, has neuroleptic malignant syndrome until proved otherwise. Stop the drug and treat it as a medical emergency. Do not mistake the stupor for catatonia and give more antipsychotic.

The other side effects follow from the receptors that antipsychotics block besides the nigrostriatal D2 receptor, and Table 4.40 maps them out. Four deserve a closer look.

Hyperprolactinaemia. Dopamine released by the tuberoinfundibular neurons of the hypothalamus is the brake on the release of prolactin from the anterior pituitary; it is the "prolactin-inhibiting factor". Block D2 receptors there and prolactin rises: hyperprolactinaemia. In women it causes irregular or absent periods (amenorrhoea), milk secretion from the breasts (galactorrhoea) and infertility; in men, breast enlargement (gynaecomastia) and erectile difficulties; in both, loss of sexual desire and, over years, thinning of the bones. Four months after her admission Asha stopped menstruating and noticed milk leaking from her breasts; she was terrified that she was pregnant. Her prolactin level was several times normal. Her psychiatrist switched her to aripiprazole, and her periods returned. The worst offenders are risperidone, paliperidone, amisulpride and the typical drugs; aripiprazole, quetiapine and clozapine raise prolactin least. Because patients are often too embarrassed to mention these effects, a good clinician asks about them directly, since they are a common hidden reason for stopping treatment.

Weight gain and the metabolic syndrome. Many antipsychotics increase appetite, largely through blockade of histamine H1 and serotonin 5-HT2C receptors, and some also impair the body's response to insulin directly. The result can be rapid weight gain, type 2 diabetes, raised triglycerides and cholesterol and high blood pressure: together, the metabolic syndrome. Clozapine and olanzapine are the worst offenders, quetiapine and risperidone come in between, and aripiprazole, ziprasidone, lurasidone and haloperidol carry the least risk. This matters enormously, because people with schizophrenia already die years earlier than the general population, mostly from heart disease (Section 4.6), and because South Asians develop diabetes at lower body weights than Europeans. Good practice is to measure weight, waist, blood pressure, blood sugar and lipids before treatment and regularly afterwards, to advise on diet and exercise from the start, and, when weight climbs, to consider switching drug or adding metformin.

Anticholinergic and adrenergic effects. Blockade of muscarinic acetylcholine receptors produces anticholinergic side effects: dry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, difficulty passing urine, a fast heart rate and, especially in older people, confusion and poor memory. Chlorpromazine, thioridazine, clozapine and olanzapine have strong anticholinergic actions. Blockade of alpha-1 adrenergic receptors in blood vessels causes orthostatic hypotension, a fall in blood pressure on standing that produces dizziness, fainting and falls, the very reaction Laborit's volunteer had in 1951; it is common with chlorpromazine, clozapine and quetiapine and is one reason why doses are increased slowly. Blockade of histamine H1 receptors causes sedation, which is welcome in an acutely disturbed patient and a burden in one who is trying to study or work.

The heart and other effects. Some antipsychotics slow the electrical recovery of the heart muscle, seen on the electrocardiogram as QTc prolongation, which can lead to a dangerous irregular rhythm (torsades de pointes) and sudden death. Thioridazine, pimozide, sertindole, ziprasidone and intravenous haloperidol carry the most risk, and an electrocardiogram is advised before using them. Other effects to know are a lowered seizure threshold (especially clozapine and chlorpromazine), sensitivity to sunlight, skin pigmentation and jaundice with chlorpromazine, damage to the retina with high doses of thioridazine, sexual dysfunction with many drugs, and a raised death rate when antipsychotics are given to elderly people with dementia, about which regulators issued warnings in the 2000s. Finally there is the subjective side. When K. Windgassen questioned 61 people with recent-onset schizophrenia in 1992, about half complained of sedation ("I always have to fight to keep my eyes open"), and others described slowed thinking (18 percent), problems with salivation (16 percent) and blurred vision (16 percent). Many patients describe a dulling of feeling and drive on high doses, which they experience as a loss of themselves.

D2, tuberoinfundibularRaised prolactin: absent periods, galactorrhoea, gynaecomastia, sexual problemsRisperidone, paliperidone, amisulpride, typical drugs
D2, mesocorticalPossible worsening of negative and cognitive symptomsMainly high doses of typical drugs
Muscarinic acetylcholineDry mouth, blurred vision, constipation, urinary retention, confusionChlorpromazine, thioridazine, clozapine, olanzapine
Alpha-1 adrenergicDizziness and fainting on standing, fast pulse, ejaculatory problemsChlorpromazine, clozapine, quetiapine
Histamine H1Sedation, weight gainClozapine, olanzapine, quetiapine, chlorpromazine
Serotonin 5-HT2CIncreased appetite, weight gainClozapine, olanzapine
Cardiac potassium channelsQTc prolongation, risk of dangerous arrhythmiaThioridazine, pimozide, sertindole, ziprasidone, intravenous haloperidol
Exam trap

Typical antipsychotics act by blocking D2 receptors, and the resulting cluster is extrapyramidal side effects, tardive dyskinesia and hyperprolactinaemia. The tempting wrong pairings each describe something real but different: 5-HT2A blockade is what reduces extrapyramidal effects in atypicals (metabolic effects come mainly from H1 and 5-HT2C blockade); NMDA glutamate receptor blockade by phencyclidine and ketamine produces psychosis (Section 4.8); and GABA-A receptors are the site of action of benzodiazepines. Raised prolactin is a tuberoinfundibular effect, not mesolimbic or nigrostriatal. And do not confuse neuroleptic malignant syndrome (antipsychotics, rigidity, develops over days) with serotonin syndrome (serotonergic drugs, twitching and overactive reflexes, develops within hours).

Clozapine and treatment-resistant schizophrenia

For a sizeable minority of patients, roughly one in five to one in three, the voices and delusions persist however carefully the drugs are chosen. Their condition, treatment-resistant schizophrenia, is usually defined as the persistence of significant symptoms despite at least two adequate trials of two different antipsychotics: each given at a proper dose, for an adequate period (about six weeks), with adherence checked, since a drug that is not being swallowed has not failed. For these patients one drug stands apart, and its history is one of the strangest in psychiatry.

The drug that defined the word atypical, clozapine, was synthesised in 1958 and came into use in parts of Europe in the early 1970s. In 1975 a cluster of cases of agranulocytosis in Finland, eight of them fatal, led to its withdrawal or severe restriction in many countries. Agranulocytosis is a near-total loss of the neutrophils, the white blood cells that fight bacterial infection; the first signs are usually fever, sore throat and mouth ulcers, and without treatment the person can die of overwhelming infection. Clozapine might have disappeared altogether had a few researchers not continued to study it for patients who had nothing else.

Key study: Kane, Honigfeld, Singer and Meltzer (1988)

The question was whether clozapine could help people for whom the standard drugs had failed. John Kane, Gilbert Honigfeld, Jack Singer and Herbert Meltzer recruited, at several American centres, patients with DSM-III schizophrenia who had failed to respond to at least three different antipsychotics. To make sure that they really were resistant, every patient first received haloperidol (an average of 61 mg a day) for six weeks; only those still unimproved went on. Then 268 patients were randomly assigned, double-blind, to clozapine (up to 900 mg a day) or chlorpromazine (up to 1,800 mg a day) for six weeks. By criteria fixed in advance, 30 percent of the clozapine group responded, compared with 4 percent of the chlorpromazine group, and clozapine improved negative as well as positive symptoms. No case of agranulocytosis occurred in so short a trial, but the authors argued that the risk meant clozapine should be reserved for treatment-resistant patients. The criticisms are that six weeks is short and that a 30 percent response rate still left most patients unwell; later studies found that more patients respond if clozapine is continued for several months. The result changed practice everywhere. Clozapine was approved in the United States and marketed from February 1990, but only with compulsory blood monitoring, and it has been the treatment of choice for resistant schizophrenia ever since.

Every comparison since has confirmed that clozapine is the most effective antipsychotic; it came top for efficacy in Leucht's 2013 network meta-analysis. It also carries the lowest risk of extrapyramidal side effects and tardive dyskinesia of any antipsychotic. And it does something no other antipsychotic has been shown to do. In the International Suicide Prevention Trial (InterSePT), Meltzer and colleagues in 2003 randomised 980 people with schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder at high risk of suicide to clozapine or olanzapine for two years: suicidal behaviour was significantly lower with clozapine (34 versus 55 suicide attempts). On the strength of these results the United States Food and Drug Administration approved clozapine in December 2002 for reducing suicidal behaviour in schizophrenia, shortly before the trial was published. Large Finnish registry studies have since found the lowest overall death rates among patients taking clozapine.

Why, then, is clozapine a third-line drug and not a first-line one? Because of its risks, which every patient and family must understand. The most famous is agranulocytosis. When Jose Alvir, Lieberman and colleagues analysed the first American monitoring data in 1993 (11,555 patients), agranulocytosis had developed in 73, two of whom died; 61 of the 73 cases began within the first three months, and the cumulative risk was 0.8 percent at one year, higher in older people and in women. Because the danger is concentrated early, monitoring is most intensive at the start: a white cell and neutrophil count before treatment, then weekly for the first few months (18 weeks in the United Kingdom, six months in the United States), then less often but for as long as the drug is taken, with the drug stopped at once if the neutrophil count falls below a set threshold. (In June 2025 the United States ended its compulsory national registry for clozapine, but the blood counts are still recommended on the same schedule.) Clinicians sum the rule up as "no blood, no drug". The other risks are these:

  • Inflammation of the heart muscle, myocarditis, can occur, almost always in the first two months, and is signalled by fever, chest pain, breathlessness and a fast pulse.
  • Seizures become more likely at high doses and high blood levels.
  • Drooling, or hypersalivation, especially at night, troubles a large proportion of patients; it is a paradox, since clozapine otherwise has strong anticholinergic actions.
  • Severe constipation is common and can progress to bowel obstruction, which can kill if it is ignored.
  • Sedation, dizziness on standing and a fast heart rate are common at the start, which is why the dose is built up slowly from 12.5 mg over several weeks.
  • Marked weight gain and diabetes are frequent, and some patients develop fever in the first weeks or bedwetting at night.
Clinical link: clozapine and cigarettes

Tobacco smoke (not nicotine itself) speeds up the liver enzyme CYP1A2, which breaks down clozapine, so smokers have clozapine levels roughly 30 percent lower, sometimes up to 50 percent lower, than non-smokers on the same dose. Because so many people with schizophrenia smoke heavily, this matters in both directions. A patient who stops smoking suddenly, for example on admission to a smoke-free ward, can develop toxic levels with sedation and seizures; one who starts smoking again after discharge may relapse. Blood levels of clozapine should be checked whenever smoking habits change.

Dinesh had failed two adequate trials, first of fluphenazine and then of risperidone, before his psychiatrist suggested clozapine, and his family worried about the blood tests. They were reassured that he would have weekly counts at the district hospital and would be told to report any fever or sore throat at once. The dose was built up over a month to 300 mg a day. He drooled onto his pillow at night, needed a daily laxative and put on six kilograms, but by the fourth month the voices that had followed him for twenty-eight years were rare and quiet, and the chewing movements of his tardive dyskinesia were less noticeable. Despite stories like his, clozapine is underused all over the world: many eligible patients never receive it, or receive it only after years of delay, mainly because clinicians overestimate its dangers and dislike the monitoring.

Even clozapine is not enough for many resistant patients: George Petrides and colleagues estimate that up to 70 percent of people with treatment-resistant schizophrenia do not respond adequately to it. The next steps are to check that the blood level is adequate, to add a second antipsychotic such as amisulpride or aripiprazole (aripiprazole also helps to limit weight gain), or to add electroconvulsive therapy. In a randomised study published in 2015, Petrides's group gave 39 clozapine-resistant patients either clozapine alone or clozapine plus a course of ECT: half of the ECT group responded and none of the clozapine-alone group did, and 47 percent of those later crossed over to ECT also responded. Figure 4.18 sets out this stepwise approach to drug treatment.

Exam trap

Clozapine is the first atypical antipsychotic and the drug of choice for treatment-resistant schizophrenia (after two failed adequate trials). It is reserved because of agranulocytosis, which requires regular blood counts, not because it is weaker; it is in fact the most effective antipsychotic. It causes very few extrapyramidal effects and the least tardive dyskinesia, it reduces suicidal behaviour, and it causes drooling despite its anticholinergic action. Do not confuse clozapine with clomipramine (an antidepressant used in OCD), clonazepam (a benzodiazepine) or clonidine (a blood pressure drug used in opioid withdrawal and ADHD).

Staying well: maintenance treatment, adherence and long-acting injections

Prabhu's voices faded within a month and he went back to college. Six months later he felt entirely well, he had put on weight, his friends teased him about "mad pills", and he quietly stopped taking them. Four months after that the voices returned, and he was readmitted. His story is the commonest story in the treatment of schizophrenia, and the evidence behind it is unusually clear.

Key study: relapse after a first episode (Robinson and colleagues, 1999)

Delbert Robinson and colleagues at Hillside Hospital in New York followed 104 patients who had recovered from a first episode of schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder. Within five years of recovery the cumulative rate of a first relapse was 81.9 percent, and most of those who relapsed went on to relapse again. The strongest single predictor was stopping medication: discontinuing antipsychotic treatment raised the risk of relapse almost fivefold (a hazard ratio of 4.89). Poor adjustment at school and social withdrawal before the illness predicted earlier relapse as well. The conclusion: relapse after a first episode is the rule rather than the exception, and maintenance medication greatly reduces it.

The term maintenance treatment means continuing an antipsychotic after the acute episode has resolved, in order to prevent relapse. The strongest evidence for it comes from the 2012 meta-analysis by Leucht and colleagues of 65 randomised trials involving 6,493 patients who had been stabilised on medication and were then either continued on an antipsychotic or switched to placebo. At one year, 27 percent of those continued on medication had relapsed, against 64 percent of those on placebo; only three patients needed to be kept on medication to prevent one relapse. Readmissions were 10 percent against 26 percent, and there was some evidence of better quality of life and fewer aggressive acts. The price was paid in side effects: more weight gain (10 versus 6 percent), more movement disorders (16 versus 9 percent) and more sedation (13 versus 9 percent). How long should maintenance last? Guidelines generally advise at least one to two years after a first episode and longer, often indefinitely, after repeated relapses. The question is not closed. In 2013 Lex Wunderink and colleagues in the Netherlands reported a seven-year follow-up of a trial in which patients who had recovered from a first episode were randomly assigned either to maintenance or to a cautious reduction or discontinuation of their dose. The reduction group had about twice as many relapses early on, but seven years later twice as many of them had recovered (40.4 versus 17.6 percent), the difference lying in everyday functioning rather than in symptoms. The authors themselves warned that the finding needs replication before it changes practice; what it shows is that the lowest effective dose, not simply the most medication, is the goal.

None of this helps if the medicine is not taken. Treatment adherence, the extent to which a person takes treatment as agreed with the clinician (the older word "compliance" implied obedience), is poor in schizophrenia, as it is in most long-term illnesses. In CATIE, as you saw, 74 percent of patients had stopped their assigned drug within 18 months, and partial adherence, missing some doses, is even commoner. The reasons are many: side effects such as sedation, weight gain, sexual problems and akathisia; lack of insight, since a person who does not believe he is ill sees no reason to take medicine for it (Section 4.2); feeling well and concluding that the drug is no longer needed; stigma; cost and distance from the clinic; a poor relationship with the prescriber; substance use; and family members who prefer a faith healer or fear that the tablets are addictive. Some patients, like a young man described by Barlow and Durand, "cheek" their tablets, holding them in the mouth until the nurse has gone and then spitting them out. Adherence improves when side effects are asked about and treated, the regimen is simple (one daily dose), decisions are shared, patient and family understand the illness, and a trusted relative helps; psychological approaches to adherence are discussed in Book 2.

For patients who repeatedly relapse after stopping tablets, or who prefer not to take a daily pill, there is the long-acting injectable antipsychotic, also called a depot injection. The drug is chemically bound to a fatty acid (as a decanoate, for example) and dissolved in oil, or made into tiny crystals or microspheres, and injected deep into a muscle, from which it is released slowly over weeks. The first-generation depots are fluphenazine decanoate (every two to four weeks), haloperidol decanoate (about every four weeks), and flupentixol and zuclopenthixol decanoate. The second-generation long-acting injections include risperidone microspheres (every two weeks), paliperidone palmitate (monthly, with three-monthly and six-monthly forms), long-acting aripiprazole (monthly) and olanzapine pamoate. Olanzapine pamoate requires the patient to be observed for three hours after each injection because of a rare post-injection delirium and sedation syndrome. Depots have real advantages. Delivery is guaranteed; a missed injection is known at once, so the team can act before a relapse; and the patient is spared the daily reminder of illness. In Leucht's 2012 meta-analysis, depot preparations reduced relapse more than oral drugs did. The disadvantages are pain at the injection site, less flexible dosing, side effects that take weeks to wear off once they appear, and, for some patients, a feeling of being controlled. After his second admission Prabhu chose a monthly injection at the district hospital, where it is free; he has not been readmitted in three years and has finished his diploma.

Exam trap

A depot is not a different or stronger drug: it is the same antipsychotic in a slow-release form, and its benefit comes from better adherence and the early detection of missed doses. Fluphenazine decanoate and haloperidol decanoate are typical depots; paliperidone palmitate and risperidone microspheres are atypical long-acting injections. Maintenance medication prevents relapse; it does not cure the illness, and most first-episode patients who stop medication relapse within a few years. Medication and family work reduce relapse in additive ways (Section 4.9 explains expressed emotion).

Other physical treatments: ECT and brain stimulation

Of the physical treatments of the 1930s, only one survives. In electroconvulsive therapy (ECT), doctors produce a brief, controlled seizure by passing a current through the brain of an anaesthetised patient whose muscles have been relaxed with a drug, usually in a course of six to twelve treatments given two or three times a week. How it is given, how it is thought to work and its main use, in severe depression, are taught in Chapter 5. Meduna's theory that seizures antagonise schizophrenia was wrong, and Barlow and Durand note that the early enthusiasm for ECT in schizophrenia faded once it proved unhelpful for most patients. It keeps a definite place, however, in four situations. The first is catatonia, in which ECT is the treatment of choice if a benzodiazepine (the lorazepam challenge described in Section 4.5) fails, and the emergency treatment for malignant catatonia. The second is severe acute psychosis with dangerous refusal of food and drink, or a violent or suicidal crisis, where a rapid response is needed. The third is schizophrenia with prominent mood symptoms, including schizoaffective disorder. The fourth is treatment resistance, especially as an addition to clozapine, as the Petrides trial in Section 4.10.6 showed. ECT is used more often for schizophrenia in India than in most Western countries.

Indian law regulates it closely, as Book 2, Section 1.10 explains. Section 95 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 prohibits ECT without muscle relaxants and anaesthesia (the old "unmodified" ECT) and prohibits ECT for minors, except that a psychiatrist may give it to a minor with the informed consent of the guardian and the prior permission of the Mental Health Review Board. Section 94 forbids the use of ECT as emergency treatment, and Section 96 allows psychosurgery only with the person's informed consent and the Board's approval.

Newer forms of brain stimulation are still being tested. The best studied, repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation (rTMS), uses a coil held against the scalp to produce rapidly changing magnetic fields that induce small currents in the cortex beneath. Ralph Hoffman and colleagues reported in 2000 that slow, repeated stimulation over the left temporoparietal cortex, an area active during auditory hallucinations, reduced persistent voices in some patients. Later studies confirmed a modest effect, but Barlow and Durand note that it lasts less than a month. Transcranial direct current stimulation, which passes a weak steady current through the scalp, is experimental. Insulin coma therapy and lobotomy are of historical interest only.

Psychosocial treatments that complete care

Antipsychotic medication is the front-line treatment for schizophrenia: no psychological treatment on its own has been shown to match it in the acute illness, and clinical guidelines everywhere place it first. But medicine reduces symptoms; it does not teach a person how to live with an illness, talk to a stranger, hold a job or rebuild a family's trust. Most patients therefore need psychosocial treatment alongside the drug, and the evidence shows that the combination does better than either alone. These treatments belong to the Clinical Psychology book, which teaches how each is done; what follows is a map of what each offers in schizophrenia and where to find it.

Cognitive behavioural therapy for psychosis (CBTp; Book 2, Section 5.6) helps the person examine the evidence for delusional beliefs, reinterpret voices so that they are less frightening and less powerful, cope with distress and plan to prevent relapse. Its effects on positive symptoms are real but modest, larger on distress than on the symptoms themselves, and guidelines such as those of the British National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) recommend offering it to everyone with schizophrenia, as an addition to medication.

Family psychoeducation and family intervention (Book 2, Section 7.2) grew directly out of the expressed emotion research described in Section 4.9. Families learn about the illness, its treatment and the early warning signs of relapse; they practise calmer communication and joint problem solving; critical comments and emotional over-involvement are gently reduced; and, where tension at home is high, the hours of face-to-face contact between patient and relative are reduced, for example through a day centre or a job. Family intervention reduces relapse and readmission, especially when combined with medication. It is the treatment for the family climate that predicts relapse; it was never a treatment for a family that "caused" schizophrenia.

Social skills training (Book 2, Section 4.7), developed in particular by Robert Paul Liberman and colleagues, breaks social behaviour (starting a conversation, asking for help, refusing a request, handling a job interview) into small steps that are modelled, rehearsed in role play, corrected with feedback and practised as homework. People do learn the skills that are taught; how well the skills carry over into everyday life is less certain. The token economy (Book 2, Section 4.5), introduced on psychiatric wards by Teodoro Ayllon and Nathan Azrin in their book The Token Economy (1968), rewards self-care and constructive activity with tokens exchangeable for privileges. In the best-known comparison, Gordon Paul and Robert Lentz (1977) found that a social learning programme built on these principles helped chronic hospital patients more than milieu therapy or routine care. Token economies are used much less today, as long-stay wards have closed and concerns about coercion have grown.

The aim of cognitive remediation is to improve the attention, memory, processing speed and problem solving that antipsychotics leave untouched (Section 4.3), through graded practice exercises, often on a computer, together with teaching strategies. In a 2011 meta-analysis of 40 studies with 2,104 participants, Til Wykes and colleagues found small to moderate, durable gains in cognition and in everyday functioning, with much larger gains in functioning when cognitive remediation was combined with other rehabilitation. The approach called supported employment, especially the Individual Placement and Support model, reverses the old sequence of "train, then place": the person is helped to find an ordinary paid job quickly and then supported in it, and it gets far more people with schizophrenia into work than traditional vocational rehabilitation.

The model of assertive community treatment (ACT; Book 2, Section 9.8), pioneered by Leonard Stein and Mary Ann Test in Madison, Wisconsin, and reported in 1980, brings treatment to people who do not come to clinics: a multidisciplinary team with small caseloads visits patients at home and at work, delivers medicine, helps with housing, money and benefits, and is available around the clock. It reduces time spent in hospital for people with severe illness who repeatedly disengage from services. Finally, early intervention services aim to shorten the duration of untreated psychosis (Section 4.6) and to give intensive, hopeful, youth-friendly care in the first years of the illness, when the course is thought to be most open to change. The model was developed in Melbourne in the early 1990s and has spread to many countries.

Exam trap

The front-line treatment for schizophrenia is antipsychotic medication. CBT, social skills training, family work and stress management (training in relaxation, problem solving and coping with daily stress) are valuable additions, not substitutes. Reducing face-to-face contact hours with a high-EE relative is a strategy for managing expressed emotion, and family intervention reduces relapse, not the onset of the disorder. Token economies are behavioural (operant) treatments for chronic institutionalised patients; social skills training is based on modelling and rehearsal.

Treating schizophrenia in India

Most people with schizophrenia in India live with their families, and the family is the main provider of care: it notices the illness, chooses where to seek help (very often a temple, dargah or faith healer first), supervises the medicine, absorbs the cost and carries the burden for decades. This is both a strength and a strain. Indian families have been found to show lower expressed emotion than Western ones (Section 4.9), and family involvement may be one reason for the better outcomes in the WHO studies described in Section 4.6. But caregivers, most often mothers and wives, report heavy financial and emotional burden, and services that support them are scarce.

Specialist care is concentrated in institutions such as the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS) in Bengaluru, the Central Institute of Psychiatry in Ranchi and the Institute of Human Behaviour and Allied Sciences (IHBAS) in Delhi, and in the psychiatry departments of medical colleges. Non-governmental organisations such as the Schizophrenia Research Foundation (SCARF) in Chennai, long led by R. Thara, have pioneered community care and research. To reach the districts, the National Mental Health Programme set up the District Mental Health Programme in 1996, and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 gave every person a legal right to access mental health care (Book 2, Section 1.10). Section 18 of the Act also requires that all medicines on the government's Essential Drug List be provided free of cost to people with mental illness at government health establishments, from Community Health Centres upwards. In practice, many patients receive inexpensive typical drugs, often haloperidol or trifluoperazine with trihexyphenidyl, or fluphenazine decanoate injections, alongside newer generic atypicals such as risperidone and olanzapine, which are now cheap in India.

Can care be delivered where specialists are scarce? The COPSI trial (Community care for People with Schizophrenia in India), led by Sudipto Chatterjee with Vikram Patel, R. Thara, Graham Thornicroft and others and published in 2014, tested this at three sites: Goa, Satara in Maharashtra, and rural Tamil Nadu. It randomly assigned 282 people with schizophrenia either to usual clinic-based care or to clinic care plus collaborative community care delivered by trained lay health workers under specialist supervision, including home visits, adherence support, psychoeducation for the family and help with rehabilitation. After a year the community care group had less disability and slightly fewer symptoms (the symptom difference fell just short of statistical significance), with the clearest benefit at the rural Tamil Nadu site, and the authors concluded that the approach is best used where services are scarce, such as rural areas. The lesson fits the whole of this section. The antipsychotic drug is the foundation of treatment, but recovery from schizophrenia is built by people: families, health workers, employers and the patient, over years.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Chlorpromazine was introduced into psychiatry in 1952 by Jean Delay and Pierre at the Sainte-Anne Hospital in Paris.
  2. Haloperidol, a butyrophenone, was made in 1958 by the team of Paul .
  3. All antipsychotics reduce transmission at dopamine receptors; clinical response becomes likely when about 65 percent of them are occupied.
  4. The extrapyramidal side effects arise from receptor blockade in the pathway, and raised prolactin from blockade in the tuberoinfundibular pathway.
  5. A subjective inner restlessness with a compelling need to pace, beginning within days or weeks of starting haloperidol, is .
  6. Involuntary lip smacking, chewing and tongue protrusion after years of antipsychotic treatment is , the last extrapyramidal effect to appear and the most likely to be irreversible.
  7. Fever, lead-pipe rigidity, autonomic instability, confusion and a raised creatine kinase after starting an antipsychotic indicate neuroleptic syndrome.
  8. Aripiprazole differs from other antipsychotics because it is a D2 agonist.
  9. After two adequate trials of antipsychotics have failed, the drug of choice is , which requires regular blood counts because of the risk of agranulocytosis.
  10. Fluphenazine decanoate and paliperidone palmitate are long-acting injections used to improve adherence and prevent relapse.

Answers: (1) Deniker, (2) Janssen, (3) D2, (4) nigrostriatal, (5) akathisia, (6) tardive dyskinesia, (7) malignant, (8) partial, (9) clozapine, (10) depot (antipsychotic).

The case of the Genain quadruplets, revisited

Go back to the four identical sisters born in Lansing, Michigan, on 19 May 1930, and to the National Institute of Mental Health, where David Rosenthal's team studied them from 1955 to 1958. You can now read their story as this chapter has taught it. Their genes loaded the dice heavily. Gottesman's figures put the risk for the identical twin of a patient at about 48 percent, and twin studies estimate the heritability of liability at around 80 percent, so four sisters who shared one genome and all fell ill (an outcome Mirsky put at about one in 1.5 billion) are what a strongly genetic, polygenic illness can occasionally produce. Yet the same figures say that more than half of identical co-twins never fall ill, and the Genains show why the inherited DNA sequence cannot be the whole story: their age at onset, their symptoms, their hospital stays and their eventual functioning differed widely. Hester, the first to fall ill, severely so by 18, and the sister who had more social problems than the others as she grew up, had the worst course and spent most of her adult life in hospital. Myra, the last to fall ill, was never readmitted after her first episode, and she married and raised a family. Early onset and poor premorbid adjustment on one side, a later onset on the other: these are the poor and good prognostic signs of Table 4.26. And the sisters' test scores at 66, stable or better than in their twenties, answer Kraepelin's word "dementia": the cognitive impairment of schizophrenia is largely present early and then stays fairly stable, rather than marching downhill.

What made four lives out of one genome? Section 4.7 gave the answer that behaviour geneticists call the non-shared environment: even identical sisters differ in their position in the womb, their blood supply and birth weight, the complications of their delivery, the illnesses of their childhood and the way their parents treat them, and the neurodevelopmental hypothesis explains how such early differences can lie silent until the maturing brain unmasks them in adolescence and early adulthood. The controlling, abusive home that later accounts describe, and parents who did not treat the four alike, belong to the other side of the vulnerability-stress model of Section 4.9. Family life does not cause schizophrenia (the schizophrenogenic mother and the double bind were rejected as causes), but stress acting on a vulnerable brain can trigger an episode, and a critical, hostile or over-involved family climate, high expressed emotion, predicts relapse. A clinician meeting four such sisters today would start antipsychotic medication early, to shorten the duration of untreated psychosis, and continue it to prevent relapse; would offer clozapine to any sister whose illness resisted two adequate trials of other drugs; and would add family psychoeducation, CBT for psychosis and rehabilitation. Bleuler's plural, the group of schizophrenias, fits the Genains well: one diagnosis, one genome and four different illnesses. That is why this chapter has treated schizophrenia not as a fixed fate but as a vulnerability whose course genes, development, stress and treatment decide together.

Chapter summary

4.1 From Dementia Praecox to Schizophrenia. Schizophrenia is a concept built in layers over about a hundred and fifty years. Haslam (1810) recorded in Matthews's "Air Loom" a near-complete set of first-rank symptoms. Morel gave démence précoce (1852) and degeneration theory; Kahlbaum classified by clinical course, Hecker described hebephrenia (1871) and Kahlbaum catatonia (1874). Kraepelin's dementia praecox (1893) absorbed these forms, and in 1899 he split it by course from manic-depressive insanity, the Kraepelinian dichotomy. Bleuler named schizophrenia (1908, 1911), a splitting of psychic functions, not of personality, and ranked fundamental symptoms (the four As: associations, affect, ambivalence, autism) above accessory hallucinations and delusions. Jaspers (1913) founded phenomenology and defined the primary delusion. Schneider's first-rank symptoms (1939) are characteristic but not pathognomonic. The US-UK Diagnostic Project (Cooper, Kendell and Gurland, 1972) exposed American breadth, and the Feighner criteria (1972) and DSM-III (1980) narrowed the concept with a six-month rule.

4.2 Positive Symptoms: Delusions, Hallucinations and Disorganisation. Positive symptoms add something unusual to experience; negative ones take something normal away. Jaspers gave delusions three marks (certainty, incorrigibility, impossibility) and separated ununderstandable primary delusions from secondary ones. Delusional mood (Conrad's trema, 1958) precedes delusional perception (his apophany); Hambrecht and Häfner (1993) confirmed only trema as a common first phase. Persecutory delusions are commonest, held with Bleuler's double bookkeeping, and DSM-5 ended the special weight of bizarre delusions. Auditory hallucinations dominate (about 60 to 80 percent); third-person voices and thought echo are first-rank, second-person voices are not. Kandinsky described pseudohallucinations; Waters (2014) found visual hallucinations in 27 percent and McGrath (2015) psychotic experiences in 5.8 percent of adults; Romme and Escher began the Hearing Voices Movement. Bleuler's loosening, Meehl's cognitive slippage (1962), was defined form by form by Andreasen (1979). Disorganised behaviour, inappropriate affect and catatonia follow; insight has David's three dimensions (1990) and Amador's continuum (1993).

4.3 Negative and Cognitive Symptoms, and the Symptom Dimensions. Negative symptoms, losses of normal function, account for much of schizophrenia's disability; the terms date from Reynolds (around 1861) and Hughlings Jackson. The NIMH-MATRICS consensus (2006) fixed five: blunted affect, alogia, avolition, anhedonia and asociality. Berenbaum and Oltmanns (1992) found blunted patients feel but do not express emotion; Gard and Kring (2007) located anhedonia in anticipatory, not consummatory, pleasure. Secondary negative symptoms arise from positive symptoms, depression, antipsychotics and understimulation (Wing and Brown's institutionalism study); Carpenter's deficit syndrome (1988) requires primary ones for 12 months. Cognitive deficits are broad (Heinrichs and Zakzanis, 1998), span Nuechterlein's seven MATRICS domains (2004) and predict functioning better than psychotic symptoms (Green, 1996). Crow (1980) proposed positive Type I and negative Type II syndromes, Andreasen and Olsen (1982) positive, negative and mixed subtypes, Liddle (1987) reality distortion, disorganisation and psychomotor poverty, and PANSS analyses five factors; DSM-5 and ICD-11 rate symptom dimensions.

4.4 Diagnosing Schizophrenia: DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11. With no test or pathognomonic symptom, operational criteria decide; the Feighner criteria (1972) gave DSM-III (1980) its six-month rule. DSM-5-TR Criterion A needs two of five symptoms, at least one being delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech; B, functional decline; C, six months of continuous signs, including at least one month of active phase; D to F exclude mood disorders and substance or medical causes and, after autism, demand prominent delusions or hallucinations. DSM-5 (2013) dropped the unreliable subtypes. ICD-10 F20 needs one very clear symptom from groups (a) to (d), largely Schneider's first-rank symptoms, or two groups from (e) to (h), for one month; its subtypes are paranoid, hebephrenic, catatonic, undifferentiated, residual, simple and post-schizophrenic depression. ICD-11 6A20 needs two of seven, at least one from (a) to (d), for one month, with course and symptom specifiers, not subtypes; neither ICD requires decline. Mimics include anti-NMDA receptor encephalitis (Dalmau, 2007).

4.5 The Rest of the Spectrum. The duration ladder separates brief psychotic disorder (at least 1 day and under 1 month, full recovery), schizophreniform disorder (1 to under 6 months, no decline needed; Langfeldt, 1937 and 1939) and schizophrenia (6 months). ICD's acute and transient psychotic disorders absorbed Magnan's bouffée délirante and Kleist's and Leonhard's (1957) cycloid psychosis. Kasanin coined schizoaffective (1933); DSM-5-TR requires a major mood episode with Criterion A, two weeks of psychosis alone, and mood for most of the illness; ICD uses the single episode. Delusional disorder has seven types, persecutory the commonest; erotomanic is de Clérambault's syndrome, jealous Todd and Dewhurst's Othello syndrome (1955), somatic Ekbom's delusional parasitosis (1938). Lasègue and Falret (1877) described folie à deux, ICD-10's induced delusional disorder. Secondary psychoses have known causes. Catatonia (three of twelve signs; lorazepam, then ECT) is a DSM-5 specifier across disorders. McGorry and Yung's high-risk state led to attenuated psychosis syndrome.

4.6 Who Develops Schizophrenia and What Happens Next. About 1 percent develop schizophrenia (DSM-5-TR: 0.3 to 0.7 percent). Onset peaks in men's early to mid-20s and women's late 20s, with a second female peak around 45; men's incidence is about 1.4 times women's, prevalence roughly equal. Risk rises with urban birth (Mortensen, 1999), migration (Cantor-Graae and Selten, 2005) and winter and spring birth (Torrey, 1997); low social class largely reflects social drift (Goldberg and Morrison, 1963). The phases are premorbid, prodromal, active and residual, and longer untreated psychosis goes with worse outcome (Marshall, 2005). Manfred Bleuler (1972) and Ciompi (1976) overturned Kraepelin's pessimism. The WHO's International Pilot Study (1973) and Determinants of Outcome study (Jablensky and Sartorius, 1992) found better outcomes in developing countries, questioned by Cohen and colleagues (2008); SCARF (Sarada Menon, Thara) ran the Madras Longitudinal Study. Acute, late onset bodes well; life is about 15 years shorter, and 5 to 6 percent die by suicide.

4.7 Genes, Development and the Brain. Kallmann (1938) found that a general vulnerability runs in families. Gottesman's lifetime risks (1991) rise from 1 percent (general population) to about 46 (child of two affected parents) and 48 (identical twin). Shields and Gottesman's polygenic liability-threshold theory (1967) explains why most identical co-twins stay well. Adoption studies (Heston, 1966; Kety, Rosenthal and Wender, from 1968) tied risk to biological, not adoptive, families; Tienari found gene-environment interaction. Genome-wide studies found 108 loci (2014); the 22q11.2 deletion, older fathers (Malaspina) and C4 variants (Sekar and McCarroll, 2016) add risk. Holzman's smooth pursuit eye movement dysfunction (1973) is the classic endophenotype. The neurodevelopmental hypothesis (Weinberger; Murray and Lewis, 1987) holds that an early lesion is unmasked by adolescent maturation; Mednick's second-trimester influenza effect supports it. Johnstone found enlarged ventricles (1976) and Ingvar and Franzén hypofrontality (1974); Feinberg proposed faulty pruning (1982); Andréasson linked heavy cannabis use to sixfold risk (1987).

4.8 Neurochemistry: Dopamine, Glutamate and Serotonin. The dopamine hypothesis began with drugs. Antipsychotics caused parkinsonism; Carlsson showed dopamine is a neurotransmitter, Hornykiewicz found it depleted in Parkinson's disease (1960), and Carlsson and Lindqvist proposed receptor blockade (1963). Amphetamine psychosis (Connell, 1958; Angrist and Gershon, 1970) mimics paranoid schizophrenia. Decisive evidence came in 1976: clinical potency tracks D2 binding (Creese, Burt and Snyder; Seeman). D2 blockade eases positive symptoms (mesolimbic), may worsen negative ones (mesocortical), causes extrapyramidal effects (nigrostriatal) and raises prolactin (tuberoinfundibular). The theory's weaknesses led to Davis's revised hypothesis (1991), prefrontal deficit plus subcortical excess, and Howes and Kapur's version III (2009), presynaptic striatal excess as the final common pathway to psychosis, experienced as aberrant salience (Kapur, 2003). Luby's phencyclidine psychosis (1959) led Javitt and Zukin (1991) to NMDA receptor hypofunction, confirmed with ketamine (Krystal, 1994); Hofmann's LSD is a poor model, and Meltzer (1989) tied atypicality to stronger 5-HT2A than D2 blockade.

4.9 Psychological, Family and Social Explanations. Psychodynamic theories are now historical: Freud (Schreber case, 1911) saw libido withdrawn into the self, Federn weak ego boundaries, Sullivan interpersonal anxiety, Arieti palaeologic thinking and Mahler fixation at the autistic phase without a stable symbiosis. Cognitive accounts explain symptoms: jumping to conclusions on the beads task (Huq, Garety and Hemsley, 1988), Bentall's externalising and personalising attributions, Frith's theory of mind and self-monitoring failures (1992) and Garety and colleagues' cognitive model (2001). Fromm-Reichmann's schizophrenogenic mother, the double bind (Bateson and colleagues, 1956), marital schism and skew (Lidz) and pseudomutuality (Wynne) were rejected as causes. Expressed emotion (critical comments, hostility, emotional over-involvement) predicts relapse, not onset (Brown, Birley and Wing, 1972; Vaughn and Leff, 1976); medication and reducing contact hours (below about 35 a week) protect. In Zubin and Spring's vulnerability-stress model (1977), stress triggers episodes. Labelling (Scheff) and Laing fail as causes; social defeat links exclusion to dopamine.

4.10 Treating Schizophrenia: Antipsychotic Drugs and Beyond. Sakel's insulin coma, Meduna's convulsions, Cerletti and Bini's ECT and Moniz's leucotomy gave way to chlorpromazine, noticed by Laborit, introduced by Delay and Deniker (1952), and to Janssen's haloperidol (1958). Every antipsychotic reduces D2 transmission in all four dopamine pathways; Kapur and Zipursky (2000) found a therapeutic window of occupancy. Atypicals, beginning with clozapine, cause fewer movement but more metabolic effects than typical drugs, though in CATIE (Lieberman, 2005) perphenazine matched most atypicals. The four extrapyramidal effects appear in order: acute dystonia, akathisia, parkinsonism and tardive dyskinesia. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (fever, rigidity, confusion, raised creatine kinase) is an emergency. Clozapine, the most effective drug, is reserved for treatment resistance (Kane and colleagues, 1988) because of agranulocytosis. Stopping medication predicts relapse (Robinson, 1999); hence maintenance and depots. CBT for psychosis, family intervention, social skills training (Liberman), cognitive remediation (Wykes), supported employment and assertive community treatment (Stein and Test) complete care.
