hypotension, QTc prolongation, treatment-resistant schizophrenia, clozapine, agranulocytosis, myocarditis, hypersalivation, maintenance treatment, adherence, long-acting injectable antipsychotic, depot injection, electroconvulsive therapy, repetitive transcranial magnetic stimulation, cognitive remediation, supported employment, assertive community treatment, early intervention services

From the past papers

  1. The concept of "Dementia Praecox," which translates to "premature dementia," is historically most directly associated with which contemporary diagnostic category? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Alzheimer's Disease (b) Bipolar I Disorder (c) Schizophrenia (d) Frontotemporal Dementia

    Answer: (c) Schizophrenia. Emil Kraepelin used the term in the fourth edition of his textbook (1893) and in the sixth edition (1899) separated dementia praecox from manic-depressive insanity by course; Bleuler renamed it schizophrenia (1908, 1911). "Dementia" referred to a supposedly deteriorating course, not to a dementia in the modern sense: Alzheimer's disease and frontotemporal dementia (Pick's disease) are neurocognitive disorders, and bipolar I disorder descends from the other half of Kraepelin's dichotomy, manic-depressive insanity.

  2. The term 'Ambivalence' was coined by: (RML 2024)

    (a) Kraepelin (b) Eugen Bleuler (c) Richmond Fromm (d) Sigmund Freud

    Answer: (b) Eugen Bleuler. Ambivalence, opposite feelings, wishes or ideas held towards the same thing at the same time, is one of Bleuler's four As, the fundamental symptoms of his "group of schizophrenias". Freud borrowed the word from Bleuler, and Kraepelin defined dementia praecox by its course, not by this symptom. "Richmond Fromm" is a garbled name: Frieda Fromm-Reichmann proposed the schizophrenogenic mother (1948), and Erich Fromm was a social psychoanalyst.

  3. Eugen Bleuler's four A's of schizophrenia include: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Attention, Affect, Ambivalence, Aphasia (b) Affect, Amnesia, Autism, Anxiety (c) Affect, Ambivalence, Autism, Association disturbances (d) Affect, Anhedonia, Autism, Avolition

    Answer: (c). The four As are loosening of associations, blunted or incongruous affect, ambivalence and autism (withdrawal into an inner world); hallucinations and delusions were only accessory symptoms for Bleuler. Option (d) mixes in anhedonia and avolition, which belong to the later negative-symptom list associated with Nancy Andreasen; attention appears on Andreasen's negative-symptom scale, not Bleuler's list; aphasia, amnesia and anxiety are on no list at all.

  4. "First rank symptoms" of schizophrenia given by Schneider include all except (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Depersonalization (b) Hallucinatory voice in the form of running commentary (c) Delusional perception (d) Thought broadcasting

    Answer: (a) Depersonalization. Kurt Schneider's first-rank symptoms are three kinds of voices (audible thoughts, voices arguing or discussing, a running commentary), somatic passivity, thought insertion, withdrawal and broadcasting, made feelings, impulses and volitional acts, and delusional perception; in almost every one the normal boundary between self and world is breached. Depersonalisation, the feeling that one's self is unreal or changed, is experienced "as if", with insight, and is common in anxiety and dissociative states (Chapter 7).

  5. Which of the following is NOT a Schneider's first-rank symptom? (RML 2024)

    (a) Auditory hallucination (b) Delusional perception (c) Passivity phenomenon (d) Delusion of self-reference

    Answer: (d) Delusion of self-reference. Delusions of reference, the belief that remarks, broadcasts and ordinary events are aimed at oneself, are common in schizophrenia but are not first-rank. Delusional perception and the passivity (made) experiences are. Strictly, only three forms of auditory hallucination are first-rank (audible thoughts, voices arguing or discussing, and a running commentary), and a voice simply addressing the patient as "you" is second-rank, so read option (a) as standing for Schneider's voices and choose the delusion of self-reference.

  6. We know that a distinction is made between positive and negative symptoms of schizophrenia. What is the difference between these symptoms? (RML 2020)

    (a) Negative symptoms indicate the presence of something normal and negative symptoms indicate the absence of something unusual (b) Positive symptoms indicate the presence of something normal, and negative symptoms indicate the absence of something unusual (c) Positive symptoms indicate the presence of something unusual, and negative symptoms indicate the absence of something normal (d) Positive symptoms indicate the absence of something normal, negative symptoms indicate the presence of something unusual

    Answer: (c). Positive symptoms add something that a healthy mind does not produce, an excess or distortion of normal function: delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech and grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour. Negative symptoms take away or reduce something normal: blunted affect, alogia, avolition, anhedonia and asociality. Every other option swaps "normal" and "unusual" or presence and absence.

  7. Hallucinations most often occur in people suffering from: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Mania (b) Schizophrenia (c) Depression (d) Personality Disorders

    Answer: (b) Schizophrenia. Auditory hallucinations alone are found in about 60 to 80 percent of people with a schizophrenia spectrum diagnosis, and hallucinations are central to the picture. Mania and depression with psychotic features produce mood-congruent voices, and in borderline personality disorder brief hallucinations can appear under stress, but in all three they are less frequent and less central. No hallucination is specific to schizophrenia.

  8. Negative symptoms of schizophrenia are all except (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Marked apathy (b) Paucity of speech (c) Incongruity of emotional responses (d) Delusion

    Answer: (d) Delusion. A delusion is a positive symptom. The other three options are, almost word for word, ICD-10's description of the negative symptoms of its group (h): "marked apathy, paucity of speech, and blunting or incongruity of emotional responses". Incongruity is the tempting wrong pick, because Liddle grouped inappropriate affect with disorganisation, but ICD-10 counts it among the negative symptoms.

  9. Following are negative features of schizophrenia except: (RML 2024)

    (a) Avolition (b) Alogia (c) Ambivalence (d) Affective blunting

    Answer: (c) Ambivalence. Ambivalence is one of Bleuler's four As, not a negative symptom. Avolition, alogia and affective blunting are three of the five negative symptoms fixed by the NIMH-MATRICS consensus (2006), with anhedonia and asociality. The trap is the letter A: Bleuler's As (associations, affect, ambivalence, autism) and the negative As are different lists.

  10. The ICD-10 duration criterion for schizophrenia requires symptoms to persist for at least: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) 6 months (b) 2 weeks (c) 3 months (d) 1 month

    Answer: (d) 1 month. ICD-10 (F20) requires the symptoms for most of the time during a period of one month or more, and ICD-11 (6A20) keeps the same rule (Real PYQ 2025 asked it for ICD-11). Six months is DSM-5-TR's total, including at least one month of active-phase symptoms; 2 weeks is the ICD onset window of acute and transient psychotic disorder; 3 months is the ICD minimum for delusional disorder.

  11. Which of the following are not included in the ICD criteria for Schizophrenia? (RML 2021)

    (a) Delusions (b) Catastrophic Thinking (c) Disorganised speech (d) Hallucinations

    Answer: (b) Catastrophic Thinking. Catastrophic thinking is a cognitive distortion from Aaron Beck's cognitive model of anxiety and depression, not a psychotic symptom. Delusions (groups (a), (b) and (d) of ICD-10), hallucinations (groups (c) and (e)) and disorganised speech (group (f): breaks in the train of thought, incoherence, neologisms) are all part of the ICD criteria.

  12. The presence of delusions, hallucinations, disorganized thinking, and grossly disorganized or abnormal motor behavior, lasting for at least one month but less than six months, is indicative of: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Schizoaffective Disorder (b) Delusional Disorder (c) Brief Psychotic Disorder (d) Schizophrenia

    Answer: schizophreniform disorder, which is not among the options; the official key, (c) Brief Psychotic Disorder, is wrong. In DSM-5-TR, brief psychotic disorder lasts at least 1 day but less than 1 month, with full return to the premorbid level; 1 month to less than 6 months is schizophreniform disorder; 6 months or more is schizophrenia (Figure 4.8). Schizoaffective disorder needs a major mood episode, and delusional disorder has delusions without the other symptoms. Under ICD-10 or ICD-11, with their one-month rule, the same illness would be schizophrenia. If you meet this exact question again, know that the key was (c); whenever schizophreniform disorder is offered, choose it.

  13. "Folie à deux," or Shared Psychotic Disorder, is characterized by: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Two individuals simultaneously experiencing independent psychotic episodes (b) The transmission of delusional beliefs from one individual to another who are in a close relationship (c) A delusion that is shared by an entire cultural or subcultural group (d) A brief psychotic episode triggered by extreme stress in two closely related people

    Answer: (b). Lasègue and Falret (1877) described a delusion passing from a primary partner with an established psychotic illness, usually the more dominant, to a more passive, dependent partner in a close and socially isolated relationship; on separation the secondary partner's belief often fades. Two independent psychoses (a) and a brief stress psychosis in two relatives (d) involve no transmission, and a belief shared by a whole cultural group (c) is a shared belief, not a delusion. ICD-10 codes the condition F24, induced delusional disorder.

  14. Catatonia is classified in DSM-5 as: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) An independent disorder (b) A specifier across several disorders (c) A personality disorder (d) A subtype of bipolar disorder

    Answer: (b) A specifier across several disorders. Since DSM-5, catatonia (three or more of twelve psychomotor signs) is a specifier added to psychotic, bipolar, depressive, neurodevelopmental and other disorders, alongside catatonic disorder due to another medical condition and unspecified catatonia. It is not an independent disorder, not a personality disorder and not a subtype of bipolar disorder (nor of schizophrenia, whose subtypes DSM-5 dropped); it is in fact more often associated with mood disorders than with schizophrenia.

  15. Schizophrenia is most commonly found in: (RML 2022)

    (a) Adolescents (b) Young Adults (c) The Middle-Aged (d) The Elderly

    Answer: (b) Young Adults. DSM-5-TR states that psychotic features typically emerge between the late teens and the mid-30s, with the peak onset in the early to mid-20s for men and the late 20s for women. Onset before adolescence is rare, and the middle-aged and elderly account only for a smaller second peak in women around the menopause and for late-onset cases.

  16. The chance of developing schizophrenia if you have an identical twin with the disorder is approximately: (RML 2021)

    (a) 1% (b) 10% (c) 80% (d) 50%

    Answer: (d) 50%. Irving Gottesman's pooled figures (1991) put the lifetime risk for the identical twin of a patient at about 48 percent, against 17 for a fraternal twin, 9 for a sibling and 1 for the general population. So 1 percent is the population risk and 10 percent roughly a sibling's, while 80 percent is the approximate heritability of liability, not a concordance. Modern register studies give lower concordance (33 percent in Hilker's Danish twins, 2018), but "about 50 percent" is the exam answer.

  17. Schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase of development and the person fails to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis, was the developmental theory of schizophrenia given by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Ping-Nie Pao (b) Sylvano Arieti (c) Margaret Mahler (d) Edith Jacobson

    Answer: (c) Margaret Mahler. Mahler, the child analyst of separation-individuation, held that the infant passes from a normal autistic phase through a normal symbiotic phase to separation, and in a 1952 paper she described autistic and symbiotic psychoses of childhood. Silvano Arieti is palaeologic thinking and the von Domarus principle, Ping-Nie Pao organismic panic, and Edith Jacobson the fusion of self and object representations in psychotic regression.

  18. Gregory Bateson describes a classic example of the interaction between a mother and her son who had fairly well recovered from an acute schizophrenic episode. Bateson described this interaction as follows: '[The son] was glad to see her [his mother] and impulsively put his arm around her shoulders, whereupon she stiffened. He withdrew his arm and she asked, Don't you love me anymore? He then blushed, and she said, Dear, you must not be so easily embarrassed and afraid of your feelings. The patient was able to stay with her only a few minutes more, and following her departure, he assaulted an aide and was put in the isolation room.' Bateson used this interaction to illustrate which ONE of the following concepts? (RML 2021)

    (a) The cybernetic system (b) The homeostatic system (c) The incongruent family dyad (d) The symptomatic double bind

    Answer: (d) The symptomatic double bind. Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland (1956) offered this episode as their example of the double bind as a supposed cause of symptoms: the mother's stiffening and her words contradict each other at two levels, and the son can neither comment on the contradiction nor leave. "Cybernetic system" (a family as a self-regulating system steered by feedback) and "homeostatic system" (Don Jackson's idea that a family resists change to keep its balance) are general systems ideas, and incongruence is Satir's and Rogers's word. The double bind is no longer accepted as a cause of schizophrenia.

  19. Expressed Emotion (EE) is a significant predictor of relapse in schizophrenia. Which of the following is NOT one of the three core dimensions of high Expressed Emotion? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Hostility (b) Critical Comments (c) Emotional Over-involvement (d) Warmth and Positive Regard

    Answer: (d) Warmth and Positive Regard. The Camberwell Family Interview rates critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement, and these three make up high EE. It also rates warmth and positive remarks, but they are not part of the high-EE index; López and colleagues (2004) even found that family warmth predicted fewer relapses in Mexican-American families.

  20. High EE (Expressed Emotion) is known to cause: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Poor treatment outcome (b) Relapse of psychotic illness (c) Negative symptoms of schizophrenia (d) Violent behavior in schizophrenic patients

    Answer: (b) Relapse of psychotic illness. Brown, Birley and Wing (1972) found that patients returning to high-EE homes were roughly three to four times as likely to relapse in the nine months after discharge, Vaughn and Leff (1976) replicated the finding, and Butzlaff and Hooley's meta-analysis (1998) confirmed it. High EE predicts relapse, not the onset of the illness, its negative symptoms or violence, and "poor treatment outcome" is too vague to be the best answer.

  21. Reducing contact hours is one of the important strategies in the management of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Interpersonal violence (b) Para suicidal risk (c) Expressed emotion (d) Stubbornness in children

    Answer: (c) Expressed emotion. Vaughn and Leff (1976) found two protective factors in high-EE homes: regular maintenance medication and less than about 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact with the high-EE relative. Patients with neither protection relapsed almost without exception. Reducing contact hours, together with family psychoeducation, is therefore a way of managing high EE; it is not a standard strategy for violence, parasuicide risk or a stubborn child.

  22. The front-line treatment for schizophrenia is: (RML 2021)

    (a) Cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT) (b) Stress management training (c) Antipsychotic medication (d) Social skills training

    Answer: (c) Antipsychotic medication. Since Delay and Deniker introduced chlorpromazine in 1952, antipsychotics (neuroleptics, used primarily for schizophrenia and the other psychotic disorders, as RML 2025 asked) have been the front-line treatment: no psychological treatment on its own matches them in the acute illness, and stopping them is the strongest predictor of relapse after a first episode. CBT for psychosis, social skills training and stress management are valuable additions, not substitutes.

  23. A 45-year-old man with schizophrenia has been on antipsychotic medication for years. He presents with repetitive, involuntary, and purposeless movements of his mouth, tongue, and face, including lip-smacking and grimacing. This condition is most likely: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Akathisia (b) Acute Dystonia (c) Tardive Dyskinesia (d) Parkinsonism

    Answer: (c) Tardive Dyskinesia. Years of treatment plus involuntary chewing, lip smacking, tongue protrusion and grimacing is tardive dyskinesia: it appears after at least 3 months (usually months to years), is the last extrapyramidal effect to appear and the most likely to be irreversible, and is caused by neuroleptics, not by antidepressants, anxiolytics or barbiturates (RML 2022). Akathisia is a subjective restlessness, acute dystonia sudden painful spasms within hours to days, and parkinsonism slowness, rigidity and tremor after a few weeks.

  24. A client recently started antipsychotic medication and reports an intense, subjective feeling of inner restlessness and a compelling need to pace or move constantly. This side effect is best described as: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Akathisia (b) Tardive Dyskinesia (c) Dystonia (d) Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome

    Answer: (a) Akathisia. Akathisia is an inner restlessness with an urge to move (pacing, rocking from foot to foot) that begins within hours to about four weeks of starting or raising the dose; lowering the dose, propranolol or a benzodiazepine help. Tardive dyskinesia comes after months or years and is involuntary rather than driven by an urge, dystonia is a painful muscle spasm, and neuroleptic malignant syndrome is the emergency of fever, rigidity, autonomic instability and confusion with a raised creatine kinase.

  25. Which movement disorder is a secondary one? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Huntington's chorea (b) Parkinson's (c) Akathisia (d) None of these

    Answer: (c) Akathisia. A secondary movement disorder has an identifiable outside cause such as a drug, a toxin, a stroke or an infection, and akathisia, like all four extrapyramidal side effects, is caused by antipsychotic drugs. Huntington's disease (described by George Huntington in 1872; an inherited expansion in the huntingtin gene) and Parkinson's disease (James Parkinson, 1817; degeneration of the substantia nigra) are primary movement disorders, so (d) is wrong too.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Positive symptoms are the presence of something unusual (delusions, hallucinations, disorganised speech, grossly disorganised or catatonic behaviour); negative symptoms are the absence of something normal: blunted (flat) affect, alogia (poverty of speech), avolition (apathy), anhedonia and asociality (NIMH-MATRICS, 2006). ICD-10 adds "marked apathy, paucity of speech, and blunting or incongruity of emotional responses". In an "all except" list the exception is a positive symptom (delusion, auditory hallucination) or ambivalence, one of Bleuler's four As. asked 8 times
  2. Schneider's first-rank symptoms: audible thoughts (thought echo, Gedankenlautwerden), voices arguing or discussing, voices giving a running commentary, somatic passivity, thought insertion, thought withdrawal, thought broadcasting, made feelings, made impulses, made volitional acts, and delusional perception; delusions of control are first-rank. Not first-rank: depersonalisation, loose associations, delusions of self-reference, catatonic signs, negative symptoms, second-person voices. asked 7 times
  3. Expressed emotion (George Brown and colleagues; the Camberwell Family Interview) is a family climate of critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement; warmth and positive regard are rated but are not high-EE dimensions. High EE predicts relapse (Brown, Birley and Wing, 1972: about three to four times the risk within nine months), not onset, negative symptoms or violence. Vaughn and Leff (1976): maintenance medication and less than about 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact protect, so reducing contact hours is a way of managing high EE. asked 4 times
  4. DSM-5-TR schizophrenia needs at least 6 months of continuous signs of disturbance, including at least 1 month of active-phase (Criterion A) symptoms, or less if successfully treated; prodromal and residual periods count towards the six months. Criterion A is two of five symptoms, at least one of them delusions, hallucinations or disorganised speech. asked 7 times
  5. Folie à deux (Charles Lasègue and Jules Falret, 1877): a delusion transmitted from a primary partner with an established psychotic disorder, usually the more dominant, to a more passive partner in a close, socially isolated relationship; on separation the secondary partner's belief often fades. ICD-10: F24 induced delusional disorder; DSM-IV: shared psychotic disorder; DSM-5-TR: delusional disorder or other specified; ICD-11: within delusional disorder (6A24), among the schizophrenia or other primary psychotic disorders. A belief shared by a whole cultural group is not folie à deux. asked 6 times
  6. Dopamine is the transmitter most implicated in the positive symptoms: in the classic hypothesis they arise from hyperactivity of the mesolimbic pathway, which runs from the ventral tegmental area to the nucleus accumbens (ventral striatum) and limbic structures and serves reward. Not serotonin, GABA or acetylcholine. asked 6 times
  7. Bleuler's four As (Eugen Bleuler; lecture 1908, monograph 1911): loosening of Associations, blunted or incongruous Affect, Ambivalence and Autism, his fundamental symptoms; hallucinations and delusions were only accessory. Bleuler coined ambivalence (not Kraepelin; Freud borrowed it). Schizophrenia means a splitting of psychic functions, not a split personality. asked 4 times
  8. The double bind (Gregory Bateson with Don Jackson, Jay Haley and John Weakland, 1956) was a family-communication theory of the cause of schizophrenia: contradictory messages at two levels from someone one depends on, with no way to comment or escape. The mother who stiffens at her son's embrace and asks "Don't you love me any more?" is the symptomatic double bind. Not a cause today, but seminal for family therapy. asked 4 times
  9. Tardive dyskinesia: involuntary chewing, lip smacking, tongue protrusion and grimacing after at least 3 months, usually months to years, of antipsychotic treatment; the last extrapyramidal effect to appear and the most likely to be irreversible; caused by neuroleptics, not antidepressants, anxiolytics or barbiturates; explained by dopamine receptor supersensitivity. asked 4 times
  10. The primary target of typical antipsychotics is the D2 receptor (not D1, 5-HT2A or muscarinic M1), and D2 blockade in the nigrostriatal pathway (substantia nigra to caudate and putamen) causes the extrapyramidal side effects; D2 blockade in the tuberoinfundibular pathway raises prolactin. asked 5 times
  11. ICD-10 (F20) and ICD-11 (6A20) require schizophrenia's symptoms for at least 1 month; the 6-month rule is DSM's. Two weeks is the ICD onset window of acute and transient psychotic disorder; 3 months is the ICD minimum for delusional disorder. asked 3 times
  12. Kurt Schneider (1887 to 1967) described the first-rank symptoms (list first published 1939; Clinical Psychopathology, English edition 1959), not Kraepelin, Bleuler, Jaspers, Bateson or Wynne (printed "Wayne"). They are most associated with schizophrenia but are not pathognomonic: they also occur in mania. asked 3 times
  13. Emil Kraepelin's dementia praecox ("premature dementia"; fourth edition, 1893) is the ancestor of schizophrenia, not of Alzheimer's or frontotemporal dementia. In the sixth edition (1899) he separated his two clinical types, dementia praecox and manic-depressive insanity, by course: deteriorating versus episodic with recovery. asked 3 times
  14. Akathisia: a subjective inner restlessness with a compelling need to pace, within hours to about four weeks of starting or raising an antipsychotic; lower the dose, add propranolol or a benzodiazepine. Akathisia and tardive dyskinesia are secondary (drug-induced) movement disorders; Huntington's chorea and Parkinson's disease are primary. asked 2 times
  15. Antipsychotic medication is the front-line treatment for schizophrenia; CBT, social skills training and stress management are additions, not substitutes. Neuroleptics are used primarily for schizophrenia and the other psychotic disorders (also acute mania), not for depression or anxiety. Chlorpromazine: Jean Delay and Pierre Deniker, 1952. asked 2 times
  16. The duration ladder (DSM-5-TR): brief psychotic disorder, at least 1 day but less than 1 month, with full return to premorbid functioning; schizophreniform disorder, 1 month to less than 6 months; schizophrenia, 6 months or more. The IHBAS 2025 key that calls a 1 to 6 month episode "brief psychotic disorder" is wrong (schizophreniform was not offered). asked once
  17. For the identical twin of a patient the lifetime risk is about 48 percent ("about 50 percent"); child of two affected parents 46, fraternal twin 17, child of one affected parent 13, sibling 9, general population 1 (Irving Gottesman, 1991). About 80 percent is the heritability, not the concordance. asked once
  18. Catastrophic thinking is a cognitive distortion from Beck's model of anxiety and depression and is not in the ICD criteria for schizophrenia; delusions, hallucinations and disorganised speech are. asked once
  19. Schizophrenia is most commonly found in young adults: onset between the late teens and the mid-30s, peaking in the early to mid-20s in men and the late 20s in women, with a second female peak around the menopause; incidence about 1.4 times higher in men, lifetime prevalence roughly equal. asked once
  20. Margaret Mahler: schizophrenia corresponds to the autistic phase, with failure to develop an adequate and stable symbiosis (symbiotic psychosis). Silvano Arieti: palaeologic thinking and the von Domarus principle; Ping-Nie Pao: organismic panic; Edith Jacobson: fusion of self and object representations. asked once
  21. Hallucinations most often occur in schizophrenia; auditory ones are the commonest kind (about 60 to 80 percent of people with a schizophrenia spectrum diagnosis). Mania, psychotic depression and, briefly under stress, borderline personality disorder can produce them too. asked once
  22. In DSM-5, catatonia is a specifier across several disorders (three or more of twelve signs), plus catatonic disorder due to another medical condition and unspecified catatonia; not an independent disorder or a subtype of bipolar disorder. Treat first with lorazepam, then ECT. asked once
  23. The mesocortical pathway (ventral tegmental area to prefrontal cortex) is underactive in schizophrenia, linked to negative and cognitive symptoms; the tuberoinfundibular pathway (hypothalamus to median eminence and pituitary) controls prolactin, which rises when its D2 receptors are blocked. asked once
  24. The original support for the dopamine hypothesis: the clinical potency of typical antipsychotics correlates with their affinity for D2 receptors (Philip Seeman and colleagues; Ian Creese, David Burt and Solomon Snyder; both 1976). asked once
  25. Erotomanic delusional disorder (de Clérambault's syndrome): the belief that a person, often famous or of higher status, is secretly in love with the patient. Jealous type is the Othello syndrome, somatic type includes delusional parasitosis (Ekbom), and persecutory is the commonest type. asked once
  26. Schizoaffective disorder (DSM-5-TR): a major mood episode concurrent with Criterion A of schizophrenia, plus at least 2 weeks of delusions or hallucinations without a major mood episode, with mood episodes present for the majority of the illness; bipolar and depressive types; term coined by Jacob Kasanin (1933). asked once
